<note>original: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA". The term "Hermetic" refers to the secret philosophical and alchemical traditions attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure who was believed to have lived in ancient Egypt.</note>

<note>The central image on this title page is an emblem of a "Pelican in her piety." In this traditional symbolic scene, a mother pelican is depicted feeding her young with blood from her own breast. While commonly a Christian symbol of sacrifice, in this alchemical context, it represents the process of "nourishing" the matter during the Great Work and the life-giving nature of the philosopher's stone. Below this image is a heraldic shield displaying four red roses, a clear reference to the Rosy Cross or Rosicrucianism—a secret spiritual and cultural movement that emerged in early 17th-century Europe.</note>

<summary>This frontispiece introduces a treatise on "Hermetic Philosophy," using the symbolic "Pelican in her piety" and the Rosy Cross to establish its roots in alchemical and Rosicrucian traditions.</summary>

# ATALANTA FLEEING,
-> that is, <-
# NEW EMBLEMS
## CONCERNING THE CHEMICAL <note>original: "Chymica." In the 17th century, the boundary between alchemy and chemistry was fluid; here it refers to the "Great Work" of transforming matter and the soul.</note> SECRETS OF NATURE,

<margin>Hercules <note>The hero who stole the golden apples from the Hesperides.</note></margin>
Adapted partly for the eyes and the intellect, with copper-plate engravings and added sentences <note>or mottos</note>, epigrams, and notes; and partly for the ears and the recreation of the mind with approximately 50 musical <term>Fugues</term> <note>A musical composition where a theme is introduced by one voice and then taken up by others.</note> for three voices, two of which correspond to one simple melody suitable for singing in distichs <note>Two-line verses.</note>; to be seen, read, meditated upon, understood, judged, sung, and heard, not without singular delight.

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page follows the title page of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens" (1618). Having introduced the work's title and its multidisciplinary nature (images, music, and text), Maier now provides an introductory poem—an "Epigram"—that explains the mythological allegory of Atalanta and Hippomenes and how it relates to the alchemical process and the structure of this book.</meta>

# EPIGRAM OF THE AUTHOR.

*The diligent youth <note>Hippomenes.</note> carried away the prize of the Hesperian garden,*
*As the goddess Cypris <note>original: "Cypride." An epithet for Venus, who was famously born from the sea foam near Cyprus.</note> gave him the triple apple:*
*Following the fleeing maiden, he rolled it from the ground before her face,*
*And by this means, she draws out her slow delays:*
*Soon he shines, and soon she shines—she who was once swifter than the East Winds—*
*As he scatters the golden gifts upon the soil.*
*He delayed the slow footsteps of the girl,*
*But she, in turn, turns her back in flight,*
*Until the lover repeated the third weight; then yielded*
*ATALANTA to her victor as his reward.*

*Hippomenes is the power of sulfur; she is that of fleeing*
*Mercury <note>original: "Mercurii." In alchemy, "Philosophical Mercury" represents the volatile, feminine principle, while "Philosophical Sulfur" represents the fixed, masculine principle.</note>; in the race, the woman is overcome by the man.*
*After they embrace each other with greedy love,*
*Anger reproves the Goddess <note>Cybele, the Mother Goddess.</note> in her temple;*
*The avenger clothed them both in a lion’s skin,*
*Thereafter they are red of body, and they are fierce.*

*So that my Muse might express the most faithful images of this race,*
*She gives you the flights <term>Fugues</term> in three voices:*
*One voice remains simple and represents the delaying apple,*
*But the second is fleeing, and the third is duly following.*
*Let these Emblems stand before your ears and eyes,*
*But let reason seek out the secret marks within them:*
*I have brought these things to the senses so that the intellect might seize,*
*By those attractions, the precious things that lie hidden.*
*Whatever wealth the world holds, or medicine for health,*
*The twin Lion <note>In alchemical symbolism, the "Lion" often refers to the prepared material; the "twin lion" represents the union of the fixed and the volatile (Sulfur and Mercury) reached at the end of the Great Work.</note> is able to provide it all.*

<summary>In this introductory epigram, Michael Maier explains the alchemical allegory of Atalanta's race: Hippomenes represents Sulfur and Atalanta represents Mercury. He also explains the book's musical structure, where three voices in a fugue mirror the three characters and the golden apples of the myth.</summary>

<keywords>Atalanta, Hippomenes, Venus, Sulfur, Mercury, Alchemy, Fugue, Cybele, Lion, Symbolism</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Following the introductory Epigram which explained the alchemical allegory of Atalanta and Hippomenes through the metaphor of musical fugues, Michael Maier now presents the formal dedication of the work. As was customary for 17th-century scholars, he addresses the governing body of an important city—in this case, Mühlhausen—and a specific legal official, seeking their patronage and connecting his intellectual labor to classical traditions of civic virtue and divine wisdom.</meta>

->To the Most Distinguished, Most Celebrated, and Most Excellent<-

# OF MÜHLHAUSEN

## AN IMPERIAL CITY IN THURINGIA

### TO THE SENATORIAL ORDER OF THE REPUBLIC,

->To the Men most outstanding in Virtue, Learning, and true Nobility<-
->of soul;<-

->As well as to the most vigilant Syndic <note>A 'syndic' was a high-ranking municipal magistrate or legal representative for a city.</note> of the same city,<-

# CHRISTOPHER
## REINART, Doctor of both Laws, <note>Doctor of both Civil and Canon Law.</note>
->Most Learned Counselor to his Imperial Majesty,<-
->most delightful, etc.<-

*To all and each of these his Lords, ever to be honorably revered, he most dutifully declares, dedicates, and offers this whatever <term>token</term> <note>original: "tecmerion." From the Greek τεκμήριον (tekmerion), meaning a sign, proof, or sure evidence of something.</note> of his benevolence and gratitude.*

->MICHAEL MAIER, Doctor of Medicine, Count of the Imperial Consistory, Imperial Count Palatine.<-

Most distinguished and most learned men, they tell a story concerning that TRIPOD <note>A three-legged sacrificial altar or seat associated with Apollo, the god of prophecy and music.</note> given as a gift by Vulcan <note>The god of fire and metalworking.</note> to Pelops when he was betrothed to Hippodamia, the daughter of Oenomaus, King of Elis. They say that, since it was a work of singular craftsmanship, it was later offered again by Pelops to the Pythian Apollo at Delphi. From this tripod, thereafter, the oracles of that God were issued by a maiden through his divine inspiration—

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens" to the city council of Mühlhausen and the jurist Christopher Reinart. Maier frames his dedication using the mythology of the Tripod of Pelops, connecting his alchemical and musical work to the divine craftsmanship of Vulcan and the oracles of Apollo.</summary>

<keywords>dedication, Mühlhausen, Thuringia, Christopher Reinart, Michael Maier, Pelops, Apollo, Delphi, alchemy, patronage</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the formal dedication to the city officials of Mühlhausen. Michael Maier is transitioning from the classical myth of Vulcan’s tripod (begun on the previous page) to his personal reasons for dedicating this specific work, *Atalanta Fugeins*, to these patrons, citing his past service to Emperor Rudolph II.</meta>

<header>E P I S T O L A</header>

handed down: So, when this TRIPOD, fashioned by Vulcan, came into my hands, moved by the example of Pelops, I decided to consecrate and offer it to a most worthy place and order. I do this indeed before all others to your Magnificences and Excellencies—not indeed with the intention that Oracles should be drawn from it (although even these, of an alchemical <note>original: "chymica." Referring to the "secrets" or "oracles" of alchemy contained in the book.</note> nature, might not be found wanting here), but rather that I might publicly testify in some way to the readiness of my mind and my benevolent will toward you. A few years ago, while I was passing through—at that time serving as a medical counselor to His Imperial Majesty **RUDOLPH II** of blessed memory—you wished to declare to me, his servant, the disposition you held toward your Lord: namely, an excellent one, most worthy of your high standing. Since that time, I have not only honored your virtues among foreigners with words as much as I could, but I have also focused on this: to reveal my mind more clearly through the work itself and to pour it out more abundantly. Since I sensed that this could not be done otherwise than through some small literary gift <note>original: "Xeniolo." A Greek-derived term for a guest-gift or a token of hospitality.</note>, and having spent no small effort on this *Atalanta Fleeing*, I wished to dedicate all of it, whatever it may be, to your Magnificences and Excellencies. In this, I have imitated the writers of our own and ancient times, who never wished to go out among the public or "walk through the mouths of men" <note>A classical idiom for achieving fame or public reputation.</note> without a Scipio <note>Maier uses Scipio (the Roman general) as a metaphor for a powerful and virtuous patron who protects the author's reputation from critics.</note> as a leader or companion. For if they had fallen, who would have helped them? I ask that I may be allowed to call you the Patrons of this little work—not because you have applied your hand or mind to this truly uncertain study <note>Referring to the difficult and often disparaged art of alchemy.</note>, as far as I know,

-># D E D I C A T O R Y <-

(for there was not so much leisure time left over from my most serious business) but because you seemed to me sufficient to protect any branch of learning, and no other subject matter, whether more worthy or more honorable (excluding its abuse <note>Maier likely refers to the "abuse" or "misuse" of alchemical secrets by charlatans, a common theme in his work where he distinguishes "true" alchemy from "false" chemistry.</note>), occurred to me for the time. Whatever it may be, you will—as I know you will—judge my efforts not by the cheapness of the paper, but by the sincerity <note>original: "candore." Literally "whiteness" or "purity," a common Latin metaphor for honest, transparent intent.</note> of my mind; and you will henceforth hold and count me among the most devoted servants of your Excellencies <note>original: "Amplituddd." --- Page 15 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page begins the formal "Preface to the Reader" following the dedication to the officials of Mühlhausen. Michael Maier moves from civic addresses to a philosophical justification for the study of chemistry (alchemy), grounded in the nature of man and the divine order of the universe.</meta>

## Preface to the Reader.

*It is acknowledged by all, honest reader, that Man, in his very composition, reflects a summary of the universe <note>original: "univerſi compendium." This refers to the concept of the Microcosm, the idea that the human being contains all the elements and principles of the greater world (the Macrocosm).</note>, and that he is destined to live three kinds of life: namely, the vegetative in the mother’s womb, where he grows and increases just like a plant; the sensible <note>Meaning the life of the senses.</note> in this world, where for the most part he is led by the senses like other animals—from which he differs because he begins to use his intellect, albeit imperfectly; and finally the intellectual, in the other world with God and the intelligences or good Angels who assist Him. In this present life, the more one approaches the divine nature, the more one rejoices and delights in things to be investigated by the intellect—those things that are subtle, wonderful, and rare. And conversely, the more one sinks toward the level of cattle <note>original: "pecuinuum genus." Refers to beast-like or animalistic nature.</note>, the less he is captured by these things, and is instead bound to the more bodily senses. We see examples of both: some, as the more learned, are devoted to being polished by the arts and sciences; while many more are devoted to these things: namely, the pleasures of the body, lust, gluttony, external pomp, and similar pursuits. However, for the cultivation of the intellect, God has hidden infinite secrets in nature, which are struck out through innumerable sciences and arts—like fire from flint <note>A common metaphor for the hard work required to extract hidden truths from nature.</note>—and are transferred into practical use. Among these are the Chemical*

<meta>The catchword "ſecre-" at the bottom of the page indicates the next word is "ſecreta" (secrets).</meta>

<summary>Michael Maier introduces the "Preface to the Reader," arguing that humans are a microcosm of the universe who progress through vegetative, sensible, and intellectual stages of life. He asserts that the pursuit of hidden natural secrets, such as those in chemistry, is a divine calling that separates the learned from those who live purely by their animal senses.</summary>

<keywords>Microcosm, three stages of life, intellect, divinity, human nature, secrets of nature, chemistry, Michael Maier</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the "Preface to the Reader." Michael Maier is explaining that the secrets of nature (specifically alchemical secrets) are intellectual pursuits that require deep study before one ever attempts practical laboratory work. He is now transitioning into how these secrets relate to the arts of sight and sound, particularly Music.</meta>

->TO THE READER.<-

*...secrets, which are not the least, but after the investigation of things divine, are the first and most precious of all. These are not intended for street-vending deceivers and pseudo-chemical frauds <note>original: "fucis pseudo-chymicis." The author is distinguishing true alchemy from the "puffers" or con artists who sought only to make gold.</note> (who in these matters are like asses at the lyre <note>A classical proverb (asinus ad lyram) describing someone who is tone-deaf to art or lacks the natural ability to understand a subject.</note>, being utterly estranged from all good teaching and intention), but rather for higher intellects, liberally educated and born to investigate greater things. Since these secrets are exceedingly subtle, majestic, sacred, rare, and hidden, they must therefore be grasped by the intellect before the senses—rather through profound contemplation from the reading of authors and the comparison of those authors with each other and with the works of nature, than through sensory operation or manual experimentation, which is blind without a preceding Theory.*

*Directly after these intellectual sciences are numbered those which deal with visible and audible objects, such as Optics (or perspective) and Painting—which is called by some poets "silent poetry" <note>Maier alludes to the famous phrase "ut pictura poesis," suggesting that painting and poetry share the same soul.</note>, just as poetry is, conversely, a "speaking painting"—and likewise Music, whether vocal or instrumental. In this art, the ancient Philosophers exercised themselves so much that a man was considered unlearned and was forced to "sing to the myrtle" <note>original: "ad myrtū canere." At ancient Greek banquets, those who could not play the lyre were expected to hold a branch of myrtle and sing a song instead, a sign of lower social or intellectual standing.</note> if he had refused the lyre at banquets, as is told of Themistocles <note>A famous Athenian general who was reportedly embarrassed by his lack of musical training.</note>. Socrates was instructed in Music, as was Plato himself, who determined that a man is not harmonically composed if he does not rejoice in musical harmony. Pythagoras was also very famous in this art; he is said to have used musical symphony in the morning and evening to compose <note>meaning to calm or organize.</note> the souls of his disciples.*

*For Music has this peculiar power: it can excite or soothe the passions <note>original: "affectus."</note> according to the different musical mode used. For instance, the Phrygian Mode <note>One of the ancient Greek musical scales, traditionally associated with energy, excitement, and martial spirit.</note> was called "warlike" among the Greeks because it was played when entering war and battle, and it possessed a singular power to stir the souls of soldiers; in its place today, the Ionic mode <note>Another musical scale, which Maier suggests has taken over the role of the Phrygian in his contemporary era.</note> is used for the same purpose, which formerly...*

<meta>catchword: ama-</meta>

<summary>Maier argues that alchemical secrets are intellectual and sacred, requiring study over "blind" experimentation. He links the pursuit of these secrets to the liberal arts, specifically highlighting the philosophical and psychological importance of Music as practiced by Pythagoras and Plato.</summary>
<keywords>pseudo-chemistry, theory vs practice, optics, painting, music, Socrates, Plato, Pythagoras, musical modes, Phrygian mode, Ionic mode</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, Michael Maier argued that the secrets of alchemy are intellectual and should be studied through contemplation and theory before manual work. He began discussing how these secrets are linked to the arts of sight (Painting) and sound (Music), mentioning the profound effect music had on the ancient Philosophers.</meta>

->### PREFACE<-

*...was esteemed as amatory (just as the Phrygian mode <note>Ancient musical "modes" or scales were believed to evoke specific emotions; the Phrygian was often associated with excitement or passion.</note> is considered now), from which we infer that the modes were exchanged with one another. They say Timotheus of Miletus used the Phrygian mode to make Alexander the Great more eager and spirited in matters of war—a fact which Cicero mentions in the second book of* On the Laws. *But Terpander of Lesbos used the Ionian mode; he was summoned by the Spartans when they were rioting in civil strife, and by the sweetness of his song, he soothed their minds to such an extent that they returned to friendship and ceased their rebellion. From this, Lesbian singers always earned first prize in the judgment of the Spartans. Concerning Music, Fabius <note>original: "Fabius," referring to the Roman rhetorician Quintilian (Marcus Fabius Quintilianus).</note> says thus: "Music is an honorable and pleasant delight, most worthy of liberal minds." Therefore, so that we might have these three objects of the more spiritual senses—namely sight, hearing, and the intellect itself—in a single view and embrace, and instill them more effectively into the mind all at once, behold: we have joined Optics with Music, and sense with intellect. That is to say, we have joined things rare to see and hear with chemical emblems, which are proper to this science. For if other arts publish emblems concerning morals or matters other than the secrets of nature, this seems foreign to their scope and aim, since they wish and ought to be understood by everyone. Not so with Chemistry <note>original: "Chemia." In this context, Maier refers to Alchemy, the "Hermetic" art of nature's secrets.</note>, which, like a chaste virgin behind a lattice, or like Diana <note>The goddess Diana was famously seen bathing by Actaeon; here Maier suggests alchemy hides its "naked" truth behind layers of allegory.</note> herself, must not be seen without a garment of varied colors, for reasons expressed elsewhere. These four things, I say—the fictional, poetic, and allegorical; the painted Emblems, engraved upon Venus (or copper) <term>Venus: The alchemical and astrological symbol for the metal copper.</term>, not without "Venus" (or grace); the most secret Chemical matters to be investigated by the intellect; and finally, the rarer Musical pieces—take these all at once in this single book and apply them to your use. If your use be intellectual rather than merely sensory, it will be all the more [useful]...*

<meta>catchword: lior (from "utilior," meaning "more useful")</meta>

<summary>Maier explains his decision to combine music, emblems (optics), and poetry in this volume, arguing that alchemy—unlike other arts—must remain veiled in allegory and "garments of varied colors." He cites historical examples of music's power to influence the soul, from Alexander the Great to Spartan civil unrest, to justify the inclusion of musical scores.</summary>
<keywords>Phrygian mode, Timotheus of Miletus, Alexander the Great, Terpander of Lesbos, Quintilian, Optics, Emblems, Alchemy, Diana, Venus, Copper, Intellectual vs Sensory</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, Michael Maier explained that by combining images (Optics) and Music with alchemical theory, he aims to engage both the senses and the intellect simultaneously. He argued that alchemy should not be a purely manual craft but a deeply intellectual pursuit that benefits from the beauty of the arts.</meta>

->### TO THE READER.<-

*...will eventually be more fruitful and pleasant. If, however, it is first subjected to the sense, there is no doubt that a transition will be made from the sense to the intellect, as if through a doorway. For nothing is said to be in the intellect that did not first enter through some sense, since the intellect of a newborn human is considered to be like a* <term>tabula rasa</term> <note>original: "tabulæ rasæ"; a Latin phrase meaning "scraped tablet" or "blank slate," a philosophical concept that the mind is born without innate ideas.</note> *upon which nothing has yet been written, but anything one wishes can be inscribed by the mediation of the senses, acting like a stylus. It is a common saying that* <note>original: "Ignoti nulla cupido"</note> *there is no desire for what is unknown, because it is necessary that the senses, acting as scouts and messengers, first bring every knowable thing to the intellect—the dictator and judge—and make it known, just like sentinels keeping watch at the gates of a city (their own organs). Regarding the reason for the title of these emblems, I will add a few words so that it does not seem monstrous or out of place to you. Atalanta was celebrated by the Poets for her flight <note>In Greek mythology, Atalanta was a swift-footed huntress who challenged her suitors to a footrace.</note>, by which she outran all her suitors in competition; therefore, death was the fate of those defeated for the sake of the virgin, who was the prize offered for victory. This continued until she was surpassed and won by Hippomenes, a daring and far-sighted youth, who threw down three golden apples* <note>original: "malo aureo"</note> *one by one during the race. While she stopped to pick them up, he overtook her and reached the goal first. Just as this Atalanta flees, so one musical voice always flees before another, and the other follows like Hippomenes. Yet, in the third voice they are stabilized and fixed, which is simple and of a single value, just like a golden apple. This same virgin is purely Chemical* <note>original: "Chymica," referring to the art of Alchemy.</note>—*namely, she is the Philosophical Mercury* <note>The "volatile" or elusive principle in alchemy.</note> *fixed and held back in her flight by the golden sulfur. If anyone knows how to stop her, he will have the bride he seeks; if not, he will face the loss of his property and his own ruin. From Hippomenes and Atalanta coming together in the temple of the Mother of the Gods* <note>original: "Matris Deûm," referring to Cybele; in alchemy, this signifies the sealed vessel or "philosopher's egg."</note>, *that is, the vessel, they become lions, or rather, they acquire a red color <note>A reference to the "rubedo" or reddening, the final stage of the alchemical Great Work.</note>. He also obtained...*

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: these</meta>

<summary>Maier explains the Aristotelian theory that all knowledge begins in the senses and uses the myth of Atalanta and Hippomenes as a complex allegory for the musical and chemical processes of alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>tabula rasa, empiricism, Atalanta, Hippomenes, golden apples, Philosophical Mercury, Sulfur, rubedo, alchemical vessel</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, Michael Maier introduced the mythological figure of Atalanta, focusing on her famous footrace and the three golden apples used by Hippomenes to distract and eventually defeat her. This page continues her story, transitioning from her physical exploits to the intellectual and musical structure of the book.</meta>

-># P R E F A C E <-

*this virgin won victory over men by killing a boar of extraordinary size <note>original: "apro quodam insignis magnitudinis"; a reference to the Calydonian Boar, a monster in Greek mythology sent by Artemis to ravage the region of Calydon</note>, for which she was rewarded with a prize by Meleager. At Psythaeum, in the shrine of Aesculapius <note>The Greco-Roman god of medicine and healing.</note>, she drew water from a struck rock, which she drank when thirsty. Since these things are truly allegorical and emblematic and in no way historical, I wished to title this intellectual and emblematic treatise in commemoration of this heroine, especially since the apples thrown to her from the Hesperian gardens <note>The garden of the Hesperides, where Hera’s golden apples grew, granting immortality.</note> by Venus, the goddess of sweetness, were provided to Hippomenes. In those measures or musical fugues <term>fugis Musicis</term> <note>In this context, these are "canons," where one voice follows another, mimicking a chase—perfectly representing the race between Atalanta and Hippomenes.</note>, you see this observed: that individual couplets <term>disticha</term> <note>A pair of poetic lines; Maier's book features fifty of these, each set to music.</note> adapted to those three voices can be fittingly expressed through singing. Since so many variations of fugues are accommodated to one simple voice, every wise and intelligent person will take in good part and value this emblematic figuration, both in these musical pieces and in the adaptation of the couplets to the individual voices. For if some merchants value and buy a piece of artistic painting for a great sum of money, where only the eyes are deceived because they judge the art to be closest to nature, why should not the learned hold in high price and value these figures? For they serve both the intellect and several senses in such a way that great utility is to be hoped for, besides the delight they provide. Farewell.*

<meta>catchword: E P I-</meta>

<summary>Michael Maier explains that the myth of Atalanta is an allegory rather than history, justifying his use of her name for this multimedia work. He describes how the book combines poetry, music (in the form of three-voice fugues), and imagery to engage both the senses and the mind.</summary>

<keywords>Atalanta, Meleager, Calydonian Boar, Aesculapius, Hesperian apples, musical fugue, canons, alchemy, allegory, emblems, three voices</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the frontispiece for the work, featuring a portrait of the author, Michael Maier. It follows the preface where Maier explains the allegorical nature of the Atalanta myth and the musical structure of the book. This page establishes Maier's professional credentials, his standing at the Imperial court, and his personal spiritual motto.</meta>

<margin>
AT THE AGE 
OF 49. 
YEAR 1617.
</margin>

->THREE SCHOOLS GAVE THREE TITLES, CAESAR GAVE THREE; THESE REMAIN FOR ME:<-
->TO BE ABLE TO LIVE WELL IN CHRIST, AND TO BE ABLE TO DIE. <note>original: "Tres scholæ, tres Cæsar titulos dedit; hæc mihi restant, Posse bene in Christo vivere, posse mori." A personal motto reflecting that despite his many worldly honors, his faith is his ultimate security.</note><-
->MICHAEL MAIER, <term>COUNT OF THE IMPERIAL CONSISTORY</term> <note>original: "Comes Imperialis Consistorii"; a high-ranking title granted by the Holy Roman Emperor, indicating Maier served as a legal and administrative official at court.</note> etc: DOCTOR OF PHILOSOPHY AND MEDICINE, <term>IMPERIAL POET LAUREATE</term> <note>original: "Poeta Caesareus Coronatus" (P.C.C.); an honor bestowed for excellence in Latin poetry.</note>, <term>EXEMPT NOBLEMAN</term> <note>original: "Nobilis Exemptus"; a status of nobility that granted him exemption from certain local taxes or legal jurisdictions.</note>, FORMERLY <term>PHYSICIAN TO THE EMPEROR</term> <note>original: "Medicus Caesareus"; Maier served as the personal physician to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II, a famous patron of the occult and scientific arts.</note> etc:<-

<summary>This frontispiece presents the formal portrait and professional titles of Michael Maier, emphasizing his transition from a celebrated court physician and scholar to a man focused on his spiritual well-being. It establishes his authority as an expert in philosophy, medicine, and poetry before the reader begins the alchemical emblems.</summary>

->I. German translation of the Latin Epigram.<-

The fruit in the belly of the wind, which still lives hidden,
Once it is raised up into this light,
It can far surpass the counsel and deeds of all high heroes
Through art and strong power and the labor of its body;
See to it that he is not born improperly before his time,
But rather comes living onto the earth in due measure.

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>EMBLEM I. On the secrets of Nature.</header>

->The wind has carried him in its belly.<-

## EPIGRAM I.

The embryo who is enclosed in the windy womb of **BOREAS** <note>original: "Boreae"; the North Wind, personified in the accompanying engraving as the source of the "philosophical child"</note>,
  *If once he shall have been born alive into this light;*
This one is able to surpass all the labors of *Heroes* <note>original: "Heroum"; referring to legendary figures like Hercules, suggesting the alchemical "Stone" is more powerful than physical strength</note>
  *By his art, his hand, his strong body, and his mind.*
Let him not be a *Caeso* <term>Caeso: a child delivered by cutting the mother's womb, or a Cæsarean birth; here implying an unnatural or forced alchemical process</term> for you, nor that useless miscarriage,
  *Nor an Agrippa* <term>Agrippa: in Roman tradition, a child born feet-first, signifying a difficult or ill-omened beginning</term>, *but one born under a favorable star.*

<sig>B 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: HER-</meta>

<note>Vocabulary: Embryo, the North Wind, Hero, Cæsarean-born, Feet-first-born (difficult birth), Secrets of Nature.</note>

<summary>This page presents the first epigram of the collection, detailing the birth of the alchemical "child" from the wind. It warns the practitioner that the "Stone" must be born naturally and under the right conditions, rather than being forced or arriving in an ill-omened manner.</summary>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page begins the first discourse (Discursus I), providing a philosophical and scientific commentary on the first emblem. It follows the previous page which presented the epigram about the birth of the alchemical child from the wind. This section focuses on interpreting the specific line from the Emerald Tablet: "The wind carried him in its belly."</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE I</header>

Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus: a legendary Hellenistic figure viewed as the founder of alchemy and author of the 'Emerald Tablet.'</note>, the most diligent investigator of every natural secret, graphically although briefly describes the work of nature in his **Emerald Tablet** <note>original: "tabula sua smaragdina"; one of the most famous foundational texts of Western alchemy</note>, where among other things he says: *The wind carried him in its belly*, as if he were to say: He whose father is the **Sun** and whose mother is the **Moon**, before he is brought forth into the light, shall be carried by windy vapors, just like a bird in the air while it flies. Furthermore, from these vapors or winds (which are nothing other than moved air) when they are coagulated, water is made; and from this water, when mixed with earth, all minerals and metals are formed. Indeed, it is established that these very things consist of and are immediately coagulated from vapors. Whether, therefore, it is placed in water or in vapor, the matter returns to the same thing, because each is the material of the wind. The same must be said of minerals and metals, though more remotely.

<margin>Lullius <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1316): a major philosopher and purported alchemist whose works were highly influential in the 17th century.</note> in the same place: "the wind carries him in its belly, that is, sulfur is carried in quicksilver [mercury]." And Chapter 47: "The Stone is fire carried in the belly of the air."</margin>

It is asked, however: who is he who must be carried by the wind? I answer: *Chemically*, it is sulfur, which is carried in quicksilver <note>original: "argento vivo"; literally 'living silver,' the common term for mercury</note>, as Lullius testifies in the *Codicil*, chapter 32, and all others as well. *Physically*, it is the fetus which must soon be born into the light. I say also *Arithmetically*, that it is the **Cube root**; *Musically*, that it is the **Double Octave** <term>Disdiapason: a musical interval spanning two octaves, representing harmony across a wide range</term>; *Geometrically*, that it is the **point** which is the beginning of a flowing line; *Astronomically*, the **center** of the planets Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars. Although these are different subjects, nevertheless, if they are well compared among themselves, they will easily demonstrate the "offspring of the wind," which must be left to the greater or lesser industry of anyone [studying them]. 

But to explain the matter more plainly: All **Mercury** is composed of vapors, that is, of water lifting the earth with it into an aerial rarity, and of earth forcing the air to return into a watery earth or earthy water. For since the **Elements** are in it through all things—both mixed and trampled and reduced together into a certain viscous nature—they do not easily recede from one another. Instead, either the volatile parts follow the higher parts, or they remain below among the fixed parts. The first of these appears in common Mercury; the second appears in the **Philosophical Mercury** and the fixed metals. In these latter, the fixed elements predominate over the volatile; in the former, the volatile predominate over the fixed. Truly, it is not without cause why **Mercury** is said and held to be the messenger, interpreter, and as it were, the running intermediate servant of the rest of the Gods, with wings adapted to his head and feet. For he is windy and flies through the air like the wind itself, as is indeed proved in common experience to the loss of many... 

<sig>C 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: vincitur (continues the thought that Mercury "is conquered" or "bound")</meta>

<summary>In this discourse, Maier explains the alchemical meaning of the "wind" from the Emerald Tablet, identifying it as the carrier of the philosophical child (sulfur within mercury). He links this process to various disciplines like arithmetic, music, and astronomy to show the universal nature of this "offspring."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the first emblem of Atalanta Fugiens, specifically expanding on the Hermetic phrase "The wind carried him in its belly." It connects mythological figures like Mercury, Boreas, and the Argonauts to alchemical processes of distillation and purification.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
### DISCOURSE I.

is bound: But because he carries a caduceum <note>original: "caduceum"; the staff of Hermes, entwined by two serpents</note> encircled by two serpents crosswise, who leads souls out of bodies and brings them back, and effects many contrary things of this kind, he expresses the symbol of **Philosophical Mercury** <term>Philosophical Mercury: not common quicksilver, but the "universal solvent" or refined spirit sought by alchemists</term> best. Therefore Mercury is the wind, which receives the sulfur or *Dionysus*, or, if you prefer, *Aesculapius* <note>Dionysus and Aesculapius were both "twice-born" or rescued from a dying mother, paralleling the alchemical extraction of sulfur from its "mother" substance</note>—an as yet imperfect fetus—from the maternal womb, or even from the ashes of the maternal body burned, and carries it to where it can be matured. 

And the Embryo is **Sulfur**, which is infused by the celestial **Sun** into the North wind (*Boreas*), so that it may bring forth what is matured; he who, when the time of his gestation is finished, gives birth to twins: one with white hair, called *Calais*, the other with red, *Zetes*. These were the sons of *Boreas* (as *Orpheus* the chemical poet writes) who were with *Jason* among the *Argonauts* to carry away the **Golden Fleece** from the *Colchians*. 

However, *Phineus* the blind seer, infested by the *Harpies*, could not be freed except by these sons of *Boreas*: for which benefit obtained from them, he, grateful to the *Argonauts*, declared the whole nature of the way. But the *Harpies* are nothing other than corrupting sulfur, which is driven away by the sons of *Boreas* when they have come to the right age; and from being imperfect or troubled by noxious volatiles, it becomes perfect and no longer subject to that evil, which then indicates the way to obtaining the **Golden Fleece** for *Jason* the physician <note>The "Golden Fleece" is a common metaphor for the Philosopher's Stone; Jason's name is linked to the Greek 'iasthai' meaning 'to heal.'</note>. These winds are also mentioned by our **Basil Valentine**, among others, who clearly says in his sixth [key]:

->*For a double wind must come, called Vulturnus <note>The East-Southeast wind</note>, and then a single one, called Notus <note>The South wind</note>, which will blow impetuously from the east and south; when their motion ceases, so that water is made from air, you may boldly trust that what is spiritual will become corporeal.*<-

And **Ripley** in his 8th Gate <note>referring to *The Compound of Alchemy* by George Ripley</note> says our infant must be reborn in the air, that is, in the belly of the wind. To the same sense, the *Ladder of the Philosophers* <note>original: "scala philos."; an alchemical treatise</note> says in its 6th step: *And it should be known that the offspring of the wise is born in the air.* And in step 8: *The aerial spirits climbing together into the air love one another; as Hermes says, the wind carried him in its belly.* Because the generation of our offspring happens in the air, and the one being born in the air is born wisely: *For he ascends from the earth to heaven, and descends again to the earth, acquiring the power of the things above and the things below.* <note>A direct quote from the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, describing the circulation of the "Stone" through distillation.</note>

<summary>This page uses the myth of Boreas’s sons (Calais and Zetes) and the Harpies to explain how volatile spirits (Mercury) purify the alchemical sulfur to achieve the Golden Fleece (the Stone). It cites authorities like Basil Valentine and George Ripley to emphasize that the alchemical "infant" must be matured and reborn within the element of Air.</summary>

is conquered: But because he carries a **caduceum** <note>the herald's staff of Mercury</note> encircled by two crossing serpents, which leads souls out of bodies and brings them back, and performs many contrary things of this kind, he expresses the symbol of **Philosophical Mercury** extremely well. **Mercury**, therefore, is the wind, which receives the **sulfur**—or **Dionysus**, or if you prefer, **Aesculapius** <note>Dionysus and Aesculapius were both "twice-born" or rescued from their mothers' bodies, serving here as symbols for the alchemical substance</note>—as a still-imperfect fetus from the mother's womb, or even from the ashes of the mother's burnt body, and carries it to that place where it can be matured. For which favor obtained from them, he, being grateful, revealed the whole nature of the journey to the Argonauts. But the **Harpies** are nothing other than corrupting sulfur, which is driven away by the sons of Boreas when they have reached the proper age; and from the imperfect state, or that troubled by harmful flying creatures, it becomes perfect and not subject to that evil, which then points out the way for **Jason** the physician <note>The name Jason, *Iasōn*, was often linked by alchemists to the Greek word *iasis*, meaning "healing."</note> to obtain the Golden Fleece. Our own **Basil** <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist</note> also mentions these ancients, who says clearly in his sixth treatise:

> For a twin wind must come, called **Vulturnus** <note>a dry wind from the East-South-East</note>, and then a simple one, called **Notus** <note>the South Wind</note>, which will blow impetuously from the east and south. When their motion ceases, so that water has been made from air, you may confidently trust that from the spiritual, the corporal will be made.

And **Ripley** <note>Sir George Ripley, a famous 15th-century English alchemist</note> in his eighth "Gate" says that our infant must be reborn in the air, that is, in the belly of the wind. To the same sense, the *Ladder of Philosophers* <note>original: "scala philos."; an alchemical text titled *Scala Philosophorum*</note> in its sixth step [says]:

> And it must be known, it says, that the offspring of the wise is born in the air. And at the eighth step: Aerial spirits climbing together into the air love one another, as **Hermes** says: *the wind carried him in its belly*: Because the generation of our offspring takes place in the air, and being born in the air, he is born wisely. For he ascends from the earth to heaven, and descends again to earth, gathering the superior and inferior force.

### II. German version of the Latin Epigram.

**R**omulus was suckled by a wolf, but Jupiter
->By a goat, as rumor testifies.<-
What wonder is it, if we say that the Child of the Sages <term>Child of the Sages: the developing Philosophers' Stone, which must be "nourished" by the proper elemental environment</term> is nourished
->By the Earth, who has granted him her milk?<-

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>EMBLEM II. Concerning the secrets of Nature.</header>
<sig>C</sig>

# The earth is its nurse.
<note>This title is the second line of the "Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus," a foundational text for alchemists. It suggests that after the "wind" has carried the alchemical embryo, the "earth" provides the physical nourishment and stability required for its growth.</note>

## EPIGRAM II.

**R**omulus is said to have pressed the shaggy teats of a wolf, but
*Jupiter those of a goat, and credit is given to these deeds:*
What wonder is it, if we say the EARTH has nourished the tender vitals of the OFFSPRING OF THE WISE <note>original: "SAPIENTUM PROLIS." In alchemical literature, this "offspring" or "child" represents the burgeoning Philosopher's Stone or the purified matter in the flask.</note> with its own milk?
If a small beast fed such great Heroes,
How GREAT <note>original: "QUANTUS"</note> shall he be, whose NURSE is the TERRESTRIAL GLOBE?

<meta>catchword: Among</meta>

<summary>This page presents the second emblem's poetic epigram, which uses classical mythology (the nursing of Romulus and Jupiter) as a metaphor for the Earth nourishing the "Offspring of the Wise," or the Philosopher’s Stone.</summary>

<keywords>Romulus, Jupiter, breastfeeding, nursing, Earth as nurse, Emerald Tablet, alchemical child, Michael Maier, Philosopher's Stone, nourishment</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page begins the "Discourse" (Discursus) for the second emblem. It continues the exploration of the "Earth as nurse" theme by drawing parallels between Aristotelian physics, human embryology, and the alchemical process.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE II.</header>
<sig>C 2</sig>

->Among the **Peripatetics** <note>The followers of Aristotle, who typically walked about while teaching.</note> and rightly judging **Philosophers**, it is established that the **Nourishing substance** is turned into the substance of the nourished, and is assimilated to it—not indeed before, but after its alteration; and this is held as a most true **Axiom**. For how could the nourishing substance, if it were already similar and identical to the nourished, have any need for a change of its essence? If such a change occurred, it would no longer remain the same nor similar. Or how could those things which cannot be assimilated to the nourished be taken as nourishment, such as wood, stones, and other things of that kind? Therefore, just as the first scenario is useless, the second is contrary to nature.<-

That a newborn human is nourished by the milk of animals is not foreign to nature, because its assimilation can occur, but it is far easier with maternal milk than with that of another. From this, physicians conclude that for health, for the similarity of substance and character, and for strength, it benefits the infant if it is always fostered and raised on its own mother’s milk; and on the contrary, if it is raised on a stranger's milk, it is hindered. This is the harmony of all nature: that like delights in its like, and follows its footsteps as much as it can in all things, out of a certain silent consent and conspiracy.

The same occurs in the natural **Philosophical work** <note>A standard term for the process of creating the Philosopher’s Stone.</note>, which is governed by nature in its shaping just as much as an infant within the maternal womb. And although a father, a mother, and the nurse herself are attributed to it by way of likeness, it is nonetheless no more "artificial" than the generation of any animal. Two **seeds** <note>original: "Semina bina." In alchemy, these usually refer to the masculine (sulfur/Sun) and feminine (mercury/Moon) principles.</note> are joined together by a certain delightful artifice by animals and by both sexes of man; from these, once united through successive alteration, an **Embryo** is formed, which grows and increases, acquires life and motion, and is then nourished by milk.

A woman, indeed, during the time of her conception and pregnancy, must behave temperately in heat, food and drink, motion and rest, and other things, lest a miscarriage follow and the conceived fetus be destroyed. This observation of the **six non-natural things** <note>term: "6. rebus nõ naturalibus." In medieval and Renaissance medicine, these were the six external factors that governed health: air; food and drink; sleep and wakefulness; motion and rest; evacuation and repletion; and the passions of the mind.</note>—because it is prescribed by physicians according to their art—is also artificial. In the same way, if the seeds are not joined in the Philosophical work, they must be joined. But if they were found joined somewhere—as the seed of the rooster and the substance of the hen are held together in one container in an egg—then the work of the Philosophers would be even more natural than the generation of animals. But let us grant, as the philosoph...

<meta>catchword: testan (likely beginning "testantur" or similar on the next page)</meta>

<summary>This discourse argues that alchemy is a natural process akin to human pregnancy and breastfeeding, governed by the same biological "axioms" of nutrition and growth. It emphasizes that the alchemist's "Art" simply facilitates these natural laws, particularly the union of "seeds" and the proper maintenance of the environment.</summary>
<keywords>Peripatetics, Aristotle, nutrition, assimilation, breastfeeding, embryology, six non-naturals, Philosophical work, seeds, Michael Maier, alchemy as nature</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse II, expanding on the analogy between biological incubation and the alchemical process. It transitions from discussing the necessity of maternal milk to explaining how the Earth acts as a "nurse" by providing the "fixation" or solidifying quality needed for the "Philosophical Offspring."</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE II.</header>
<sig>C iij</sig>

...they testify that one comes from the east and the other from the west, and they become one; what more is provided to these than mixing in their own vessel, tempered heat, and nourishment? The vessel is indeed artificial, but in this no difference is made, whether a nest is made by the hen herself, or is arranged by a peasant woman in some uncertain place (as is usual), the generation of eggs and the hatching of chicks from them will be the same. Heat is a natural thing, whether it comes from the tempered fire of ovens and the **putrefaction of manure** <note>original: *fimi putredinis*. In alchemy, the heat generated by decaying organic matter was often used to maintain a low, steady temperature.</note>, or from the sun and air, or from the mother’s internal organs, or from elsewhere. Thus **Egypt** provides natural heat for hatching eggs by art through its ovens. We read that the female of the **Silkworm** <note>original: *Bombycum*</note>, and indeed even chicken eggs, have been hatched by the warmth of virgins’ breasts. **Art and Nature** <note>original: *Ars et Natura*</note> thus lend mutual hands to one another, so that the latter becomes the deputy of the former, and the former the deputy of the latter; nonetheless, Nature remains the Mistress and Art the handmaid.

**WHY THE EARTH** is called the nurse of the **Philosophical Offspring** <note>original: *Nati Philosophici*; referring to the Philosopher's Stone or the alchemical result.</note> might raise a doubt for someone, since the earth among the elements is the most parched and arid—for which dryness occurs as its proper quality. But it must be answered that "elemented" earth is understood here, not the Element itself, of which we have made abundant mention and explained the nature in the first day of the **Philosophical Week** <note>original: *Septimanæ philosophicæ*; likely a reference to Maier’s work "Septimana Philosophica," which organizes alchemical knowledge into seven days.</note>. This is the nurse of Heaven, not because she dissolves, washes, or moistens the fetus, but because she coagulates, fixes, and colors it, and converts it into pure juice and blood. For **Nourishment** <note>original: *Nutritio*</note> encompasses growth in length, width, and depth—that is, extending through all dimensions of the body—which, since it is present here and is provided to the philosophical fetus by the earth alone, the earth should not undeservedly be called by the name of nurse.

Yet something contrary to other kinds of milk (which are converted by the body and do not themselves convert) occurs in this wondrous earthly juice; on account of its most powerful force, it greatly alters the nature of the one nourished, just as the milk of the wolf is believed to have disposed the body of **Remulus** <note>original: *Remulus*; Maier refers to Remus, one of the legendary founders of Rome, who was suckled by a she-wolf.</note> into a spirited nature, ready for war.

<meta>catchword: Sche</meta>

### III. German version of the Latin Epigram.

He who desires to practice in secret teachings should pay attention
To every kind of example, and consider them wisely:
Look at a woman and learn how she washes her linens <note>original: *Leinlachen*. This refers specifically to bedsheets or large cloths.</note>
With water poured on warm and mixed with ashes: <note>original: *Aschen*. Wood ash was mixed with hot water to create lye, a powerful cleaning agent. In alchemy, this "washing" represents the purification of the *nigredo* or black stage of the matter.</note>
Follow her lead, and all will turn out well and fine for you,
For the body, which is black, the water washes entirely clean.

# EMBLEM III. On the Secrets of Nature.

->Go to the woman washing clothes, and do likewise.<-

### EPIGRAM III.

*Whoever you are who loves to search out hidden doctrines,* <note>original: *dogmata*. In this context, it refers to the secret philosophical and practical principles of alchemy.</note> *do not be*
*Idle; take every example that helps you to heart:*
*Do you not see how the woman is accustomed to wipe away the stains*
*From the clothes with the hot waters she pours over them?*
*Imitate her, and you will not be frustrated in your art,*
*For the water washes away the dregs of the black body.* <note>original: *nigri fæcem corporis*. This refers to the *nigredo* or "blackness," the initial stage of the alchemical "Great Work" characterized by decomposition. The "washing" represents the process of purification or *ablutio*, where the impurities are removed to reveal the white stone (*albedo*).</note>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's epigram about the laundress, this page provides the philosophical discourse explaining why domestic washing is the perfect metaphor for alchemical purification. It bridges the gap between everyday labor and the "Great Work."</meta>

# DISCOURSE III.
<meta>catchword: manner</meta>

If any dirt should come upon linen cloths, by which they are stained and blackened—such as earthy dregs—they are washed away by the nearest element, namely water; and the cloths are then exposed to the air, so that by the heat of the sun, as if by fire (the fourth element), the moisture may be extracted along with the dregs. If this happens often enough, those cloths which were previously filthy and foul-smelling become pure and purged of stains. This is the art of women, which they have learned from nature herself. For we see that the bones of animals exposed to the air, which are at first black and squalid, if they are often moistened by rain and dried again by the sun's heat many times over, they are reduced to the highest whiteness, as *Isaac* <note>original: *Isaac*. Likely referring to Isaac Hollandus, a 15th-century alchemist often cited for his practical laboratory descriptions.</note> observes. In the same manner in the philosophical subject, whatever crudities and dregs are present are purged and deleted by "their waters" being poured over them, and the body is reduced to great clarity and perfection.

For the whole of the chemical operations—such as <term>calcination</term> <note>The reduction of a solid to powder by heat.</note>, <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of turning a solid into vapor and back to solid to purify it.</note>, <term>solution</term> <note>The dissolving of a solid into a liquid.</note>, <term>distillation</term> <note>The purification of a liquid by heating and cooling.</note>, <term>descension</term> <note>A method of distillation where the vapor is forced downward.</note>, <term>coagulation</term> <note>The thickening of a liquid into a solid.</note>, <term>fixation</term> <note>Making a volatile substance stable under heat.</note> and the rest—are completed in one washing. For he who washes an impure thing with waters performs the same thing that is perfected by so many forms of operating. For by fire, as the *Rosary of the Philosophers* <note>original: *Rosarius Philoſophorum*. One of the most famous medieval alchemical treatises, known for its use of allegorical illustrations.</note> says, the linens of Prince *Duenech* <note>original: *Duenech*. A traditional alchemical name, often representing the metal lead or antimony in its crude, "sweating" state of impurity.</note>, stained with sweat, must be washed and burned with waters. Whence fire and water seem to have given their qualities to each other, or perhaps that the species of Philosophical fire is not the same as common fire, and the same must be thought regarding the water.

Concerning <term>quicklime</term> <note>Calcium oxide, which reacts violently with water.</note> and <term>Greek fire</term> <note>A legendary incendiary weapon used by the Byzantine Empire that continued to burn on water.</note>, we have found by experience that they are set on fire by waters, and are by no means extinguished, contrary to the nature of other flammable things. So too they affirm that <term>camphor</term> burns in waters after being ignited. And the <term>Jet stone</term> <note>original: *Gagates lapis*.</note> (as *Anselm de Boodt* <note>Anselmus de Boodt (1550–1632) was a famous Flemish mineralogist and physician.</note> testifies), when ignited, is more easily extinguished by oil than by water. For oil is mixed with it and suffocates the ignited body; but since water cannot be mixed with fatness, it yields to the ignited body, unless it covers and overwhelms the whole thing entirely. But that cannot easily happen, because it is a stone and seeks the top of the water, just as all oil does. Thus <term>naphtha</term>, <term>petroleum</term>, and similar things, when set on fire, do not greatly fear waters. Concerning the subterranean coals of the territory of Liege <note>original: *Leodienſis ditionis*.</note>, some write that when they are ignited underground, they are not extinguished by waters, but only by the dust of the earth cast over them, just as

<summary>This page explains the alchemical process of purification (ablution) using the metaphor of a laundress washing clothes and the whitening of bones in the sun. It argues that all chemical operations are essentially a single process of cleansing the "philosophical subject" using specialized fire and water.</summary>
<keywords>purification, ablution, four elements, Isaac Hollandus, Rosarium Philosophorum, Prince Duenech, chemical operations, Greek fire, Jet stone, Liege coal</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of chemical elements, specifically focusing on the paradoxical relationship between fire and water in the alchemical "Great Work." It follows a discussion on the various modes of chemical operation (calcination, sublimation, etc.) and compares the purification of the soul/matter to the cleaning of specialized fabrics.</meta>

# DISCOURSE III.

...the manner of the heart. <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on chemical operations behaving like biological rhythms.</note> **Tacitus** <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56 – c. 120 AD), a Roman historian who described a peculiar "earth-fire" in his *Annals*.</note> tells of a similar kind of fire, which could not be suppressed by water, but only by clubs and clothes stripped from the body. Therefore, there is a great diversity of fires, both in being sparked and in being extinguished; nor is the variety of liquids any less, for milk, vinegar, spirits of wine <note>Ethanol or high-proof alcohol.</note>, **aqua fortis** <term>Strong Water</term> <note>Nitric acid, used to dissolve silver.</note>, **aqua regia** <term>Royal Water</term> <note>A mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids, so named because it can dissolve the "royal" metal, gold.</note>, and common water differ greatly among themselves when compared with fire.

Not only this, but matter itself sometimes withstands fire, such as those linen cloths <note>original: *panni byssini*. While byssus usually means fine linen, in alchemical contexts it often refers to asbestos, which was called "stone flax" and cleaned by throwing it into a fire.</note> held in great price and use by the wealthy in antiquity, which were washed with fire, not water; that is, they were restored to their former cleanliness by burning away the dross. Whatever others may babble about the "hairs of the **Salamander** reptile" <note>A persistent medieval myth claimed the lizard-like salamander lived in fire and that its "fur" could be woven into fireproof cloth; in reality, these were asbestos fibers.</note>, as if incombustible wicks could be made from them, is not to be listened to. There are those who truly affirm that a texture was once prepared from **Talc**, plumose alum <note>A form of asbestos (halotrichite) that appears as white, feathery crystals.</note>, and other such materials, which was accustomed to be purged by fire; but the secret of the craftsmanship perished, extinguished by the envy of the woman who knew it (a woman from **Antwerp**), nor could that tempering be found again afterward.

We are not speaking here of common combustible materials. According to all these differences, the **Philosophical subject** must be considered whenever it is prepared. For here, fire, water, and matter itself are not common. To the **Philosophers**, fire is water, and water is fire. Their water simultaneously washes and <term>calcinates</term> <note>The process of reducing a substance to a powder by heat or "chemical fire."</note>, and so does their fire. And the cloths to be washed have the nature of those byssus or prepared talc cloths, the tempering and craftsmanship of whose preparation is not known to everyone. To wash these, they make a lye <note>original: *lixivium*. A strong alkaline solution used for cleaning.</note> not from oak ashes or their salt, but from a **metallic salt**, which is more durable than the rest; and they use not common water, but that which has been congealed into ice and snow under the sign of **Aquarius** <note>The zodiac sign of the Water-Bearer (mid-January to mid-February). Alchemists believed winter precipitation contained a purer celestial "spirit" than stagnant summer water.</note>. This is because it consists of finer parts than the stagnant waters of ponds or marshes, and thus it can more easily penetrate to the interior of the **Philosophical body**—which is black and unclean—in order to wash and purge it.

<meta>catchword: Sucking</meta>

# FUGUE IV. In the Fourth [Interval], above. <note>original: *in Quarta, suprà*. This refers to the musical structure of the fugue, where the voices enter at the musical interval of a fourth above one another.</note>

-># Join the brother to the sister / and give them the cup of love.<-

<margin>*Atalanta fleeing.*</margin>
The human race would not be so great in the world now, if the first sister had not been given to her brother as a wife. <note>original Latin: *Non hominum foret in mundo nunc tanta propago, si fratri Conjux non data prima soror.* Maier uses the biblical necessity of Adam’s children marrying one another as a biological justification for the alchemical "incest"—the union of two substances derived from the same "root" or mineral source.</note>

The human race would not be so great in the world now, if the first sister had not been given to her brother.

The human race would not be so great in the world now, if the first sister had not been given to her brother as a wife.

### IV. *German version of the Latin Epigram.*

> <insert>T</insert>he <note>The OCR "S Er" is a misreading of the decorative initial "D" in the German *Der* (The).</note> human race in this world would not have increased so well,
> Had the sister not turned herself toward her brother.
> Therefore, join together two siblings of one blood <term>Geblütes</term> <note>Meaning of the same ancestry. Alchemists believed that for a chemical reaction to be successful, the "male" (sulfur) and "female" (mercury) components had to be extracted from the same base metal or "prima materia."</note>,
> So that they live in marriage, husband and wife of one mind.

# EMBLEM IV. On the Secrets of Nature.

-># Join the brother with the sister and offer them the cup of love:<-

### EPIGRAM IV.

> The human race would not be so great in the world now,
> If the first sister had not been given as a wife to the brother. <note>Maier refers to the children of Adam and Eve to justify "alchemical incest." In alchemy, the "brother and sister" usually represent Sulfur and Mercury, or the Sun and Moon, which must be joined to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note>
> Therefore, willingly join the two born from both parents,
> So that they may be male and female in the marriage bed. <note>original: *toro*. This refers to the chemical vessel or "nest" where the union occurs.</note>
> Drink first to both with the cup of friendship <term>Philothesia</term> <note>original: *Philothesia*. From the Greek *philotesia*, a cup drunk for the sake of friendship or a welcoming toast.</note> filled with nectarean liquid,
> And love will bring forth the hope of offspring.

<meta>catchword: Law <note>original: *Lex*</note></meta>

<summary>This page presents the fourth emblem of the collection, using the allegory of sibling marriage to describe the "conjunction" of two related chemical substances. It emphasizes that for the alchemical process to succeed, the "male" and "female" principles must be united in a ritual of love and mutual affinity.</summary>

<keywords>conjunction, brother and sister, Philothesia, alchemical marriage, progeny, union of opposites</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the opening epigram of Emblem IV, which introduced the concept of the alchemical "marriage" between siblings. Here, the author provides the philosophical and legal justification for this metaphorical incest, arguing that the rules governing human society do not apply to the chemical substances (the "Philosophical persons") used in the Great Work.</meta>

# DISCOURSE IV.

Divine and civil law prohibits those joined closely by nature through blood from being coupled in marriage, such as those directly ascending and descending in the **genealogical tree** and those meeting at the sides <note>referring to collateral relatives like siblings or cousins</note>, and this for very certain reasons. But when the **philosophers** <note>In this context, "philosophers" almost always refers to alchemists.</note> speak of joining in marriage a mother with a son, or a father with a daughter, or a brother with a sister, they do not say or do anything against the aforementioned law: because the subjects distinguish the attributes, and the causes distinguish the effects. For the "persons" of the Philosophers differ from human persons; they are as free as the sons and daughters of Adam, who took each other in marriage without the imputation of any crime. 

The most powerful reason for human laws seems to be so that the human race might coalesce and be associated more firmly through affinity and friendship, and not be divided into hostile and hereditary family factions. Since this same cause remained intact for the first Adamic brothers and sisters, nothing prohibited them from being joined in marriage. For in them alone did the human race then exist, nor did others live besides them and their parents; therefore, as they were connected by blood, they were necessarily to be connected by **affinity** <note>Marriage-based relationship.</note>. 

But as the multitude of men grew, distributed into innumerable families, this true and equitable cause was found: that brothers should not marry sisters. Among the alchemists, there is another reason why sisters marry brothers: namely, the **likeness of substance**, so that "like may be joined to like." There are, however, in this genus at least two things most similar to each other in species, yet different in sex, one of whom is greeted by the name of **brother**, the other of **sister**. 

These, therefore, with the same liberty, condition, and even inevitable necessity by which the first blood-related humans were joined, must be legitimately committed to a single marriage. The **brother** is hot and dry, and therefore very **choleric**; the **sister** is cold and moist, having much **phlegmatic** matter in herself. <note>Maier is using the theory of the four humors: the "brother" represents the active, fiery principle (Sulfur), while the "sister" represents the passive, watery principle (Mercury).</note> These natures, though so different in temperament, are accustomed to agree best in love and fecundity, and in the propagation of offspring. 

For just as fire (which can be propagated) is not easily struck from steel and steel—two most hard bodies—nor from flint and flint—two brittle bodies—but from the hard and the brittle together (that is, steel and flint); so it is not from a burning male and a fiery female, as is necessary...

<meta>catchword: for both <note>original: *utrifqu* [utrisque]</note></meta>

<summary>Maier explains that alchemical "incest" (joining related substances) is permissible because the substances act like the children of Adam, who had no other choice for mates. He argues that the union of the hot, "choleric" brother (Sulfur) and the cold, "phlegmatic" sister (Mercury) is essential for creating the "fire" of the Stone, much like flint and steel.</summary>

<keywords>alchemical marriage, Adam and Eve, Sulfur and Mercury, four humors, choleric, phlegmatic, steel and flint, conjunction of opposites</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the "philosophical marriage" of siblings in the alchemical process. The previous page established that while human laws forbid incest to encourage broader social bonds, these laws do not apply to the first humans (like the children of Adam) or to alchemical substances, which require "homogeneity" or sameness to interact successfully.</meta>

# DISCOURSE IV.

...from both being cold (for the coldness of the sea <note>Latin: *maris*; the author may be playing on the similarity between *mare* (sea) and *mas/maris* (male), while referencing the "cold" nature of the unrefined alchemical matter</note> results in infertility), a lively offspring is conceived, but from him being hot and her being colder. For even the hottest woman, within the range of human temperament, is judged colder than the coldest (yet healthy) man, as **Levinus Lemnius** <note>A 16th-century Dutch physician and author of *De occultis naturae miraculis* (The Secret Miracles of Nature).</note> proves in his book on the *Hidden Miracles of Nature*. 

Thus, sister and brother are rightly joined by the **Philosophers** <note>In alchemical texts, "the Philosophers" refers to the long lineage of alchemical authorities.</note>. If someone desires offspring to be born from a hen, a bitch, a goat, a sheep, or other beasts, he matches them with a rooster, a dog, or a ram—an animal of its own species to which it is most similar—and thus his wish is not thwarted. Indeed, he does not consider the blood-relation of the beasts, but only the nobility of each and their natural compatibility. Should the same not be said of a tree trunk and the graft to be inserted into it? Nay, even of the **metallic nature**, which, possessing the greatest similarity and homogeneity of substance of all things, requires the same whenever it demands that something be joined to it. 

Yet, the joined brother and sister do not become fertile, nor do they long persist in love, unless a <term>Philothesium</term> <note>original: *Philothesium*; from the Greek *philothesia*, meaning a "toast of friendship" or a "loving-cup."</note>—or cup of love, like a potion—is offered to them. For by this, their spirits are so calmed and reconciled that, as if intoxicated (like **Lot** <note>A reference to the biblical story in Genesis 19 where Lot’s daughters intoxicated him to ensure the survival of their lineage; here used as an alchemical metaphor for removing the "shame" or resistance of the substances to unite.</note>), and having cast aside all shame, they unite and procreate offspring—not illegitimate, but lawful. 

For who is unaware that the human race owes much to **Medicine**, seeing as many thousands of men now exist in the world through its benefit and labor who would not have been here had their parents or ancestors not been rescued and freed from the defect of sterility by the removal of its cause—lifting both immediate and distant impediments—or had their mothers not been preserved from miscarriage? This cup of love is therefore given to these new newlyweds for the same reasons, three of which anyone can perceive from what has been said: namely, the **constancy of love**, the **removal of sterility**, and the **prevention of miscarriage**.

D ij

# FUGUE V. in the Fifth, below. <note>original: "FUGA V. in Quinta, infrà." In musical theory, "in the fifth" indicates that the second voice begins a fifth interval away from the first. This structure mirrors the alchemical pursuit of harmony between opposing forces.</note>

## Place the toad upon the woman's breast, so that it may suck, and the woman may die; thus the toad will grow very large from the milk.

### V. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Swiftly place a toad upon the woman’s breasts,
So that it may nourish itself with milk, just like a child,

Then from this make a medicine, for it shall be of such noble powers,
That it allows no poison to cling to the human heart.

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Emblem V. The previous page introduced the musical fugue and the German verse, which established the metaphor of the toad and the woman.
