# EMBLEM V. On the secrets of Nature. <note>original: "De secretis Naturæ." Michael Maier's work often frames alchemical processes as the uncovering of Nature's hidden mechanisms.</note>

## Place the toad upon the woman's breasts, so that she may suckle it, and let the woman die, and let the toad be swollen with milk.

### EPIGRAM V.

> Let the cold toad be placed upon the woman's breast, <note>original: "Fœmineo gelidus ponatur pectore Bufo." In alchemical symbolism, the toad (bufo) often represents the "fixed" or heavy, earthy part of the matter, which is initially perceived as "poisonous" or base.</note>
> *So that it may drink the milky draughts just like an infant.* <note>original: "Instar ut infantis lactea pocla bibat." The "milk" represents a volatile, nourishing mercurial substance that the "fixed" matter must absorb to be transformed.</note>
> Let it grow into a great swelling as the breasts are emptied,
> *And let the sick woman depart from her life.* <note>The "death" of the woman represents the "mortification" stage of alchemy, where the initial form of the matter is destroyed so that its essence can be reorganized into a higher state.</note>
> From this, you shall make a noble medicine for yourself,
> *A venom that shall drive out poison from the human heart and lift the plague.* <note>original: "virus quod fuget humano corde, levétque luem." This refers to the concept of "allopathy" or "like curing like"—the alchemist believes that a poison, once purified through this symbolic death and rebirth, becomes the ultimate antidote (the Philosopher's Stone).</note>

<sig>D 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: TOTA</meta>

<summary>This page presents the Latin motto and epigram for Emblem V, describing a macabre allegory where a toad drains a woman's life-force through her milk to create a "noble medicine." This symbolizes the alchemical process of "mortification," where base matter is nourished by a volatile spirit until the original form dies and a potent medicinal essence is born.</summary>
<keywords>Toad, Milk, Mortification, Poison as Cure, Michael Maier, Alchemy, Medicine, Transformation</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page begins the "Discourse" or prose commentary for Emblem V. It expands upon the shocking imagery of the toad suckling at a woman's breast by discussing the biological and symbolic relationship between the "male" and "female" principles in alchemy.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE V.</header>

THE whole assembly of Alchemists <note>original: "Philosophorum." In this context, "philosophers" refers specifically to practitioners of the Hermetic art.</note> agrees on this: that their work is nothing other than the male and the female. Truly, it belongs to the male to generate and to rule over the female; to her, it belongs to conceive, to be impregnated, to give birth, to suckle, and to nourish the offspring, and to be subject to the command of the male. For just as she fosters and nourishes the conceived fetus beneath blood before it is brought into the light, so she nourishes it with milk once it is born.

Hence nature has prepared for the tender little boy <note>original: "pusioni." A term for a young child or lad.</note> in the female breasts a digestible and proportionate food, which awaits his arrival like a first supply and provision <note>original: "viaticum." Literally, provisions for a journey, often used here to describe the start of life's path.</note> in this course of the world. And so he is nourished with milk, grows, and increases until he has the instruments for breaking down bread—that is, teeth—then he is rightly weaned, because nature has provided for him another, more solid nourishment.

But here the Alchemists say that a toad must be placed upon the woman's breasts, so that she may nourish it with her milk just like an infant. This is a wretched and horrendous sight—indeed, even impious—that the milk destined for an infant should be offered to a toad, a venomous beast and an adversary to human nature. We have heard and read of serpents and dragons sucking dry the udders of cows; perhaps toads would seek the same if the opportunity were offered with cattle.

The story of the toad is well known: it so occupied the mouth and inner lips of a sleeping peasant that it could be driven away by no trick, except by violence, which would have been joined with the danger of death from the spitting of its poison (which it is said to use for defensive or offensive weapons), and therefore it was not to be attempted. Therefore, from antipathy <note>original: "antipathia." In Renaissance science, this referred to a natural or occult repulsion between certain animals or substances.</note> a remedy was found for the wretched man: namely, that of a very large spider and the toad, who pursue each other with lethal hatred. Thus the man was carried with the toad directly to the place where a swollen spider had laid out its webs; when the spider saw the toad, it soon lowered itself onto the toad's back and pricked it with its sting. When, however, the toad received no harm from that, the spider descended a second time and pierced it again more strongly; whereupon the toad soon swelled up and fell dead from the man's mouth without any injury to him.

But here the opposite happens: because the toad occupies not the mouth, but the breast of the woman, and by her milk it grows so much that it turns out to be of enormous size and strength. The woman, however, with her strength consumed, wastes away and dies; for the poison easily through the veins...

<meta>catchword: veins</meta>

<summary>Maier explores the alchemical allegory of the toad and the woman, explaining the "natural" roles of male and female principles before recounting a folk tale about the medicinal use of the natural "antipathy" between spiders and toads.</summary>
<keywords>male and female principles, alchemy, toad (bufo), spider (araneus), antipathy, nursing, poison and remedy</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse V, expanding on the allegory of the woman and the toad. It moves from the physical horror of the image to its alchemical significance, referencing historical anecdotes and the mythical "toad-stone."</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE V.</header>

is communicated to the heart through the pectoral veins, and it infects and extinguishes it, 
<margin>Theophilus, in the *Assembly [Turba]*, mentions a dragon joined to a woman. <note>original: "Theophilus in turba meminit draconis mulieri juncti." Referencing the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts.</note></margin>
just as is established from the case of Cleopatra, who applied vipers to her breasts when she wished to be preempted by a voluntary death, lest she be dragged alive into the hands and triumphs of her conquerors. Truly, lest anyone should think the Philosophers <note>As before, "Philosophers" refers to the Alchemists.</note> so cruel as to command that a poisonous reptile be applied to a woman, it must be known that this toad is the fetus or son of that same woman, brought forth by a monstrous birth, and therefore by natural right, it ought to enjoy and be nourished by its mother's milk; but that the mother dies is not by the will of the son: For he could not infect the mother, who had been formed within her bowels and increased by her blood until the birth. Indeed, it is a truly ominous thing for a toad to be born from a woman, which we know to have happened elsewhere: William of Newburgh, an English writer, writes in his commentaries (how faithfully, let others judge) that in a certain stone quarry of the jurisdiction of the Bishop of Winchester, when a certain large stone was split open, a living toad was found inside with a golden chain, and by the Bishop’s order it was hidden back in the same place and immersed in perpetual darkness, lest it carry some evil omen with it: Such also is this toad: for it is distinguished by gold, if not externally by the artificial work of a chain, yet internally by nature—namely, by the stone which others call <term>Borax</term> <note>In medieval lore, this refers to a "toad-stone," not the modern mineral salt.</note>, <term>Chelonitis</term>, <term>Batrachites</term>, <term>Crapaudina</term> or <term>garatronium</term>. For this stone far surpasses gold in virtue against any poisons of animals, and it is accustomed to be set in gold, as if in a case or covering, lest it be harmed or lost. But it ought to be legitimate, sought from the animal; but if a stone taken from underground ditches, as is common, is fashioned into its shape and used in its place, it should be chosen from the best minerals that assist the heart: For in these the <term>Philosophical toad</term> <note>The "Philosophical Toad" represents the "fixed" or earthly part of the alchemical matter that must consume the "volatile" part.</note> is truly found, not in a stone quarry (as that storyteller asserts), and it has gold within itself, not outside for show: For why would a toad adorn itself, lurking in darkness and enclosures? Perhaps so that it might be magnificently greeted by a beetle, if it should walk toward it in the twilight? What subterranean goldsmith would have fabricated a golden chain for it? Perhaps the father of the green children, who came forth from the land of St. Martin—indeed, from the earth itself—as well as two dogs from a stone quarry, according to the same author? <note>Maier is mocking William of Newburgh’s inclusion of folk legends, such as the Green Children of Woolpit, to argue that the "gold" and the "toad" are metaphors for chemical properties rather than literal biological or historical anomalies.</note>

<meta>The catchword "Súbet" (He/It rises) indicates the beginning of the next page's text.</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that the toad is the woman's own "monstrous" offspring, representing the alchemical product that must be nourished by its source. He dismisses literal accounts of toads found in stones with golden chains as fables, arguing instead for the "Philosophical Toad" that possesses internal, medicinal virtues.</summary>

->VI. German version of the Latin Epigram.<-

> The farmers sow their seed into the fat earth,
> When it is well crushed with iron teeth; <note>Refers to the teeth of a harrow or rake used to break up soil.</note>

> Which is quite white like leaves, so that gold may come from it;
> So that you do this rightly, look closely at the work of the peasants,
> For gold grows like wheat, and has its own life.

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>EMBLEM VI. On the Secrets of Nature.</header>
<sig>E</sig>

### Sow your gold in the white foliated earth. <note>The "white foliated earth" (terra foliata) refers to a specific stage in the alchemical process where the matter becomes white (Albedo) and takes on a flaky or layered crystalline structure, resembling the leaves of a book or a flower.</note>

---

->EPIGRAM VI.<-

> The country-dwellers entrust their seeds to the fertile earth,
> When it has been made foliated <note>original: "foliata." In a farming context, this refers to soil that has been turned and broken into thin layers or crumbs by a rake or harrow.</note> by their rakes.
> The Philosophers <note>Meaning the Alchemists, often referred to as "the Sages" or "Philosophers of Fire."</note> taught to scatter gold through snowy fields,
> Which have the appearance of a thin leaf;
> That you may do this, observe the former <note>The farmer's work.</note> well, for how gold
> Germinates, you shall see from the wheat, as if in a mirror. <note>Maier argues that alchemical processes are not supernatural, but follow the same natural laws of growth and germination as grain in a field.</note>

**Vocabulary:** <note>original: "vocab."</note> Sow, gold, white foliated earth, country-dwellers, Philosophers, wheat, mirror.

<meta>Catchword: PLATO</meta>

<summary>This page presents Emblem VI, using the metaphor of a farmer sowing seeds to explain how alchemists must "sow" their gold into a prepared, white, layered medium. It emphasizes that the growth of the Philosopher's Stone mimics the natural germination of wheat.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, agriculture, gold, germination, white earth, Albedo, nature's secrets, Michael Maier</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of Emblem VI, which compared the alchemical process to the natural growth of wheat. Here, the author begins the detailed philosophical discourse (Discursus VI) explaining why agriculture is the perfect metaphor for alchemy.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE VI.</header>

Plato says that a state consists not of a doctor and a doctor, but of a
doctor and a farmer, that is, of men of diverse functions: He
mentions the farmer and the doctor especially because their works are
highly conspicuous in the imitation, correction, and perfection of nature. For
each of them takes up a natural subject, in which he adds certain
necessary things according to art, and removes deficiencies or even superfluities:
Hence the art of each can be defined (as medicine was by Hippocrates
<note>Hippocrates (c. 460–370 BCE) was an ancient Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine."</note>)
by the addition of what is lacking and the subtraction of the superfluous:
For what more does the farmer do than take the field, as it was left by nature,
and add plowing, ridging <note>original: "lyrationem," likely a variant of "lirationem," the act of creating ridges in a field.</note>, harrowing, 
fertilizing <note>original: "laetificationem," literally "making the earth happy/joyful" through manuring.</note> or manuring, and sowing,
and then commit the growth and yield to nature, which administers the
heat of the sun and the rain, and by these multiplies the seeds and
brings them forth into crops soon to be harvested: Meanwhile, as the grass
springs up, the farmer removes the thistles and all impediments, 
reaps the mature crops, and takes away the superfluities from the harvest, 
namely the straw, the chaff, and other such things: So also the doctor (and 
the chemist <note>original: "chymicus"</note> in a different respect) undertakes to preserve 
present health for the human body and to recover it when absent by various remedies;
he removes the cause of disease, cures the illness, soothes symptoms, 
diminishes superfluous blood by bloodletting <note>original: "venæ sectione."</note>, 
restores what is deficient through an ordered system of good diet, 
evacuates noxious humors by purging, and thus in a thousand ways 
imitates, supplies, and corrects nature by the works of intellect and art; 
since these things are well known, Chemistry <note>original: "Chymica"</note> should 
rather come under our consideration: For Chemistry <note>original: "Chemia"</note> 
especially bears witness to the operations of agriculture in its secret 
terms and ways of working: Farmers have earth in which to sow their seeds, 
and so do the Chemists: They have dung <note>original: "fimum," meaning manure or compost.</note> 
with which to fertilize the fields; so do these men, without 
which nothing would happen, nor would any fruit be hoped for: The former 
have seeds whose multiplication they desire. If the chemists did not have 
these, they would be imitating the painter (as Lullius <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a polymath and mystic to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note> says) 
trying to express the face of a certain man whom he himself had 
never seen, nor even his image: Farmers wait for the rain and the heat 
of the sun; so indeed do the chemists administer heat and rain <note>In alchemy, "rain" often refers to the moisture produced during distillation or the condensation of vapors within a vessel.</note> 
suitable for their work: Why say more? Chemistry is entirely parallel 
to agriculture, and its substitute, which expresses its roles in every respect, 
but under a most perfect allegory: Hence the ancients brought forth 
Ceres, Triptolemus, Osiris, and Dionysus <note>Goddesses and gods associated with grain, the plow, and the vine; Maier views them as alchemical symbols.</note> 
as golden gods, or as those pertaining to chemistry

<meta>Catchword: as if (original: "quasi")</meta>

<vocab>Plato, Hippocrates, Lullius, Ceres, Triptolemus, Osiris, Dionysus, Chemistry, Medicine, Agriculture</vocab>

<summary>Maier compares the "Art" of chemistry (alchemy) to medicine and agriculture, arguing that all three work by imitating nature, adding what is missing, and removing what is superfluous. He asserts that alchemy is a perfect allegory for farming, requiring its own "earth," "seeds," and "manure" to produce its golden harvest.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, agriculture, medicine, nature, Plato, Hippocrates, fertilization, seeds, allegory, Ceres, Osiris</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse of Emblem VI. The previous page established the farmer and the doctor as the primary models for the alchemist, as both work to assist and perfect nature by adding what is missing and removing what is superfluous.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE VI.</header>

<sig>E ij</sig>

...as if teaching mortals to cast the seeds of crops into their earth, demonstrating agriculture and the propagation of vines, and the use of wine—all of which the ignorant have twisted into common farming tasks, but falsely so. For these are the most secret mysteries of nature, which are hidden from the common folk under these veils of agriculture and revealed only to the wise. Hence the Philosophers say that their gold must be sown in "white foliated earth" <note>original: "terra alba foliata," a technical alchemical term for a purified state of matter often associated with the silver or whitening stage of the process.</note>, as if they intend for the sowing of wheat to be viewed as an example and imitated—something the author of the *Treatise on Wheat* and Jodocus Greverus <note>Jodocus Greverus was a 16th-century priest and alchemist known for his work "Secretum et Alchimia," which used agricultural metaphors to describe the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> have done excellently in their writings. For each of them has most elegantly adapted the individual operations of agriculture used in producing wheat to the sowing of gold or the generation of the tincture <note>In alchemy, the "tincture" is the transformative substance capable of perfecting metals.</note>. White earth, being sandy, provides little fruit to common farmers, who prefer black and rich soil; but to the Philosophers, that white earth is of primary importance if it be "foliated"—that is, well prepared—because they know how to fertilize it with their own dung, whereas the farmers do not.

Sowing is, moreover, the propagation of the world, by which that which cannot endure in the individual is preserved through the species. This occurs in humans, animals, and plants: in the latter <term>hermaphroditically</term> <note>Meaning having both male and female reproductive organs in one individual, allowing for self-fertilization, as seen in many plants.</note>, and in the former under two distinct sexes. In metals, however, it happens in a far different way: for in them, from the flow of a point comes a line, and from the surface of a line comes a body. The stars produced that very point before the line, surface, or body, because it is the beginning of them all. Nature added the "flow" over a long span of time; that is, the celestial Phoebus <note>The sun, commonly used as a symbol for gold or the "sulfur" of the philosophers.</note> generated a little son beneath the earth, whom Mercury <note>The messenger god, representing the fluid, "mercurial" principle in alchemy.</note> offered to Vulcan <note>The god of fire, representing the heat required for the alchemical work.</note> and Chiron to be raised—that is, to the manual artisan to be educated—just as they write of Achilles, who was held under the flames and hardened by his mother, Thetis. From Chiron he learned, among other things, music and the skill of playing the lyre <note>original: "Cythara," a stringed instrument of the ancient world.</note>: 

<margin>*Hence Ovid says: "The son of Philyra [Chiron] made the boy Achilles master of the lyre."* <note>original: "Hinc Ovid Phyllirides puerum Cythara præfecit Achillem." A quote from Ovid's "Ars Amatoria."</note></margin>

Since Achilles is actually nothing other than the Philosophical Subject <note>The primary matter or substance being worked upon in the alchemical process.</note> (whose son Pyrrhus had red hair <note>Pyrrhus, also known as Neoptolemus. In alchemical symbolism, "red hair" or "redness" signifies the final stage of the work, the "Rubedo."</note>, and without these two Troy could not be conquered, as we have demonstrated at length in Book 6 of our *Hieroglyphics*), we therefore discuss Music in this very work of ours where we describe Achilles and his heroic virtues and deeds, and not without cause (though only in passing). For if Music adorned so great a Hero, why should it not also make this little work of ours more diverse and pleasing? For the Angels sing (as the Holy Scriptures testify), the heavens sing (as Pythagoras established) and "declare the glory of God" (as the Psalmist says), the Muses and Apollo sing (according to the poets), humans sing—even infants—birds sing, and even sheep and geese sing through musical instruments <note>A playful reference to the materials used for music: "sheep" providing gut strings and "geese" providing the quills (plectrums) or pens used to write music.</note>; if we, therefore, also sing, we do not do so without reason.

<meta>catchword: Concerning (De)</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that the "sowing of wheat" is an alchemical metaphor for planting gold in "white foliated earth." He compares the refinement of the alchemical subject to the education of Achilles by Chiron, justifying his use of music in this work by noting its presence throughout the divine and natural order.</summary>
<keywords>agriculture, sowing gold, white foliated earth, Jodocus Greverus, Achilles, Chiron, Vulcan, Music, Pythagoras, alchemy</keywords>

### German version of the VII. Latin Epigram.

In a hollow stone the eagle had made its nest,
In which it secretly nourished its young;
Of these, one could rise easily with feathers,
But the other, featherless, would not yield to such a thing.
Therefore the flying one falls back into its nest, from which it went;
Join the head to the tail <note>original: "Füg jhn das Haupt zum hindern." This is a classic alchemical instruction referring to the "circulatio" or the Ouroboros—the serpent eating its own tail. It signifies the process of making the volatile substance stable (fixed) by joining it with a solid base.</note>, and so you will attain your goal.

The text and accompanying musical fugue illustrate how a "feathered" (volatile) spirit must be joined to a "featherless" (fixed) body to complete the alchemical Work.</summary>

-># EMBLEM VII. Concerning the secrets of Nature.<-
<sig>E 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Because (original: "Quod")</meta>

## A young bird flies from the nest, and falls back into the nest again.

### EPIGRAM VII.

On a hollow rock the **BIRD of JOVE** <note>original: "Jovis ALES" – The eagle, the sacred bird of Jupiter. In alchemical imagery, the eagle represents "volatility" or the spirit that rises as vapor during heating.</note> had built its nest, in which
  It hid itself away, and nourished its young:
One of these wished to raise itself on light wings,
  But was held back by its brother, a featherless bird <note>The "featherless brother" represents the "fixed" or heavy part of the matter that remains at the bottom of the vessel, preventing the "volatile" part from escaping entirely.</note>.
Thus, though flying, it returns to the nest it had left; to them
  Join the head to the tail <note>A reference to the *Ouroboros* or the circular nature of alchemical distillation: the "head" (the beginning or the volatile) must be united with the "tail" (the end or the fixed).</note>, and then you will not be empty-handed.

<summary>This page presents the poetic epigram for Emblem VII, using the metaphor of two eagles—one winged and one flightless—to describe the alchemical process of "fixing the volatile." It instructs the practitioner to unite these opposing forces to achieve success in the Great Work.</summary>

### VII. English version of the Latin Epigram. <note>Translated from the original German translation of the Latin.</note>

In a hollow stone the Eagle had made its nest,
Wherein it nourished its hidden young;
From these, one was able to rise easily with feathers,
But the other, featherless, would not permit this.
Therefore, the flying one falls back into its nest, from which it came;
Join the head to the tail <note>A reference to the Ouroboros or the "circular distillation" where the vapor (head) returns to the base (tail).</note>, and so you will attain it.

<image-desc>An intricate woodcut engraving depicting an alchemical or moral emblem. In the foreground, a tall, craggy rock formation rises sharply. ### EPIGRAM VII.

In a hollow rock the bird of Jupiter <note>original: "Jovis ALES," referring to the eagle. In alchemy, the eagle typically symbolizes the "volatile" or spiritual part of a substance that rises during heating.</note> *had built its nest, in which*
*it lay hidden, and nourished its young:*
*One of these wished to raise itself on light wings,*
*but was held back by its wingless brother.* <note>The "wingless brother" represents the "fixed" or heavy part of the matter—the "body" or salt—that prevents the spirit from escaping entirely.</note>
*From there, though flying, it returns to the nest it had left;*
*Join the head of those to the tail, and then you will not be empty.* <note>original: "Junge caput caudæ." This refers to the Ouroboros, the serpent eating its own tail, symbolizing a closed system where the volatile spirit is recaptured and "fixed" by the body, completing the alchemical cycle.</note>

<sig>E 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: BECAUSE</meta>

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of two eagles—one capable of flight and one flightless—to illustrate the alchemical necessity of binding the volatile spirit to a fixed physical base. This process ensures that the "spiritual" essence does not escape but instead transforms the "body" of the matter.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, eagle, bird of Jupiter, volatile and fixed, Ouroboros, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens, distillation, fixation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem VII. Having presented the image of the two eagles and the poetic epigram on page 37, the author now provides the "Discursus" or philosophical explanation, moving from medical theories of humors to the elemental composition of the world and the specific alchemical behavior of the "Philosophical Subject."</meta>

# DISCOURSE VII.

What Hippocrates, the leader of physicians, asserts concerning the humors <note>original: "humoribus" — In ancient medicine, these were the four vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to govern health and temperament.</note>—that they are diverse and several in number within a person, and not merely one, otherwise various diseases could not arise—is exactly what we notice to be true concerning the Elements of the world. If there were but one single element, no change of one into another would occur, nor would any generation or corruption take place; indeed, all things would be one unchanging mass, and no meteors, minerals, plants, or animals could be produced from it by nature. Hence, the Supreme Creator harmonized this entire System of the world out of diverse and contrary natures—namely, from the light and the heavy, the hot and the cold, the moist and the dry—so that one might pass into another through affinity <note>The idea that elements can transform because they share "bridge" qualities (e.g., Fire and Air are both hot).</note>. In this way, the composition of bodies is achieved, which differ greatly among themselves in essence, qualities, powers, and effects. For in imperfectly mixed bodies <term>inperfectè miſtis</term> <note>A term for substances like vapors or stones that are not yet fully "perfected" or balanced.</note>, there are light Elements such as fire and air, as well as heavy ones like earth and water; these are so tempered by balances among themselves that one does not flee from the other. Instead, each individual part is easily caught and held by its neighbor.

Earth and air are naturally opposed to each other, as are fire and water; nevertheless, fire fosters a friendship with air through the heat common to both, and with earth through dryness. Likewise, air shares a bond with water, and water with earth. From this, all things are connected by bonds of affinity—or rather, of blood-relation—and they remain together in a single composition. If this composition abounds in light things, it carries the heavy ones up with it; if it abounds in heavy things, it presses the light ones down with it. 

This is illustrated by the two **Eagles**, the winged and the featherless <note>In alchemy, the winged eagle represents the volatile spirit that evaporates, while the featherless eagle is the "fixed" matter that remains at the bottom of the flask.</note>, of which the former, attempting to fly, is held back by the latter. An evident example of this matter is seen in the battle between the Falcon and the Heron: the Falcon, having become superior in the air through swift flight and rapid wings, seizes the Heron with its talons; then, by the weight of the Heron, both fall together to the ground. 

The opposite appeared in the artificial pigeon (or automaton) of Archytas <note>Archytas of Tarentum, a Greek philosopher, was said to have built a wooden pigeon that could fly using compressed air or steam.</note>, where heavy things were lifted by light ones—that is, its wooden body was snatched into the air by the spirit <note>original: "ſpiritu" — In this context, it refers to the air or steam powering the device.</note> enclosed within it. In the "philosophical subject" <note>The raw material of the Alchemical Work.</note>, the light parts at first predominate over the heavy in quantity; however, they are eventually overcome by the power and virtue of the heavy parts. In the course of time, the eagle's wings are clipped, and from the two birds, one massive bird is made—namely, the **Ostrich** <note>original: "Strutio camelus"</note>—which is able to consume iron, and which, being hindered by its weight, runs upon the ground more than it [flies]...

<meta>catchword: runs (original: "rat", part of "currat")</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the necessity of contrary elements (hot, cold, moist, dry) in the creation of life and alchemy, using the metaphor of the winged and flightless eagles to describe how "volatile" spirits are eventually "fixed" by heavy matter.</summary>

<keywords>Four Elements, Hippocrates, Humors, Harmony of Contraries, Winged and Featherless Eagles, Falcon and Heron, Archytas’s Pigeon, Ostrich, Alchemical Fixation</keywords>

# DISCOURSE VII.

<meta>catchword: Nim</meta>

...d <note>Continuing from the previous page: "...each individual part is easily caught and held."</note>, than it flies through the air, although it has beautiful feathers. Concerning this bird, or one similar to it, **Hermes** (as the **Author of the *Aurora*** <note>original: "Author Aurorae" — likely referring to the *Aurora Consurgens*, a famous 15th-century alchemical manuscript attributed to Thomas Aquinas.</note> testifies in chapter 5) writes thus: "I have considered the bird venerable to the wise, which flies while it is in **Aries, Cancer, Libra, or Capricorn** <note>These are the four cardinal signs of the zodiac, marking the start of the four seasons; in alchemy, they often represent the stages of the "Great Work."</note>; and you shall acquire it for yourself as a perennial thing from pure minerals and rocky mountains." **Senior** <note>Zadith ben Hamuel, an influential 10th-century Arab alchemist known in the West as Senior Zadith.</note> writes of the same in his **Table**, where a pair is seen—one flying and one without wings—of which one holds the other's tail in its beak, and vice versa, so that they cannot easily be separated. For this is the mechanism of Universal Nature: that it always lifts the heavy by means of the light, and in turn depresses the light by means of the heavy. This is as the **Author of the *Perfect Magistery*** <note>A title often associated with the works of Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan).</note> declares: he established that the seven <term>mineral spirits</term> <note>Volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, and arsenic.</note>, like wandering stars <note>Planets.</note>, and as many <term>metallic bodies</term> <note>The seven metals: gold, silver, iron, mercury, tin, copper, lead.</note>, like fixed stars, must be married to one another.

Hence also **Aristotle the Chemist** <note>Pseudo-Aristotle, the name given to the unknown author(s) of alchemical works attributed to the Greek philosopher.</note> says: "When the Spirit has dissolved the body and soul so that they exist in their own form, it does not remain fixed unless you occupy it; but the 'occupation' is that you join it with the body from which you prepared it in the beginning; because in that body, the spirit is occupied <note>original: "occupatur" — here meaning 'held' or 'bound' from escaping.</note> from its flight by the 'staying-power' <note>original: "supersistentia"</note> of the body."

In **camphor**, as **Bonus** <note>Petrus Bonus, author of the *Precious New Pearl* (14th century).</note> records, the light Elements—namely air and fire—prevail over the heavy; therefore it is said to exhale entirely and depart into the air. In **quicksilver**, **flowers of sulfur**, **antimony**, **salt of hartshorn** <note>original: "sal sanguinis cervini" — literally "salt of deer's blood," chemically ammonium carbonate.</note>, **sal ammoniac**, and others of this kind, the earth flies with the air into the <term>alembic</term> <note>A distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube.</note>, and is not separated from it. In **gold**, **glass**, **adamant** <note>diamond</note>, **emery stone**, **garnets**, and similar things, the Elements remain joined and unharmed for a very long time, even when the fire presses them, and the earth retains the others with itself. In other combustible things, a division and separation of one from another occurs, so that the ashes are left at the bottom, while the water, air, and fire seek the higher regions.

Therefore, one must not look to the unequal composition of these latter things (which are not so strongly mixed), nor to the mixture of the first things (which, though more durable, are yet volatile), but rather to the solidity, constancy, and fixity of the middle things: for thus the featherless bird will detain the feathered one, and the fixed substance will fix the volatile, which is what must be done.

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical principle of fixation, using the metaphor of a winged and wingless bird to describe how volatile substances (spirits) must be bound by solid bodies (metals) to achieve stability.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Senior Zadith, planetary metals, mineral spirits, fixation, distillation, elements, camphor, quicksilver, chemical marriage</keywords>

A bird is found in the world higher than any other,

->Burn it with a glowing sword (as is required);<-

### VIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

A bird is found in the world higher than any other,
->To seek its egg, let your mind be bound;<-
The white surrounds the yellow yolk so soft,
With a glowing sword (as is the custom) strike it oft:
Let Mars lend his help to Vulcan's might:
From this a cockerel shall spring to light,
Who shall overcome both fire and sword's bite. <note>This rhyming German verse translates the Latin Epigram, emphasizing that the "cockerel" (the alchemical result) will eventually be stronger than the very forces (fire and iron) used to create it.</note>

The page combines an engraving, a Latin epigram, and a three-part musical fugue to illustrate this central alchemical process.</summary>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the eighth discourse, moving from the poetic epigram and musical score of the "Philosophical Egg" to a prose explanation of its natural and mystical secrets. It connects the biological process of avian birth with the alchemical transformation of matter.</meta>

# DISCOURSE VIII,

Many and varied are the kinds of birds; as they are indefinite in number, so they remain unknown to us by name. They tell of a certain very large bird called the Ruc <note>The Roc, a legendary giant bird of prey from Middle Eastern mythology, famously described by Marco Polo as being capable of carrying off elephants.</note> appearing at a certain time of year on a small island of the ocean; they say it can lift an elephant into the air with it. India and America provide parrots of various colors, crows, and others like them. But to seek out the eggs of these birds is not the Philosophical intention. The Egyptians annually attack the eggs of crocodiles with iron and pursue them as if by a publicly declared war; Philosophers strike their egg with fire, not so that it might be destroyed and perish, but so that it might receive life and grow. 

For while the chick is produced from there, animated and living, it should not be called its corruption <note>original: "corruptio"; in natural philosophy, this refers to the breakdown of one form to allow for the generation of another.</note> but its generation. Indeed, it ceases to be an egg through the loss of its egg-like form, and begins to be a two-legged and winged animal through the introduction of a more noble form. There are, moreover, in the egg the seeds of the male and female joined together under one roof or shell. The yolk constitutes the chick and its fundamental limbs and internal organs, with the seed of the male acting as the internal former and shaper. The white <note>original: "Albumen"</note> provides the matter or the foundation and growth for the first sketch or thread of the chick. 

External heat is the "first mover," which by a certain circulation of the elements and the transformation of one into another, introduces a new form, by the instinct or guidance of nature. For water passes into air, and air into fire, and fire into earth; when all these are joined, and a specific form is sent down from the stars, an individual of a certain bird species is made—namely, of that species whose egg and infused seed it is. 

Truly, the egg is said to be struck by a fiery sword while Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and blacksmiths, representing the alchemist's furnace.</note> provides an exit for the chick with his axe (just as he did from the brain of Jupiter for Pallas <note>Referring to the myth of Athena (Pallas) being born fully formed from the head of Zeus (Jupiter) after it was split open by Vulcan.</note>), performing the duty of a midwife. This is what Basil Valentine <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist whose writings were highly influential in the 17th century.</note> asserts: that Mercury was shut in a prison by Vulcan at the command of Mars, and was not released until he had been completely putrefied and died. But this death exists for him as the beginning of a new life, just as the corruption or death of the egg brings a new generation and life to the chick. Thus for the human fetus, once the "vegetative" life (which it enjoyed alone in the mother's womb) has ended, another more perfect life is added through the passage into this worldly light, or rather, through birth. Indeed, for us also, when we are deprived of this present life which we lead, another most perfect and eternal life looms,

F
<meta>catchword: sic (so/thus)</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical "Philosophical Egg" by comparing biological bird development and mythology to the process of transmutation. It argues that the "death" or destruction of the egg's initial form is a necessary step for the generation of a higher, living form.</summary>

<keywords>Philosophical Egg, generation and corruption, Vulcan, Basil Valentine, Ruc bird, alchemy, elements, transmutation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the prose explanation of Discourse VIII, moving from the physical properties of the "Philosophical Egg" to the nature of the heat required to transform it. It connects the biological observations of bird digestion with the alchemical concept of a "fiery sword" used to pierce and soften hard matter.</meta>

### DISCOURSE VIII.

Thus Lullius <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and polymath to whom many later alchemical works were attributed.</note> calls this fiery sword <note>original: "gladium igneum"</note> a "sharp lance" in many places. This is because fire, like a lance or a sharp sword, pierces bodies and makes them porous and permeable, so that water can penetrate them to dissolve them and bring them from a state of hardness to softness and flexibility. In the stomach of the sea-raven <note>The cormorant, a bird known for its intense appetite and powerful digestion.</note>, which is the most voracious of all birds, there are found long, slender living worms. These serve as his instruments of heat; as soon as he captures eels or fish, they attack them and pierce them like the sharpest needles (as we ourselves have observed on occasion). In this way, they consume the prey in a very short time through a wonderful operation of nature. Therefore, just as heat stings, so too does a stinging thing sometimes play the role of heat. For this reason, it is not without merit that we might speak of a "fiery sword" with which the philosophical egg <note>original: "ovum philosophorum"; the sealed vessel containing the alchemical matter, or the matter itself.</note> must be struck or bound.

However, the Philosophers would rather have this understood as a "temperate heat," by which the egg is fostered, as Morfoleus declares in the *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum," one of the earliest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a debate between ancient philosophers.</note> when he says: *“It is necessary, wise men, that the humidity first be burned away by a slow fire, as is shown to us by the example of the generation of a chick; and when the fire is increased, the vessel must be sealed on all sides, lest the body of the air and its fleeing spirit be drawn out.”* But to which bird does this egg belong? Moscus <note>Another speaker featured in the "Turba Philosophorum."</note> says in the same place: *“I, however, say that no instruments are made except from our white, starry, and splendid powder, and from the white stone; from this powder, suitable instruments are made for the egg; yet they have not named the egg, nor stated which bird’s egg it might be.”*

<sig>F iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Closing (original: "Schlieβ")</meta>

<summary>This page explores the alchemical concept of "fiery heat" as a penetrating force, comparing it to the digestion of a cormorant and the incubation of a chick. It references the Assembly of Philosophers to emphasize that the alchemical "egg" requires a carefully tempered fire and a sealed vessel for successful transformation.</summary>

<keywords>Ramon Lull, fiery sword, sea-raven, cormorant, digestion, philosophical egg, temperate heat, Turba Philosophorum, Morfoleus, Moscus, white stone, alchemical fire</keywords>

### IX. *German version of the Latin Epigram.*

In the Garden of Wisdom, a tree of golden fruit can be found,
Which you should join to our old man;
And shut them both in a house of glass surrounded by dew <note>The "house of glass" refers to the alchemical flask or cucurbit, while "dew" represents the mercurial water or solvent used in the process.</note>,
And let him live there many days thus enclosed.
Then the man will (wonderfully) eat the fruits of the tree quite soon,
And become young, though he was previously very old in years.

### EPIGRAM IX.

A tree is in the gardens of Wisdom <note>original: "Sophiæ"; refers to the personification of Wisdom or the alchemical Sophia, often associated with the divine source of the Stone.</note> bearing golden apples,
This must be taken by you along with our old man <note>In alchemical symbolism, the "old man" (senex) often represents Saturn or the "prime matter"—the raw, unrefined material that must undergo a process of death and rebirth.</note>;
And let them be enclosed in a house of glass, filled with dew <note>original: "roréque plenâ"; the "dewy house" refers to the sealed glass vessel or flask (cucurbit) used in the laboratory, where heated liquids evaporate and condense like dew.</note>,
And leave these two joined together for many days:
Then (wonderfully!) he will be satisfied by the fruit of the tree
So that he who was before an old man may become a youth <note>This "rejuvenation" symbolizes the purification and perfection of the matter as it transforms into the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.

### IX. *English version of the German Epigram.*

In the Garden of Wisdom, a tree of golden fruit can be found,
Which you should join to our old man;
And shut them both in a house of glass surrounded by dew,

<sig>F 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Which-</meta>

<summary>Emblem IX depicts the alchemical process of "rejuvenation," where the base matter (represented by an old man) is transformed into a perfected state (a youth) by being sealed in a glass vessel with the "Tree of Wisdom." This symbolizes the circular process of distillation and maturation required to achieve the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>rejuvenation, Sophia, old man, prime matter, glass vessel, philosophical tree, golden apples, Michael Maier, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical interpretation of Emblem IX from Michael Maier’s *Atalanta Fugiens*. While the previous page provided the image and epigram of the "Old Man" in the "Dewy House," this discourse provides the theoretical and medical justification for the concept of rejuvenation through the interaction of heat and moisture.</meta>

# DISCOURSE IX.

Whatever grows in length, breadth, and depth—that is, whatever arises, is nourished, increases, reaches its peak, and is propagated—likewise decreases; which is to say, it diminishes in strength, dies, and falls away completely, as is evident in all plants and animals. Hence, for man too, when he has reached his greatest growth, a decline—that is, old age—occurs, in which his strength is gradually diminished until death arrives.

Indeed, the cause of old age is the same as that of a lamp flickering and shining dimly when its oil is almost consumed. For just as in a lamp there are three things—the **wick**, the **fat** (oil), and the **flame**—so in man, the **wick** represents the vital members, the internal organs, and the limbs; the **fat** is the <term>radical moisture</term> <note>Latin: *humidum radicale*. In medieval medicine, this was the fundamental life-fluid gifted at birth that sustained the body's internal heat; its exhaustion led to aging and death.</note>; the **flame** is the <term>native heat</term> <note>Latin: *calor nativus*. The innate warmth that drives digestion and life functions.</note>. The only difference is that the flame of a lamp shines, while the native heat does not at all, since it is not fire but merely heat; and that the fat is oily, while the radical moisture is viscous, being of a seminal principle.

Just as a lamp is extinguished from a lack of oil, so man, from old age and without any other disease, falls into wasting and senile decay, and finally death. They say of the eagle that in old age, hindered by a curved beak, it would die of hunger unless, by nature casting off its beak, it were restored to a kind of youth. So too deer by shedding their antlers, snakes by their skins or sloughs, and crabs by their shells, seem to be rejuvenated—not truly, of course, because their consumed radical moisture is not restored to them, but in appearance.

There is nothing that can make a man rejuvenate except death itself and the beginning of the eternal life that follows. However, as for the restoration of the external form and strength in some way, and the removal of wrinkles and grey hairs, there are those who say a remedy can be found, as <term>Lullus</term> <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher to whom many alchemical works regarding the "Fifth Essence" or alcohol were attributed.</note> affirms in his *On the Fifth Essence*, and <term>Arnoldus</term> <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famed physician and alchemist who wrote on the medicinal properties of "potable gold."</note> in his work *On Prepared Gold*. 

But here the Philosophers <note>Meaning the alchemists.</note> say that for an old man to become a youth, he must be enclosed with a certain tree in a dewy house, and then he will eat of the fruit of the tree and thus recover his youth. That such trees exist in the nature of things is now scarcely believed by the common people. Regarding <term>myrobalans</term> <note>Astringent, plum-like fruits used in Ayurvedic and medieval medicine to balance the humors.</note>—fruits of a tree—physicians write wonderful things, as if they could perform such a feat, removing grey hairs, purifying the blood, and prolonging life. But these claims are called into doubt by many, unless they are said to achieve this by accident, like other things that purge the mass of blood from the dregs mixed into it, or those that dye grey hairs with the blackness with which myrobalans are said to color the hair and the pupils of the eyes. 

<term>Marsilio Ficino</term> <note>The 15th-century Neo-Platonist who sought to reconcile medicine, magic, and philosophy.</note>, in his book *On Preserving the Life of Scholars*, writes that to reach a long age it is useful for one to daily suck milk from the breasts of a certain beautiful and young woman; others in her place [recommend] the flesh of vipers...

<meta>catchword: co- (likely "comedere" or "cooked")</meta>

<summary>This discourse compares the human life cycle to a lamp's oil, explaining aging as the exhaustion of 'radical moisture.' It explores biological and alchemical myths of rejuvenation, specifically the 'dewy house' where an old man recovers his youth by eating the fruit of Wisdom.</summary>

<keywords>radical moisture, native heat, rejuvenation, Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Ramon Llull, Marsilio Ficino, myrobalans, alchemy, aging, lamp analogy</keywords>

# DISCOURSE IX.

...having eaten it, he praises it. But truly, these remedies are themselves more rigid than old age and are hardly to be used by one in a thousand, even if they did not lack a certain effect. It is written by **Paracelsus** <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim, known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), was a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note> in his book *On Long Life* that a sick man might attract another’s health, or an old man another's youth, through **imagination** alone;
<margin>imagination</margin>
but in this matter, the author seems to have used his imagination alone, rather than experience. Regarding the **Psylli** <note>A legendary tribe of North Africa said to have power over snakes and a gaze that could bewitch.</note> with their double pupils, and the witches who fascinate boys and cattle with a single look—whence comes that line of **Virgil**: *I know not what eye bewitches my tender lambs* <note>original: "Nescio quis teneros oculus mihi fascinet agnos." From Virgil's Eclogues.</note>—this is beyond doubt. But these things occur without contact, whereas the tree restores the old man to youth through physical presence. For this tree possesses sweet, ripe, and red fruits which easily pass into the highest quality blood, since they are easy to digest and provide excellent nourishment, leaving no excess or dregs in the body. The old man, however, abounds in white phlegm, and is of a white color and hair; this moisture, color, and hair are changed by eating these fruits, and they become red, which is the color of youth. Hence the **Philosophers** say that the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical "Great Work."</note> is first old—that is, white—and then young—that is, red—because this latter color belongs to youth, just as the former belongs to old age. It is added that the old man must be enclosed with the tree, not under the open sky, but in a house, and not a dry one, but one moist with dew. It is considered a miracle for trees to spring up or grow in an enclosed place; nevertheless, if that place be moist, 
<margin>A tree in an enclosed space</margin>
there is no doubt they may endure for a long time. For the nourishment of a tree is moisture and "aerial earth"—that is, rich soil—which can ascend into the trunk and branches, and there produce leaves, flowers, and fruits. In this natural work, all **elements** <note>The four classical elements: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth.</note> concur: while **fire** provides the first motion as the efficient cause, **air** provides thinness and penetrability, **water** provides fluidity, and **earth** provides coagulation. For air returns into water, and water into earth, if any excess of them has ascended. By fire, I mean the <term>native heat</term> <note>Latin: *calor nativus*. The innate biological warmth believed to drive life processes.</note>, which, like a blacksmith, crafts and forms fruits similar to those from which the seed came, through the power of the stars. Truly, the dewy evaporation is profitable not only for moistening the tree so that it may bear fruit, but also for the old man, so that he may more easily be rejuvenated by those fruits; for it softens, fills, and restores his wrinkled and dry skin with temperate heat and moisture. Indeed, for <term>marasmus</term> <note>A form of severe malnutrition or wasting away, often associated here with the decay of extreme old age.</note> and senile decay, physicians command and prescribe warm baths with great reason and utility. If the matter be well considered, that tree is the daughter of the old man, who, like **Daphne** <note>In Greek mythology, the nymph Daphne was transformed into a laurel tree to escape the pursuit of Apollo.</note>, was changed into such a plant; thus the old man quite rightly hopes for youth from her, of whose very existence he was the cause.

# Give fire to fire, Mercury to Mercury, and it is enough for you.

### EPIGRAM X.

**T**he whole machine of the world hangs connected by this chain, <note>original Latin: "Machina pendet ab hac mundi connexa catena." This refers to the "Golden Chain of Homer," a Neoplatonic and alchemical concept suggesting that all levels of creation are linked by a series of divine sympathies.</note>
*In its entirety,* BECAUSE EVERY LIKE REJOICES IN ITS LIKE: <note>original: "SUO QUOD PAR GAUDEAT OMNE PARI." This is a core alchemical maxim: like is attracted to like, and the perfection of the work requires using materials that share an essential nature.</note>
*Thus Mercury is joined to Mercury, thus fire to fire,*
*And let this be the goal set for your art.*

*Releases you, Cynthia, and you, sister of Apollo.* <note>Maier uses mythological names to represent metals and processes: Hermes (Mercury) is the transforming agent; Cynthia (the Moon) represents Silver; and Apollo (the Sun) represents Gold. The "release" refers to the purification and extraction of these noble metals.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Emblem X, providing the "Discursus" (Discourse or commentary) on the motto "Give fire to fire, Mercury to Mercury." The previous page introduced the concept of the Golden Chain and the attraction of like to like; this page explains the physiological and alchemical reasoning behind using similar substances to achieve transformation.</meta>

## DISCOURSE X.

This sentence, if taken literally as it sounds, at least increases the quantity of fire and Mercury, but it does not introduce any new quality into the subject. For everything like, when added to its like, becomes more of that same kind. Hence, doctors assert that contraries are cured by contraries <note>The medical principle of *contraria contrariis curantur*, where a "hot" disease is treated with a "cold" remedy.</note> and that the one is removed by the other; thus we see fire extinguished by water, but fostered by added fire. The Poet <note>A reference to the Roman poet Ovid, specifically his work *Remedia Amoris* (The Remedies for Love), line 807.</note> feels the same when he says: *And Venus rages in wines, and fire in fire.* <note>original: "Et Venus in vinis, ignis in igne furit." This suggests that adding wine to lust (Venus) is like adding fire to fire—it intensifies the condition.</note>

But it must be answered that fire and fire, and Mercury and Mercury, differ greatly from one another. For there are many kinds of fires among the Philosophers, and many kinds of Mercuries as well. Furthermore, the same heat or cold, differing at least in place or position, differs from another of its own kind in such a way that the one which is similar to itself calls the other out to itself. We notice this when the heat of fire impressed upon a limb is attracted and drawn out by that same heat. <note>An old medical belief that "drawing out" a burn with more heat (like holding it near a stove) would heal it faster than using cold.</note> Likewise, limbs made numb and almost dead by winter cold are restored by cold water if they are immersed in it, rather than by the immediate application of external heat.

Just as a greater light obscures a lesser one, so a greater heat overcomes a lesser, and a greater cold a lesser. Nevertheless, it is necessary that the external heat or cold be less than that which was previously impressed upon the limbs or joints; otherwise, the same impression would occur as before, and like would be increased much more by its like than it would be drawn out. For the "evocation" <note>The process of "calling out" or extracting a quality from a substance.</note> of cold by cold water, and of fiery heat by heat, is suitable to nature, because every sudden change into a contrary is dangerous to her and less acceptable, whereas that which happens gradually and as if by degrees is tolerable.

So we say that one fire is internal, and exists already fixed or essential within the Subject of the Philosophers, <note>The "Subject of the Philosophers" refers to the raw material or "Prima Materia" used at the start of the alchemical Great Work.</note> while another is external; which is also to be understood regarding Mercury. That internal fire is so called equivocally <note>In Aristotelian logic, an "equivocal" term is one where the name is the same but the definition is different—like the "fire" of passion versus the "fire" of a candle.</note> because of its fiery qualities, powers, and operations; the external is so called unequivocally. Therefore, external fire is given to the internal, and Mercury of a certain kind to Mercury, so that the intention of the Art may be achieved.

To soften or ripen everything hard or raw by cooking, we use fire and water. For water dissolves hardness and penetrates into compact parts, while heat adds power and motion to it. This appears, for example, in the cooking of peas, which by themselves are whole and hard, but they swell from the water, break, and are reduced to a pulp, as the heat of the fire rarefies the water through boiling and renders it a thinner or almost airy substance. Thus, the heat of fire resolves the raw parts of fruits or meats into water, and makes them vanish with it into the air. In the same way, fire and Mercury here are fire and water, and these same, fire and Mercury, are the parts both mature and raw...

<meta>catchword: raw [dæ]</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical principle of "like to like," using analogies from medicine and cooking to show how external fire and Mercury are used to activate and mature their internal, "essential" counterparts within the alchemical subject.</summary>
<keywords>homeopathy, sympathy, internal fire, external fire, Mercury, Aristotle, Ovid, alchemy, coction, ripening, evocation</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the Discourse on Emblem X. The previous page discussed how "like is attracted to like" and compared alchemical processes to medical treatments where heat is used to draw out heat. It concluded by mentioning limbs that had become numb or frozen.</meta>

## DISCOURSE X.

...frozen, from which the raw parts must be matured through thorough cooking, or the mature parts must be purged of superfluities by the ministry of fire and water. That these two fires and two Mercuries are primarily and uniquely necessary to the Art <note>The "Art" refers to alchemy, the practice of transforming base metals into gold or seeking the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, we shall briefly demonstrate here. 

Empedocles <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher (c. 490–430 BC) who proposed that the universe is composed of four elements acted upon by two opposing forces.</note> established two principles for all things: strife and friendship. Through strife, corruptions occur; through friendship, generations. A strife of this kind is clearly seen between fire and water, for fire makes water evaporate if it can, and water, on the other hand, extinguishes fire if applied to it. Yet it is evident that from these same things, generations occur through a certain friendship. For by heat, a new generation of air is made from water, and by that same heat, the hardening of water into stone occurs; and thus from these two elements, as if they were the primary ones, the remaining two are also made, and consequently, the production of all things. 

Water was the matter of heaven and of all corporeal things; fire, acting as the "form," moves and informs this matter. In this way, this water or Mercury provides the matter, and fire or sulfur provides the form. So that these two may reach their operation and move each other by dissolving, coagulating, altering, coloring, and perfecting, there was a need for external aids, like instruments, without which no effect would follow. For just as a smith accomplishes nothing without a hammer and fire, so neither does the Philosopher <note>In alchemical texts, "the Philosopher" usually refers to the practitioner or a specific authority like Aristotle or Hermes Trismegistus.</note> without his instruments, which are water and fire. 

And this water is called by some the "water of the clouds," just as this fire is called "occasional fire" <note>original: "ignis occasionatus." This refers to the external, artificial fire of the furnace, as opposed to the internal "innate" fire of the substance.</note>. It is undoubtedly called the "water of the clouds" because it is distilled, like the May dew, consisting of the most subtle parts. For just as the dew of the said month, when enclosed in an eggshell, is said to lift the egg (or whatever contains it) on high by the heat of the sun, so also this water of the clouds or dew makes the Philosophical Egg <note>The sealed glass vessel, often called the "Hermetic Egg," where the alchemical transformation takes place.</note> ascend—that is, it sublimates, exalts, and perfects it. 

This same water is also the "sharpest vinegar," which makes the body into pure spirit. For as vinegar is of various qualities—namely, to penetrate deep and to bind—so this water dissolves and coagulates; yet it is not itself coagulated, because it is not from its own subject. Moreover, this water is sought from the fountain of Parnassus <note>A mountain in Greece sacred to the Muses; the fountain Hippocrene was said to have been created by the hoof-strike of the winged horse Pegasus.</note>, which, contrary to the nature of other springs, exists at the summit of the mountain, made by the hoof of Pegasus, the winged horse.

Furthermore, it is necessary that actual fire be present, which should nevertheless be tempered by its stages, as if by reins. For as the Sun, proceeding from Aries into Leo <note>The sun's path through the zodiac from the beginning of spring (Aries) to the height of summer (Leo), representing a steady increase in heat.</note>, gradually increases the heat for growing things by always approaching closer, so it must happen here. For the Philosophical Infant <note>A metaphor for the Stone or the developing alchemical work in its early stages.</note> must be nourished by fire as if by milk; the more it grows, the more abundant the nourishment must be.

<summary>This page explains the dual roles of Mercury (as matter/water) and Fire/Sulfur (as form) in the alchemical process, using the philosophy of Empedocles to describe their interaction. It compares the external fire and distilled water to tools like a smith's hammer, essential for the "Philosophical Egg" to mature.</summary>

<keywords>Empedocles, Mercury, sulfur, water of the clouds, May dew, Philosophical Egg, Parnassus, Pegasus, Aries, Leo, Philosophical Infant, alchemy</keywords>

# Whiten Latona and tear up the books.

<image-desc>An engraving from Michael Maier's alchemical work 'Atalanta Fugiens'. In a landscape near a body of water, a woman (Latona) sits while a man to her left pours liquid from a shallow bowl onto her head. To her right stands a man in classical attire and a plumed hat who is actively tearing pages out of books and throwing the fragments on the ground. ### EPIGRAM XI.

> *No one is ignorant of Latona’s twin offspring,*
> *(As ancient fame reports) who were born of Jupiter <note>original: "Jove"; the king of the gods.</note>.*
> *Others hand down that she is the light of the Sun joined*
> *With the Moon, though she has black spots upon her face.*
> *Therefore, prepare to whiten Latona, and without delay,*
> *Tear up the books that offer only loss and ambiguity.*

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Emblem XI, "Whiten Latona and tear up the books." While the previous page provided the symbolic image and verse, this page begins the "Discursus" (Discourse), providing the philosophical and practical justification for the emblem's instructions.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XI.

So great is the diversity among authors in their writing that those seeking the truth almost despair of ever finding the goal of the art <note>The "art" refers to alchemy, the "Great Work" of transforming matter and spirit.</note>. For not only are allegorical speeches difficult to grasp and the cause of many errors in themselves, but they are especially so when the same words are applied to different things, and different words to the same things. If anyone wishes to extricate themselves from these difficulties, they must either possess a divine genius to perceive the truth lurking beneath such great shadows, or possess inexhaustible labor and wealth to test through experience what is true and what is not.

But the philosophers say that one is not sufficient without the other: neither a sharp mind without the labor of the hands, nor labor without the mind; just as theory is nothing without practice, and vice versa. For no one is of such discerning intellect that they can avoid a hundred thousand twists, errors, spoken falsehoods, diversions, and ambiguities to remain on the true path of Nature. Hence the Philosophers say:
> He who has not yet erred, has not yet begun, and errors are the teachers of those things which ought to be done or not <note>original: "Qui nondum erravit, nondum incepit, & errores esse magistros eorum quæ fieri debeant aut non."</note>.

Likewise, they emphasize that someone could consume their entire life distilling and re-distilling, even if they lived a thousand years, before they arrived at the truth through experimentation alone. That little progress is made without study and the reading of authors is suggested by the *Corrector of Fools* <note>original: "Corrector fatuorum"; a title of a well-known medieval alchemical treatise often attributed to Richardus Anglicus.</note>, when he says:
> Study removes ignorance and leads the human mind back to true knowledge and to the understanding of any matter <note>original: "Studium amovet ignorãtiam & reducit humanum ingenium ad veram cognitionem & ad cujuslibet rei scientiam."</note>.

Therefore, it is primarily necessary to acquire knowledge through the study of this sweet work and to sharpen the mind through physical sayings <note>or "the sayings of the physicists/philosophers"</note>, since the knowledge of the truth is contained within them. If, therefore, those who labor do not despise study, they will sweetly taste the fruit resulting from it. But those who abhor study and yet wish to labor should see whether the art is an imitation of Nature herself, which the art desires to improve. For it is impossible for such a person to prepare the secrets of the Philosophers to a perfect end. Of these people, the wise say that they "approach practice like a donkey to hay," <note>original: "transeant ad practicam sicut asinus ad foenum"</note> not knowing toward what they stretch their snouts, except insofar as the external senses of sight and taste lead them to food without the use of intellect. So he says. Yet, lest anyone should exhaust themselves with excessive study (which is an inexhaustible sea of immense depth) and attempt to put into action every letter—even those that agree with many others—one should endeavor—

<meta>catchword: -tur (completing "conetur")</meta>

<summary>This discourse argues that the "whitening of Latona" (alchemical purification) requires a perfect balance of deep theoretical study and practical laboratory work, warning that trial-and-error alone is a path of endless failure.</summary>
<keywords>theory vs practice, allegory, alchemy, experimentation, Latona, Corrector fatuorum, philosophy of science</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Discourse" (Discursus) associated with Emblem XI. The previous page discussed the necessity of combining theory (reading books) with practice (the labor of the hands) and warned that even a thousand years of experiment without guidance might lead to failure.</meta>

...[consum]ed (though in vain) their strength, the age of their body, fame, wealth, and resources being meanwhile used up or diminished, the Philosophers use this emblematic speech: that **Latona must be whitened, and those books torn up, lest their hearts be broken.** For most books are written so obscurely that they are understood only by their own authors. Not a few were left behind out of envy to mislead others, or rather to delay them in their course so they might not reach the goal without difficulty, or to obscure what those authors had written previously. 

But what it means to *whiten Latona*—this is the work, this is the labor <note>original: "hoc opus hic labor est," a reference to Virgil's Aeneid regarding the difficulty of returning from the underworld</note> to investigate. *The Clanging of the Trumpet* <note>original: "Clangor buccinae," a well-known 14th-century alchemical treatise</note> asserts that *Latona is an imperfect body composed of the sun and moon.* The poets and most ancient writers establish that Latona is the mother of the Sun and Moon, or <term>Apollo and Diana</term> <note>In alchemy, Apollo represents Gold/the Sun and Diana represents Silver/the Moon</note>; others call her their nurse. They say Diana was brought forth first (for the Moon and whiteness appear first), and she then performed the duty of a midwife on the same day during the birth of her brother, Apollo. 

Latona is also one of the twelve <term>Hieroglyphic</term> gods of the Egyptians, from whom these and other allegories were spread to the remaining nations. Only the very few Egyptian priests understood their true mind and meaning, while everyone else interpreted them as various gods and goddesses or other subjects that did not exist in the nature of things. For this reason, after <term>Vulcan</term> <note>The god of fire, representing the alchemist's heat</note>, Latona had the most sumptuous temple in that land, roofed and adorned with gold, since she was the mother of the Philosophical Apollo and Diana. 

However, this Latona is dark and somewhat blackish, and she has many spots on her face which must be removed by art, namely through "whitening" <note>original: "dealbationem"</note>. Some people perform whitenings using <term>ceruse</term> <note>White lead, often used as a pigment or cosmetic</note>, sublimated mercury, talc reduced to oil, and similar substances, with which they encrust and cover the outer skin and thus whiten it. But these whitening encrustations fall off with any breath or liquid because they do not penetrate to the interior. But the Philosophers do not care for such colorings, which are used more to deceive the eyes than to tinge the interior substance of the body. They want the face of Latona to be whitened penetratively and the skin itself to be altered—that is, truly and not deceitfully. 

It might be asked, how does this happen? I respond: Latona must first be sought out and recognized; and even if she is extracted from a vile place, she must nevertheless be <term>sublimated</term> <note>The process of turning a solid into vapor to purify it</note> into a more worthy state. If, however, she is from a more noble place, she must be submerged into something viler, namely into dung <note>original: "fimum"; alchemists used the gentle, steady heat of rotting horse dung as a natural incubator for their flasks</note>. There, indeed, she will truly grow white and become "white lead" <note>original: "plumbum album"</note>. Once this is obtained, there is no reason to doubt success, or the "red lead," which is the beginning and the end of the work.

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical process of "whitening Latona," interpreting the mythological mother of Apollo and Diana as a dark, imperfect substance that must be purified into a "white lead" stage through penetrative chemical change rather than superficial coating.</summary>
<keywords>Latona, Apollo, Diana, whitening, dealbatio, Clangor Buccinae, Egyptian hieroglyphics, Vulcan, white lead, philosophical gold, alchemy</keywords>

### XII. English version of the German Epigram.

If you wish to know why the poets so often remind us,
That all our desires should be directed toward Helicon,
It is because of the stone placed there, famous far and wide as a memorial,
Which Saturn <term>Saturnus</term> <note>In alchemy, Saturn often represents lead or the "prime matter" that must be transformed</note> devoured instead of Jupiter <term>Jove</term> and then vomited up.
If you only look at the sound of the words, you are not wise,
For it is clear enough that this stone was alchemical <note>original: "Chymisch"</note>.

### EPIGRAM XII.

Do you desire to know the reason why so many poets speak of HELICON <note>Mount Helicon was the mythological home of the Muses and a symbol of poetic and philosophical inspiration</note>,
And why its peak must be sought by everyone?
There is a STONE <note>original: LAPIS</note> placed on the very summit as a MEMORIAL,
Which the father swallowed instead of Jupiter and then vomited up.
If you grasp the matter only as the words sound, your mind is foolish <note>The Latin phrase "mens tibi læva est" literally means "your mind is on the left," a common idiom for being misguided or foolish</note>,
For that is the CHEMICAL STONE <term>Lapis Chemicus</term> <note>The "Stone of Saturn" refers to the *materia prima* or the heavy, base matter—often associated with lead—that must be "vomited" or transformed to reveal the hidden "Jupiter" or gold within</note> of Saturn.
