<meta>This page continues the prose commentary (Discursus) for Emblem XII. The previous page introduced the myth of Saturn swallowing a stone instead of his son Jupiter, framing it as an alchemical allegory. This page expands on the various interpretations of Saturn, focusing on his association with "blackness" (nigredo) and the initial stage of the alchemical Great Work.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XII.

We find that the allegory of Saturn has been understood in various ways. For astronomers refer him to the highest of the planets in the order; beginners in Chemistry <note>original: "Chymiæ tyrones"</note> refer him to the lowest of the metals, namely lead. Heathen poets take him for the father of Jupiter and the son of Heaven. Mythologists have held him to represent Time. But although all these people seem to have understood correctly in their own respect and to have had a probable reason for their opinion, they nevertheless will not explain those other things said about Saturn: why he devoured and vomited up his sons and a stone in place of Jupiter; why he is the inventor of truth; and why he is conspicuous for his scythe, his serpent, his blackness, his moroseness, and for being club-footed <term>loripes</term> <note>Literally "strap-footed," referring to Saturn's slow, dragging gait, often associated with the slow movement of the planet lead and the onset of old age or decay</note>? 

The last of these groups <note>The Mythologists</note> think they interpret this best when they say that time opens up the truth and rescues it from the shadows; that time, like a serpent, slips away by coiling; that like a scythe, it mows down all things by death; that it devours its sons—that is, all things which it has generated; and that it is unable to digest or consume hard stones, and therefore vomits them up, as it were. But these explanations, while partially fitting, do not square with the reality of the matter and all the circumstances.

Truly practiced Philosophers say that in their work Saturn is present first; and if he is indeed present, they say it is impossible to go astray, for truth is discovered in the shadows. Indeed, nothing exists there except blackness. Whence they say: *Whichever color arrives after the blackness is praiseworthy in the crowd of philosophers.* And: *When you see your matter being blackened, rejoice; because it is the beginning of the work.* And: *When it is first blackened, we say it is the key to the work, since it does not happen without blackness,* as the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a seminal 16th-century alchemical text</note> has it from Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova, a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</note>. 

And from the *Mirror* <note>Likely the *Speculum Alchemiae*, attributed to Roger Bacon</note>: *When you are in the work, see to it that in the beginning you have the color black, and then you will be certain that you are putrefying and proceeding on the right path.* And soon after: *That blackness is called Earth, which is produced by a light decoction repeated so often until the blackness stands out on top.* Hence the same authors say that Saturn holds the earth, Mercury the water, Jupiter the air, and the Sun <note>original: "Sol"</note> the fire. 

Blackness, therefore, is Saturn, the indicator of truth, who devours the stone instead of Jupiter. For blackness—that is, a dark cloud—covers the stone at first, so that it cannot be seen. Regarding this, Morienes <note>A legendary hermit-alchemist said to have taught the King of Egypt</note> says: *Every body, when it lacks a soul, is found to be dark and obscure.* And Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy</note> says: *Take its brain, grind it with the sharpest vinegar, or with the urine of boys, until it is darkened.* Once this is performed, it lives in putrefaction <term>putrefactio</term> <note>The stage of alchemical "rotting" or breakdown required for rebirth</note>, and the dark clouds which were over it

H
<meta>catchword: &</meta>

<summary>Maier argues that while Saturn represents Time or Lead to others, to the Alchemist he represents the essential first stage of the Great Work: the "blackness" or putrefaction that proves the process is working correctly. He cites various authorities like Hermes and Arnold of Villanova to equate Saturn with the "Earth" element and the discovery of truth within darkness.</summary>

<keywords>Saturn, Jupiter, Lead, Blackness, Nigredo, Putrefaction, Alchemy, Rosarium Philosophorum, Arnold of Villanova, Hermes Trismegistus, Elements, Earth, Truth</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical commentary on the myth of Saturn and the stone. While the previous page introduced Saturn as a representation of Time or the metal lead, this page moves deeper into the specific chemical transformations, explaining how "whiteness" (the second stage of the work) is extracted from the "blackness" (the first stage) symbolized by Saturn's belly.</meta>

# DISCOURSE VI.

...and they return into his body before he dies. This stone is vomited up again by <term>Saturn</term> <note>In alchemy, Saturn represents the "nigredo" or black stage—the starting point of the work associated with lead and decay.</note> when it turns white, and it is then placed on the summit of <term>Helicon</term> <note>A mountain in Greece sacred to the Muses; here it represents the high achievement of the alchemical process.</note> as a monument for mortals, as <term>Hesiod</term> writes: for truly, **whiteness** <note>original: "albedo"</note> lies hidden beneath **blackness** <note>original: "nigredine"</note>, and it is extracted from his belly—that is, from the stomach of Saturn.

Hence <term>Democritus</term> <note>An ancient Greek philosopher often cited in alchemical texts as an authority on the transmutation of matter.</note> says: *Purify the tin with an exceptional washing, draw out from it its blackness and obscurity, and its brilliance will appear.* And in the **Turba** <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the earliest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a fictional debate among ancient sages.</note> it is said: *Join the dry with the moist, that is, the black earth with its water, and cook it until it is whitened.* <term>Arnold</term> <note>Arnold of Villanova, a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note> declares this best in *The New Light*, chapter 4, when he says: *Therefore, the moisture which managed the blackness during the cooking process reveals itself as dried out once the white color begins to appear.* And a little later: *And my master told me that this brownness ascended, and that whiteness was extracted from the belly of its blackness, just as it is said in the Turba.* *For when you see the thing itself as black, know that its whiteness is hidden in the belly of the blackness that first appeared.*

This same **blackness** is called **Saturn**, and likewise **lead** <note>original: "plumbum"</note>. Thus <term>Agadimon</term> <note>A legendary figure in Hermetic literature, likely a corruption of "Agathodaimon."</note> says in the **Turba**: *Cook the copper until the blackness comes out, which they call the "coin" <note>original: "numum"; likely a reference to a specific metallic state or symbol.</note>, and mix the materials of our art well, and you will immediately find the blackness, which is the lead of the wise, about which the wise have written much in their books.* To this the saying of <term>Emiganus</term> refers: *The splendor of Saturn, while it climbs into the air, does not appear except as darkened.* And in <term>Plato’s</term> *Rosarium*: *The first regimen of Saturn is to putrefy and to place in the sun.* From these things it is clear that the mind of the Philosophers is far different when they say anything about **Saturn** than how it is understood by the common people.

This **Saturn** begets **Jupiter**, that is, a dusky **whiteness**; then **Jupiter** begets **Diana** from **Latona**, which is perfect **whiteness**, and **Apollo**, which is **redness** <note>original: "rubedinem," the final "rubedo" stage of the Great Work.</note>. And this is the successive transformation of all the perfected colors. This stone, thrown out by **Saturn** and placed as a monument for mortals on the mountain peak, is spoken of as a most certain truth.

<sig>H ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: The <note>original: "Das"</note></meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical transition from the "black" stage (Saturn/Lead) to the "white" stage (Jupiter/Diana), using the myth of Saturn vomiting the stone as an allegory for extracting light from darkness. It cites various authorities like Democritus and the Turba Philosophorum to prove that the "Lead of the Wise" contains a hidden brilliance.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Nigredo, Albedo, Rubedo, lead of the wise, Turba Philosophorum, Arnold of Villanova, chemical colors, Jupiter, Diana, Apollo, alchemical stages</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical sequence from Discourse VI, moving from the stage of Saturn and the extraction of whiteness from blackness into Fugue XIII. While the previous page discussed the theoretical extraction of the stone from Saturn's "belly" (the core of the matter), this page presents the practical requirement for "washing" or purifying the material, using the biblical metaphor of Naaman the Syrian.</meta>

<header>FUGUE XIII. In the 4th, or the 11th above.</header>

# The Philosophers' Ore <note>original: "Ertz der Weisen"</note> is dropsical <note>original: "wassersüchtig" / "hydrope"; a historical medical term for edema or fluid retention, here symbolizing an excess of "crude moisture" in the material.</note>, and seeks to be bathed seven times in the river, like the leper Naaman in the Jordan.

### XIII. *German version of the Latin Epigram.*

The Philosophers’ Ore is completely overburdened with water in its body,
Therefore it desires water, to bathe itself well therein:
Just as <term>Naaman</term> <note>A biblical commander (2 Kings 5) who was cured of leprosy by washing seven times in the Jordan River. In alchemy, "leprosy" refers to the impurities and corrosion of base metals.</note> drove away leprosy through the <term>Jordan</term>,
It will be well-rubbed seven times with water.
Therefore, plunge your bodies into the sweet rivers,
And so in its sickness it will soon enjoy relief.

# EMBLEM XIII. On the Secrets of Nature.

H 3
<meta>catchword: Because [original: Quod]</meta>

->**The Bronze of the Philosophers is dropsical, and seeks to be washed seven times in the river, just as Naaman the leper was in the Jordan.**<-

### EPIGRAM XIII.

*Languishing with a very swollen <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "hydrope"; a historical medical term for edema or fluid retention. In alchemy, it symbolizes an excess of "crude moisture" or impurities in the base metal that prevents it from reaching perfection.</note>, the <term>Bronze of the Wise</term> <note>original: "æs... Sophorum"; refers to the "copper" or base material of the philosophers at the start of the work.</note> swells up,*
*From there, it seeks those health-giving waters.*
*And just as Naaman removed the contagions of leprosy in the Jordan,*
*It is washed three and four times <note>original: "térque quatérque"; a poetic way of saying seven times (3+4), directly referencing the seven washings of the biblical Naaman and the seven stages of alchemical purification.</note> in its own waters:*
*Therefore, plunge your bodies into the sweet water,*
*And soon that water will bring the aid of health to your diseases.*

The "dropsy" of the metal represents a state of impurity and excess moisture that must be cleansed through seven successive washings or distillations.</summary>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem XIII. While the previous page established the visual and poetic metaphor of Naaman the Syrian being healed in the Jordan, this discourse explains the scientific and spiritual rationale behind the "washing" of the Philosophers' Bronze. It transitions from a biblical miracle to an alchemical process of purification.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XIII.

That Naaman the Syrian, at the Prophet's command, betook himself to Judea and washed himself seven times in the river Jordan, is to be ascribed to his confidence in the Prophet's words. But that he was actually freed from leprosy by that washing is a miracle of divine omnipotence. For leprosy <gloss>enim</gloss>—which is embedded in the blood and the foundational members of the human body and exists like a universal cancer—cannot be cured or removed by any external washings, much less by cold water such as the Jordan was.

In the same way, that the Bronze of the Philosophers <note>original: "æs Philosophorum"; the base material or "subject" of the alchemical work, often symbolizing a state of matter that is "sick" or "imperfect" before its transformation.</note> suffering from <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "hydropisi"; a historical medical term for edema or fluid retention. Alchemically, it represents an excess of crude, watery moisture that must be removed.</note> is freed from it by washings of water—indeed, that the imperfect is brought back to perfection and the sick to health, so much so that it can impart the same health to others who are sick—is something very close to a miracle. For <gloss>enim</gloss> no other such example exists in nature, nor is it nature’s ordinary way to produce the most absolute Philosophical <gloss>Philosophicam</gloss> Tincture unless it is governed by a craftsman and suitable subjects are administered to it with an external agent. Just as the resetting of dislocations is not proper to nature but to art, nevertheless, miraculously, in human birth the *os sacrum* <note>The "sacred bone" at the base of the spine; Maier suggests its movement during birth is a natural miracle orchestrated by God.</note> opens itself so that the fetus can exit through it, as <gloss>per</gloss> if through a door; and in <gloss>inque</gloss> this, God, the Best and Greatest, works through nature above nature.

Thus, that the Stone is perfected can seem to be something <gloss>quoddam</gloss> hyperphysical (beyond nature), although in truth it is physical. Whence the Philosopher <gloss>Philosophus</gloss> in the *Rosary* <note>original: "apud Rosarium"; referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a seminal 16th-century alchemical text.</note> says: "Know that <gloss>quod</gloss> our Stone is airy and volatile; in its manifest [outer] part it is cold and moist, and in its hidden [inner] part it is hot and dry. And that coldness and moisture, which in the manifest is a watery smoke—corrupting, blackening, and destroying itself and all things—flees from the fire. And the heat and dryness, which is in the hidden part, is hot and dry gold, and is a most pure oil, penetrating into bodies; and it is not fugitive <note>In alchemy, "fugitive" refers to substances that evaporate or escape when heated; "fixed" substances remain.</note>, because the heat and dryness of Alchemy performs the tinting, and nothing else. Therefore, ensure that <gloss>quod</gloss> the watery coldness and moisture, which is in the manifest, becomes a partner to the heat and dryness, which is in the hidden, so that they come together and are joined, and become together a single penetrating, tinting, and deep-reaching thing. However, these moistures must be destroyed by fire and stages of fire with a gentle temperament and a suitable and moderate digestion."

Thus far the author. But if this is true, how can it be freed from waters by waters? The answer is that there are certain waters hot and dry in quality, such as many thermal springs, in which it must be washed Philosophically <gloss>Philosophice</gloss>. For <gloss>enim</gloss> this is what <gloss>quod</gloss> they say: "Wash with fire and burn with water." For the fire that washes and the water that burns differ from one another only in name; in effect and operation, they agree. And so with <gloss>Hacitaque</gloss> this water, or this fire, that bronze...

<meta>The text cuts off here with the catchword "abluen-" for "abluendum" (to be washed), continuing the theme of purification on the following page.</meta>

<summary>Maier compares the biblical healing of Naaman's leprosy to the alchemical purification of "Philosophical Bronze." He argues that both require a "miraculous" intervention through a specialized water that acts like fire to remove internal impurities.</summary>
<keywords>Naaman the Syrian, Jordan River, Philosophical Bronze, leprosy, dropsy, Rosarium Philosophorum, alchemical washing, purifying fire, os sacrum, Tincture</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and medical justification for the "washing" of the alchemical matter mentioned on the previous page. It draws parallels between the treatment of dropsy (edema) in human patients and the purification of the "Philosophical Bronze" from its superfluous moisture.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XIII.

...it must be washed from its superfluous humidities, that is, it must be dried out. Regarding those suffering from dropsy <gloss>hydropicis</gloss> cured by a six-month abstinence from all drink, by burial in hot sand or cow dung, by enclosure in a hot oven, and by sweating, and by countless other aids, we have known these experiments; indeed, even through drying baths <term>thermas</term>, such as those of Carlsbad and Wiesbaden not far from Mainz <note>original: "Moguntia"; a major city in Germany.</note>. In these same ways, this patient <note>The "patient" refers to the alchemical material being refined.</note> must be cured: sometimes with waters, sometimes with the hot air of ovens, sometimes with dung, sometimes with sands and by abstinence from drink. For these remedies are most effective in both cases, to be applied here and there. In all of these, however, heat is the operator, which draws out and consumes the superfluous waters through their emunctories <term>emunctoria</term> <note>Historical term for organs or pathways that carry off waste, such as the kidneys or pores.</note> or even the pores of the body. For external heat excites the internal heat—that is, the vital spirits <term>spiritus vitales</term>—so that they may expel the humor harmful to themselves as if it were useless waste, by which the natural heat had previously been suppressed as if by an enemy.

In this cure, there is need for great diligence and precaution, lest while one of the internal organs <gloss>viscerum</gloss> is being helped, another is harmed. In Quartan fever <term>Quartana</term> <note>A form of malaria where paroxysms occur every fourth day, historically associated with "black bile."</note> (with Platonic medical confirmation), we have experienced that thick and viscous humors, like gum or the birdlime of trees, gathered from all the veins and the mass of the blood, descend through the *vena cava* or "great vein" to the depths of the back. There, since they obstruct the emulgent veins <note>The renal veins, which "milk" or filter the blood.</note> which draw the serous <note>watery</note> humor from the blood, or obstruct their openings, those veins will be able to function less, and more serous liquid remains in the body; and thus shortly, unless it is foreseen, dropsy can occur even if other primary organs are unharmed. Diuretics here do little or nothing; purgatives even less, unless in the course of time a reduction and drawing out of the impacted matter occurs. Sudorifics <note>sweat-inducing medicines</note> also clearly harm, because they draw out the more subtle parts while leaving the thicker ones behind, and by their continued force, they overthrow the strength. For nature is accustomed to find a way for itself through the pores to evacuate the serosities when the path to the bladder is blocked. This, therefore, is a Scylla, the other a Charybdis <note>A classic metaphor for being caught between two equally dangerous options.</note>, both of which he who wishes to be well-provided for must avoid.

But the dropsy which happens from a damaged liver <term>epate</term> or spleen <term>liene</term>, if it be confirmed, is of the most difficult cure. In the Philosophical Bronze <term>ære Philosophico</term>, however, the cure is not impossible, since the affliction is rather accidental and secondary than essential or primary—provided that one begins cautiously, as we said regarding the abundance of iron in Quartan fever—lest, namely, by too much drying it falls into a wasting disease <term>tabes</term> or by too much moistening it falls into a dropsy that is difficult to cure.

<summary>This page uses the medical treatment of dropsy (excess fluid) as a metaphor for the alchemical purification of "Philosophical Bronze," emphasizing the need for controlled heat and careful balance to avoid destroying the material.</summary>

<keywords>dropsy, edema, Quartan fever, Philosophical Bronze, alchemy, Wiesbaden, Carlsbad, vital spirits, emunctories, humoral medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Discourse XIII, moving from medical analogies of dropsy to the biblical story of Naaman the Syrian's cure from leprosy. It transitions into a technical alchemical discussion quoting the "Rosarium Philosophorum" about the dual nature of the Stone—distinguishing between its manifest (visible) and hidden qualities—and the paradoxical use of "water" and "fire" in the purification process.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XIII.

**T**hat Naaman the Syrian <note>A commander of the Syrian army mentioned in the Hebrew Bible (2 Kings 5) who was cured of leprosy.</note>, at the command of the Prophet, traveled to Judea and washed himself seven times in the river Jordan, is to be ascribed to his confidence in the Prophet's words; however, that he was freed from leprosy by that washing is a miracle of divine omnipotence. For leprosy, which is embedded in the blood and the radical <note>In historical medicine, "radical" refers to the root or fundamental components of the body.</note> members of the human body, exists like a universal cancer; it cannot be cured or removed by any external washings, much less by cold water such as that of the Jordan. In the same way, that the Philosophical bronze <gloss>æs Philos.</gloss> suffering from dropsy <gloss>hydropisi</gloss> <note>A historical term for edema or an excess of watery fluid in the body.</note> should be freed from it by washings with water—indeed, that the imperfect should be led back to perfection and the sick to health, so that it might even bestow that same health upon others who are sick—is next to a miracle. For such an example does not otherwise exist in nature, nor is it the ordinary way of nature to produce the most absolute Philosophical tincture <term>tinctura Philos.</term> <note>A substance capable of transforming or "staining" metals into gold or healing the body.</note> unless it is governed by the artist and suitable subjects are administered to it with an external agent. Just as the resetting of dislocated limbs is not a property of nature, but of art; nonetheless, in human birth the sacred bone <gloss>os sacrum</gloss> miraculously opens itself so that the fetus can exit through it as if through a gate, and in this, God the Best and Greatest works through nature above nature. Thus, that the Stone <gloss>Lapis</gloss> is perfected can be seen as something hyper-physical <note>Beyond the laws of physics or nature.</note>, even though it is in truth physical.

Wherefore the Philosopher in the *Rosarium* <note>original: "Philosophus apud Rosarium"; referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, or "Rose Garden of the Philosophers," a highly influential 16th-century alchemical text.</note> says: "Know that our stone is airy and volatile; in its manifest <note>Visible or external.</note> state it is cold and moist, and in its hidden state it is hot and dry. And that coldness and moisture, which is in the manifest state, is a watery smoke, corrupting, blackening, and destroying itself and all things, and it flees from fire. But the heat and dryness, which is in the hidden state, is hot and dry gold, and is the purest oil, penetrating into bodies; and it is not fugitive, because the heat and dryness of Alchemy stains <note>tinctures or transforms</note>, and nothing else. Therefore, see to it that the watery coldness and moisture, which is in the manifest, becomes the equal of the heat and dryness which is in the hidden, so that they may come together and be joined, and become together one penetrating, staining, and deepening substance." These humidities, however, must be destroyed by fire and stages of fire with a gentle temperament and suitable and moderate digestion. Thus it is said there: But if this is true, how can it be freed from waters by waters? The answer is that there are certain waters that are hot and dry in quality, such as many thermal springs <term>thermę</term>, in which the Philosophical matter must be washed. For this is what they say: "Wash with fire and burn with water." For the fire that washes and the water that burns differ from each other only in name; they agree in effect and operation. Therefore, with this water, or with this fire, that bronze...

->is to be washed<-

<summary>This page compares the alchemical purification of "Philosophical bronze" to the miraculous cure of Naaman the Syrian's leprosy, arguing that such transformation requires the "art" of the alchemist to transcend ordinary nature. It quotes the Rosarium Philosophorum to explain that the Stone's external moisture must be balanced with its internal heat using paradoxical "burning water."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medical and alchemical analogy from page 62. While the previous page discussed the miraculous nature of curing "leprosy" and "dropsy" in the Philosophical bronze, this page details the specific methods of treatment—moving from physical medical practices (like heat therapy and mineral baths) to the internal chemical requirements for the alchemical work.</meta>

->### DISCOURSE XIII.<-

...must be washed from its excess moisture—that is, it must be dried out. We know of experiments concerning those suffering from **dropsy** <gloss>hydrops</gloss> <note>A condition involving an excess of watery fluid in the body, today called edema.</note> who were cured by a six-month abstinence from all drink, by burial in hot sand or cow dung, by being enclosed in a hot oven, by sweating, and by countless other aids. Indeed, we also know of cures by means of drying mineral baths, such as those at **Carlsbad** <note>original: "Carolinæ"</note> and **Wiesbaden** <note>original: "Wisbadenses"</note> not far from **Mainz** <note>original: "Moguntia"</note>. By these same methods, this patient <note>The alchemical substance or "Philosophical bronze."</note> must be cured: sometimes with waters, sometimes with the hot air of ovens, sometimes with dung, sometimes with sands, and by abstinence from drink. For these are the most effective remedies in either case, to be applied here and there. In all these treatments, however, **heat** is the operator; it draws out and consumes the superfluous waters through the body's emunctories <term>emunctoria</term> <note>Organs or pathways that carry off waste from the body.</note> or even through the pores. For external heat excites the internal heat—that is, the **vital spirits**—so that they might expel the harmful humor from themselves as if it were useless excrement; this is the same humor by which the natural heat had previously been suppressed, as if by an enemy.

Great diligence and precaution are needed in this cure, lest while one of the internal organs is being helped, another is harmed. In the case of **Quartan fever** <term>Quartana</term> <note>A form of malaria where the fever recurs every fourth day; historically associated with "black bile" and blockages.</note> (as confirmed by the medical approval of **Plato**), we have experienced that thick and viscous humors—resembling gum or the sticky "birdlime" of trees—gather from all the veins and the mass of the blood. These descend through the *vena cava* (the great vein) even to the depth of the back. When they obstruct the **emulgent veins** <note>The renal veins, which were thought to "milk" or filter the serum from the blood into the kidneys.</note>—which draw the watery humor out of the blood—or the openings of those veins, the veins are less able to function. Consequently, more watery liquid remains in the body, and thus in a short time, unless care is taken, dropsy can occur even if the other primary organs remain uninjured.

Diuretics <note>Medications that encourage urination.</note> profit little or nothing here; purgatives even less, unless over time the blockage is diminished and drawn out. Even sweat-inducing remedies are clearly harmful, because they draw out the thinner, subtle parts of the fluid while leaving the thicker parts behind, and their continued use prostrates the patient's strength. Nature usually finds a way for itself through the pores to evacuate these watery fluids when the path to the bladder is closed. This, therefore, is a **Scylla** on one side and a **Charybdis** on the other <note>A classical metaphor for being caught between two equally hazardous choices.</note>, both of which must be avoided by anyone who wishes to be well-cared for. Indeed, when dropsy occurs because of a damaged **liver** <gloss>epar</gloss> or **spleen** <gloss>lien</gloss>, if the condition becomes "confirmed," it is extremely difficult to cure. In the **Philosophical air**, however, a cure is not impossible, since the affliction is secondary or accidental rather than essential or primary—provided that one begins cautiously. As we said regarding the abundance of fluid in the Quartan fever, one must take care that through excessive drying the patient does not fall into **consumption** <note>A wasting disease (tabes).</note>, or through excessive moistening into a dropsy that is difficult to cure.

<summary>This page compares the alchemical purification of "Philosophical bronze" to the medical treatment of dropsy and Quartan fever. It emphasizes the use of heat to drive out excess moisture and warns the practitioner to balance drying and moistening to avoid damaging the "organs" of the work.</summary>

<keywords>dropsy, Quartan fever, vital spirits, mineral baths, liver and spleen, Scylla and Charybdis, alchemical heat, emunctories</keywords>

->XIV. German version of the Latin Epigram.<-

Hunger forces the many-footed fish to consume its own feet,
And humans often nourish themselves with the flesh of humans;
Thus, it does not grieve the dragon to bite and swallow its tail,
So that it enjoys a part of its own body for its food;
This same dragon is to be tamed through the sword, hunger, and imprisonment,
Until it eats itself entirely and vomits, kills itself, and brings forth life.

> Terrible hunger taught the Polypi <note>original: "Polypos." The octopus or "many-footed fish." In ancient natural history (such as in Pliny), it was believed that octopuses would eat their own tentacles when starving.</note> to gnaw their own limbs,

> it devours and vomits itself up, kills itself, and gives birth to itself.

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XIV of Michael Maier's 'Atalanta Fugiens'. The previous page introduced Emblem XIV, depicting the Ouroboros—the dragon biting its own tail—through an engraving, a poetic epigram, and a musical fugue. This page provides the 'Discursus' or prose commentary, explaining the natural history and alchemical symbolism of the self-devouring dragon.</meta>

-># DISCOURSE XIV.<-

The ancients said that a serpent that devours a serpent becomes a dragon. For it rages against its own kind like a thief and a murderer against his own. It is established that such creatures exist in Africa in such great size and abundance that they destroyed a large part of Alexander’s army. Among the Asachaeans <note>A people of Ethiopia mentioned in Pliny’s Natural History.</note>, a people of Ethiopia, the largest are born; they entwine themselves like wicker-work and, with their heads raised, "sail" toward better pastures. It is said that the kings of the Indians raised two dragons, one eighty cubits in size and the other ninety. From the observations of more recent men at this time, it has become known that such creatures are still found near Angola, so large that they equal the massive masts of ships. Likewise, in certain mountains of India and Africa, they report a great force of gold, but say it is guarded by dragons so that no one may approach and take it away. For dragons gather at the springs or streams that fall from the mountains, and thus by chance they are said to keep watch over the gold.

Hence, so many dragons or serpents are attributed by the Philosophers to their treasures, such as the Golden Fleece, the Garden of the Hesperides, and other chemical persons or subjects: Cadmus, Saturn, Aesculapius, and Mercury, whose caduceus <note>The herald’s staff.</note> is encircled by twin serpents, male and female. By "Dragons," however, they understand nothing other than chemical subjects. Hence they say:

> The mountains give dragons to the Rebis <note>original: "Dant Rebis montes dracones." The Rebis—from the Latin 'res bina' or 'double thing'—is the alchemical hermaphrodite representing the union of spirit and matter.</note>, and the earth gives fountains.

And they say that the Dragon devours its own tail to denote its extreme hunger. Although others explain this as the year returning into itself and forming a circle, it was originally adapted by the philosophers to their own affairs. They wish this dragon to be understood as the serpent that devours another of its own kind, and this is properly called **sulphur**, as all testify in innumerable places. Lull <note>Raymund Lull (c. 1232–1315), a polymath and reputed alchemist.</note> says in his *Codicillus*, chapter 31:

> "This, my son, is the **sulphur**; this is the serpent and the dragon devouring its own tail, the roaring Lion and the sharp sword, cutting all things, mortifying and shattering them."

And the *Rosarium* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> says:

> "The dragon does not die unless it is killed with its brother and its sister." <note>original: "Draco non moritur, nisi cum fratre & sorore sua interficiatur."</note>

And soon after:

> "The Dragon is the **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum."</note> extracted from bodies, having in itself a body, a soul, and a spirit."

In the same place, this water is called by another name, the "stinking water," which is obtained after the separation of the Elements. Moreover, the dragon is said to devour its tail when it takes the volatile, poisonous, and moist part...

<meta>The catchword "midam" refers to the continuation of the word "humidam" (moist) on the next page.</meta>

<summary>This page explains the natural and mythical history of dragons as a metaphor for alchemical processes, specifically identifying the Ouroboros (tail-eater) with the substance of sulphur and the extraction of mercury.</summary>

<keywords>Ouroboros, dragon, sulphur, quicksilver, Raymund Lull, Rosarium Philosophorum, Rebis, alchemy, Ethiopia, India</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the prose 'Discourse' for Emblem XIV. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how the mountains provide dragons to the "Rebis" (the alchemical hermaphrodite or composite matter). This page explains the physical and symbolic results of the dragon devouring its own tail, linking it to the properties of mercury, the behavior of serpents, and the concept of rejuvenation.</meta>

-># DISCOURSE XIV.<-

...[tail] it consumes, so that afterwards, without its tail, it appears more corpulent and slower, since its movement and agility occurred in large part by reason of the tail. Other animals rely on feet, but dragons, serpents, and that genus of worms use the constriction and unfolding of their bodies in place of feet; and just as poured-out water defines its path in certain circuits, bending itself now here, now there, so it is possible to see in almost all rivers how they bend their courses and twist in coils like serpents.

For this reason, they have called quicksilver <term>Quicksilver: the liquid metal mercury (argentum vivum)</term> a "serpent" not without cause, and have assigned serpents to Mercury <note>The Roman god of commerce and alchemy, often depicted with the caduceus or serpent-staff.</note>, since it both draws a tail, as it were, and runs forward now here, now there with a rolling weight. For just as the serpent creeps <note>original: *Serpens enim ut serpit*</note>, so also does Mercury, who for that reason has wings on his feet and head. 

In Africa, they say there are winged serpents which would devastate everything if they were not destroyed by the Ibis. Hence the Ibis is placed among the sacred Egyptian images, both because of the manifest utility it provides to the whole region, and because of an occult reason which few have understood. 

This dragon, having bitten off its tail and cast off its old skin, is said to acquire a new one along with youth; thus nature has granted a longer life not only to crows, ravens, eagles, and stags, but also to the serpent kind. An ant growing old acquires wings, as do many worms. A man growing old is committed to the earth, but being brought back to life from the earth, he will be consecrated to eternal life. From any burned serpent a powder is made, which is taken harmlessly with great effect against all poisons. An antidote <term>Alexipharmacum: a protective medicine or "ward against poison"</term> of this kind ought also to be made from this dragon, which has now devoured its own tail (which is usually cut off in vipers), as a most ready remedy against the evils of fortune and the body.

<keywords>Ouroboros, mercury, quicksilver, Ibis, rejuvenation, alexipharmacum, serpent symbolism, alchemy, immortality</keywords>

### XI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Behold the potter, who makes his vessel with the wheel,
How he drives the water into the clay <note>original: *Leim* (loam or clay)</note> with his feet,
In these two his hope stands, so that suitable moisture
May moisten the dry powder, and take away its thirst;
So you must also do, as this example has taught you,
That water may not overcome the earth, nor be overcome by it.

<image-desc>A detailed woodcut illustration depicts a potter at work in his workshop. The potter, a bearded man wearing a cap and a simple tunic, is seated on a low three-legged stool. He leans forward, using both hands to shape a tall clay vessel on a potter's wheel, which he powers with his bare left foot. ## EPIGRAM XV.

> ***B**ehold with what speed the potter shapes his vessels*
> *On the axle of the wheel, while with his foot he mixes clay with water:*
> *His confidence lies in these two things, so that the moisture*
> *May skillfully temper the thirst of the dry dust.*
> *Thus you shall also do, made wiser by this example,*
> *Lest the water overcome the earth, or the earth be overcome by the water.* <note>This refers to the alchemical need to balance the "Fixed" (earth/dry) and "Volatile" (water/moist) elements so neither destroys the other.</note>

# EMBLEM XV. On the Secrets of Nature. <note>original: "De secretis Naturæ"</note>

## Let the work of the potter, consisting of the dry and the moist, be your teacher.

<image-desc>A square woodcut engraving depicts a potter's workshop. In the center, a bearded potter wearing a cap and tunic sits on a three-legged stool, shaping a large vessel on a manual kick-wheel. His bare right foot is placed on the base of the wheel to spin it. The room is filled with finished and unfinished ceramic wares: plates and bowls stacked on the wooden floor to the left, and several tall jars and pitchers arranged along the right wall. A window with leaded diamond-paned glass on the right wall illuminates the scene. On a workbench beneath the window, there is a pitcher and two books or tablets leaning against the wall. A doorway in the back-left corner reveals another interior space with more vessels.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XV.

> *Behold how swiftly the potter shapes his vessels* <note>original: "Aspice quàm celeri figulus sua vasa figuret"</note>
> *On the axle of the wheel, while with his foot he mixes clay with water:*
> *In these two things he places his trust: that the moisture* <note>original: "humor"</note>
> *May skillfully temper the thirst of the dry powders.*
> *So you also shall do, made more learned by this example,*
> *Lest the water overcome the earth, or the earth be overcome by the water.* <note>The "dry" and the "moist" are primary qualities in alchemy; finding their perfect proportion is essential to the Great Work.</note>

I 3
<meta>catchword: U.T</meta>

<summary>Emblem XV uses the craft of pottery as an allegory for the alchemical process of 'tempering,' where the artist must find a perfect balance between dry earth and liquid moisture to create a stable form. It emphasizes the physical mastery and proportions required to prevent one element from overwhelming the other.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, potter's wheel, dry and moist, elements, proportion, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens, craftsmanship</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XV, expanding on the analogy of the potter introduced on the previous page. It explores the elemental balance of the 'dry' and the 'moist' in both nature and the alchemical laboratory, concluding with a cautionary anecdote about a fraudulent or misguided operator.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XV.

<margin>T II</margin>

JUST as this world is rounded together into one spherical body by the embrace of earth and water, so too the potter's work seems to be composed piece by piece from the same elements, namely from the **dry** and the **moist**, so that one may temper the other. For if the earth were without water, and no Ocean, sea, lakes, rivers, or springs existed near the land, it would bring forth nothing from itself but would remain sterile. Likewise, unless the water were received within the cavities of the earth, but instead stood around it, it would easily cover the whole earth, and so it would remain uninhabitable. 

But since one has entered the other in a friendly manner, and the water has moderated the earth's dryness, while the earth has moderated the water's wetness through their mutual mixing, from this the fertility and usefulness of both elements are seen. In a similar way, the potter mixes clay <term>lutum; in alchemy, this often refers to the 'lute' or sealing clay</term> with water to create a manageable mass from it, which he shapes on his wheel and exposes to warm air so that it may dry out step by step. 

Afterward, he adds the violence of fire so that they are properly hardened, and his vessels coalesce into a durable stone <note>original: "lapidem durabilem"</note> that resists both water and fire. The Philosophers themselves attest that the process in the natural work is the same, and therefore they assert that a lesson should be taken from the potters. For regarding the **dry** and the **moist**—that is, earth and water—there is much affinity in both works. 

However, we do not doubt that a great deal of difference exists in the method of cooking <term>coctio; the controlled heating of the alchemical vessel</term> and the matter and form of the elements being composed. For while the potter's vessel has an artificial form, the **Philosophical Tincture** <note>original: "tinctura Philosophica," a name for the elixir or Stone</note> has a completely natural one, and it is as much nobler than those vessels as its material is more excellent than theirs. 

Both works are indeed earthly, but in the Philosophical work it is said that nothing exists that has not sought the heaven of the air <note>this refers to the process of sublimation, where the substance is vaporized and purified</note>; in the potter's work, thick and drossy earth predominates. The result in both cases is a **stone**: in the one it is common, in the other it is **Philosophical**. 

By this name <note>the word "Stone"</note> a certain person was seduced and shut up a great number of artificial stones or bricks in one chest, and whiter flints in another for a certain time, with Diabolical conjurations performed over them, by which the flints were supposed to be converted into silver <note>original: "argentum"</note> and the bricks into pure gold <note>original: "aurum"</note>. But since these were thought to be the **Stones of the Philosophers**, and therefore a great sum of money had been poured out to purchase various things while the new gold and silver were awaited at the appointed time, when it did not appear, and the stones had not been converted into the gold that was hoped for, shame— <note>The text breaks off mid-word: "pudo-" (pudor), meaning shame or embarrassment.</note>

<meta>Catchword: rem</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that the alchemist must balance moisture and dryness like a potter, though the alchemical 'Stone' is a natural, celestial product far superior to common ceramics. He concludes with a warning about a fool who tried to magically transform literal bricks and flints into precious metals.</summary>

<keywords>potter's wheel, dry and moist, elements, Philosophical Tincture, sublimation, alchemy vs. pottery, fraud, gold and silver</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XV, moving from the physical labor of the potter to the philosophical theory of metallic generation. It establishes a clear boundary between divine alchemy and "diabolical magic," then debates whether metals possess a "seed" similar to plants and animals.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XV.

death has finished the matter: For gold or silver ought not to be sought from that in which it does not naturally exist, since diabolical Magic finds no place in these divine works, but is as far from them as its author <note>Referring to the devil.</note> is from a devout and pious man, or hell from heaven. So also if the true *Philosopher’s Stone* <term>lapis Philosophicus</term> is possessed, let no one persuade himself that impossible things can be achieved by it, as *Isaac* <note>Likely referring to Isaac Hollandus, a 15th-century alchemical author frequently cited for practical guidance.</note> warns: Since no one is bound to the impossible, whether by the laws of nature or of the State. From this, regarding the transmutation of gems through it, and the granting of malleability to glass <note>The "malleable glass" (vitrum flexile) was a legendary material often sought by alchemists but generally considered a myth.</note>, let each person judge for himself whether these things are possible and suit this nature or not. *Geber* <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most influential authority in medieval Latin alchemy.</note> affirms that the Philosophers speak many things through allegory, and says of himself that where he has spoken clearly, he has said nothing, but where he spoke through a figure, there he has hidden the truth, like wheat hidden under chaff.

What a man sows, those same things he shall reap; this holds true in plants and animals, although sometimes different species may come forth from what was sown. Whether these principles ought to be applied to metals—which are not propagated through seeds like plants and animals—will have to be considered. In metals, the parts are only homogeneous, namely *sulfur* and *quicksilver* <term>argentum vivum; literally "living silver"</term>; in living things, the parts exist as heterogeneous or organic. In metals there are no receptacles for seed, but in living things there are. In metals no nutrition, growth, or extension into all dimensions is found, whereas in living things these are most prominent. Indeed, metals are elemental weights knowing only mixing; living things, beyond mixing, also possess a soul, whether vegetative <note>The soul responsible for growth and reproduction in plants.</note> or sensitive <note>The soul responsible for perception and movement in animals.</note>.

Nevertheless, it is undoubtedly true that there is something in subterranean places which is not yet gold, but after a thousand years will become gold through nature. Who will deny that this is an analogous "seed" of gold? And since gold and the gold-making nature are of one origin, although the latter is of a more noble form, therefore, once the seed of gold is known, the nature of this principle will also be established. The Philosophers affirm that the **dry** and the **moist**, or *sulfur* and *quicksilver*, are that which must be sought in its purest state from "two mountains" <note>An alchemical metaphor for the primary sources or the dual reagents of the Great Work.</note>.

->Day<-

<summary>The author distinguishes alchemical science from magic, arguing that while metals lack the organic "seeds" of plants and animals, they possess a subterranean developmental process that acts as a natural analogue to biological growth. The text concludes by identifying sulfur and mercury as the core components of this metallic generation.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Isaac Hollandus, Geber, sulfur, mercury, quicksilver, transmutation, malleable glass, metallic seed, vegetative soul, alchemy vs magic</keywords>

### XVI. *German version of the Latin Epigram.*

The Lion, who overcomes the animals with strength and claws,
Fights without any fear, and allows no flight to be seen;
Match him with a Lioness who is winged, who might lift him up,
And endeavor that they both might live in the air; <note>This refers to the "volatilization" of the fixed principle, a key stage in the alchemical process where a solid is made to rise as vapor.</note>
But he remains quite firm and holds himself upon the earth;
This image of Nature shows you the way by which she governs. <note>In alchemy, the Lion is often "the Fixed" (Sulphur) and the winged Lioness is "the Volatile" (Mercury). Their struggle represents the union of opposites.</note>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical journey of Michael Maier’s *Atalanta Fugiens*. HEADER: EMBLEM XVI. Concerning the secrets of Nature.

# This lion lacks the feathers that the other one possesses.

<image-desc>An engraving of two lions in a landscape. In the foreground, a wingless male lion with a thick mane stands over a winged lioness, pinning her to the ground with his paws. The lioness has large, feathered wings spread out beneath her. The background features a rugged landscape with a ruined circular stone tower on the left, several trees, and a distant building on the right horizon under a detailed sky.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XVI.

*The Lion, victor over the four-footed beasts, strong in chest and claw,*
*Fights without fear, and disdains flight;* <note>original: "dissimulátque fugam" - This is a pun on "fuga," meaning both a musical fugue and a physical escape. In alchemy, "flight" refers to a substance evaporating when heated.</note>
*To him, you must join a winged Lioness by the feet,* <note>The winged lioness represents "Mercury" or the volatile principle that escapes the fire.</note>
*Who flies and wishes to lift the male up with her:*
*But he stands unmoving on the ground and holds back the flying one;* <note>The wingless lion represents "Sulphur" or the fixed principle that remains stable in the heat. To "fix the volatile" is a primary goal of the Great Work.</note>
*Let this image of nature show you the way.*

<sig>K</sig>
<meta>catchword: LEO- (Lion-)</meta>
<vocab>Emblem, Nature, Lion, Lioness, winged, Epigram, secrets of Nature</vocab>

<summary>This page presents Emblem XVI, using the metaphor of a wingless lion holding down a winged lioness to illustrate the alchemical process of "fixing the volatile." It depicts the union of stable, earth-bound elements with those that are vaporous and elusive.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, fixed and volatile, lion, winged lioness, sulphur and mercury, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This discourse provides the prose explanation for the imagery found in Emblem XVI, which depicts a wingless lion pinning down a winged lioness. Maier here transitions from the natural history and legendary lore of the lion to its specific symbolic application in the alchemical process of "fixing the volatile."</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XVI.</header>

It is well known from experience that the **Lion** prevails over all other animals, not so much by the size and strength of its body as by its **noble spirit**. <note>original: "generositate" - implying both nobility and innate character.</note> If seen during a hunt, feeling the shame of turning its back, it withdraws slowly if overwhelmed by a multitude; but once removed from the sight of the hunters, it hastens its flight, believing that its disgrace is purged by being hidden. It does not leap while in flight, though it uses leaps when pursuing something. Its bones are solid and not at all hollow, and are said to be so hard that when struck together they give off fire as if from steel and flint. Of all things, it fears fire most. It seems to derive its substance from the nature of the sun; for in its impulse and heat it surpasses other animals, just as the sun surpasses the stars. Furthermore, it is always seen with fiery and wide-open eyes, just as the sun looks upon the earth with an open and fiery eye. The **Lioness**, fighting for her cubs, fixes her eyes upon the ground so that she might not be terrified by the hunting spears. When the Lion senses the coitus of the **Leopard**, <note>The "Pardus" or leopard was historically believed to be a hybrid or a distinct beast that would mate with lionesses, producing "impure" offspring.</note> he takes vengeance upon the adulterous lioness and rises up with all his strength to punish her. Therefore, the lioness washes away her fault and the scent in a river, or, conscious of her crime and fearing the male, she accompanies the fleeing adulterer.

While **Philosophers** contemplate the wonderful nature of this beast, they have produced various allegories concerning the Lion—like <term>hieroglyphic</term> writings—attesting to their secret work. Observing that the Lion is a constant and firm animal, lacking in guile and suspicion, they have compared the superior part of their **Philosophical composition** to him. For just as he does not flee, so this part does not flee; and as he has solid bones, so this part is <term>fixed</term> <note>In alchemy, "fixed" refers to a substance that remains stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to high heat.</note> and knows not how to be conquered. 

However, just as the **Lioness** is not always free or immune from adultery, so neither the **Moon** nor **Mercury** <note>Maier is identifying the Lioness with the volatile "mercurial" principle of the work.</note> is free from every stain; rather, by the ignorant, she is joined now to this material, now to that. From this, there results an adulterous conjunction of things that are mismatched in nature, rather than a true marriage. For those born from the leopard and the lioness do not have the beautiful manes around their necks and shoulders, a distinction with which only those conceived by the **Lion** are adorned. 

Therefore, let the **Philosophical Lioness** be joined to her own proper mate, and from that union a genuine and noble little lion will be born, who can easily be recognized by his claws. It is necessary, however, that the lioness be not just any lioness, but a **winged** one, who can engage in battle with the lion, relying on the swiftness of her feathers; this is so that she is not suppressed by his excessive wrath, but if—

<meta>The catchword "quando" (when) indicates the start of the next page's sentence.</meta>

<summary>Maier explains the alchemical "Lion" as the fixed principle (Sulphur) and the "Lioness" as the volatile principle (Mercury). He warns that the Lioness must be joined to the true Lion rather than "adulterated" with impure substances to ensure the success of the Great Work.</summary>

<keywords>Lion, Lioness, Alchemical Marriage, Fixed and Volatile, Mercury, Sulphur, Leopard, Nobility, Natural History, Hieroglyphics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Discourse XVI, finishing the sentence from page 74 regarding the behavior of the lioness and detailing the alchemical allegory of the Red Lion and the winged lioness. This imagery represents the "fixation of the volatile," where a solid substance (the lion) is joined to a vaporous one (the winged lioness) to stabilize it.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XVI.</header>

...er, or when he falls into a rage without cause, she meditates flight. For while he delays her as she is about to flee, he is incited by a greater love toward her, friendship having been restored after the dispute. But you will say, who has ever seen a winged lioness, or of what use would feathers be to a lioness? Near Mount Cithaeron <note>A mountain range in central Greece, famous in Greek mythology as the site of many stories involving wild beasts and divine madness.</note> there is a deep valley in which only winged lionesses are seen; but on the very peak of that same mountain dwells a **Red Lion** <term>Red Lion: In alchemical symbolism, the 'Red Lion' often represents the fixed, solar principle, or the 'Philosophic Gold' that has reached the final stage of the work.</term> of that kind which Hercules slaughtered. <note>Maier refers to the Nemean Lion, the first of Hercules's twelve labors.</note> Therefore, the lion must be captured and led into the valley, and soon he will be joined with the winged lioness: she also will easily allow herself to be overcome, for like is joined to like. Afterward, both must be raised <note>original: "sublimandus" - this carries a double meaning: physically moving them to the mountain peak, and the alchemical process of sublimation, where a substance is heated into vapor and then condensed back into a solid.</note> from the said valley to the mountain peak; and from then on they will never flee from each other, but will always remain together in an inviolable bond. 

The capture of lions, I admit, is not easy but is subject to many dangers; nevertheless, it must be undertaken. The lion does not feed with the lioness, but wanders separately, as they say. Hence, the tracking and hunting of each must be done separately. But if lion cubs of both sexes are sought out from their first claws—when they first begin to walk, which happens after the second month from birth—and once they have become more mature, they are joined together as described, the matter will be free from all danger. Moreover, they are born in the springtime (which should be noted) with their eyes open. Since lions walk by a winding path after giving birth so that their den might not be discovered, they must therefore be sought with the greatest diligence and care, and their cubs carried away.

This process represents the stabilization of alchemical substances through a symbolic marriage and subsequent sublimation.</summary>

<header>EMBLEM XVII. On the Secrets of Nature.</header>

# A fourfold orbit of fire governs this work.

<image-desc>An engraving depicting four large, flaming spheres stacked vertically in the sky above a river landscape. On the left bank, there are several wooden buildings, including a house and a small bridge-like shed, shaded by trees. In the center and right of the water, two boats are visible: a small rowboat and a larger sailboat carrying several people. The background features more distant trees and a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

## EPIGRAM XVII.

You who imitate the work of Nature, you must seek
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Four spheres within, which a subtle fire <note>original: "levis ignis" — literally "light fire." In alchemical practice, this refers to a gentle, controlled heat rather than a violent, destructive flame.</note> sets in motion.
Let the lowest represent Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and smithing; here symbolizing the basic, external heat of the furnace.</note>; let the second clearly show
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Mercury <note>Representing a more refined, vaporous heat associated with the "philosophical" mercury.</note>; the third sphere holds the Moon <note>Symbolizing a gentle, moistening heat associated with the silver stage or "albedo."</note>:
The fourth is yours, Apollo <note>The sun god; representing the highest, most perfect degree of heat that completes the work.</note>, and is called the fire of nature;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Let that chain <note>original: "catena" — referring to the interconnected sequence of heating stages.</note> guide your hands in the Art <term>the Art: The "Sacred Art" of alchemy, the process of transmuting and perfecting matter.</term>.

<sig>K 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: PHI-</meta>

<note>Vocabulary: Orbit/Path, Fire, Vulcan, Mercury, Moon, Apollo/Sun, Nature, Chain.</note>

<summary>This page presents Emblem XVII, illustrating the four hierarchical "spheres" or degrees of heat necessary for the alchemical process, personified by deities. It emphasizes that the practitioner must follow a specific sequence of fire—from the lowest (Vulcan) to the highest (Apollo)—to successfully imitate Nature's work.</summary>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the text from page 77, expanding on the four types of alchemical "fire" mentioned in the previous emblem's epigram. The discourse shifts from the poetic imagery of Apollo and Vulcan to a technical explanation involving historical authorities like Ramon Lull and George Ripley.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>### DISCOURSE XVII.</header>

Philosophers have mentioned in many places the four kinds of fires necessary for the natural work <note>The "Natural Work" refers to the Alchemical Great Work, or the creation of the Philosophers' Stone.</note>, namely Lullius <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1316), a major medieval philosopher and writer to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note>, the Author of the *Ladder* <note>original: "Scala" — referring to the *Scala Philosophorum* (The Philosophers' Ladder), a famous alchemical treatise.</note>, Ripley <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), an influential English alchemist.</note>, and many others. And regarding these fires, Raymond <note>Lull</note> says in the *Ladder*: *It should be noted that here lie contrary operations: For just as the fire against nature dissolves the spirit of the fixed body into the water of the clouds <note>original: "aquam nubis" — referring to a vaporous or distilled state.</note>, and constricts the spirit of the volatile body into congealed earth, so, on the contrary, the fire of nature congeals the dissolved spirit of the fixed body into a rounded earth, and resolves the fixed body of the volatile spirit, not into the water of the clouds, but into the water of the Philosophers.* Ripley speaks more clearly of these fires in his Third Gate <note>The "Third Gate" refers to a chapter in Ripley's *The Compound of Alchemy*.</note> as follows: *There are four kinds of fires which you must know: natural, unnatural, against nature, and elemental, which lights the wood. We use these fires and no others. The fire against nature must torment the bodies; it is the dragon, as I tell you, burning violently like the fire of hell. The fire of nature is the third solvent <note>original: "menstruum" — a term used in alchemy for a solvent that can dissolve solids while preserving their "life" or essence.</note>; that fire is naturally present in every thing. We call the occasional fire "unnatural," such as the heat of ashes and baths <note>original: "balneorum" — like a water bath or bain-marie, used for gentle, consistent heating.</note> used for putrefaction. Without these fires, you will bring nothing to putrefaction, so that your matter may be separated and simultaneously proportioned for a new conjunction.* Therefore, make a fire inside your glass which burns the bodies more effectively than elemental fire.

These are called "fires" because they possess a fiery power: the **natural** by coagulating, the **unnatural** by dissolving, the **against nature** by corrupting <note>In alchemy, "corrupting" refers to the necessary breakdown of a substance before it can be reborn.</note>, and the **elemental** by providing heat and the first motion. And in these, a chained order is observed, so that the second is incited to action by the first, the third by the second, and the fourth by the third and first together. In this way, one is the active agent and the other is the passive recipient, and the same thing can be both agent and recipient in different respects. This same thing is noticed in iron rings or rods continued by a magnet and connected by mutual touch; the same occurs in these fires. For the elemental fire, like a magnet, sends its force through the second and third even to the fourth, and joins and causes one to cohere to the other through mutual operations, until the internal action among the higher spheres is completed.

The first is the **elemental fire** in both fact and name; the second is **airy** or of the air; the third is **watery** or of a lunar nature; the fourth is **earthy**. Regarding the first, it is superfluous to say anything, as it is known to everyone who sees and touches it. The remaining three are dragons, solvents, waters, sulfurs, and Mercuries. They are called dragons because, participating in a poisonous nature, they devour their own...

<meta>The text cuts off mid-word "generis" (kind/species) as indicated by the catchword.</meta>

<summary>This page defines the four types of alchemical "fire" (elemental, natural, unnatural, and against nature) required for the Great Work, citing authorities like Ramon Lull and George Ripley. It explains how these heats interact in a "chain," functioning like a magnet to transform matter through dissolution and coagulation.</summary>
<keywords>four fires, George Ripley, Ramon Lull, elemental fire, natural fire, unnatural fire, fire against nature, putrefaction, dragon, philosophical water, menstruum, alchemy stages</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the four alchemical fires from page 77. Having introduced the "fire against nature" as a dragon or serpent, the author now explains how these substances function as solvents and transformative agents, citing authorities like Ramon Lull and George Ripley to explain their various names—Menstrua, Waters, and Sulphurs.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>### DISCOURSE XVII.</header>

...kinds of serpents, they wear down and alter the bodies mixed with them; that is, they dissolve and coagulate. They are called **Menstrua** <note>original: "Menstrua" — plural of menstruum, a solvent in alchemy, named for its role in "nourishing" the philosophical offspring in the "womb" of the flask.</note>, because from them the offspring of the Philosophers is produced and nourished until its birth. Lull <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1316), a major medieval philosopher to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note> in his book *On the Fifth Essence* <note>original: "de quinta essentia"</note>, distinction 3,
<margin><unclear>soul</unclear></margin>
has a double menstruum: vegetable and mineral. Ripley <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), an influential English alchemist.</note> in the preface
<margin><unclear>sign</unclear></margin>
of his Gates <note>The "Gates" refer to the chapters of Ripley's famous work, *The Compound of Alchemy*.</note> mentions a triple kind, which in truth are one and agree. For from all of those together the generation of the offspring takes place, and a **white water** precedes its birth, which is not of the substance of the offspring but from its superfluity <note>excess fluid</note>, and therefore it must be separated. They are called **Waters**, because they show an aqueous nature in the fire—that is, a flow and liquidity which is characteristic of water. It is established that there are diverse and marvelous properties of waters, some of which are stone-forming <note>original: "lapidescentes"</note>, while they are coagulated into the hardest tufa stones <note>original: "tophos" — a type of porous rock</note> suitable for human buildings. Very similar to these are the mineral waters of the Philosophers, which harden and pass into a stony resistance. They are also called **Sulphurs**
<margin><unclear>fire</unclear></margin>
because of the virtue of sulphur which they possess within them. For the sulphur of nature is mixed with another sulphur and becomes one, and two sulphurs are dissolved by one, and one is separated from two, and sulphurs are contained by sulphurs, as Yximidius says in the *Assembly* <note>original: "Turba" — referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts appearing as a transcript of a debate between ancient sages.</note>. What these sulphurs are, Dardaris declares in the same place with these words: *The sulphurs are hidden souls
<margin><unclear>soul</unclear></margin>
within the four elements, which, having been extracted by art, contain each other naturally and are joined together.* If, however, you rule and thoroughly cleanse the hidden thing which is in the belly of the sulphur with water, the hidden thing, meeting its own nature, rejoices; likewise the water rejoices in its peer. *Mosius* <note>A legendary figure in alchemical literature often associated with Moses.</note> also says: *I shall tell you now what it is: One is indeed the fiery quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum" — mercury</note>; two, the body composed within it; and the third is the water of sulphur, by which the one is washed, ground, and ruled until the work is completed.* What has been said about sulphurs is to be understood in the same way regarding as many Mercuries. For thus Mosius himself says in the following: *The quicksilver Cambar <note>An obscure term from early alchemical texts, sometimes referring to a specific state of mercury or lead.</note> is magnesia, but the quicksilver or orpiment <note>original: "auripigmentum" — arsenic trisulfide, a yellow mineral used as a pigment and alchemical ingredient.</note> is sulphur, which ascends from the mixed compound.* But since testimonies are infinite and obvious to everyone, I will cease to bring more of them here. These four fires are enclosed, as it were, in spheres and orbs; that is, each has its own center from which or toward which its motion tends. Yet they are observed to be so bound together, partly by nature and partly by art, that one works nothing or very little without the other; indeed, the action of one becomes the passion <note>In the philosophical sense, "passion" refers to the state of being acted upon by an external force.</note> of the other, and vice versa.

<summary>This page explains the various names for the transformative substances in alchemy—Menstrua, Waters, and Sulphurs—citing authorities like Lull and Ripley to show how these "fires" interact to dissolve and coagulate matter. It emphasizes that these components, though distinct, function as a unified system where the action of one triggers the reaction of another.</summary>

### XVIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

> Every active thing of nature sends out around itself
> Its power, through which a multiplication may occur;
> Just as fire is accustomed to making fiery everything that nears it,
> And no noble work is produced without a cause:
> Gold cannot give the power of fire, nor fire the power of gold;
> In every thing, its own seed naturally remains. <note>This reflects the alchemical axiom *sine semine*—nothing grows without its own specific seed. Fire provides the energy, but only the "seed of gold" can produce more gold.</note>

<header>EMBLEM XVIII. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.</header>
<sig>L</sig>
<meta>Catchword: NATURE</meta>

# Fire loves to ignite, not to turn things to gold; but gold [turns things to gold]:

<image-desc>A woodcut engraving depicting an alchemist in his laboratory. A man wearing a conical cap and a long-sleeved tunic stands before a large, arched brick furnace built into the wall, which is filled with roaring flames. He holds a long-handled metal tool near the fire. In front of him, resting on a rustic stone pedestal or tree stump, is a large two-handled basin overflowing with gold coins or nuggets. A dog sits on the tiled floor in the lower right, scratching its ear.
