## EPIGRAM XVIII.

> **IF** there is any active agent <note>original: "agens" — in Aristotelian physics, the "agent" is the force that acts upon "matter" to create change.</note> of nature, it sends its 
> *powers into a circle and desires to multiply its own strength.*
> *In this way, fire makes all things in its path fiery, nor is there anything*
> *that produces a noble work without its own specific cause.*
> *Gold ignites nothing by itself, and fire turns nothing into gold,*
> *for each thing recognizes the seed that is within itself.* <note>The alchemical "seed" refers to the essential formal principle that allows a substance to grow or transform into its specific kind.</note>

<vocab>Emblem <note>original: "Emblema"</note>, Epigram <note>original: "Epigramma"</note>, alchemy <note>original: "alchemia"</note>, fire <note>original: "ignis"</note>, gold <note>original: "aurum"</note>, nature <note>original: "natura"</note>, seed <note>original: "semen"</note>, multiplication <note>original: "multiplicatio"</note></vocab>

<summary>This page illustrates the alchemical principle that agents can only reproduce their own nature—fire creates heat, but only gold (the "seed") can produce more gold. It emphasizes that the alchemist must use the correct "starting material" to achieve the desired transformation.</summary>
<keywords>natural agents, law of multiplication, alchemical seeds, specific causality, Michael Maier, transmutation, Aristotle's physics</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the visual emblem and epigram on page 81 to the "Discourse," which provides a detailed natural-philosophical explanation of the principles behind Emblem XVIII. The previous page emphasized that "each thing recognizes its own seed"; this discourse expands on how nature operates through simple motions and the multiplication of specific powers.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>->DISCOURSE XVIII.<-</header>

Nature’s way of operating in all the individual things of the universe is to use a simple process to achieve a single simple motion, just as appears in the **Anatomy** of the human body. In the body, one muscle serves only one motion—namely, the pulling motion—while the expanding motion is served by a second muscle opposite to the first. Thus, if a limb is to be moved in a circle, this is achieved by various muscles positioned in a ring. Likewise, the operation of fire is one and simple: namely, it heats and ignites; indeed, it assimilates to itself and burns everything to which it is applied, provided they are combustible.

Hence *Avicenna* <note>original: "Avicenna" — referring to Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian polymath whose works on medicine and alchemy were foundational in Europe.</note> says in his book *On the Congelation of Stones*: "that which falls into salt-works becomes salt, and what falls into fire becomes fire, but some things more quickly and others more slowly, according to the power of the active agents and the resistance of the passive ones." There is also a place in Arabia that colors all bodies existing within it with its own color. 

In this way, individual natural things possess a power infused into them by nature, by which they act upon things mixed with or moved near them, assimilating and altering their nature and form. What occurs in plants and animals as generation through the propagation of **seeds**, is, in simple or simply-mixed bodies, the infusion and assimilation of power. 

The Sun, the light of heaven, casts rays out from itself onto the earth. When these rays are collected in concave or "burning" **mirrors**, they demonstrate that they were produced by such a cause, appearing almost as the "projectable forms" of the Sun. From this, it is clear that solar rays are nothing other than a fiery flame extended and dispersed over a wide breadth; when collected and condensed back into themselves by concave, transparent, and circular instruments—or by reflective, concave, **steel mirrors**—they flash forth like a flame and burn whatever is in their way. 

In the same manner, a certain power, like a vapor, is dispersed within a certain elemental body. If this power is collected and drawn together into one, it becomes water, and from that water, earth. Hence *Avicenna*, in the place cited before, says: "earth is made from water when the qualities of earth overcome it, and vice versa." 

There is, moreover, a certain thing used by some ingenious people when they wish to coagulate a dry thing, which is composed of two waters and is called <term>Virgin’s Milk</term> <note>original: "lac virginis" — in alchemy, this usually refers to a white, milky liquid formed by mixing two clear solutions (often lead acetate and potassium carbonate), used here as a metaphor for a potent transformative agent.</note>, which is its most certain effect. 

There are those who promise that they can double or multiply the powers within a **lodestone** <note>original: "magnete lapide"</note>. Indeed, we have seen one of this kind encased in silver, weighing barely one pound, which attracted and held a 28-pound iron anchor—which would have been impossible unless its power had been rendered increased and stronger...

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the next page likely continues the discussion of the lodestone's increased strength.</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical principle of "like produces like" by comparing it to natural phenomena such as muscular motion, the way fire spreads, and how mirrors concentrate sunlight into fire. Maier uses the example of "Virgin's Milk" and magnetized lodestones to argue that natural powers can be concentrated and multiplied through art.</summary>

<keywords>natural law, Avicenna, assimilation, solar rays, burning mirrors, Virgin's Milk, magnetism, lodestone, multiplication of power</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Discourse XVIII," following the previous page's discussion on how natural bodies possess an internal power to assimilate or alter nearby matter. The text here moves from the general principle of seeds in living things to the specific application of these principles within alchemy and the transmutation of metals.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>### DISCOURSE XVIII.</header>

...had existed in it; which was undoubtedly done by calling back the scattered virtues into a single point or pole, as it were, or by the attraction of the same from a large body into a smaller one. Others are found who say that a "lead-making" stone can be made from the sulfurous aura of Saturn <note>original: "Saturni" — in alchemy, Saturn is the name for lead.</note> infused and retained in common Mercury until it is coagulated from it; they claim this stone would continually convert common Mercury into lead. Some boast that from antimony or its starred Regulus <note>The "starred Regulus" refers to the star-shaped crystalline pattern that forms on the surface of purified antimony as it cools.</note> they can produce copper from the "scent" of copper in the time it takes to eat an egg—indeed, they claim to have made all metals. But let them be given the credit they are due, although this does not seem likely to me. Bolder, and perhaps no more successful, are those who try to derive gold from gold, according to the saying of the Golden Poet:

> *He whose heart is for barley, sows barley at last; in gold are the seeds of gold.* <note>original: "Hordeat cui cordi, demum ferit hordea, in auro semina sunt auri."</note>

Every natural thing indeed possesses a certain power of multiplying itself, but this is brought into action only in plants and animals, and by no means in metallic minerals, fossils of the earth, or meteors <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric or geological phenomena, including minerals formed within the earth's crust.</note>. Certain plants, arising from a small grain of seed, often give a thousand or more grains of seeds, thus multiplying and propagating themselves, and that annually. Animals also have their offspring in large or small numbers, according to the nature of each. But gold, silver, lead, tin, iron, copper, or quicksilver <note>Mercury</note> have never been found to multiply themselves or their species in that manner, although it has very often been found that one is changed into another and ennobled.

Nevertheless, the Philosophers affirm that just as the principle of making fire exists in fire, so the principle of making gold exists in gold. But the tincture <note>A "tincture" is a medicinal or alchemical substance believed to have the power to change the quality of a metal.</note> is sought, by means of which gold is made. This must be investigated in its own proper principles and generations, not in those of others. For if fire produces fire, a pear tree a pear, and a horse a horse, then lead will generate lead and not silver, and gold will generate gold and not the tincture. To this, the "gold" peculiar to the philosophers is what they do not deny must be added as a ferment <note>A catalyst that triggers the final stage of the alchemical process.</note> to the gold-making Stone at the end of the work; indeed, they assert it is necessarily required. Since the ferment leads the thing being fermented into its own nature, without which the whole composition would never return to perfection.

L ij
<meta>catchword: So</meta>

<summary>This page explores the alchemical theory of metallic multiplication, arguing that while metals do not reproduce like plants, they can be "ennobled" or transmuted using a "ferment" derived from their own nature. The author critiques those who claim instant results from antimony or lead, insisting that true transmutation must follow the specific natural principles of gold.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, transmutation, seed of gold, ferment, tincture, antimony, regulus, Saturn, Mercury, multiplication of metals</keywords>

### XIX. *German version of the Latin Epigram.*

Four brothers stand in a long line, of whom one clearly
Holds Earth in his hand, the other Water very clear;
The others possess Air and Fire; if you desire that all
Should perish quickly, strike only one with death,
And soon they all will die, and end their lives,
Because they are bound together by natural bonds.

<note>This emblem illustrates the theory of the four elements—Earth, Water, Air, and Fire—which were believed to be the building blocks of all matter. In alchemy, "killing" one element refers to the process of transformation where one state of matter is destroyed to allow the others to change, reflecting the belief that the elements are so interconnected that the alteration of one necessitates the collapse or transmutation of the whole system.</note>

<summary>This page presents the 19th emblem of Atalanta Fugiens, using the metaphor of four brothers to represent the four elements and their vital, reciprocal connection. It suggests that the destruction or transformation of a single element leads to the death (or transmutation) of the entire physical structure.</summary>
<keywords>four elements, alchemy, interconnectedness, transmutation, Michael Maier, Earth, Water, Air, Fire, Fugue</keywords>

EMBLEM XIX. On the secrets of Nature.
L 3
Poets <note>original: "Poëtæ"; this is the catchword for the following page.</note>

# If you kill one of the four, all will suddenly be dead.

A copperplate engraving depicting an alchemical allegory of the four elements. On the right, a muscular man raises a large wooden club over his head, preparing to strike a group of four men on the left. These four men represent the elements: the one being struck appears to represent Earth, holding a bundle of plants; the others represent Water (holding a vessel), Air (holding a bundle of feathers), and Fire (holding a torch or flames). They are linked together, suggesting their interdependence. In the background, a river flows past a city with high-roofed buildings and a castle on a hill, with mountains in the far distance.

---

->EPIGRAM XIX.<-

Two pairs of brothers <note>The "four brothers" represent the four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note> stand in a long row;
One supports the weight of the earth in his right hand, the other that of water.
The portion of air and fire belongs to the remaining two. If you wish
Them to perish, you need only strike one down in death;
And all will be carried off in a shared funeral of kin,
Because the mutual bonds of Nature <term>Natura</term> <note>In alchemy, Nature is often personified as a guiding force that maintains the balance of the physical world.</note> bind them together.

<summary>This emblem illustrates the alchemical principle of the "interdependence of elements," suggesting that Earth, Water, Air, and Fire are so inextricably linked that the destruction of one leads to the collapse of the entire system.</summary>
<keywords>four elements, alchemy, interdependence, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens, physics of nature, elemental death</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XIX, expanding on the allegory of Geryon and the interconnected nature of the four elements. It follows the previous page's introduction to the "four brothers" who represent Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XIX.

Poets imagine that Geryon <note>A giant in Greek mythology, usually depicted as a monster with three bodies or three heads.</note>, the King of Spain <note>original: "Hispania"</note>, existed with three bodies and possessed purple-colored oxen, which were guarded by a two-headed dog and a seven-headed Dragon. Geryon himself is said to be the son of Chrysaor, born from the blood of Medusa, while the Dragon is the offspring of Typhon and Echidna. Since these stories do not align with historical or physical truth as they are told, yet they are celebrated in all alchemical <note>original: "Chymicis"</note> allegories, we have rightly included them here.

By the three bodies of Geryon, we understand three faces seen in one father, according to the mind of Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical texts are attributed.</note>; or, as others prefer, four faces, corresponding to the four elements. Indeed, from a quadrangle a triangle must be made, just as that triangle was made from a circle; so this one is destined to return into a circle <note>This describes the "Squaring of the Circle," a symbolic geometric operation in alchemy representing the reduction of the four elements (the square) into the three principles (the triangle) and finally into the unity of the Philosopher's Stone (the circle).</note>. 

Furthermore, there is such a close kinship and natural conjunction between Geryon's bodies—or the elements—that if one is overcome or killed, the others collapse of their own accord and rot without any manual force being applied. Concerning those with two bodies, it is well known that when one dies, the other also wastes away. For instance, in Italy, I saw a four-year-old boy with two bodies, who hid the head of the brother attached to him inside his own body; the rest of the brother's parts hung down as far as the navel, where they were joined or born together. If the one who hung down—and who, being much smaller, was carried by the other—was touched roughly on the hands or feet, the larger boy felt the pain; he even felt hunger if the smaller one was deprived of food. 

And this is the combination and sympathy of nature <term>sympathia</term> <note>In alchemical philosophy, sympathy is the invisible bond connecting all parts of the macrocosm (the universe) and microcosm (the human body or the alchemical work).</note>, by which the limbs and parts of one body, or one joined to another, are affected and moved together. From these parts, if one remains healthy and unharmed, it does not necessarily follow that the others remain so; however, if one is gravely injured, the others suffer with it and perish by the same evil. So, if a neighbor makes much profit, no benefit comes to his neighbor from it; but if he suffers a fire, it brings much trouble: *For it is your business when the neighbor's wall is on fire* <note>original: "Nam tua res agit paries cùm proximus ardet"; a line from the Roman poet Horace emphasizing shared risk.</note>. 

Therefore, it is not far from the truth that if one of these four brothers dies, the other three also die, since this can happen in various ways: either because they were brought forth in one birth by the same father and mother and shared the same limits of life as they did beginnings (as we read has happened to some, perhaps by the inclination of the stars), or because they are joined not only in spirit but also in the limbs of the body, or by the mind...

<summary>Maier uses the mythical three-bodied Geryon and a real-life account of conjoined twins to explain the "sympathy" between the four elements, arguing that they are so interconnected that the death of one necessitates the death of all.</summary>
<keywords>Geryon, four elements, alchemical geometry, squaring the circle, Hermes Trismegistus, sympathy of nature, conjoined twins, interdependence</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical interpretation of the myth of Geryon. Having established the "four brothers" as the four elements on the previous page, the author now explains their radical interdependence: the death or injury of one inevitably leads to the destruction of the others, a process necessary for their eventual "resurrection" as the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XIX.

...due to panic, a strong imagination during a pestilential plague, or out of a sworn vow. In India, under the empire of that Great Mughal <note>original: "Mogolli"; likely referring to the contemporary Mughal Emperor in India, whom the author identifies as the ninth descendant of Tamerlane.</note> (who now reigns there as the ninth descendant of Tamerlane), there dwells a certain ethnic group under the name of "Pythagoreans" <note>The author likely refers to Hindus or specific ascetic groups, conflating their beliefs in reincarnation with Pythagoreanism.</note>. Among them, this custom has been observed from ancient times until now: if a husband dies, his wife is burned in the fire, or—as is the case now—she must live forever in the deepest infamy, abandoned by all and regarded as if dead. This was done so that wives would not cut short the lives of their husbands with poisons, unless they themselves were willing to die.

So it is in the Philosophical Work <note>The alchemical process of the Great Work.</note>: when one of the brothers has died, the others also perish by fire—not by force, but voluntarily—lest they live as survivors in sorrow and infamy. Or, if one is attacked with a club, a blade, or a stone, he will begin an internal war with his brothers, as is evident in the "Earth-born" <term>Spartoi</term> <note>original: "Terrigenis"; the "sown men" who sprang from the dragon's teeth in Greek myth.</note> born from the dragon's teeth who fought against Jason, and elsewhere rising against Cadmus; thus, they all fall by mutual slaughter. 

For if you touch or harm the Air-bearer, he will rise against two at once who are closest to him—namely the Water-bearer and the Fire-bearer. These two, in turn, will oppose the Earth-bearer (who stirred up the first fight) for so long until they have given and received mutual wounds by which they are extinguished. For it is so arranged among these brothers that the more intensely and sharply they love one another, if they once begin to hate, they become irreconcilable and pursue each other to the death; just as the bitterest bile is generated from the sweetest honey when it is corrupted in a hot stomach or liver. 

Therefore, kill the living, but do so in such a way that you resuscitate the dead; otherwise, the death of the slain will profit you nothing. For death will reveal him—namely, when he rises again—and death, darkness, and the sea shall flee from him, as Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical texts are attributed.</note> testifies. The Dragon who watched the openings <margin>Chapter 3.</margin> shall flee from the rays of the sun, and our dead son lives, and the King comes from the fire. Belinus <note>An Arabic-Latin name for Apollonius of Tyana, a frequent authority in alchemical texts.</note> suggests the same in his metaphor within the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rosary of the Philosophers), a famous 16th-century collection of alchemical citations.</note>, when he says: *“And let this be done when you have extracted me partly from my nature, and my wife partly from her nature; and afterward, you kill the natures, and we are raised in a new and incorporeal resurrection, because afterward we cannot die.”*

<summary>The author uses ethnographic and mythological parallels, such as the Indian custom of Sati and the myth of Jason, to explain the alchemical necessity of destroying the elemental "brothers" so they may be resurrected in a perfected, incorporeal form.</summary>
<keywords>elements, Great Mughal, Sati, Jason, dragon's teeth, Hermes Trismegistus, Belinus, Rosarium Philosophorum, resurrection, alchemy</keywords>

### XX. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The flame, which eats all things just like a dragon,
Desired to consume a virginal image <note>original: "Jungfräuliches Bild." This refers to the pure, uncorrupted matter of the Philosophers.</note> by force;
For this reason, she was seen wet with tears by a man,
Who offered her help through both counsel and deed;
As soon as he covered her beneath a shield, he warded off <note>The German "erwachten" is likely a variant of "erwehren" (to defend/resist) or "erwarten" (to await), but in context, it implies neutralizing the enemy.</note> the enemy,
And taught her to utterly despise its threats thereafter.

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem XX. The previous page introduced the visual metaphor of the knight protecting the maiden from the dragon-fire. This discourse explains the philosophical and physical principles behind that protection, shifting from biological analogies to practical metallurgy.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XX.

It is a common **watchword** <note>original: "symbolum." This refers to a password or sign of recognition among members of a secret society or philosophical school.</note> of the **Philosophers**, by which they recognize one another, that nature is led, taught, ruled, and overcome by nature; namely, the student by the teacher, the handmaiden by the mistress, the subject by the queen, and indeed the daughter by the mother and the kinswoman by her kin. How true this is remains evident from daily experience: in the upbringing of offspring among humans and in other actions, such as literary instruction, governance, and similar pursuits. **Pliny** writes of nightingales that one instructs another in singing; the student listens, observes, imitates, and either surpasses the teacher or grieves at being surpassed. Sometimes, in these contests, the defeated bird dies from a ruptured throat, falling lifeless in the middle of its song. We also see how all kinds of birds begin to accustom and train their chicks—still featherless and tender—to fly, so that it is not only nature in them, but also art or practice that produces the habit of flying. Nature alone gives the power and the organs to perform that action, yet without these, neither instruction nor art would find a foundation or a place. In this same way, **foals** are trained to the race by the mare, **puppies** to barking by the dog, and **fox cubs** to cunning. No nature or species of nature—provided it is animate and sensing—is found that does not lead, teach, and rule another nature (specifically its own offspring), or allow itself to be overcome by another (specifically its parent).

In **vegetables** <note>original: "vegetabilibus." Referring generally to the plant kingdom.</note>, we do not notice this kind of discipline, yet we observe that practice and the hands of men have great power over them. For while the harvest is still in the blade, it can be purged of useless thistles and weeds; while a tree is still a twig, it can be bent and forced to grow according to one's pleasure.

So it is in metals and the subjects of Philosophy: one nature holds, preserves, and protects another in the fire. This is well known to **smelters and assayers** <note>original: "fusoribus & docimastis." A "docimastes" was a specialist in testing the purity of metals.</note>, and especially to the masters of natural things. Iron greatly assists gold and silver when they are still "tender and spiritual" (as they call it) within their ores, mixed with predatory **Cadmia** <note>An ancient term for zinc ores or cobalt, often found alongside copper and silver.</note>, **arsenic**, or **antimony**. The iron offers a "midwife’s hand" if it is thrown into the furnaces where the ores are to be roasted. In the same way, **iron** itself, if it must be changed into **steel**, is saved from being consumed by the fire by white stones found on the seashores. Some people, when melting metallic powders, throw **crystalline glass powders** or **glass-gall** <note>original: "fel vitri." Also known as "sandiver," the scum or dross that forms on the surface of molten glass, used as a flux in metallurgy.</note> over them so they do not perish from excessive heat. In place of this, the **Philosophers** use their own **Eudica**...

<meta>catchword: ...tuntur (completing "utuntur," meaning "they use"). "Eudica" likely refers to a specialized alchemical vessel or a protective seal/lute.</meta>

<summary>Maier explains the alchemical maxim "nature overcomes nature" through biological examples of teaching and metallurgical techniques where one metal protects another from the fire.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Pliny the Elder, nightingales, metallurgy, iron, steel, flux, Eudica, alchemy, education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the text from page 90, completing the sentence begun on the previous page: "In this same way..." or "In such a manner..." It transitions from the general observation of nature to specific alchemical and biological examples of how one nature governs another.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XX.

<margin>M ij</margin>

...is protected, which Morienus says is also "glass gall" <note>original: *fel vitri*. This refers to the scum or impurities (sandiver) that float to the top of molten glass during the melting process.</note> and is to be sought in glass vessels. For the heat of fire consumes the body itself in a rapid burning; but if **Eudica** <note>A term used in alchemy, often associated with the dross of glass or a specific purifying salt that protects substances from being destroyed by fire.</note> is added to it, it will care for those very bodies—once they have been turned into earth—and protect them from all burning. For bodies, once they no longer retain their souls, are quickly consumed by fire. Eudica (the dross of glass) indeed suits all bodies well; for it vivifies and prepares them, and defends them from all burning. These are the words of **Morienus the Roman** <note>A legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist said to have taught the secrets of the stone to the King of Egypt.</note>. This, therefore, is the nature which teaches another nature to fight against fire and to become accustomed to it; she is the teacher instructing the student and, if you look closely, she is the queen ruling her subjects, and the daughter who ennobles her mother.

This is that **Red Servant**, who is joined in marriage to his fragrant mother, and from her generates an offspring far more noble than its parents. This is **Pyrrhus** <note>Also known as Neoptolemus, the son of Achilles.</note>, the son of Achilles, a youth with red hair, golden clothing, black eyes, and white feet. This is the **Knight with the Collar** <note>A reference to a knight belonging to a prestigious order, like the Order of the Golden Fleece.</note>, armed with sword and shield against the dragon, so that he might rescue from its jaws the inviolate maiden **Albifica** <note>Meaning "The Whitening One."</note>, also known by the names **Beja** or **Blanca**. This is **Hercules**, the monster-killer, who freed **Hesione**, daughter of Laomedon, from the monster after she was exposed to the immense sea-beast. This is that **Perseus**, who defended **Andromeda** (daughter of Cassiopeia and Cepheus, King of the Ethiopians) from the sea monster by showing the head of **Medusa**, and after snatching her from her chains, took her as his wife. This is he who can be compared with those ancient Roman liberators and purifiers: **Marcus Curtius**, **Lucius Scaevola**, **Horatius Cocles**, **Manlius Capitolinus**, and their like, who rescued the city, as if it were a mother, from great dangers.

For this is the method and way of nature tending toward the perfection of any work: that it leads one thing from another, the more perfect from the imperfect, and brings action out of potential. It completes nothing in a single moment, but finishes one thing after another. And not this alone, but nature first appoints its own **Deputy**, to whom it leaves the power of life and death—that is, the power of forming other things. For example, in the generation of a human being, nature uses a long process of ten months where it first fashions the **heart**, according to the mind of **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle believed the heart was the first organ to form in an embryo, acting as the "source of life."</note>, as its deputy and principal organ. The heart then truly outlines, forms, and perfects the remaining limbs necessary for nutrition, life, sense, and the power of generation; it imparts life and life-giving spirits to them through **systole and diastole** <term>systole and diastole: The rhythmic contraction and expansion of the heart and arteries.</term>, or the dilation and compression of the arteries, as long as it is not hindered by disease or violence. And thus one nature teaches another; you shall note and follow this most clear example of the Philosophical work.

It highlights the role of the "Red Servant" (sulfur) in rescuing the "White Maiden" (mercury) from the fire of the dragon.</summary>

### XXI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

From man and woman make for yourself a circle, perfectly round,
From that draw a figure that has four corners at once;
Soon transform this into another that has three corners exactly,
And let this once again give you a round circle.
Then the Stone is made; which, if you cannot understand,
You must be diligent to learn the lessons of geometry.

# EMBLEM XXI. Concerning the secrets of Nature.

## From a male and female make a circle, then a quadrangle, from there a triangle; make a circle and you will have the Stone of the Philosophers <note>original: *lap. Philosophorum*, an abbreviation for *lapis Philosophorum*. This refers to the legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting enlightenment.</note>.

<image-desc>A detailed engraving showing an alchemist or philosopher standing outdoors before a stone wall. The man is bearded and wears a long, fur-lined cloak and a soft cap. He holds a large pair of compasses against the wall, measuring or drawing a geometric construction. The diagram on the wall consists of a large circle, within which is an equilateral triangle. Inside the triangle is a square, and within the square is a smaller central circle. This central circle contains a nude man and woman standing together. On the ground at the philosopher's feet are several geometric tools: a large square, a protractor-like gnomon, and a piece of parchment displaying a hexagonal figure and a six-pointed star. In the far left background, a stone tower or castle is visible.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XXI.

Let the woman and the man become one circle for you, from which
  There arises a square form having equal sides.
From this, draw a triangle, which from every part returns 
  Into a round sphere: Then the <term>STONE</term> <note>The "Stone" (Lapis) represents the finished alchemical Work, a state of perfect balance and spiritual completion.</note> will have been born.
If so great a matter does not immediately come to your mind,
  If you understand the teaching of the <term>Geometer</term> <note>original: *Geometræ*. This refers to the mathematical and structural principles of the universe, often citing the Platonic idea that "God geometrizes."</note>, you will know everything.

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page begins the "Discursus" (Discourse) for Emblem XXI. The previous page presented the visual emblem and the poetic epigram, which instructed the reader on the "Squaring of the Circle"—the geometric process of creating the Philosopher's Stone. This discourse shifts into a philosophical debate between the schools of Plato and Aristotle regarding how human beings acquire knowledge of sciences like geometry.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXI.

Plato, that most celebrated philosopher, taught that the notions in the human mind, which are the foundations of all arts and sciences, are as if engraved and impressed upon it in actuality <note>Plato’s theory of *anamnesis* suggests that the soul is immortal and already possesses all knowledge, but "forgets" it upon birth into a physical body.</note>. He held that by the recollection and repetition of these notions, all teachings can be grasped and known by anyone. To prove this, he introduces a boy, still young, unlearned, and uncultured in letters, with whom he initiates geometric questions in such a way that the boy is observed to answer all questions correctly. Thus, whether willing or unwilling, or even unknowingly, the boy is seen to have reached into the innermost depths of such a difficult science.

Hence, Plato determined that all teaching and discipline is not learned or drawn out from the beginning, but rather is recalled and revolved in the mind by children through memory. He was alluding here to his <term>Great Year</term> <note>original: *magnum suum annum*. A cosmic cycle (often calculated as 36,000 or 48,000 years) after which the stars and planets return to their original positions, supposedly causing history to repeat exactly.</note>, in which he said that 48,000 solar years ago, the same persons, things, and actions existed before the revolution of the heavens, in the same way they do now. But that these things are very like dreams, without any foundation of truth, no one fails to understand.

We do not deny that there are certain "sparks" of knowledge and mere potentialities implanted in us, to be brought into actuality through use and instruction; but we deny that they are so great and of such a kind that they are the seeds of arts and sciences without any preceding cultivation. From where, then, did the sciences and arts originate, if men did not discover them? Were they first handed down from heaven or by the Gods of the nations? Others will ask this.

I respond: it is one thing to say that burning coals are covered under ashes in such abundance that if they are brought forward once the ashes are removed, they suffice for the cooking of food or the warming of our cold limbs; it is quite another thing to affirm that only tiny sparks lie hidden there, which before they are of use for cooking or warming, must be fostered, cultivated, and increased with their own kindling <note>original: *fomitibus*, referring to tinder or fuel used to nurture a small flame.</note> through human industry, art, and care; otherwise they can easily be extinguished and entirely reduced to cold ashes. The <term>Aristotelians</term> <note>Followers of Aristotle, who argued the mind is a *tabula rasa* (blank slate) and knowledge comes through sensory experience and reason.</note> assert the latter; the <term>Platonists</term> the former. But reason and Experience support the latter, while the former is supported only by fantasy or imagination.

Hence it could be asked: why did Plato write on the door of his school, "Let no one ignorant of Geometry enter here" <note>original: *Geometriæ rudem in id non admitti*. This famous inscription hung over the entrance to Plato’s Academy in Athens.</note>, when he affirmed that even small children know it in actuality? Are men perhaps less learned than children? Or have those who are more grown-up consigned to oblivion what they knew as boys?

<meta>catchword: runt?</meta>

<summary>Maier discusses the origin of knowledge, contrasting Plato's theory of innate memory (anamnesis) with the Aristotelian view of potentiality, using the "Squaring of the Circle" as a springboard for exploring how geometric truths are realized.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Aristotle, Geometry, anamnesis, Great Year, innate knowledge, education, philosophy of science</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>The catchword on the previous page was "Wenn," which is German for "If," suggesting this Latin discourse may be part of a bilingual edition or a work by a German-speaking author. This page continues a discussion on natural instinct versus learned knowledge, moving into alchemical geometry.</meta>

### DISCOURSE XXI.

...[learned]? That should not be thought; because we see that a brute animal, taught by nature, abhors and avoids dangers from fire, water, steep falls, and similar things, even when it has only recently been brought into the light. A human infant, however, does not know or avoid such things unless it has been hurt, or has had its finger slightly burned at a candle flame like a fire-moth <term>pyrausta: a mythological insect said to live in fire, often used as a metaphor for a moth attracted to a flame</term>, which burns its wings and falls. Why do the little bee, the fly, and the gnat not hurl themselves into the fire in their swiftest flight, since they do not know from experience that danger will arise from it? It is because nature taught them, but not the newborn human. 

If Geometry is so natural and easy for children, how is it that the "squaring of the circle" <term>quadratura circuli: the ancient mathematical challenge of constructing a square with the exact same area as a circle using only a compass and straightedge</term> was not known to Plato himself? Indeed, Aristotle—Plato’s own disciple—affirmed that it was "knowable, but not yet known." 

However, it appears that this was not unknown to the natural philosophers <note>original: "Philosophis naturalibus," here referring specifically to Alchemists</note>, given that they command the circle to be converted into a quadrangle, and this, by way of a triangle, back again into a circle. By the "circle," they understand the most simple body without angles; just as by the "quadrangle," they understand the four Elements. It is as if they are saying that one must take a simpler corporeal figure that can be found, and divide it into the four elemental colors to become an equilateral quadrangle. 

Anyone can understand that this "squaring" is physical and in harmony with nature. From this, far more utility for the Commonwealth <note>original: "Rempub." or Respublica</note> and more illumination for the human mind comes than from that mathematical and purely theoretical version, which is abstracted from matter. 

To learn the latter, a Geometer dealing with solid bodies must investigate the depth of solid figures—for example, the Sphere and the Cube—to see how that knowledge can be transferred to manual use or practice. If the capacity or circumference of a sphere is 32 feet, how long must one side of a cube be to equal the capacity of this sphere? Conversely, if a sphere holds 32 measures within such a circumference, how long will one side of a cube be to hold the same amount, whether looking at the measures the sphere or cube can hold, or the feet of each circumference?

Similarly, the Philosophers <note>the Alchemists</note> want the quadrangle to be led into a triangle—that is, into body, spirit, and soul. These three appear in three colors that are precursors to the final "redness" <term>rubedo: the final, highest stage of the alchemical process</term>. Namely:
*   The body or earth appears in the "blackness of Saturn" <term>nigredo: the first stage of alchemy, associated with decay and the planet Saturn</term>.
*   The spirit appears in the "lunar whiteness" <term>albedo: the second stage, associated with purification and the Moon</term>, acting like water.
*   The soul or air appears in the "solar yellowness" <term>citrinitas: the third stage, associated with the Sun and the transition to the final goal</term>.

Then the Triangle will be perfect. But this, in turn, must be changed back into a circle—that is, into the invariable redness. By this operation, the female is converted into the male and made one with him. Thus the number six <term>senarius: considered the first "perfect number" because its divisors (1, 2, and 3) add up to itself</term>, the first of the perfect numbers, is completed through the one and the two, when it has returned again to the Monad <term>monadem: the ultimate unity or the One</term>, in which there is eternal rest and peace.

<summary>The author argues that while animals have natural instincts for safety, humans must learn through experience and geometry. He explains the "squaring of the circle" as an alchemical process of transforming elements through specific colors and geometric stages to reach spiritual perfection.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, squaring the circle, alchemy, elements, nigredo, albedo, citrinitas, rubedo, perfect numbers, Monad, Plato, Aristotle</keywords>

## If you have the white lead, then perform the work of women,

### XXII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

<margin>(to cast/</margin>
You who desire to accomplish much with little effort,
Should cast a portion of snow into Saturn's face (which is so black),
And it will become for you a lead of very white clarity,
After which nothing remains but the work of women;
Then cook it, as a woman does when she leaves her pots on the fire,
But let the beloved <note>original: "Trawt"—an archaic term for a "beloved," "bride," or "dear one," used here to represent the alchemical substance or "bride" being treated in the vessel</note> dissolve in her own water.

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>EMBLEM XXII. On the Secrets of Nature.</header>

# Once the white lead is obtained, perform the work of women, that is, COOK.

<image-desc>A copperplate engraving depicting an interior scene that serves as an alchemical allegory. On the left, a large stone hearth features a blazing fire with thick clouds of smoke rising into a chimney; one metal pot hangs over the fire from a chain, while another sits directly on the burning logs. A woman in a long-sleeved gown stands in the center of the room, looking toward the hearth. Behind her, a pair of bellows rests on a stone ledge. To the right, a wooden tub on the floor contains two fish. In the background, a window reveals a landscape of hills and trees, and several jars are visible on a shelf above the bellows.</image-desc>

## EPIGRAM XXII.

> Whoever you are who loves to accomplish much with easy labor,
>    Sprinkle snow upon the face of Saturn (which is black): <note>In alchemy, Saturn is the traditional name for lead. "Sprinkling snow" refers to the process of purification or "whitening" the dark, raw material.</note>
> And the whitest substance of lead will be given to you,
>    After which, nothing remains but the work of a woman. <note>The "work of women" (opus mulierum) is a common alchemical phrase for the stage of "decoction"—a slow, gentle heating likened to simmering a pot on a kitchen stove.</note>
> Then COOK, like a woman who places her pots on the fire,
>    But ensure that the <term>trout<note>Latin: *truta*. This is an allegorical name for the alchemical matter or "mercury" which must be dissolved and matured in its own fluid.</note></term> melts in its own waters.

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the exposition of Emblem XXII, transitioning from the symbolic image of the woman cooking to a detailed philosophical discourse on the sequence of metallic transformations. It follows the instruction that after "whitening" the lead, only the gentle heat of the "work of women" remains to perfect the stone.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXII.</header>

**JUST AS** Mercurial statues <note>original: "statuae Mercuriales"; these are "Herms," or boundary markers topped with the head of Hermes/Mercury used in antiquity to guide travelers at crossroads.</note> are usually placed at crossroads along with a pointer and an inscription to lead hesitant travelers onto the right path, so too have the Philosophers scattered certain sharp sentences here and there throughout their allegorical and ambiguous books. These serve as signs so that the seeker of truth may be warned by them and led, as if by the hand, into the true path. Among these signs, this emblematic inscription is one. Its meaning is this: out of **philosophical bronze** <note>original: "aere Philosophico"</note> one must make lead, and out of lead, tin—which <term>Geber<note>An 8th-century Arab alchemist (Jabir ibn Hayyan) whose Latinized works were foundational to European alchemy.</note></term> also calls "white lead." He teaches the method by which **Saturn** <note>The alchemical name for Lead.</note> migrates into **Jupiter** <note>The alchemical name for Tin.</note> through washing with **Mercury**. 

Therefore, one must trust this pointer more than any babbler <note>original: "Battologo," a reference to Battus from Ovid’s Metamorphoses, who was turned to stone for betraying Mercury’s secrets.</note> whenever it reveals where the **Philosophical cattle** are located. "While they were on the mountains," it says, "it [the stone] was also under those mountains." For many, as <term>Arnold of Villanova<note>A famous 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note></term> testifies in his *New Light*, chapter 1, wander in the mountains and do not recognize these animals, even though they are sold openly for a very low price. On the peaks of the mountains, even in summer, snows are sometimes observed, and very often clouds; by these, as if by vapor and water, the **black lead** is washed and turned into whiteness. 

In the lowest valleys and mines, however, their crystals—congealed and hardened from ice—are found together with <term>specular stone<note>Latin: "lapide speculari." Transparent minerals like mica or selenite, used in the ancient world for windows or mirrors.</note></term> and **talc**, which is praised for the whiteness and beauty it gives the face if an oil is made from it. In these places is found, above all, the clear and running **Mercury**, which, when well-prepared, corrects the blemishes of Saturn and elevates him to the throne of Jupiter. 

However, one must not understand Saturn and Jupiter as the common metals, because common metals do not enter into the physical work; rather, one must understand them as those metals made "physical" and purified through a long preparation. **Saturn** is the father of all the gods—or rather, the father of the "golden ones" <note>original: "aureolorum"; likely referring to the alchemists or the golden substances themselves.</note>—and the first gate of the secrets. "With this," says <term>Rhasis<note>Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (865–925 AD), a Persian polymath and influential figure in alchemical history.</note></term> in his *Epistle*, "the gates of the sciences are opened." He is succeeded by his son **Jupiter**, who cast out his father from the kingdom and castrated him, so that he would not generate more sons. 

From the virile member thrown into the sea, **Venus**, the most beautiful of women, was born. From Jupiter—who is the prepared "white lead"—the remaining planets were born: **Mars** from Juno; **Mercury** from Maia (the daughter of Atlas of the Mauritanian mountain); and the **Moon** and the **Sun** <note>original: "Luna & Sol"</note> from Latona. These four are brought into the light solely by "cooking," or the "work of women."

<meta>catchword: pro- (likely proceeding into "prodeunt," meaning "they come forth")</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical transformation of lead (Saturn) into tin (Jupiter) and eventually into the Sun and Moon. It uses the myth of Saturn’s castration and the birth of the gods as a metaphor for the chemical separation and refinement of substances.</summary>

<keywords>Saturn, Jupiter, Geber, Arnold of Villanova, Mercury, work of women, mythological alchemy, planetary metals, purification</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the text from page 98, concluding the reference to Arnold of Villanova and moving into a detailed explanation of "coction" (cooking/digestion) as a metaphor for the alchemical process. It shifts from the preparation of the metal (lead and tin) to the application of heat.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXII.</header>

...emerge: By **cooking**, however, we understand maturation and the dispersal of the cruder parts, which is accomplished by **Vulcan** <note>The Roman god of fire, used here by alchemists to represent the element of heat or the furnace itself.</note> within the vessels of **Philosophy**. For it should not be thought that this is common cooking—at least regarding the method of operation—though it agrees with it in its goal. Just as a woman matures fish in water—that is, she softens, boils, and cooks them by releasing all superfluous humidity from them into the water and air—so the Philosopher treats his subject. He does this by macerating, melting, dissolving, coagulating, and fixing it in his own **water**, which is stronger than the sharpest vinegar, within the **vessel of Hermes** <note>original: "Hermetis vase"; a reference to the "hermetically" sealed glass flask used in alchemy.</note>. The joints of this vessel must be most strictly closed, as is proper, so that the water does not evaporate and that which is in the vessel is not burned. This is the "vessel upon a vessel" and the "Philosopher's pot," the **Laconic bath** <note>original: "balneum laconicum"; historically a dry-heat sweat bath or sauna used by the Spartans (Laconians). In alchemy, it refers to a gentle, indirect heat.</note> in which the **old man** <note>A symbolic name for the raw matter or the "Stone" in its initial, unrefined state.</note> sweats.

There are those who boil fish, crayfish, crabs, or fresh peas in a double pot, arranged so that the items are in the upper pot and only water in the lower. These pots are placed one above the other and fitted with rings <note>original: "orbibus"; likely sealing gaskets or circular supports.</note> so that no vapor escapes. By this method, only the rising vapor of the water penetrates the contents and matures them, making them tender and soft far more perfectly than if they had been boiled in the water itself.

This is the most highly praised method of the Philosophers, by which they soften what is hard, dissolve what is compact, and **rarefy** <note>The process of making a substance less dense or more spiritual/gaseous.</note> what is dense. For it is **Air**—or rather, an imperceptible vapor—that matures, cooks, and perfects fruit on the trees, not raw and cold water as such. It is also Air that tints and colors the **golden apples** in the **gardens of the Hesperides** <note>In Greek myth, these were the golden apples of immortality; in alchemy, they represent the final stage of the work, where the substance turns to gold.</note>. For if it is well considered, the boiling of water—by which raw meats are cooked until they are fit for eating—is nothing other than the **rarefaction** of water and its **transmutation** into aerial vapor. Since bubbles are merely air contained within water, they easily vanish as the air moves from the waters into its own sphere, and the water subsides back to its center.

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical concept of 'coction' (cooking), comparing the maturation of the Philosopher's Stone to a woman steaming food in a double-vessel or the natural ripening of fruit by warm air.</summary>
<keywords>coction, Vulcan, heat, maturation, vessel of Hermes, Laconic bath, vapor, rarefaction, Hesperides, transmutation</keywords>

### XXIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

It is a wondrous thing, which the Greeks teach us for truth,
Which was famous at Rhodes in ancient times;
They testify that a fruitful rain of gold fell,
While the **Sun** <note>Sol, representing the masculine principle or sulfur.</note> lay with **Venus** <note>The goddess of love, representing the feminine principle or a specific metallic quality.</note> in the heat of love;
Also, from the brain of **Jupiter** <note>The king of the gods; in alchemy, often associated with tin or the atmospheric spirit.</note>, as **Pallas** <note>Athena, goddess of wisdom. Her birth from the head of Jupiter symbolizes the emergence of the "Philosophical Stone" or the hidden spirit from the matter.</note> did ascend,
So too in your **vessel** <note>The "philosopher's egg" or glass flask.</note>, like rain, **gold** must reveal itself.

## It rains gold while Pallas is born at Rhodes, and the Sun lies with Venus.

<image-desc>A detailed copperplate engraving depicting a series of mythological and alchemical allegories. In the foreground, the god Jupiter reclines on a stone plinth; next to him, Vulcan strikes Jupiter's head with an axe, from which a small, fully-armed Pallas Athena emerges. An eagle sits beside Jupiter. In the background to the right, Sol (the Sun) and Venus are shown in an embrace on a bed inside a tent. Over the entire scene, and especially over the sea and the city of Rhodes in the background, a thick rain of gold falls from the clouds. Several ships are visible on the water, and a statue on a pedestal—likely the Colossus of Rhodes—stands in the distance.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XXIII.

It is a wondrous thing, but Greece has given us proof,
    Of that which was celebrated among the inhabitants of Rhodes.
They report that a golden rain fell from the clouds,
    When the **Sun** was joined in love to the **Cyprian Goddess** <note>Venus, often called the "Cyprian" because she was said to have risen from the sea near Cyprus.</note>:
Then also, when **Pallas** <note>Athena, the goddess of wisdom.</note> emerged from the brain of **Jupiter**, gold
    May fall within its vessel just like raining water.

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page begins the prose commentary for Discourse 23, following the emblem and epigram on page 101. It explores the allegorical and alchemical meanings behind the myth of the golden rain at Rhodes.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
->### DISCOURSE XXIII.<-

To assert that **Gold** once rained upon the earth would be madness, unless it is understood allegorically. For there are no gold-bearing rivers or gold-ore mines in the clouds where it could be said to be generated, nor is gold of such light weight that it could be thought to be drawn up with the vapors. But <term>figurative language<note>original Latin: "tropus"</note></term> allows and excuses all these things. For as truly as **Pallas** <note>Athena, the goddess of wisdom.</note> was actually born from the brain of **Jupiter**, and the **Sun** was joined with **Venus** in adultery, just as truly did the golden rain fall. We do not say this because we doubt that both happened, but rather so that we may remove the common and literal sense from these allegorical sayings. For if we follow the bare words of this Emblem, nothing is more absurd; yet if we follow the mind behind them, nothing is truer.

Now, the island of **Rhodes** was first called <term>Ophiusa<note>From the Greek word for "serpent."</note></term> because of its multitude of snakes; later it was called Rhodes from the <term>rose-beds<note>Rhodes is associated with the Greek "rhodon," meaning rose.</note></term> blooming there; and finally, <term>Colossicola<note>Meaning "Dweller by the Colossus."</note></term> from the **Colossus of the Sun**, which, standing there, was numbered among the seven wonders of the world. From this, the ancient Philosophers—since their <term>crude mercurial matter<note>The raw starting material in the alchemical "Great Work," often compared to a serpent or dragon because of its volatility.</note></term> behaves like a serpent, but later, when prepared and cooked, assumes the purple color of a **Rose**—took certain similarities from the island of Rhodes. This is also the same reason why they attributed a golden rain to it when the **Sun** (or Apollo) lay with **Venus**. This story, originally told figuratively, provided the reason why the Rhodians became prouder; because such great Gods were occupied with producing children among them, they erected an idol of the Sun of astounding size and expense.

History records that this **Colossus** was seventy cubits high, positioned so that ships could sail between its legs with their sails spread. Its fingers were equal in size to ordinary statues, and few men could put their arms around its thumb. The architect was **Chares of Lindos**, a disciple of **Lysippus**, by whom it was built over twelve years. After fifty-six years, it was thrown down by an earthquake, but even lying on the ground, it was a marvel. It is said that when the **Sultan of Egypt** occupied Rhodes, he loaded nine hundred camels with the bronze from this statue.

"What the Sun is among the planets," says the Philosopher, "that is gold among the metals." This metal is especially assigned to the Sun because of its heat, color, power, and essence. Hence, a golden rain is owed to the generation of the Sun when "little suns" are conceived by **Venus**. For **Venus** has a rosy color in her face, which, if it is poured into the seed of the **Sun**, the off-

<meta>catchword: spring</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that the "golden rain" at Rhodes is an alchemical allegory, linking the island's name and its famous Colossus to the transformation of "crude mercury" into the "purple rose" of the philosopher's stone.</summary>
<keywords>gold, Rhodes, Colossus, Chares of Lindos, alchemy, Venus, Sun, Pallas, Jupiter, allegory, mercury</keywords>

The previous page ended by discussing how ancient philosophers compared their initial mercurial matter to serpents and later to roses.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>

<header>DISCOURSE XXIII.</header>

[resemb]lance; from this, it must truly be that **Rhodes** was born: This is the beautiful, rose-like **Son of the Philosophers**, who entices and draws the eyes and minds of all to himself, and deserves their love; at whose birth it is no wonder if miracles occur: for he himself will be miraculous in all his deeds and shall call forth the golden rain. He is the brother of **Augeas**, **son of the Sun**, who inherited oxen from his patrimony, whose stable-dung **Hercules** <note>Referring to the fifth labor of Hercules: cleaning the Augean stables.</note> cleansed in a single day. He is also the brother of **Aeetes**, who possessed the golden fleece seized by **Jason**. Regarding **Pallas** <note>Athena.</note> coming forth from the brain of **Jupiter** without a mother, they relate that she was brought forth near the river **Triton**, and therefore called **Tritonia**. She is depicted as the Goddess presiding over **Wisdom**, and is not undeservedly said to be born from the brain, where the seat of wisdom is. A golden rain also decorated her birth at **Rhodes**, so that it might remain in the memory of men when she came into this light. In public joy or festivals—as in the coronation of a **King** or the birth of a royal heir—gold coins are sometimes cast among the common people like a golden rain: so the same occurred at the birth of **Pallas**. For **Pallas** is wisdom or <term>sophia<note>Greek for wisdom.</note></term>, who carries health in her right hand and riches in her left, and looks out for the safety and benefit of mankind. To her **Perseus** brought the stone-making head of **Medusa**, horrific with its serpentine and viperous hair, which she used on her shield against her enemies—the uncultivated and rude peoples—changing them into stones. And in truth, wisdom or natural Philosophy renders those incredulous haters and envious ones stupid and empty of sense and intellect through that very thing from which **Chrysaor** <note>A giant born from the blood of Medusa.</note> was born—he who was the father of the aforementioned triple-bodied **Geryon**—that is, the petrifying **Gorgon** blood, which is nothing other than the **Tincture of the philosophical stone**.

### XXIV. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Let it be your charge to catch the greedy wolf,
And throw to him the King’s body, that he may swallow it whole;
Lay him upon the wood, and let Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire; in alchemy, this refers to the controlled heat of the furnace.</note> kindle the flames,
So that this monstrous beast may be consumed by fire.
Do this repeatedly, and the King will rise again from death,
And step forth proudly and boldly with a lion's heart. <note>The "lion's heart" refers to the King—representing gold or the philosopher's stone—having reached a state of fixed strength and perfection after being purified by the "wolf" (antimony).</note>

<header>EMBLEM XXIV. Concerning the secrets of Nature.</header>

# The wolf devoured the King and, once burned, returned him to life.

<image-desc>A copperplate engraving depicting an alchemical allegory in two stages. In the foreground, a large wolf stands over the supine body of a crowned king, biting into his midsection. In the middle ground to the right, a wolf is being consumed by a large fire on a wooden pyre, while the king, restored and still crowned, walks away from the flames. The background features a river with a small boat and a detailed cityscape with a large dome and several towers under a clear sky.</image-desc>

## EPIGRAM XXIV.

*Take care to catch the gluttonous wolf, to him*
*Throwing the King’s body, so that his gluttony*
*He may satisfy; then place him on a pyre, where Vulcan* <note>Vulcan is the Roman god of fire; in this context, he represents the intense heat of the alchemical furnace required for transmutation.</note>
*Stirs the fire, so that the beast may return to ashes.*
*Do this again and again; thus from death shall rise*
*The King, and he will be proud with a lion’s heart.* <note>The "lion's heart" signifies that the King (representing gold) has reached a state of supreme strength and purity, no longer vulnerable to "predators" or chemical corruption.</note>

<sig>O</sig>
<meta>catchword: How great (original: "Quanta")</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the alchemical process of purification using "the wolf" (antimony) to cleanse "the king" (gold). Through a cycle of consumption and destruction by fire, the king is resurrected in a more perfect, "lion-hearted" state.</summary>
<keywords>Antimony, Gold, Purification, Vulcan, Fire, Resurrection, Alchemy, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse of Emblem XXIV, expanding on the allegory of the wolf and the king. It provides mythological and historical context for the ritual of consumption and rebirth described in the previous epigram.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

### DISCOURSE XXIV.

How great the hunger and voracity of the wolf is, is commonly known: for where food is lacking, in extreme hunger it feeds on earth; it is also said to fill its belly with earth when it is about to attack large herds, so that, having become more heavily burdened as if by a pack-saddle, it is not so easily shaken off, but resists more strongly. When it enters the sheepfolds, it does not only kill what is enough for its stomach, but out of greed it slaughters the whole flock here and there. It is sacred to Apollo and Latona, <note>In Greek mythology, Latona (Leto) was said to have been guided by wolves to Delos, or to have taken the form of a wolf, when she was about to give birth to the twins Apollo and Diana.</note> because a wolf stood by her as she was giving birth: for without the presence of a wolf, Latona could not have brought forth her offspring. For this reason, the wolf is believed to be accepted by Apollo—whose birthday it celebrated—and not without merit: additionally, this is because its eyes shine at night and dart out light.

To this beast, therefore, when it is beset by immense hunger, the dead Royal body is thrown—not so that the wolf might consume it entirely and reduce it to nothing, but so that it might restore life and strength to the King by its own death. For there is a certain "amatory virtue" <note>An attractive or magnetic power used in alchemical transformation.</note> in the tail of the wolf, which is infused into the semi-dead King, whence he becomes most pleasing to all men, recovering his health and original beauty. 

The Hyrcanians <note>An ancient people living near the Caspian Sea.</note> bred dogs for no other use than to cast the bodies of the deceased to them to be devoured, as Cicero records. So also the Massagetae <note>A nomadic tribe of Central Asia.</note> give those who died of disease to dogs as prey. The Philosophers, however, give their King to the wolf. 

Neither did the custom of the Sabaeans <note>People of ancient Sheba (modern Yemen).</note> please them, who treated the bodies of the dead in the same manner as dung, even casting out their kings among the dunghills; nor did the custom of the Troglodytes, <note>Literally "cave-dwellers."</note> who bound the neck of the dead to the feet and carried them out hastily with jokes and laughter, and without any consideration for the place, committed them to the earth. Instead, they preferred in this matter to follow the institution of the Magi, <note>The priestly caste of ancient Persia.</note> who would not bury the bodies of their own people unless they had first been torn by wild beasts; or that of the Indians, who ordered themselves to be burned alive while crowned and singing the praises of the gods, lest old age should overtake them. 

Yet for all those peoples, a final fate imposed this custom without any hope of resurrection or renewal of life. It is arranged far differently among the Philosophers: for they know most certainly that from the dead King devoured by the wolf, a living, strong, and young King will appear, and the wolf will be burned in his place by fire. For the wolf is easily killed if it has so weighed down its belly; the King, however, although dead, nevertheless prevails with a Martial or Cygnian strength, <note>A reference to either the god Mars (iron/strength) or the Swan (purity/whiteness), suggesting the King/Gold cannot be truly destroyed by the process.</note> such that he cannot be wounded nor consumed. But where must this wolf be hunted, and from where is the King to be received? 

The Philosophers respond...

<meta>catchword: The Philosophers (original: "Philof.")</meta>

**Vocabulary:**
wolf, voracity, Apollo, Latona, Hyrcanians, Massagetae, Sabaeans, Troglodytes, Magi, Indians, resurrection, Philosophy, Cicero, Martial.

<summary>This discourse provides mythological and historical parallels for the alchemical "death" of gold, comparing it to ancient burial customs while emphasizing that the alchemical process—unlike pagan death—leads to a literal physical and spiritual resurrection.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, antimony, gold, purification, Apollo, burial rites, transubstantiation, resurrection, metallurgy</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse of Emblem XXIV, expanding on the allegory of the wolf and the king. It provides mythological and historical context for the ritual of consumption and rebirth described in the previous epigram.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>

<header>### DISCOURSE XXIV.</header>

The Philosophers say that the wolf wanders here and there through mountains and valleys to seize his prey; he must be drawn out from his dens and kept for our use. The King, however, coming from the East, exhausted by the weariness of a long journey, eventually collapses. Grief also hastens his death, for he sees that among strangers he is held in no honor and is so little esteemed that he is sold into slavery for a few gold coins. 

It is necessary, however, that the wolf be taken from a colder region; for those born in a cold climate are more savage than those in Africa or Egypt, owing to the greater hunger triggered by the external cold. Once devoured by this wolf, the King revives with the heart of a lion, and is then able to tame all wild beasts. And although he appears most base in appearance compared to his six brothers—being the youngest of them all <note>The "six brothers" are the other six metals known to alchemy: Lead, Tin, Iron, Copper, Mercury, and Silver. The "youngest" is often Gold or the transformed Philosopher's Stone.</note>—yet after many tribulations and miseries, he will finally attain a most powerful kingdom. 

Hence Gratian says in the *Rosary of the Philosophers* <note>original: *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a foundational 16th-century alchemical text.</note>: "In Alchemy," he says, "there is a certain noble body which is moved from master to master; in its beginning there shall be misery and vinegar <note>original: *aceto*. Vinegar represents the acidic, corrosive, or purifying stage of the alchemical process.</note>, but in the end, joy and gladness." And Alanus <note>Alan of Lille, a 12th-century scholar and theologian often cited in alchemical works.</note> states in the same work: "One thing must be chosen from all others; it is of a leaden <note>original: *lividi*, meaning a bluish-gray or leaden color.</note> color, possessing a clear and liquid metallic appearance. It is a substance both hot and moist, watery and combustible; it is a living oil and a living tincture, a mineral stone, and a 'water of life' of wondrous efficacy." 

It is not always safe for Kings to travel beyond the borders of their own realms; for if they are recognized while wishing to remain hidden, they are treated as traitors by their adversaries and imprisoned. If they proceed openly without an army, they face the same danger. So it happened to this Indian King <note>A reference to the "King from the East," representing the solar principle or gold.</note>, or would have happened had death not intervened. This is the first <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of vaporizing a substance and condensing it back into a solid to purify it.</note>, washing, and ennobling used by the Philosophers, so that the second and third stages may be completed more successfully. 

For those later stages are of no consequence without this one, as the King would remain faint-hearted, sluggish, and sickly. He must first demand tribute and taxes from his subjects to provide himself with clothing and other necessities; afterward, he will become so wealthy that he can bestow new garments upon all his subjects as often as he desires. For great things, having mostly risen from small beginnings, can then support lesser things or even suppress greater ones if they wish. Consider the example of cities that were small at their founding, which later commanded great kings and transformed mere villages into spacious and populous towns.

<sig>D ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical allegory of the King from the East who must be devoured by a "cold-climate" wolf to be purified and reborn with lion-like strength. It emphasizes that this initial purification (sublimation) is essential for the King to eventually achieve his full power and wealth.</summary>

# The dragon does not die, unless he be killed by his brother and sister, who are the Sun and Moon.

->XXV. German version of the Latin Epigram.<-

**IT** is a task of no small skill to overcome the dragon,
Lest he crawl back and begin to live once more;
His brother and sister together must strike his head,
With clubs; no one else would dare attempt his death.
Apollo is the brother <note>In alchemical symbolism, Apollo represents the Sun and Gold.</note>, and the sister is Diana <note>Diana represents the Moon and Silver.</note>;
Python <note>A monstrous serpent from Greek mythology, representing the chaotic or "corrupt" state of matter.</note> was slain by him, and Orion <note>A giant hunter; his death here symbolizes the "fixing" of volatile substances.</note> by her.

<summary>Emblem XXV describes the alchemical process of "mortification," where the volatile "Dragon" of raw matter must be destroyed by the combined forces of the Sun and Moon (Apollo and Diana) to prevent it from reverting to its base state. This ritualized death is a necessary step before the matter can be purified and reborn in the Great Work.</summary>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXV.</header>

->The dragon does not die, unless he be killed with his brother and sister,<-
->who are the Sun and Moon.<-

<image-desc>An engraving showing a large, winged dragon on the ground. Two human figures, one male and one female, stand on either side of it, striking its head with heavy wooden clubs. The man on the left has a sun-disc with rays on his head; the woman on the right has a crescent moon on her head. In the middle distance, two archers (representing Apollo and Diana) draw their bows. The background features a landscape with mountains, a sea with a ship, and a walled city in the far distance.</image-desc>

## EPIGRAM XXV.

*It is a work of no small skill <note>original: "Exiguæ est non artis opus," literally "a work of no meager art."</note> to have laid low the Dragon*
    *In death, lest he soon creep revived from the ground.*
*The brother and the sister herself, joined together, strike*
    *His head with a club; no outside force <note>original: "nec res fert aliena," meaning nothing foreign to the work can achieve this.</note> brings about his death.*
*Phoebus <note>A name for Apollo, the Sun god; in alchemy, he represents the Solar principle or Gold.</note> is his brother, while Cynthia <note>A name for Diana, the Moon goddess; she represents the Lunar principle or Silver.</note> is his sister; Python*
    *Fell by the hand of the former, and Orion by the hand of the latter.*

<sig>O 3</sig>
<meta>Catchword: IN</meta>

<summary>This page presents the twenty-fifth emblem of Maier’s work, using the myth of Apollo and Diana to symbolize the alchemical process of fixing volatile matter. The "Dragon" must be overcome by the combined masculine and feminine principles—the Sun and Moon—to progress in the Great Work.</summary>

<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXV.</header>

<margin>
Jason

**IN** the quest for the Golden Fleece, the Dragon had to be killed first of all. Since this labor had been attempted by many in vain, they were overcome by the Dragon and killed by its fatal poison. The reason was that they were not sufficiently protected against the dragon’s venom, nor were they instructed in the cunning by which it was to be slain. Jason, however (acting as a physician <note>The name "Jason" was often etymologically linked to the Greek word for healing, *iasis*.</note>), neglected no kind of remedies, various types of which he received from Medea (through her wise counsel); and among these, he received images of the Sun and Moon. By using these, he successfully achieved victory along with the prize—that is, the GOLDEN FLEECE. Thus the Dragon was removed from the scene by the Sun and Moon, or by their images, a point which the Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists.</note> emphasize everywhere. 

For so the author of the *Rosarium* <note>Referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> relates from others: **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> says: *The Dragon does not die, unless he be killed with his brother and sister; not by one alone, but by both together, namely by the Sun and the Moon.* The Philosopher says: *Mercury never dies, unless he be killed with his sister—that is, one must congeal him <note>Original: "congelare," meaning to solidify a liquid or stabilize a volatile substance.</note> with the Moon or the Sun.* Note that the Dragon is the Quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"</note> extracted from bodies, having in itself body, soul, and spirit. Concerning this, it is said: *The Dragon does not die, except with his brother and his sister,* that is, Sun and Moon—meaning the extracted Sulfur, which possesses the nature of humidity and coldness by reason of the Moon. With these the dragon dies—that is, the quicksilver extracted from those same bodies from the beginning, which is the **Permanent Water** <note>original: "aqua permanens"; a central alchemical concept referring to a stable, transformative solvent.</note> of the Philosophers, which is produced after putrefaction and after the separation of the elements. That water is also known by another name: **Fetid Water** <note>original: "aqua fœtida"; named for the unpleasant smell of the initial stages of chemical decomposition.</note>. 

So says that author, and all the rest agree with him, so that there is no need to cite the authority of each individual. The people of Epirus worshiped a dragon in the temple of Apollo because of the slaying of Python. The Dragon has a natural war and hatred with the Elephant; he seeks the elephant's eyes and throat until the Elephant, falling to the ground, kills the dragon as well by the weight of its body. From this struggle, "dragon's blood" <note>A red resin used in medicine and alchemy, sometimes associated here with the red stage of the alchemical work.</note> is brought to our shores. The dragon has eyes prized like gems; he has a very sharp and clear sight, for which reason he is often employed to guard treasures, as with the apples of the Hesperides, and as was said, the Golden Fleece among the Colchians. 

The ancients also added the dragon to Aesculapius <note>The god of medicine, whose staff is entwined with a serpent.</note> as a symbol <note>original: "hieroglyphicum"</note>. But the Chemists <note>Alchemists.</note> appropriate dragons to their works, not indeed in reality, but by allegory. For the dragon always denotes **Mercury**, whether it be fixed or volatile. Hence Mercury is to be seen with a double serpent entwined on his caduceus (since a dragon is a massive serpent), and Saturn with one that devours its tail, just like Ja[nus]...

<sig>P 2</sig>
<meta>The text cuts off mid-word "Janus" as a catchword for the next page.</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that the dragon represents Mercury (quicksilver), which must be "killed" or stabilized by the Solar and Lunar principles (Sulfur and Salt/Moon) to achieve the Golden Fleece of the alchemical Great Work. He draws parallels between chemical processes and classical myths of Jason, Python, and Aesculapius.</summary>
<keywords>Jason, Medea, Golden Fleece, Mercury, Sun and Moon, Quicksilver, Permanent Water, Dragon's Blood, Aesculapius, alchemical allegory</keywords>
