<page-num>III</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXV.</header>

...e: For Aesculapius, the son of Apollo and the inventor of medicine (**Philosopher of Medicine**), a serpent is said to be dedicated. It is in this form that he is believed to have been transported from Epidaurus to Rome, and he was always worshiped there because he was thought to have caused the cessation of a pestilential plague. 

Moreover, the Dragon of the Philosophers <note>In alchemical texts, "the Philosophers" refers to the long line of alchemical authors and authorities.</note> is always most vigilant and alive; he is not easily wounded, both because of the density of his skin and the sharpness of his teeth and the venom with which he is armed. For although common dragons are said to be devoid of poison, this one is not without it; unless he is handled cautiously, he discharges it upon anyone he meets. Therefore, he can rarely be overcome by force, unless the cunning of his kinsmen is also applied. For truly the poet <note>Referring to Ovid in *Ars Amatoria*.</note> said:

> *Safe and frequent is the way to deceive through the name of a friend,*

which in other places and in other affairs—though not here—might be a safe and frequent path, yet it carries the weight of a crime. 

<margin>
Aesculapius
Dragon
Philosopher

Moon
Mercury
Herod

Pliny
Balis
Tacitus
Rebis
Africa
</margin>

Mountebanks and street-healers <note>Latin: *agyrtæ*, referring to traveling quacks or peddlers of charms.</note> are said to expel worms from children by using the powder of other similar dead worms; that is, killing brothers with brothers and sisters. In the same way, here the Dragon must be killed together with his brother and sister, which is to say, with the Sun and Moon. From this it appears that the Dragon is also numbered among the planets; namely, as has already been made clear from the *Rosarium*, he is Mercury extracted from the bodies. 

There are some among the Greeks who relate that a Dragon, during the reign of Herod among the Jews, loved a beautiful girl of marriageable age and lay with her in bed. Another is said to have been kept by the Emperor Tiberius as a pet, whom he was accustomed to feed from his own hand. In the same way, the Dragon of the Philosophers, if handled rightly, lays aside its savagery and becomes a friend to man—though it remains hostile if treated otherwise. Xanthus, an author of histories cited by Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of *Natural History*.</note>, is a witness that a slain dragon cub was restored to life by its parent using a herb which he calls *Balis*. I refer to this, however, as an allegory of the Philosophers rather than as true history, since in chemical matters alone is life restored to the dead Dragon, and death to the living, by alternating turns. 

But one might ask, from where and how is the dragon to be captured? The Philosophers respond very briefly through a verse:

> *The mountains, dragons, and the earth give the Rebis <note>The <term>Rebis</term> (from the Latin *res bina*, meaning "double thing") is the alchemical hermaphrodite, symbolizing the union of opposites required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note> to the fountains.*

Regarding its capture, one may look to Tacitus <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus, the Roman historian.</note> to see how many men watched with great care and industry to capture a huge dragon observed in Africa and bring it to the Emperor Tiberius. They fenced in the dragon's usual path among the stones and gradually narrowed it into a tight space; there, enclosed by nets and chains, and finally tamed by clubs and lashes, it was carried to Rome in a ship like the weight of many wagons.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that the alchemical Dragon represents Mercury, which must be "slain" and transformed by the Sun and Moon (Sulfur and Sophia). He draws parallels between this process and classical myths of dragons being tamed or used in medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Aesculapius, Mercury, Dragon, Sun and Moon, Rebis, alchemy, symbolism, Pliny, Tiberius, medicinal serpent</keywords>

### XXVI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

No greater wisdom in human affairs has been made known to us,
Than that through which wealth comes, and a long, healthy life.
Her right hand points toward attaining great age without hardship;
The left, as many goods as one could ever desire.
If someone attains this with both reason and skill <note>original: *mit Vernunfft vnd Hand*, literally "with reason and hand," implying both theoretical knowledge and practical laboratory work</note>,
He shall feel the fruit of the Tree of Life in all his deeds.

### EPIGRAM XXVI.

*There is no greater wisdom in human affairs,*
*Than that through which riches and a healthy life come.*
*Her right hand holds the healthy duration of a vast span of time,*
*While her left hand stores up heaps of wealth.*
*If anyone approaches her with both reason and by hand* <note>original: *ratione manuque*; this is a common alchemical refrain emphasizing that one must possess both theoretical knowledge and practical laboratory skill</note>,
*The fruit of life within her will be like that of a tree.*

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page begins the prose discourse for Emblem XXVI, following the visual personification of Wisdom on the previous page. It transitions from the poetic epigram to a philosophical argument for why human reason finds its highest expression in the study of alchemy.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXVI.</header>

*Tullius* <note>The Roman orator Marcus Tullius Cicero.</note> brilliantly described the essential difference of man, by which he differs from the rest of the animal kingdom, in this manner: Just as a bird is born for flight and a horse for running, so man is born for reasoning. For just as lions, bears, and tigers exercise and show themselves in ferocity; elephants and bulls in the strength of the body; and eagles, falcons, and hawks in the preying upon birds and the swiftness of their wings; so man excels them and all others in reason, the discourse of the mind, and the intellect. Hence, there is no ferocity, bodily force, speed, or anything of that kind in beasts that cannot be mitigated, tamed, and overcome by reason alone. For reason is not something merely human or sprung from the soil, but as the Poet says, it is a "small spark of the divine breath" <note>original: *divinæ particula auræ*; a phrase from Horace’s *Satires* suggesting the soul has a divine origin</note>, sent from heaven into the human body. It is called at times memory, and at times intellectual power; if use or experience is added to this, wisdom is born from it, which is the most precious thing to man. For it is said that Experience is the father and Reason (or Memory) is the mother of such noble offspring. Hence that common saying: "Experience begot me, and Memory, my mother, gave me birth" <note>A popular Latin proverb regarding the origins of wisdom</note>.

But it is asked: what is true wisdom and most worthy of human inquiry, since there are infinite opinions about it, with everyone transferring it to their own imaginations? It must be answered that wisdom (always excepting divine matters regarding the salvation of the soul) in human affairs consists not in sophisticated quibbles, nor in flowery and decorated speeches, nor in the poetic sounds of verses, nor in the critical subtleties of grammarians, nor in the cleverness of amassing coins and wealth through right or wrong, through deceits and perjuries, through deceptions and lies, or through pitilessness and the sweat of the poor. Rather, it is nothing else but the true knowledge of <term>Alchemy</term> <note>original: *Chymiæ*; in the 17th century, the terms for chemistry and alchemy were used interchangeably to describe the study of natural transformations</note>, joined with practice, which is most useful to the human race. This is the wisdom surpassing all others, which penetrates with its right hand to the East and its left to the West, and embraces the whole earth. Concerning this wisdom, Solomon discourses with discernment in the Book of Wisdom. For so he says in chapter 8, verses 9 and 18:

<margin>
Sirach 4:13
Wisdom 8:16
Sirach 4:20
</margin>

> Those who are her kin have eternal permanence, and those who are her friends have sincere pleasure, and he who inquires after her diligently shall receive great joy. For there is no weariness in being occupied with wisdom, nor any distaste in being in her presence, but only gladness and joy. And although wine and music also gladden the heart of man, yet wisdom is [more]

<meta>The text breaks off here; the catchword "jucundior" translates to "more pleasant." This continues the biblical comparison from the Book of Wisdom.</meta>

<summary>This discourse argues that reason is the unique gift of humanity and that true wisdom is found in the practical and theoretical study of alchemy, rather than in rhetoric or greed. It supports this by citing Cicero's views on human nature and Solomon's praises of wisdom from the scriptures.</summary>

<keywords>Reason, Cicero, Wisdom, Alchemy, Experience, Memory, Solomon, Human Nature, Intellectual Power</keywords>

<margin>

...is more pleasant. For she is a **tree of life** <note>original: *arbor vitæ*; a metaphor from Proverbs used by alchemists to describe the transformative power of their "Stone" or Wisdom.</note> to all who lay hold of her, and they are blessed who keep her. Lactantius <note>Lactantius (c. 240–320 AD) was an early Christian author and advisor to Emperor Constantine, often cited for his eloquent style.</note> calls it the "nourishment of the soul." Indeed, the wise shall be honored, and whoever holds wisdom in high regard shall be exalted and receive honor through her. For wisdom lifts up her children, and whoever clings firmly to her shall enjoy honor. An immortal name among posterity also comes from her. She is more powerful than all things, and strengthens a wise man more than ten powerful men who are in a city. To this worldly wisdom may also be applied what is said by the Prophet Baruch: "Learn, therefore, true wisdom, that you may experience who it is that grants long life, riches, joy, and peace." And in the Book of Wisdom, chapter 9, verse 17, it is affirmed that wisdom is the **hidden counsel** <term>arcanum</term> <note>From the Latin *arcanum*, meaning a "secret" or "mystery." In alchemy, it refers to the deep, hidden secrets of nature.</note> in the knowledge of God. Wisdom bestows all things, and infinite riches come from the labor of her hands. Rather, all good things proceed from her; great riches and wealth are in her hands, and whoever is joined to her is lifted up by her with honor. And Sirach <note>Also known as the Book of Ecclesiasticus.</note>, chapter 1, verse 26: "In the treasures of wisdom are understanding and the religiousness of knowledge," and elsewhere he calls it the "discipline of the senses" or the "doctrine of the intellect." 

Concerning this, Morienus the Philosopher <note>Morienus was a legendary 7th-century hermit of Jerusalem, famous in alchemical lore for teaching the craft to the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note> says: "For this is the science," he says, "which draws its master away from the misery of this world and leads him back to the knowledge of future goods." That it is a gift of God, the same author testifies with these words: "For God bestows this divine and pure knowledge upon his faithful and servants—namely, those to whom he has chosen to grant it from the very first nature of things by his wonderful strength." And soon after: "For this thing is nothing but a gift of the Most High God, who commits and shows it to whomever of his servants and faithful he wishes, just as he wills." It is fitting, therefore, to be humble and entirely subject to Almighty God in all things. And later: "It is also appropriate for you to know, O King, that this mastery is nothing other than the secret and **secret of secrets** <term>secretum secretorum</term> <note>A common title for medieval works claiming to reveal the ultimate hidden truths of nature, governance, and alchemy.</note> of the Great and Most High God. For he himself entrusted this secret to his Prophets, whose souls he has placed in his paradise." 

Truly, it is called the **Tree of Life**, not because it contains eternal salvation within itself, but because it shows the way to it, as it were, and bears fruits useful for this life, which this life cannot do without—such as health, good fortune, and peace of mind. For without these, a man is like the living dead, not unlike a beast, even if he outwardly represents a human; he lacks the better part of himself which he ought to have, but does not.

It emphasizes that this wisdom provides essential earthly benefits like health and peace while pointing the soul toward eternal truths.</summary>

->He who attempts to enter the Philosophical Rose Garden without a key<-

<image-desc>An engraving of an alchemical allegory. In the foreground, a man in Renaissance-style tunic and hose stands before a high, windowless stone wall. Set into the wall is a grand classical gateway, which is heavily barred and secured with three large padlocks. The gate leads into a formal garden, the "Rosarium Philosophicum," containing manicured rectangular beds and a central tree or fountain. ## EPIGRAM XXVII.

The Rose Garden <note>original: "Rosetum"; a common alchemical title and metaphor for the "Great Work" or the chemical vessel where the various stages and colors of transformation bloom like flowers.</note> of Wisdom <note>original: "Sophiæ"; refers to divine or philosophical wisdom, the goal of the alchemist.</note> abounds with many a flower,
But the gate is always closed with firm bars:
The only key to it is considered a lowly thing in the world <note>original: "res vilis"; this refers to the *materia prima*, the basic starting substance of alchemy which is said to be "vile" or common because it is overlooked by the ignorant despite being everywhere.</note>,
Without this, you pursue your way just as if you lacked legs.
In vain do you attempt the steep heights of Parnassus <note>A mountain in Greece sacred to Apollo and the Muses, used here to represent the difficult ascent to the peak of human knowledge and art.</note>, you who can scarcely
Keep your footing on level ground.

<sig>P 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: On</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the central alchemical metaphor of a locked Rose Garden, warning that the secrets of nature cannot be accessed without the "key"—a substance often despised by the common world.</summary>
<keywords>Rose Garden, key, materia prima, Parnassus, Sophia, Wisdom, alchemical allegory, Rosarium Philosophicum</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the text for Emblem XXVII, following the image and epigram on page 117. It provides the theoretical "Discourse" explaining the symbolism of the Rose Garden and the "Key" required to enter it.</meta>

<page-num>118</page-num>

->### DISCOURSE XXVII.<-

They write of **Erichthonius** <note>A legendary early ruler of Athens who, according to myth, was born from the earth after an encounter between Vulcan and Athena; he was often depicted with serpent-like legs.</note> that he was born from the seed of **Vulcan** <note>The god of fire and the forge.</note>—which was cast upon the earth while he struggled with **Pallas**, the Goddess of Wisdom—and that he possessed serpent-like feet rather than human ones. Very similar to him are those who, lacking the wisdom of Pallas and relying solely on the labor of Vulcan, produce offspring that are monstrous, **limbless**, and abortive; such works can neither support themselves nor benefit others. It is a wretched thing for a man to walk like a quadruped—that is, on hands and feet at once—but it is far more wretched to be entirely deprived of feet and to use arms in their place. For those who crawl in the manner of worms or serpents seem to have degenerated into the nature of creeping things. In a human being, the two legs are the organic members without which true walking cannot occur, just as there is no vision without eyes or grasping of tangible things without hands. In the same way, **Medicine** and every operative art is said to possess two legs upon which it stands: namely, **Experience** and **Reason**. Without one of these, that art would be mutilated and crippled; it would neither be perfect in its traditions and precepts, nor would it reach its intended goal.

In particular, **Alchemy** <note>original: "Chymia"</note> enjoys a double subject (acting as its legs), of which one is the **Key** and the other is the **Strap of the Bolt**. By these, the **Philosophical Rose Garden**, which is locked on all sides, is opened, and entry is granted to those who come legitimately. But if one of these is missing for the person attempting to enter, it will be as if a "lame-foot" <note>original: "loripes"; literally "strap-foot," referring to someone with weak or twisted legs.</note> tried to outrun a hare in a race. He who enters a garden closed or fenced on every side without a key is like a thief; coming in the dark of night, he will discern nothing of what grows in the rose garden, nor will he be able to enjoy what he has stolen.

Now, this **Key** is a most lowly thing <note>original: "res vilissima"; a recurring theme in alchemy is that the "Great Work" begins with a common substance that most people ignore or find disgusting.</note>, which is properly called the **Stone** in the various chapters of our books; it is the "Root of Rhodes" <note>A pun on the Greek word "rhodon," meaning rose.</note> without which no sprout is pushed forth, no bud swells, no Rose blooms, nor do the thousand-fold leaves unfold. But one might ask: where is this key to be sought? I respond with the Oracle's answer: it must be investigated in the place where the **bones of Orestes** were said to have been found: namely, **WHERE WINDS, THE STRIKER, THE REBOUNDER, AND THE RUIN OF MEN** are found together. This is exactly how **Lychas** <note>A Spartan figure from Herodotus's *Histories* who solved the riddle to find the remains of Orestes.</note> interpreted it: in a blacksmith’s workshop. For the Oracle intended the "winds" to be the **bellows**; the "striker" to be the **hammer**; the "rebounder" to be the **anvil**; and the "ruin of men" to be **iron** <note>Iron was considered the "ruin of men" because it was the material used to forge weapons of war.</note>. This key in the hemisphere of the Zodiac...

<meta>catchword: pten</meta>

<summary>This discourse uses the myth of Erichthonius to argue that alchemy requires both practical experience and rational theory—the "two legs" of the art—to successfully enter the metaphorical Rose Garden. It identifies the "Key" to this garden through a classical riddle pointing toward the blacksmith's forge, representing the union of fire, metal, and force.</summary>

<keywords>Erichthonius, Vulcan, Pallas Athena, Experience, Reason, Philosophical Rose Garden, Alchemical Key, Bones of Orestes, Blacksmith Forge, Iron</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the text for Discourse XXVII. The previous page ended describing the "two legs" of Alchemy (Experience and Reason) and the "Key" and "Strap" required to open the Philosophical Rose Garden, which is locked on the northern side.</meta>

<page-num>119</page-num>

->**DISCOURSE XXVII.**<-

...on the northern side, he will indeed find it, if he knows how to correctly number and distinguish the signs, and he will find the strap of the bolt on the southern side. Once these are taken hold of, it will be easy to open the door and enter. In the very entrance, he will see **Venus** with her lover **Adonis**; for she stained the white roses purple with her own blood. In that same place, a **dragon** is noticed—just as in the **Gardens of the Hesperides** <note>In Greek mythology, the Hesperides were nymphs who tended a garden with golden apples, guarded by the dragon Ladon.</note>—which keeps watch to guard the roses. It is said that the scent of roses is intensified when other plants are set near them, and this is because of the excellent degree of heat present in the others, by which they resist cold poisons. For roses require the heat of the sun and the earth before they acquire a color and scent most pleasing to the eyes and nostrils. Furthermore, the smoke of common sulfur <note>original: "sulphur commune"</note> turns red roses white wherever it touches them; on the contrary, **spirit of vitriol** or **aqua fortis** <note>Strong mineral acids; spirit of vitriol refers to sulfuric acid, while aqua fortis is nitric acid. In alchemy, these are "corrosive waters" used to change the state of metals.</note> restores them with a saturated red color that lasts. For common sulfur is opposed to **philosophical sulfur**, although it cannot destroy it; but the dissolving water is a friend to it and preserves its color. 

The Rose is dedicated to Venus because of its beauty, by which it surpasses all other little flowers. For she is a maiden whom Nature has armed so that she might not be violated with impunity and without vengeance. Violets are unarmed and are trampled underfoot; roses, hidden within thorns, have golden hair <note>Refers to the yellow stamens inside the rose.</note> on the inside and a green garment on the outside. No one but a wise man will pluck these and separate them from the thorns; if it be otherwise, he will feel the sting in his fingers. Thus, none but the most cautious of Philosophers will gather the flowers, unless he wishes to experience the little bees and stings in the hives, and gall in the honey. Many have secretly entered the **Rose Garden** <note>original: "Rosarium"</note> with predatory hands, but they have brought back nothing from there but pain; that is, they have lost both their oil and their labor. Hence **Bacusser** <note>A name appearing as a speaker in the *Turba Philosophorum*, an early and influential Latin alchemical text.</note> says in the **Assembly [of the Philosophers]** <note>original: "in turba"; referring to the *Turba Philosophorum* or "The Assembly of the Philosophers."</note>: 

*“For our books,”* he says, *“seem to inflict a great injury upon those who read our writings only once, twice, or thrice; for, frustrated in their understanding and all their study, they also (which is worst of all) lose the wealth, labor, and time they have spent on this art.”* 

And a little later: *“For while someone thinks he has done it, and possesses the world, he will find nothing in his hands.”*

<summary>This page describes the symbolic entrance into the Alchemical Rose Garden, guarded by a dragon and associated with Venus and Adonis. It warns that the work requires extreme caution and patience, as superficial reading of the texts leads only to the loss of time and resources.</summary>

<keywords>Venus, Adonis, Rose Garden, Dragon, Sulfur, Vitriol, Bacusser, Turba Philosophorum, Alchemy, Alchemical Marriage</keywords>

### XXVIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

> King Duenech (who bears a green lion upon his shield)
> Seemed quite wild in his manners through an abundance of bile;
> Therefore he summoned the physician Pharut <note>Pharut is a figure from alchemical allegory, appearing in works like the "Treatise of Bernard, Earl of Trevisan," representing a healer or a specific chemical agent of change.</note> to himself, who promised
> To help him, and soon prepared an airy water-bath.
> In this he bathed very often, surrounded by a glass vault,
> Until, moistened by the dew, he lived free from the bile.

## EPIGRAM XXVIII.

King *Duenech* <note>original: "Rex Duenech." An alchemical pseudonym, likely derived from Arabic, often identifying the metal antimony or iron, which must be "cleansed" to progress in the work.</note> (whose weapons shine with the *Green Lion* <note>The "Green Lion" typically refers to a corrosive mineral acid or the raw, unrefined state of the metallic matter.</note>)
Was accustomed to harsh manners, swelling with bile. <note>In the medical theory of the time, an excess of "black bile" led to melancholy and a rigid, gloomy disposition.</note>
Hence he calls the physician *Pharut* <note>Pharut is a symbolic healer in alchemical literature, representing the agent or process that restores balance to the "sick" metal.</note> to himself; who promises health,
And provides airy waters from a spring:
With these he washes and washes again, under a glass arch, <note>The "glass arch" refers to the distillation vessel or retort where the substance is heated and purified.</note> until
By the dripping dew, all the bile was driven away.

<sig>Q</sig>
<meta>catchword: As</meta>

<summary>This page presents the emblem of King Duenech’s vapor bath, an allegory for the chemical purification of a "melancholic" or crude metallic substance through heat and moisture. The physician Pharut represents the guiding hand of the alchemist using controlled "dew" or steam to remove impurities.</summary>

<keywords>King Duenech, Pharut, Green Lion, black bile, vapor bath, distillation, purification, Michael Maier, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXVIII, expanding on the medical and alchemical theory behind the "King’s bath" described in the previous page's epigram. It establishes a parallel between the human body's digestive processes and the refining of metals.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXVIII.</header>

Just as there are three concoctions <note>original: "concoctiones." In early medicine, these were the stages of digestion or "cooking" that transformed food into body tissue.</note> in man—the first in the stomach, the second in the liver, and the third in the veins—so there are also just as many universal evacuations of excrements which correspond to them, carrying away their superfluities daily. Namely, the first is through the bowels, which is proper to the first concoction; the second through urine, which belongs to the second; and the third through expiration from the pores of the entire body, or through sweat, which fits the third. In the first stage, chyle <term>chyle: A milky fluid produced from food in the stomach.</term> is produced; in the following, chyme <term>chyme: A more refined fluid processed by the liver.</term>; and in the last, the "dew" <note>original: "ros." In Galenic medicine, this was the final, most refined stage of nutrition that moistened the smallest parts of the body.</note> or dewy substance is elaborated and applied to each part of the body. The waste or feces of the first are thick, bilious, and fatty, which are carried out through the hindquarters of the intestines; if these should ever be halted, they are ejected by purgatives <note>original: "purgantia." Medicines used to cleanse the bowels.</note> or bowel-movers, whether mild, moderate, or strong. The excrements of the second are liquid, thinner, bilious, and salty, which are led out from the veins through the kidneys and bladder as if through water-conduits; the sediment <note>original: "hypostasis." The solid matter that settles at the bottom of a urine sample, used by physicians for diagnosis.</note> of these serves as an index of the urine. The superfluities of the third are even thinner, and therefore mostly expire on their own through the thinnest pores, or are carried out along with the watery humors as sweat. These are aided by sudorifics <note>medicines or treatments that induce sweating.</note>, just as the previous ones are aided by diuretics.

The ancient Greeks and Romans were very concerned with evacuating these final wastes. Hence, they employed so many kinds of games and exercises, such as the morning rubbing of all the limbs, anointing with oil, wrestling, the art of boxing, racing contests, and games with the palm-ball, the racket-ball, and the great-ball; also daily washing and bathing in rivers or artificial baths. For the convenience of these, so many magnificent buildings were constructed at Rome that it is more possible for us to admire them than to imitate them. An example is the Baths of Diocletian <note>The largest of the Roman imperial baths, built around 300 CE.</note>, which for the most part still remain and are dedicated to the Archangels <note>original: "Archāgelis... inscriptæ." Referring to the church of Santa Maria degli Angeli e dei Martiri, which Michelangelo built inside the ruins of the Baths of Diocletian.</note>, if I am not mistaken; they show how high, proud, and splendid these works were.

Almost the same species of concoctions as those mentioned before exist in metallurgy: for the first occurs in its own way in the "Great Year" <note>original: "magno anno." A cosmic cycle representing a full revolution of the heavens.</note>, that is, in the revolution of the highest sphere; the second in the revolution of the lowest sphere; and the third in the middle sphere. But the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" here refers specifically to alchemists.</note>, so that they may more conveniently lead out these heaps of excremental superfluities through the service of the parts, also find various ways—such as washings, purgations, bathings, and steam-baths <term>laconica: The dry sweating-room of a Roman bath; a sauna.</term>—by which they perform that in the Philosophical Work which physicians do in the human body. Duenech <note>Returning to the subject of the King mentioned on the previous page.</note>...

<summary>This page explains the medical theory of the "three concoctions" (digestions) and their corresponding wastes (feces, urine, and sweat), using the grandeur of Roman baths as a historical example of waste management. It then draws an alchemical parallel, suggesting that metals undergo similar stages of refinement that require "cleansing" by the philosopher.</summary>

<keywords>three concoctions, digestion, waste evacuation, Roman baths, Baths of Diocletian, alchemy vs medicine, metallurgy, chyle, chyme, sudorifics, Great Year</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXVIII, moving from the general theory of the "three concoctions" to the specific allegorical treatment of the King—representing the alchemical matter—who must be purified in a "Laconic" or sweating bath to remove the melancholic impurities of Saturn and Mars.</meta>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXVIII.</header>

Therefore, from Pharos <note>original: "à Pharu." Pharos was the site of the famous lighthouse of Alexandria; in alchemy, it often symbolizes the initial stage of a journey or the emergence from the "sea" of raw matter.</note> he is led into a Laconicum <term>Laconicum: A dry, intense heat room or sweat-bath, named after the Spartans of Laconia.</term>, so that he may sweat there and excrete the dregs of the third concoction <note>See previous page: the third concoction is the final stage of "digestion" or metabolism within the tissues themselves.</note> through the pores. Now, the disposition of this King is melancholic or atrabilious <term>atrabilarius: Relating to black bile, the humor associated with the planet Saturn, lead, and a gloomy or "leaden" temperament.</term>; for this reason, he is held in lower authority and esteem by all other princes, as he is burdened by the moroseness of Saturn and the choler <term>choler: Yellow bile, the humor associated with the planet Mars, fire, and anger.</term> or wrath of Mars. He himself, therefore, wished either to die or be cured, if such a thing were possible. One physician was found who took this task upon himself, moved by prayers and rewards.

This allegory is quite frequent in the writings of the Philosophers, such as those of Bernard, Alan, the little treatise on Duenech <note>original: "tractatulo de Duenech." This refers to a specific medieval alchemical text describing the "cure" of a melancholic king, often identified with the metal lead.</note>, and countless others; therefore, we do not add other details which can be sought from them. Let it suffice to have observed here which concoction's waste is drawn out through bathing. For on this the whole matter hinges.

It is customary in hot springs or warm baths that the heat enclosed in the body is recalled to the surface of the skin along with the blood, from which a beautiful color is won for the face and the whole body. If this color appears, it will be a sign that the melancholic blackness which infects the skin can be gradually evacuated, and all the humors corrected, so that the best and most rosy blood may afterwards be generated. It is necessary that the entire temperament of his body be amended, which now rebels against good blood and is cold and dry, whereas blood, on the contrary, is hot and moist. Whether this transformation can be achieved, it befits the physician to foreknow and predict by prognostics <note>original: "prognosticis." Signs or symptoms used to predict the outcome of a disease.</note>.

Sometimes men have been found who mistook a Cerdo <term>Cerdo: A common craftsman or cobbler; used here to mean a person of low or common birth.</term> for a great prince or the son of a king, but at last perceived his true origin and education from certain signs. The artisan must take care that this does not happen; namely, that he chooses a true royal offspring in the first place. Even if the King is not splendid in golden ornament, but is in a despised and vile garment, and even of a leaden and melancholic color, the artisan should not for that reason reject him or seek another in his place. For if he is washed thoroughly, his royal nature will soon appear—just as it did in Cyrus, Paris, and Romulus <note>Historical and mythological figures who were of royal birth but were famously raised as common shepherds or rustics before their true nature was revealed.</note>, who were raised among country folk.

But it must be noticed that the bath be "Laconic"—that is, vaporous and sweat-inducing—lest the water burn his tender flesh or obstruct the pores, from which more harm than benefit would result, and his condition would not be cured. Let no one be anxious about the royal garments to be put on him after bathing; for just as the daughter of King Alcinous <note>Nausicaa, from Homer's *Odyssey*, who discovered the shipwrecked Ulysses and provided him with clothing.</note> once provided clothes to the shipwrecked and naked Ulysses, so there will be one who sends him the most precious garments, so that he may rightly be recognized as the Son of the Sun.

It emphasizes the need for a skilled physician-alchemist to recognize the King's true royal nature even when he appears in a leaden, "vile" state.</summary>

->*English translation of the German version of the XXIX. Latin Epigram.*<-

The salamander lives powerfully and unharmed in the fierce fire,
And Vulcan, heeds your threats not at all;
So too does he regard the cruel ignition of the flames as a small thing—
He who is born in constant fire: the Philosophers’ Stone.
The former <note>The salamander.</note> is cold, and thus extinguishes the fire and escapes unhurt;
But the latter <note>The Stone.</note> is hot, and therefore remains stable within the same heat.

## EPIGRAM XXIX.

> ->*The more powerful Salamander dwells in the burning fire,*<- <note>original: "Degit in ardenti Salamandra potentior igne"</note>
> ->*Nor does she heed your threats, O Vulcan:*<- <note>Vulcan was the Roman god of fire and metalworking; here he represents the intense heat of the alchemist’s furnace.</note>
> ->*In the same way, he does not shrink from the cruel conflagration of flames,*<-
> ->*The Stone, which was born in constant fire.*<- <note>original: "Qui fuit assiduo natus in igne Lapis." The "Stone" refers to the Philosophers’ Stone, the sought-after substance of alchemical transformation.</note>
> ->*The former, being cold, extinguishes the heat and emerges free,*<- <note>According to ancient lore, such as that found in Pliny the Elder, the salamander was thought to be so cold that its touch would extinguish fire.</note>
> ->*But the latter is hot, and is sustained by a similar heat.*<- <note>The alchemist notes a distinction: while the salamander survives by opposing the fire with cold, the alchemical Stone survives because its nature has been made identical to the fire through the "Great Work."</note>

<meta>Continuing from page 125, which featured the emblem and epigram of the Salamander, this page begins the "Discourse"—a prose commentary explaining the natural history and alchemical symbolism of the creature. The previous page established that while the natural salamander resists fire through coldness, the alchemical Stone thrives in fire because of its perfected nature.</meta>

# 126 DISCOURSE XXIX.

There are two Elements in which animals live—water and air—and just as many in which nothing animate can survive—earth and fire. For just as those former ones are of a middle and temperate composition in their primary and secondary qualities <note>In ancient and medieval science, "primary qualities" were Heat, Cold, Moisture, and Dryness; "secondary" referred to density or texture.</note>, so these latter ones are extremes: they are bodies either too thick or too subtle. Their thickness does not admit other bodies into them, while their subtlety may admit them, but only to penetrate and burn them. As for the fact that men may live in cellars and subterranean mines, this happens by reason of the air descending that far and filling those places so they are not a vacuum.

But here we are speaking of each Element on its own. In the water, fish live in incredible number, variety, and fertility—indeed, the largest of all animals. In the air live humans, four-footed beasts, birds, worms, and insects. Whatever is said concerning spirits wandering in the hidden depths of the earth is another matter, for they are not animals. In the fire, truly, they say only the Salamander lives.

The Salamander is a crawling worm, not unlike a lizard, but with a slower gait, a larger head, and a different color. I remember seeing such a creature myself in the Alps, on the ridge of the Splügen Pass, after thunder and rain; it had come out from the caverns of the rocks and was lingering in the path. A local peasant <note>original: "ruricola"</note> called it *a Molch* <note>original German: "ein Molch"; a term still used for a newt or salamander.</note>. It had around it a slow and viscous moisture, by which it passes through fires freely and without harm. 

But the Salamander of the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the Alchemists.</note> is far different, although it is compared to this one. For the former is born in the fire, whereas the natural one is not; rather, if the natural one falls into the fire, it is not immediately consumed because of its copious and extreme coldness and moisture, allowing it to pass freely through the flame. The Alchemical Salamander is hot and dry, but the natural one is cold and moist. For everything reflects the nature of its mother’s womb, or imitates its place and country of origin. Fire produces nothing except what is hot and dry, as something like unto itself; and on the contrary, the moist and cold caverns of the rocks, filled by rains, send forth this worm, which is cold and moist. The Alchemical creature enjoys the fire by a similarity of nature, while the natural one extinguishes the fire by opposition, or repels the fire's force from itself for a short time.

They say that from the copper-smelting furnaces of Cyprus, a fire-fly <note>original: "pyrausta"; a mythical insect described by Pliny the Elder as living in the fire.</note> flies out, having been born in the fire. But no one would believe this to be true except by way of allegory. For fire destroys and seizes the bodies of any animals whatsoever if the contact is continued, since it burns even the earth into glass, and the most solid woods into ashes, and all other compound things—with a few exceptions, such as mercurial substances, which either remain whole or fly away from the fire in their entirety, with no separation of parts occurring in them. Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire, used here as a metaphor for the alchemist's furnace.</note> is a most rigid executioner, who brings all things mixed from the Elements to the test <note>original: "in examen"</note>...

<meta>The text ends mid-sentence; "in examen" implies a trial or weighing. Catchword: suum (his/its own).</meta>

<summary>This discourse distinguishes between the natural salamander (a cold, moist amphibian) and the 'Philosophical Salamander,' which represents the Alchemical Stone's ability to endure and thrive in fire.</summary>
<keywords>Elements, Salamander, Splügen Pass, Molch, fire-fly, pyrausta, Vulcan, alchemy, primary qualities, Cyprus</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 126, where the author discussed the physical nature of the Salamander as a creature of fire, we now move into the philosophical and alchemical "judgment" of the elements. The text continues the metaphor of Vulcan (fire) as a judge presiding over the court of nature.</meta>

# 127 DISCOURSE XXIX.

...it calls to its own judgment, although several things are exempt from its court by the privileges and pardons of Empress Nature. In these, fire holds no jurisdiction on its own unless it joins other "Areopagites" <note>The Areopagus was the high court of ancient Athens; here, it refers to a council of judges or elements.</note> to itself. These things are like Salamanders in the face of fire's violence, which they do not fear. **Avicenna** lists various temperaments of bodies in a different section, all of which are unequal and therefore corruptible by fire and other injuries. But he describes one that is "optimally balanced" <note>original: "equatū"</note>, which possesses as much heat as cold, and as much dryness as moisture—not by weight, but by **justice**, as physicians call it <note>In medieval medicine and alchemy, "justice" or "justitia" refers to a proportional balance of qualities suited to a specific function, rather than an equal physical weight.</note>. This is the body that is more passive than active. In this body, if fire strives to dissolve its enemy, water, into its relative, air, the earth does not permit this dissolution because it is incorporated with the water. And the internal fire of the compound supports this decision of the earth, because fire is the earth's closest friend.

Thus the judgment of **Vulcan** <note>The Roman god of fire, representing the alchemist’s furnace or the destructive power of heat.</note> ceases. He then tries another trick: he attempts to burn the earth into ashes, as is his custom; but the water adhering to the earth brings forward "exceptions" <note>Legal defenses or formal objections in a court of law.</note>, showing itself to be united to the earth, and the air to itself, while fire is on the other side of the earth. Therefore, if anyone wishes to incinerate the earth, he must also incinerate the rest of the elements. Thus **Vulcan**, having been cheated of his goal, suspends his judgment so as not to become a laughingstock to all. This is the body most truly like the Salamander, in which the Elements are balanced by a scale of virtues. Concerning this, the **Rosary** <note>A reference to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> quotes from **Geber**: *Likewise,* it says, *this Philosopher requires this substance of Mercury to be mortified <note>original: "mortificatam." In alchemy, "mortification" refers to the symbolic death or transformation of a substance, often by stripping away its volatile qualities.</note>; but naturally his Mercury is in that venerable stone, as is manifest to everyone.* Therefore, etc. *Likewise, this Philosopher requires that substance of Mercury to be fixed, as is evident, because he teaches the skills of fixation with many cautions and techniques; but who would doubt that the substance of this precious stone is most fixed?* Certainly no one who knows it.

From this it is clear that the Stone must be reduced to the nature of the Salamander through **fixation** <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable in heat.</note>; that is, to the highest state of fixity, which does not flee or refuse the fire. For it cannot be a Salamander before it has learned to be most patient in enduring fire, which must necessarily happen through a long span of time. Concerning **Achilles** and **Triptolemus** <note>Two figures from Greek myth who were placed in fire by goddesses—Thetis and Demeter—to burn away their mortality and make them immortal.</note>, who were placed under the glowing coals of fire at night until they could bear the most intense heat, more will be said later in the 35th emblematic discourse. They also attained a "Salamandrine" property through habit and custom. For custom is a second nature; yet, unless nature communicates the power and begins the alteration like a schoolmistress, custom can achieve little or nothing. Hence it is that it is impossible to fix ice to the fire, but it is possible to fix crystal, because nature has already begun the process in the latter. In the same way, one must think of watery and volatile Mercury: it cannot be fixed by its own nature, except through the embrace of **married sulfur**, which is the **Tincture of the Philosophers** and fixes all flying spirits.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

It concludes that the volatile Mercury must be united with Sulfur—the "Tincture"—to achieve the heat-resistant nature of a Salamander.</summary>

### XXX. German version of the Latin Epigram.

O Sun, you accomplish nothing alone, unless I act with my powers,
Just as the rooster is considered useless without the hen;
And in turn, I, the Moon <note>original: "Luna"</note>, desire your present help,
Just as the rooster is greatly desired by the hen;
Those whom Nature wishes to have joined together,
They are not wise, who are not ashamed to separate them.

# EMBLEM XXX. On the Secrets of Nature.
## ->The Sun needs the Moon, just as the rooster needs the hen.<-

Just as the rooster is sought by the hen.
Those things which nature demands to be joined together, he is

<meta>This discourse follows the epigram of Emblem XXX, which established the symbolic necessity of the union between the Sun (rooster) and the Moon (hen). This page provides the philosophical and natural history justification for this metaphor, drawing on the works of Avicenna and classical mythology.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXX.

Avicenna, in his book *On the Soul*, <note>original: "lib. de anima"; referring to the *Liber Aboali Abincine de Anima in Arte Alchimiae*, a text attributed to the Persian polymath Avicenna (Ibn Sina) that was highly influential in European alchemy.</note> frequently advises that eggs should not be taken for the Art unless they come from those hens which a rooster has attended. This means that without the masculine virtue, the feminine subject is worth nothing, and vice-versa, the rooster will be of no use without the hen. For these twin sexes must be joined in the court of the Philosophers so that multiplication may occur.

The Philosophers indeed use a comparison drawn from the rooster because he, above all, corresponds more to the power of <term>sulfur</term> <note>In alchemical theory, sulfur represents the active, masculine, "hot" principle that acts upon the passive, feminine "mercury."</note> than any other male among birds, since one can preside over many hens and does not easily suffer a rival in the same dungheap, deeming himself equal to and sufficient for each one. He is the bird of *Mars*, transformed from the youth *Alectryon* <note>In Greek myth, Alectryon was a youth stationed by Mars to watch for the Sun during his affair with Venus. When he fell asleep and they were caught, Mars turned him into a rooster to announce the Sun's rising forever after.</note> who should have watched the *Sun* lest he witness the adultery of *Mars* with *Venus*, as the Poets fable. He is also quite warlike in the fight, in which he contends with his adversary unto death. 

He represents the *Sun* in the *Philosophical* work, just as the hen represents the *Moon*. Therefore, there is the same necessity for joining the *Sun* with the *Moon* as there is for joining the rooster with the hen. The rooster is also a *Sun-maker* <note>original: "Solificer"; one who announces or brings forth the sun.</note> for whom he rises as he approaches, and with whom he goes to sleep. He frequently looks toward the Heavens and raises his sickle-shaped tails on high; he fights against serpents on behalf of the hens. He is the messenger of light and dear to *Latona*, <note>Latona (Leto) is the mother of the divine twins Apollo (Sun) and Diana (Moon).</note> because he stood by her as she was giving birth. For *Latona* brought forth the *Sun* and the *Moon*, and thus the rooster is assigned to both the mother and the son. 

But the *Sun*, the *Moon*, and *Latona* are suitable for chemical subjects; and so are the rooster and the hen. Indeed, these also came forth from an egg, and in turn they leave behind eggs to be hatched into young chickens. In the same way, the *Philosophers* have their own eggs which pass into birds of the same kind, provided they are fostered by a temperate heat, just as the heat of a sitting hen is, which lasts continuously. 

For while among other birds the male also sits upon the eggs, the rooster alone shows himself exempt from this office or burden. Thus, all the care and labor of hatching the eggs and educating the chicks falls upon the hen. Here one may observe her industriousness and diligence: how quickly she eats and drinks and relieves her belly, and then immediately runs back to the eggs before they grow cold. Furthermore, with what strength and force she defends the chicks; with what kindness she receives and covers the naked ones under her wings; with what a voice, like a bell, she calls them together and keeps them; and with what zeal she breaks the harder crumbs or grains for them and serves them with her beak as if with a knife—it is a work of nature worthy of admiration. And all these things are done lest eggs or young chickens be lacking for the nourishment of man. In the same way, the *Philosopher* or artisan must most diligently and

<meta>catchword: providè (foresightedly / carefully)</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical symbolism of the rooster and hen, linking them to the masculine/sulfuric and feminine/mercurial principles necessary for the "multiplication" of the Stone. It emphasizes the natural diligence required in the process, modeled on the hen's care for her eggs.</summary>

# DISCOURSE XXX.

<margin>Signature: R ij</margin>
<meta>The catchword "Der" appears at the bottom of the previous page in some editions, though it is German and the text is Latin; this likely reflects the German printing house context.</meta>

...she proceeds providently in all her operations. For she gathers eggs from their places where the rooster has been present, having examined them lest they be "wind-eggs" <note>original: "subventanea"; infertile eggs produced without a mate, believed in alchemy to be useless for generation.</note>, afterwards she cleanses, prepares, and arranges them in her vessels as if in nests, and provides the suitable heat. By this heat, as they are mixed together day by day, the subjects act and are acted upon by one another, until after a long time, passing through various colors, they arrive at a single color and essence. In this work, <term>solution</term>, <term>coagulation</term>, <term>sublimation</term>, <term>ascent</term>, <term>descent</term>, <term>distillation</term>, <term>calcination</term>, and <term>fixation</term> are completed as intermediate steps.

For that which is hard and compact cannot be altered; therefore, solution is performed first, so that it may liquefy and soften. But when something is dissolved, it is also necessary that it be coagulated, not to its former hardness, but to a honey-like pliability. Truly, <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of vaporizing a substance and condensing it again, symbolizing purification and spiritualization.</note> separates the pure from the impure, and renders the base more worthy, the lower higher. Whence this operation must not be absent, for she is like the mistress and teacher of all. While sublimation occurs, some parts rise higher, which is ascent, and others descend again, and thus descent occurs. From this, distillation clarifies the whole, being frequently repeated, and that which remains in the bottom is <term>calcined</term> <note>Reduced to a fine powder through intense heat.</note>. Both, however, are <term>fixed</term> <note>Made stable and resistant to the fire.</note>, and thus the work is perfected.

If anyone should group all these special operations under the general heading, which is **Cooking** <note>original: "Coctio"; often referred to in alchemy as the "Work of Women and Child's Play" because it requires only patience and a steady heat.</note>, he will not wander far from the goal. For just as many chicks as there are, running scattered here and there, come together under one mother or nursing hen, so do these various ways and modes of operating concur in the "work of women"—that is, in the one cooking. 

It is the Moon which comes to be exalted to the sublimity of the Sun, for whose sake all these things are done; that is, a durable marriage between the Sun and Moon is intended. If this is achieved, all delegations, betrothals, copulations, and doubts will cease, and there will be one bed and one flesh of both, mutual and constant love, eternal peace, and an indissoluble covenant. The Sun without the Moon is of no great value, and the Moon without the Sun is of a dejected condition and base origin. But she receives from the Sun, her spouse, splendor, dignity, and strength—the firmness of both mind and body—while the Sun receives from the Moon the multiplication of offspring and the propagation of the race.

Hence the *Rosarius* <note>Referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rose Garden of the Philosophers), a famous 16th-century alchemical florilegium.</note> says: *“If in our stone there were only one of these two, the medicine would never flow easily, nor would it give a tincture, and if it did give one, it would not tint, except insofar as the other was present; and the Mercury would fly away in smoke, because there would be no receptacle for the tincture.”* <note>original: "Si in lapide nostro esset tantū alterū ipsorū, nunquā facilè flueret medicina, neq; tincturā daret, & si daret, nō tingeret, nisi in quātū esset & reliquum & Mercurius in fumo evolaret, quia nō esset in eo receptaculū tincturæ."</note> And Geber <note>An 8th-century Arab chemist, Jabir ibn Hayyan, whose Latinized works were foundational to European alchemy.</note> proves in his *Book of Examinations* that if the Sun and Moon are incorporated together by Art, they are not easily separated.

<summary>This page explains the various chemical operations (sublimation, calcination, etc.) necessary to achieve the alchemical marriage. It emphasizes that these complex steps are all part of a single "cooking" process that unites the masculine Sun (Sulfur) and feminine Moon (Mercury).</summary>

<keywords>alchemical operations, sublimation, distillation, Sun and Moon, alchemical marriage, Rosarium Philosophorum, Geber, sulfur-mercury theory</keywords>

### XXXI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The King, whose head is made quite heavy by a golden crown,
Cries out thus loudly, swimming in the deep sea:
"Why do you not help me? Why does everyone not come running,
When I, once rescued from the distress of the water, can reward you?
Bring me, if you are wise, into my kingdom, and never again
Shall poverty oppress you, nor any grievous sickness of the body."

<meta>This page continues the prose commentary for Discourse XXXI. Following the image and epigram of the "Drowning King" on page 133, this text provides a historical and philosophical justification for the King’s predicament, drawing parallels between physical literacy (swimming) and intellectual literacy (letters) as essential tools for survival.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>

# DISCOURSE XXXI.

In ancient times, the very first rudiments of all education were skill in swimming and instruction in letters. From this, it was customary to say of a rude and unpolished man: *He knows neither how to swim nor how to read.* <note>original: "Neq; nare neq; literas novit." This was a common Greek proverb used to describe a person who lacked the most basic elements of a civilized upbringing.</note> For the ancients considered that swimming would often benefit the body by saving it and snatching it from the dangers of the water, whereas the knowledge of letters would benefit the soul [snatching it] from the waves of fortune. Furthermore, swimming served military service especially in time of war, and knowledge of letters served at home, in peace. 

And just as we notice that beasts have their weapons ready at hand and provided by nature, in their place, man has been granted hands and intellect against external force—so that the latter might devise and invent weapons, and the former might make and use them—so too is the ability to swim naturally implanted in those same beasts, but not at all in man. They often escape from waters by swimming even when young, where a man, whether old or very robust, is suffocated. Therefore, it was necessary to command the exercise of swimming for boys, which is always most useful to any life, so that what might be lacking through nature might be supplied by the practice of art. Great men, princes, and kings used the same exercise for the protection of their own bodies. For those born in a high position are not at all free from the accidents of fortune, but are exposed to them just as other men. 

If Dionysius <note>Dionysius II of Syracuse (c. 397–343 BCE), a tyrant who was famously deposed and lived out his days in Corinth as a humble schoolteacher.</note> had known neither how to swim nor his letters, when he was driven from the kingdom of Sicily as a tyrant, he would have perished in the waves of the sea as a shipwrecked man in the Corinthian Gulf; but having been rescued from the dangers by swimming, he betook himself to Corinth, where he opened a school for teaching boys and taught the liberal arts <note>original: "literas humaniores," referring to the classical humanities.</note>. Thus, from a king he was made a schoolmaster <term>ludimoderator|A schoolmaster or teacher of a "ludus" (elementary school)</term>, holding a rod instead of a scepter, whence the saying: *Dionysius at Corinth.* 

In a similar way, unless the royal Son of the Philosophers <note>An alchemical term for the "Stone" or the central material of the Great Work in its initial, vulnerable state.</note> were able to swim, no one would hear his cry, nor would anyone bring him help, having been submerged in the waters long ago. Swimming is therefore necessary and most useful for a man of every condition. For even if it does not immediately free him from the waves of the immense sea, yet it grants a time of life in which he might be freed by others. Indeed, the aforementioned King sustains himself for a very long time and cries out even to this point, although he is heard or seen by very few because of the vastness of the sea and its remoteness. For perhaps by swimming he has reached a reef or a very large stone <note>This "stone" likely represents the first appearance of the "fixed" or solid state within the "volatile" liquid solvent of the alchemical process.</note> by which he may be held fast if the waves prevail. 

But it is asked, what kind of sea is it? I answer: it is the Erythraean or Red Sea, situated under the Tropic of Cancer, in which, since magnets <note>original: "magnetes." In the 17th century, it was believed that the Red Sea or Indian Ocean contained magnetic islands that could pull the iron nails out of ships.</note> are very abundant

<vocab>skill in swimming, instruction in letters, Dionysius, Corinth, schoolmaster, Erythraean (Red) Sea, magnets</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the allegory of the King swimming in the sea by comparing it to the ancient necessity of being "literate" in both swimming and reading. It cites the historical example of the exiled King Dionysius to show how these skills allow one to survive reversals of fortune and transitions between states.</summary>

<keywords>Dionysius of Corinth, literacy, swimming, Red Sea, accidents of fortune, Son of the Philosophers, schoolmaster, alchemical dissolution</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the "Drowning King" (Discourse XXXI). While the previous page discussed the general importance of swimming and education, this page applies those concepts to the alchemical allegory of a king whose ship is pulled down by a magnetic seabed, requiring him to be rescued and restored through specific medicinal and chemical processes.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>

<header>DISCOURSE XXXI.</header>

at the bottom, from which it is not safe for ships joined with iron or laden with it to pass through: For they could easily be drawn to the bottom by the force of a magnet. Since the aforementioned king was ignorant of this, and with the ship submerged and the others perishing, he alone escaped by swimming. His crown remained with him, shining with remarkable rubies, by which he could easily be recognized and restored to his kingdom.

But what good things can or will this royal son impart to the one by whom he is restored to his own kingdom? Truly, not of the kind that Ptolemy, the last king of Egypt, gave to Pompey—by whom his father had been restored to the Egyptian kingdom—namely, treachery and death; but rather health, the removal of diseases, the preservation of life from them, the use of necessary things, a horn of plenty <note>The "cornu copiae," a symbol of abundance and nourishment.</note>, honor, and love. Since these are not common or mediocre things, but the greatest provisions and ornaments of this life, who, unless they were as dull as lead, would not desire them? Who would not swim toward him? Who would not offer a hand and pull him into a boat? 

But care must be taken lest, while helping him, his crown falls into the sea. For then he would hardly be recognized as a king, nor received by his subjects: because the carbuncle <note>original: "pyropus," a fiery-red gemstone or bronze alloy, here representing the king's vital solar power.</note>, venerable to all, would have been lost, and the Bezoar stone <note>A "stone" found in the stomachs of goats, historically believed to be a universal antidote to poison.</note>, promising health to everyone, would have vanished. Hence the *Rosarius* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, or "Rosary of the Philosophers," a central 16th-century alchemical text.</note> cites Aristotle in these words:

> "Choose for yourself the stone by which kings are honored in
> their crowns, and by which physicians are able to cure their sick; because that
> [stone] is close to the fire."

For without its medicinal power, the crown would be of no value. But what must be done for the king, now set free? He must be freed from the water he has swallowed by means of sudorifics <term>medicines that induce sweating to expel toxins</term>, from the cold by the heat of fire, from the numbness of his limbs by moderately warm baths, from hunger and starvation by the administration of a suitable diet, and from other external evils by their contrary and health-bringing remedies. Then, a royal marriage must be provided for him, and in due time from it will be born a most desired offspring, most pleasing to all, most beautiful and most fertile, who will surpass all his ancestors in power, kingdoms, dominions, peoples, riches, and wealth; and he will subject his enemies to himself, not by war, but by humanity—not by tyranny, but by mercy, which is genuine and proper to him.

### *English translation of the German version of Epigram XXXII.*

> A moist plant, growing in the sea not far from Sicily,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Has finely spread its little branches beneath the warm water;
> Its name is Coral, which soon changes into hardness,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Whenever a dry wind blows from cold lands;
> It becomes stone, yet keeps its red color and many branches;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This is a fitting example of the natural Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone, which Maier describes as starting in a "vegetable" or "growing" state before being fixed into a permanent, "mineral" stone.</note>.

<summary>This page explores the metaphor of coral—believed in the 17th century to be a soft plant that petrified upon contact with air—to describe the solidification and fixation of the Philosopher's Stone. It includes the musical notation for a three-part fugue, symbolizing the harmonious movement of the alchemical process.</summary>
<keywords>coral, Boreas, North Wind, Sicily, Philosopher's Stone, petrifaction, transmutation, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XXXII, expanding on the geological and biological theories introduced in the emblem. Maier argues that the "Vegetable Stone" (the Philosopher's Stone) follows the same natural laws as coral, pearls, and amber.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXII.</header>

Philosophers call their stone "vegetable" <note>original: *lapidem vegetabilem*; in alchemy, this refers to the Stone's ability to grow and multiply like a living organism</note>, because like a plant it vegetates, grows, increases, and multiplies. This indeed seems strange and far from the truth to the ignorant, since it is well known that stones do not vegetate nor grow in this manner, nor do they have any relation to meltable metals. But they are deceived in their judgment: for they think that what is unknown to them does not exist in the nature of things, measuring the immensity of the universe by their own limited grasp. Who would have ever believed that a stone grows under the waters, or that a plant generated there turns to stone, unless Experience and the constant reliability of writers proved it? Where does that stone-making power, that coloring force, which hardens and tints the coral, exist? Is it in the waters, in the air, or in the earth? It is likely, as they affirm, that the plant is soft and flexible while it is under the water; yet it is of a strongly earthy nature. When it is cut and exposed to the cold winds, it hardens and acquires a stony brittleness as the abundant watery parts are dried out by the cold and dry air—for these northern winds bring dryness with their breath—and the remaining earthy body is congealed by the qualities of the earth: coldness and dryness. For <term>constriction</term> <note>the alchemical quality of "binding" or "tightening" associated with the element Earth</note> alone belongs to the earth, not to water or air, regarding the proper and genuine virtues of each.

Furthermore, in other places the sea provides three medicinal stones, taken partly from the vegetable genus, partly from the animal, or rather partly from the secrets of nature: namely pearls, amber, and <term>ambergris</term> <note>original: *ambram gryseam*; a waxy substance produced in the digestive system of sperm whales, highly prized in perfumery and medicine</note>. The production and harvest of pearls are known to us, but not those of the others. <term>Amber</term> <note>original: *succinum*</note> is gathered on the maritime shores of Sudovia <note>a region in the Baltics, roughly modern-day Lithuania and Poland, famous for amber deposits</note>, on the beaches after a violent blast of the <term>Northwest wind</term> <note>original: *Circius* or *Corus*</note>. It undoubtedly bubbles up from veins in the earth into the sea, or is washed out by the waters and driven to the shores by the waves. For we have seen certain minerals of iron and silver attached to amber, which could not have happened except in the earth. 

But as for the fact that flies, gnats, spiders, butterflies, frogs, and snakes are seen in certain small pieces—as we ourselves possessed 120 beads turned from amber, each of which contained flies, gnats, spiders, and some butterflies (one single piece even contained nine), not without a singular miracle of nature—this happens from the influence and <term>imagination of the heavens</term> <note>original: *imaginatione coeli*; a Renaissance theory that the stars and heavens could "imprint" forms or shapes onto matter on Earth</note>, as we have demonstrated elsewhere.

<meta>catchword: Ambergris</meta>

<summary>Michael Maier explains why the Philosopher's Stone is called "vegetable" by comparing its growth and hardening to natural phenomena like coral and amber. He argues that the environment—specifically cold and dry air—is responsible for the transformation of soft sea plants into hard stone.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, vegetable stone, coral, amber, Sudovia, elemental qualities, ambergris, spontaneous generation, alchemy, mineralogy</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XXXII, where Michael Maier uses natural phenomena—specifically the formation of ambergris and coral—to justify the possibility of the "Philosopher’s Stone." Having previously argued that stones can "grow" like plants, he now moves to classify these mysterious substances and link their physical properties to the alchemical process of "coagulation" and "reddening."</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>D I S C U R S U S XXXII.</header>

It cannot be denied that **Grey Amber** <note>original: *Ambra Grysea*; today known as ambergris. In the 17th century, its origin was a mystery, often confused with mineral amber (succinite)</note> is found in the same way on the shores of both the East and West Indies. Although some refer it to the sap or gum of trees (as was said of amber before), those who think it emerges from the veins of the earth judge more probably. For trees bearing amber and ambergris have never been seen; yet it is most certain that such trees, if they existed, would grow in the open air outside of the water. Therefore, we refer both types of amber to subterranean veins or stones, just as we refer pearls to **Zoophytes** <note>literally "animal-plants"; organisms like sponges or corals that appear to bridge the gap between the animal and vegetable kingdoms</note> and coral to vegetables.

Indeed, the <term>Philosopher's stone</term> <note>original: *Philosophorum lapis*</note> is compared to these stones, and especially to coral. For just as coral grows in the waters and takes its nourishment from the earth, so too the <term>philosophical stone</term> <note>original: *lapis Philosophicus*</note> congeals from **mercurial water**. From that water, it consumes whatever is earthy for its own growth, while the superfluous moisture evaporates away. A red color is also intensified in it through coagulation; this is what more recent authors call the <term>tincture of corals</term> <note>original: *tincturam coralliorum*; in alchemy, a "tincture" is a potent substance capable of transforming or coloring other matter</note>. This is no different from the <term>physical stone</term>, which in its final congealing turns red and appears like the reddest coral, which is the tincture itself. 

But whereas coral hardens through cold and dryness, the Stone hardens through heat and dryness. When this heat is increased, the Stone likewise liquefies—contrary to the nature of other stones, which may melt, but turn into glass, a thing that does not happen here. And just as coral is prepared into various medicines of great virtue, so too the "Coral of the Philosophers" has transferred into itself the powers of all herbs. This single Stone can do as much as all the vegetable medicines of the world combined. For the celestial Sun, which infuses medicinal virtue and efficacy into vegetables, has granted more to this, his son and earthly representative, than to all others. 

This is the <term>philosophical coral</term>: vegetable, animal, and mineral. It lies hidden in the vast sea and is not recognized unless it is placed before the eyes of the ignorant or put into their hands. But it must be cut away most carefully from beneath the waters, lest it lose its juice and blood, leaving nothing but a "terrestrial chaos" stripped of its true form. Indeed, the entire difficulty of capturing the coral lies in this, and hardly any other occurs: I refer to the **superfluous humidity**, which kills the stone unless it is separated. While that moisture is present, it does not allow the coral-redness to appear, nor does it permit coagulation.

<sig>S ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: The (original: *Der*)</meta>

<summary>Maier compares the formation of the Philosopher's Stone to that of coral and ambergris, arguing that the Stone is a "philosophical coral" that must be carefully harvested from its "mercurial water" and dried of excess moisture to achieve its final red form.</summary>
<keywords>ambergris, coral, Philosopher's Stone, coagulation, tincture, mercurial water, medicinal virtues, superfluous humidity</keywords>

### XXXIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The two-headed <term>Hermaphrodite</term> <note>In alchemy, the Hermaphrodite (or Rebis) represents the "two-fold thing"—the union of male/solar and female/lunar principles into a single, perfected substance.</note> resembles a man who is dead,
When he suffers great distress (death) because of stolen moisture;
As he covers himself in the dark night, you must give him more fire,
Which, as soon as you do, brings life back to him again;
All power of the <term>Stone</term> consists in the fire, all red sulfur stays in the gold,
And the white <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: *Mercurius*. Not common quicksilver, but the "philosophical mercury" or the spirit of the metal.</note> alone proceeds from the silver.

<header>EMBLEM XXXIII. On the secrets of Nature.</header>

### The Hermaphrodite, resembling a dead man lying in the dark, requires fire.

<image-desc>An engraving shows a two-headed naked figure, a hermaphrodite, lying as if dead upon a wooden board or pallet. The pallet is propped up over a large, active fire with several burning logs. Thick clouds of smoke or steam billow up around the figure. The scene is set in a dark, hilly landscape under a crescent moon in the upper right sky.</image-desc>

## EPIGRAM XXXIII.

*Behold the two-headed one <note>Maier uses the rare term "illebiceps" to emphasize the singular nature of this dual-headed being.</note> of dual sex; like a corpse*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*It appears, once it is drained of all moisture:*
*If it is hidden in the shadows of the night, it requires fire;*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Provide this to it, and life returns in an instant.*
*All the power of the Stone <term>Lapis: the Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical work</term> lies hidden in the fire; all the power*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Of Sulfur is in gold, and the vigor of Mercury is in silver.*
