--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page provides the "Discursus" or formal commentary on Emblem XXXIII. Following the previous page’s depiction of the dual-sexed figure on a fire, Maier now provides the scientific and philosophical justification for using heat to bring life to "dead" matter, drawing parallels between nature, classical myth, and alchemical theory.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXXIII.

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXIII.</header>

It is among the secrets of nature that, as winter approaches, frogs and swallows, submerged in the waters, lie as if dead, and then in the spring, by the operation of the Sun’s new heat, they recover their senses and movement, or rather, they carry out the actions of their sensitive life. <note>Maier refers to the early modern belief that certain animals did not migrate but spent the winter in a state of "suspended animation" or "spontaneous generation" in the mud.</note> Indeed, if these same creatures are found during the winter time and brought into the air or into a warm room <term>hypocaustum: a room heated from below, common in Roman and Renaissance baths or homes</term>, they soon begin to move just as they do in summer. From this it appears that they lack nothing but external heat, which excites the internal natural heat shut within them and brings it into action. 

In the same way, the Philosophers speak of their **Hermaphrodite** <note>In alchemy, the Hermaphrodite represents the union of the "masculine" Sulfur and "feminine" Mercury.</note>, who, if he appears to be lying dead in the darkness, requires the heat of fire. He is said to lie in darkness while he is left through the dark and coldest night of winter; that is, he remains in the **blackness** <note>original: "nigredine" — the *Nigredo* stage, or "the blacker than black," representing the first stage of the alchemical process, associated with decay and cold.</note>, which is a sign of coldness. From this state, he must be led to **whiteness** <note>original: "albedinem" — the *Albedo* stage, representing purification.</note> through a greater intensity of fire, and further still, through an even greater intensity, to **redness** <note>original: "rubedinem" — the *Rubedo* stage, the final perfection of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. For without heat, as Bodillus <note>A speaker in the *Turba Philosophorum*, an ancient and influential alchemical text.</note> says in the *Assembly* <note>original: "in turba" — referring to the *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers).</note>, nothing is generated; an "intense heat" in a bath causes things to perish, but if it be cold, it flees; yet if it be temperate, it becomes agreeable and pleasant to the body.

In the same work, Bonellus says: *All things that live, God so willing, also die. Therefore that nature, from which moisture has been taken away <note>original: "cui humiditas adepta est"</note>, when it is left through the nights, seems like a dead thing, and then that nature needs fire, until the body and its spirit be turned into earth, and then it becomes dust like a dead man in his tomb. These things being finished, God returns to it spirit and soul, and with all weakness removed, our nature is strengthened and improved.* It is necessary, therefore, to *burn that thing without fear*, etc. 

And so fire, which destroys all other things, constructs this one; it provides death to others, but life to this. The **Phoenix** is unique in being repaired by fire, renewed by flames, and emerging reborn from the ash. This Phoenix, known to the Philosophers alone, is burned and recalled to life—whatever others may dream about some bird or other, flying, existing nowhere, nor seen except in fables. 

The **Hermaphrodite** of whom the Philosophers speak is of a mixed nature, masculine and feminine, of which one passes into the other through the operation of heat. For the male is made from the female, which should not seem so strange in the work of the Philosophers, since many have been found to have changed their sex, if histories are to be believed. The Poets commemorate **Caeneus, Iphis, and Tiresias** <note>Characters from classical mythology famous for changing their gender.</note>, as Pontanus does in his work *On the Stars*: <note>referring to Giovanni Pontano's *Urania sive de Stellis*.</note>

<sig>X 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Pœniteat</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that heat is the catalyst that transforms the alchemical "Hermaphrodite" from a state of cold, black "death" (Nigredo) into the active, red life of the Philosopher's Stone, using the Phoenix and hibernating animals as natural parallels.</summary>
<keywords>Hermaphrodite, fire, Phoenix, Nigredo, Albedo, Rubedo, Turba Philosophorum, Bodillus, Bonellus, metamorphosis, gender change</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Discursus" (Discourse) for Emblem XXXIII. The previous page established that heat is the catalyst for life and transformation, moving the alchemical "Hermaphrodite" from the cold, dark state of Nigredo (blackness) toward the next stage of the Great Work. This page provides historical and mythological evidence for spontaneous or surgical gender changes as a metaphor for this alchemical refinement.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXXIII.

<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXIII.</header>

->Yet let them repent of their kind, and prefer to be *Caeneus*,
Or to pass entirely into a feminine form.<-
And *Ausonius*:
->*Caeneus* grieves, recalled to her ancient form.<-
<note>original: "Pœniteat generis tamen & se Cenea malint, / Aut in fœmineam penitùs transire figuram... Mœret in antiquam Ceneus revocata figuram." These verses refer to the myth of Caenis, a woman transformed into the invulnerable male warrior Caeneus by Poseidon, only to return to female form in the afterlife.</note>

Thus, during the consulship of *Licinius Crassus* and *Gaius Cassius Longinus* [108 BCE], a boy was produced from a virgin girl at Casinum. And *Licinius Mutianus*, according to *Pliny*, reports that he saw at Argos one *Ariston*, who had previously been named *Aristusa*; she had even been married, but soon a beard and virility emerged, and he took a wife. *Pliny* himself says he saw in Africa a citizen of Thysdrus, *Lucius Cossicius*, who was changed into a male on his very wedding day. That these things are true could be proven by many other examples if it were necessary. 

Indeed, it is certain that when heat is increased, the genital or male members are thrust out from the body. Since a female is much colder than a male, she keeps hidden on the inside those organs which a male carries on the outside, as the physicians say. <note>Maier is citing the common Galenic medical theory of the time, which viewed women as "inverted" or "incomplete" men who lacked the vital heat to project their reproductive organs outward.</note> Hence nature, being in doubt whether she should generate a male or a female, expressed the female externally, even though she had intended a male internally. For this reason, as heat increases with age and movement, the hidden members break forth and show themselves to be seen by all. 

In the same manner among the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to Alchemists.</note>, when heat is increased, the female becomes male; that is, the **Hermaphrodite** loses the feminine sex and becomes a strong and severe male, who possesses nothing of womanly softness or levity within him. 

Thus we have sometimes seen, by the industry of the surgical art of that most celebrated physician, *Gaspare Tagliacozzi of Bologna* <note>Tagliacozzi (1545–1599) was a pioneer in plastic and reconstructive surgery, famous for his work on reconstructing noses.</note>, a noble **Hermaphrodite** boy changed—or rather promoted—into a perfect male, fit (as was hoped) for procreating offspring. This was achieved by making a new opening through the penis (which was lacking there) and closing the lower, woman-like opening. 

The Philosophers, however, do not require these manual operations. For when the coldness and moisture of the **Moon** <note>The alchemical symbol for Silver and the "Feminine" principle.</note> are present, they call it the female; when the heat and dryness of the **Sun** <note>The alchemical symbol for Gold and the "Masculine" principle.</note> are present, they call it the male. When all four of those qualities are present together, they call it the **Rebis** <note>From the Latin *res bina*, meaning "a double thing." A synonym for the Hermaphrodite, representing the union of opposites.</note> or the **Hermaphrodite**. And thus the conversion of the female (that is, of coldness and moisture) into the male can easily be accomplished, which is done by the heat of fire alone, as has been said. For heat sequesters and separates the superfluities of moisture and establishes its own "idea" in the Philosophical subject, which is the **tincture**. <term>tincture: In alchemy, a potent substance or "medicine" capable of transmuting base metals into gold or "healing" the imperfect into the perfect.</term>

<meta>The catchword "Im" refers to the first word of the next page, likely "Immo" or "Imprimis."</meta>

<summary>Maier uses historical accounts of biological sex changes and the surgical work of Tagliacozzi to argue that heat can transform the alchemical "female" (cold/moist) into the "male" (hot/dry). This process culminates in the creation of the Rebis or "Tincture," the ultimate goal of the alchemical work.</summary>

<keywords>Hermaphrodite, Rebis, Pliny the Elder, Gaspare Tagliacozzi, sexual transformation, alchemical heat, Sun and Moon, Tincture, Caenis/Caeneus, Galenic medicine</keywords>

> He was born, and red he goes over the floods of water;

> Is accustomed to be a pleasant and singular delight of the heart;
> It is a stone, and yet it is not—what heavenly and noble gifts!
> Fortunate is he who shall have it as a gift from God.

<header>EMBLEM XXXIV. On the secrets of Nature.</header>
<sig>T</sig>

## He is conceived in the baths, and born in the air; but having become red, he walks upon the waters.

<image-desc>An alchemical engraving depicting various stages of a process. In the foreground, within a body of water, two figures representing the Sun and the Moon (Sol and Luna) are shown embracing; the figure on the left has a sunburst head, and the figure on the right has a crescent moon face. To the left, a naked figure is depicted running across the surface of the water toward a rocky shore. In the upper left, amidst thick clouds, another naked human figure lies in a reclining position. The background features a rugged landscape with cliffs, a few trees, and a distant city on the horizon.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XXXIV.

> Baths shine for the conception of the boy, and the air for his birth,
>     and from there, as a red man, he beholds the waters beneath his feet. <note>original: "Balnea conceptu pueri, natalibus aër Splendet, & hinc rubeus sub pede cernit aquas."</note>
> And he becomes white upon the mountain peaks,
>     he who remains the sole care of learned men. <note>original: "Fitque super montana cacumina candidus ille, Qui remanet doctis unica cura viris." The "learned men" refers to the Alchemists seeking the Stone.</note>
> It is a stone, and it is not—a noble gift of heaven;
>     blessed will he be, if any man shall possess it by the gift of God. <note>original: "Est lapis, & non est, cœli quod nobile Donum, Dante DEO fælix, si quis habebit, erit." This "stone that is not a stone" is a classic alchemical riddle describing the Philosopher's Stone as a spiritual substance in material form.</note>

<term>Alchemy</term> <note>The medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on the transformation of matter and the soul.</note>
<term>Sol and Luna</term> <note>Sun and Moon; representing the masculine (sulfur) and feminine (mercury) principles whose union creates the Stone.</note>
<term>Lapis Philosophorum</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the "Great Work."</note>

## XXXIV. English version of the Epigram.
<note>Translated from the original Latin and the German fragment provided.</note>

> His conception took place in a bath of water, but in the air
> He was born, and red he goes over the floods of water;
> He also becomes white on the heights of the mountains,
> He who is the only treasure and work of the wise.
> He is a stone, and yet no stone, a high gift of God's grace,
> Whosoever receives it from God’s hand is in a happy state.

<meta>catchword: For</meta>

<summary>This page presents Emblem XXXIV, illustrating the elemental transitions—water, air, and earth—required to create the Philosopher's Stone. It uses the metaphor of a "child" who is conceived in water and eventually masters all elements to become a "noble gift of heaven."</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Rubedo, Albedo, Sol and Luna, Elemental Transformation, Michael Maier, Alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem XXXIV. The previous page introduced the image and epigram of the "child" (the alchemical Stone) who is born in the air and walks on water. This discourse expands on the metaphorical nature of this birth, contrasting it with the legendary origins of historical figures and explaining the technical laboratory processes—such as distillation and putrefaction—that these metaphors represent.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXIV</header>

Human opinion or flattery has attributed miraculous, or certainly fabulous, origins to certain individuals above the rest. For instance, it was said of Alexander the Great that he was not sired by Philip, King of the Macedonians, but by Jupiter Ammon; and that Romulus and Remus were born of Mars; and that Plato was born of the virgin Perictione, who had been overpowered by a phantom of Apollo. Thus did the pagans <note>original: "Ethnici"</note> wish to demonstrate that they descended from the gods. In the same way, Thessalus, the son of the physician Hippocrates, attempted to persuade the Athenians, among others, that he derived his lineage from Apollo.

But we are not so gullible. We know that those to whom they trace their family origins were neither humans nor gods. If such "divine heroes" truly existed among mortals, we realize these stories were merely fictions—insinuated into the public mind through forged writings and the flattery of subjects, disciples, or others who peddled their own greatness to the world.

It is a different matter, however, when the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the Alchemists of old.</note> attribute an unusual conception and birth to their "son." For this son has something peculiar above all others born in the world: his conception takes place in the baths, and his birth in the air. We know that sterile women can be greatly aided by warm baths to overcome excessive coldness and dryness, making them fit and ready for conception; but that a conception should actually *happen* in the baths, or be completed there, is unheard of. This seems to apply to him alone, because of the power of his miraculous nature, which is far different from others. 

Elsewhere, they say that his conception occurs in the bottom of the vessel and his birth in the <term>alembic</term> <note>The "alembic" is the upper part of a distilling apparatus, where vapors condense.</note>. This explanation is more clear: for the waters of the baths, if any are present, will not be at the top or middle of the vessel, but at the bottom, while in the alembic there are "airy" vapors. Thus, where the conception took place, it then ascends into the alembic, and his birth occurs in the "white color." 

In the bottom, blackness dominates, concerning which the *Rosary* <note>original: "Rosarius," referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> says: "Conception," it says, "is when the earth is dissolved into black powder and begins to retain a little bit of the mercury; for then the male acts upon the female—that is, the <term>azoth</term> <note>Azoth is a term for the "universal solvent" or the essential mercury sought by alchemists.</note> acts upon the earth." And a little later: "Conception and betrothal take place in the <term>putrefaction</term> <note>The stage of "death" or decomposition in the alchemical process, often associated with the color black.</note> at the bottom of the vessel, and the generation of the offspring will take place in the air—namely, in the head of the vessel, which is the alembic." And this conception in the baths is nothing other than putrefaction in dung <note>original: "in fimo," referring to the use of rotting horse manure as a source of steady, gentle heat.</note>; for the same *Rosary* continues: "The body does nothing unless it putrefies, and it cannot putrefy except with mercury." And soon [the] pu-

<meta>catchword: trefaction</meta>

<summary>This discourse contrasts the mythical, divine lineages claimed by historical figures with the "miraculous" birth of the alchemical Stone. It explains that the Stone's conception in baths and birth in the air are metaphors for the chemical processes of putrefaction at the bottom of a flask and distillation into the alembic.</summary>

<keywords>Alexander the Great, Jupiter Ammon, Plato, Hippocrates, Alchemical Son, alembic, Rosarium Philosophorum, azoth, putrefaction, distillation, nigredo</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and mythological explanation of Emblem XXXIV. The previous page ended with the discussion of how warm baths prepare the "mother" for conception. The text here begins mid-word with the completion of the process: "pu-trefactio" (putrefaction). It transitions from the physical conditions of the laboratory to the symbolic interpretation of the apparatus as a landscape of mountains and seas.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXIV.</header>

...putrefaction <note>The alchemical stage of decomposition or "nigredo," where the matter turns black.</note> may be achieved by the slowest fire of warm and moist dung, and by no other, so that nothing ascends. For if anything should ascend, a separation of the parts would occur, which ought not to happen until the male and female are perfectly joined, and one receives the other; the sign of this is the blackness of perfect solution appearing on the surface. Its birth is white, which takes place on the "peaks of the mountains," that is, in the air or the alembic <note>The cap or "head" of a distilling apparatus.</note>. This is explained by Rosinus <note>A Latinized name for Zosimos of Panopolis, an early alchemist.</note> in his writing to Euthicia as follows: The wise man said: "Take things from their mines and exalt them to higher places, and cast them from the peak of their mountains and lead them back to their roots." And later: "By 'mountains' he signified the cucurbits <note>The gourd-shaped bottom flask of a still.</note>, and by the 'peaks of the mountains' the alembics. To 'cast down,' according to this likeness, is to receive their water through the alembic into the receiver; but to 'lead back to the roots' is to return it upon that from which it proceeded." He called the cucurbits "mountains" because gold and silver <note>original: "sol & luna"</note> are found in mountains; so also in these mountains, which are the cucurbits, their own Sun and Moon are generated. Thus he spoke.

Afterward, it becomes red and begins to "go upon the waters," that is, upon the metals liquefied in the fire, which stand like mercurial water. For this is the lord of the waters, over which he exercises power like Neptune; he is the king of the sea and the possessor of the mountains. They tell of Xerxes, King of the Persians, that when he was about to set out on an expedition into Greece, he sent an embassy to the sea and to Mount Athos, ordering them not to do him injury—the one by its waves, the other by its heat of flames—otherwise he would take punishment upon both. But the story was told to deaf ears: the sea submerged some of his ships, and Athos destroyed many by fire. Hence the angry king, as if he were the lord of the sea and mountains, ordered a certain number of lashes to be inflicted on the sea, and threw a great part of the mountain into the sea. But these things demonstrate the audacity rather than the wisdom of such a great king. 

The "son" of whom we speak, however, purges all waters of obstacles and impurities not by decree, but by deed. He walks freely through them, and—what is more—he congeals them, so that they support chariots by their hardness, though they previously bore ships. He himself levels mountains with valleys and does not fear the flames of fire; therefore, he travels freely wherever he wishes, from the Pillars of Hercules <note>The Strait of Gibraltar, the edge of the known Western world.</note> to the furthest shore of India at the Pillars of Dionysus <note>Legendary markers at the edge of the Eastern world.</note>.

<sig>T ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Wie [This German catchword suggests the following page contains the German translation of this discourse.]</meta>

<summary>This page explains the laboratory stages of putrefaction and distillation, using the metaphor of mountains and peaks to describe alchemical vessels (cucurbits and alembics). It contrasts the true power of the Alchemical Stone over the elements with the foolish, failed attempts of King Xerxes to command nature.</summary>

> Look upon Triptolemus <note>In myth, the goddess Ceres (Demeter) placed the infant Triptolemus in the fire by night to burn away his mortality and make him a god.</note> and Achilles <note>Thetis similarly placed her son Achilles in the fire to make him immortal, though in most versions she only succeeded in making him invulnerable, except for his heel.</note>, great-hearted in the fray,

<header>EMBLEM XXXV. On the Secrets of Nature.</header>

## Just as Ceres accustomed Triptolemus, and Thetis accustomed Achilles, to remain beneath the fire, so does the craftsman accustom the Stone.

<image-desc>An engraving illustrating the motto through mythological scenes of fire and infants. On the left, a seated woman (Ceres) holds an infant (Triptolemus) near tall stalks of wheat. In the center, another woman (Thetis) holds an infant (Achilles) by the foot over a large, crackling fire. In the foreground, a sword, a helmet, and other pieces of armor lie on the ground. The background features a rugged landscape with a town on a hill and a figure watching from the rocks above the fire.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XXXV.

> *Look upon Triptolemus, and Achilles, hardy in battle,* <note>original: "durúmque in prælio Achillem"</note>
> *as their mothers taught them how they might learn to endure grievous heat.*
> *The goddess Ceres hardened the former, and Thetis the latter, in the fire* <note>In Greek mythology, Ceres (Demeter) tried to make the infant Triptolemus immortal by placing him in the fire; Thetis did the same for her son Achilles to burn away his mortal parts.</note>
> *by night, while by day she offered breasts full of milk:*
> *In no different a way should the blessed medicine of the Sages* <note>original: "medicina beata Sophorum" — this refers to the Philosopher's Stone in its healing or transformative capacity.</note> *become accustomed,*
> *than a child to the breast, so that it may be able to enjoy the fire.*

<vocab><term>Ceres</term> The Roman goddess of agriculture and grain; <term>Triptolemus</term> the youth Ceres taught to use the plow; <term>Thetis</term> a sea nymph and mother of Achilles; <term>Achilles</term> the legendary Greek hero; <term>fire</term> <note>original: "ignis"</note> the primary tool for transformation in the alchemical laboratory; <term>stone</term> <note>original: "lapis"</note> the Philosopher's Stone; <term>medicine of the Sages</term> <note>original: "medicina Sophorum"</note>; <term>craftsman</term> <note>original: "artifex"</note> the alchemist.</vocab>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page provides the prose discourse for Emblem XXXV, continuing the discussion of how the alchemical Stone must be "nurtured" through a gradual habituation to heat, just as the goddesses Ceres and Thetis tempered their charges in fire.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>

# DISCOURSE XXXV.

Lycurgus, the famous legislator of the Spartans, demonstrated to the people in a public assembly <note>original: "theatrali concione" — literally a theatrical or public gathering</note> how much power habit and custom possess—whether for good or ill—by a visual example. He brought forward two puppies born of the same litter. In their midst, he placed a pot full of porridge and a hare. One of the puppies, seeing the food and the hare, left the porridge and chased the animal because he had been trained to do so. The other, however, ignored the hare and emptied the pot, as he had been accustomed to that. When this was done, Lycurgus said, "Behold, here you see how much education and habituation from a young age matters, even in those whom nature produced as equals and very similar." In this way, it is proper to correct nature or direct it toward better things, for nature is like wax, <note>original: "caerea est" — meaning malleable or impressionable</note> easily bent toward either vice or virtue.

What Lycurgus proved true in politics, the Philosophers also agree holds true in the Physical sciences. Daily examples are seen throughout the world of how much habituation matters in humans and beasts. Not a few examples also occur in the world of plants. Yet, to find the same occurring in minerals and metals is an experience of greater rarity. Nevertheless, the practitioners of the art "fix" <note>To "fix" a substance in alchemy means to make a volatile substance solid and resistant to fire.</note> their Stone through habituation in a fire suitable to it, just as they hint in countless places. For it must be nourished by fire, just as a child is nourished by milk at its mother’s breast. Whence Emiganus <note>An alchemical authority, likely a corruption of a name like Magnes or an Arabic source.</note> says: *Look upon the suckling child, and do not hinder him.* And Bodillus says: *The offspring, once extracted, is nourished only by milk, and by fire for its own sake, gradually while it is small; and the more it is burned, its bones being strengthened, it is led into youth, and reaching that stage, it is sufficient unto itself.* And Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> in the *Rosary*, Book 2, Chapter 7, says: *It is necessary, however, for the medicine itself to be roasted longer over the fire and nourished like a child at the breast.*

The most ancient Philosophers wished to demonstrate these same things in the allegories of Triptolemus and Achilles, who were accustomed to lie beneath the fire and were hardened by it. Both of these figures denote nothing other than the <term>chemical subject</term> <note>The "subject" is the base material or "prima materia" used at the start of the Great Work.</note>; otherwise, the fable would be tasteless and unworthy of being adapted to moral matters or drummed into the ears of learned men. Ceres, acting as a nurse, nourished Triptolemus by day with milk, but by night she placed him beneath the fires. Because the boy was being nourished so excellently, his father, Eleusius, eventually observed this. For this reason, Ceres killed Eleusius and gave the boy Triptolemus a chariot drawn by serpents <note>original: "anguib. tractum" — drawn by snakes or dragons.</note>...

<meta>The text continues on the next page describing Triptolemus's journey.</meta>

<summary>This discourse uses the Spartan legend of Lycurgus's dogs to argue that "habituation" (training/nurturing) is as powerful as nature. In alchemy, this serves as an allegory for the gradual "fixing" of the Stone through increasing stages of fire, likened to the mythological tempering of Triptolemus and Achilles.</summary>

<keywords>Lycurgus, Sparta, habituation, education, nature vs nurture, fixing the stone, alchemical fire, Triptolemus, Achilles, Ceres, Arnold of Villanova, Rosarium Philosophorum, chemical subject</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXXV, explaining the mythological and symbolic figures who represent the gradual "nourishing" or tempering of the Philosophical Stone through heat and habituation.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>

### DISCOURSE XXXV.

...by which he [Triptolemus] hastened through the air to all parts of the world and taught mortals the sowing of crops. But this Triptolemus is the Philosophical Tincture <note>The "tincture" is the refined substance of the Alchemical Stone, capable of transforming or "dyeing" other metals into gold.</note> nurtured under the fire in such a way that, drawn by serpents—that is, by Mercury—he taught men how the Philosophical seeds must be cast into their own earth. These same feats are attributed to Osiris, who for this reason traveled across the globe (as we have shown elsewhere), and to Dionysus, who wandered through the world teaching men the use of wine. In truth, these three—Triptolemus, Osiris, and Dionysus—share a single purpose and office; indeed, they are one and the same thing. 

The same is true of Achilles, who was to be sent as the strongest warrior to the Trojan War. His father was Peleus—that is, the earth, or Mount Pelion—and his mother was Thetis, a goddess of the sea or of the waters; from these two, Achilles is born. At their wedding, the golden apple of Eris <note>Eris is the Greek goddess of Discord; her "apple of discord" eventually sparked the Trojan War.</note> was brought forth, which was the primary cause of the Trojan War; thus, having been born from that marriage, he emerged not without reason as the war's executor. Achilles is said to have been hardened by his mother in the same way as the aforementioned Triptolemus was by Ceres <note>In myth, Ceres (Demeter) tried to make the infant Triptolemus immortal by placing him in the fire at night to burn away his mortality; Thetis did the same for Achilles.</note>. We have said enough concerning this in the sixth book of our *Hieroglyphics*, so we deem it superfluous to repeat it here.

Therefore, the nourishment of the Stone is fire; yet it is not from this that the Stone is extended in length, width, or depth, nor does it increase in weight. Rather, it acquires at least its power, maturation, and color from the fire, bringing all other things with it like provisions and travel supplies. For when its parts have been gathered from various places, purified, and joined together, it possesses within itself everything it requires. Hence, a Philosopher says in the *Rosary of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Rosarium Philosophorum" — one of the most famous anthologies of alchemical texts, first published in 1550.</note>:

->*This water carries with it all things which the offspring needs.*<-
<note>original: "Omnia fert secum, quibus indiget haec aqua foetum."</note>

Indeed, nothing foreign is added to it from the beginning to the end, unless it is of the same kind, nor is anything separated from it, unless it is of a different kind. Moreover, anyone ought to take care to recognize the dragons that must be yoked to the chariot of Triptolemus before they begin anything. These dragons are winged and volatile; if you wish to know them, you will find them in the "Philosophical dung" <note>original: "fimo Philosophico" — this refers to a source of gentle, steady heat, like a composting heap or a specialized water bath, used to slowly "digest" alchemical materials.</note>. For they are dung, and are generated from dung, and they are that vessel which Maria <note>Maria Prophetissa, or Mary the Prophetess, a foundational figure in early alchemy to whom the invention of the *bain-marie* (water bath) is attributed.</note> says is not "necromantic," but is the regulation of your fire, without which you will achieve nothing. I have opened the truth to you, which I have drawn with incredible labor and at the expense of many years from the monuments of the ancients.

<vocab>Triptolemus, Osiris, Dionysus, Achilles, Peleus, Thetis, Rosarium Philosophorum, Maria Prophetissa, fimus philosophicus, lapis philosophorum, Mercurius</vocab>

<summary>This discourse identifies mythological figures like Triptolemus and Achilles as symbols for the alchemical process of "fixing" the Stone through fire. It emphasizes that the Stone contains all its own necessary components and requires only the proper regulation of heat (the "dragons" found in the philosophical dung) to reach maturation.</summary>
<keywords>Tincture, Mercury, Fire as nourishment, internal maturation, Mary the Prophetess, regulation of heat, mythological alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the discourse on the nourishment of the "Philosophical child" (Achilles/Triptolemus) to Emblem XXXVI, which focuses on the ubiquitous yet hidden nature of the Alchemical Stone, identifying it with Mercury and describing its presence in all elements.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>
->FUGUE XXXVI. in the 7th below.<-

## This Stone is cast down upon the Earth, and raised up on the Mountains; it dwells in the Air, and is nourished in Rivers—that is, Mercury.

<image-desc>Three sets of musical staves for a three-voice fugue. The voices are labeled in the left margin. The notation is in mensural style with diamond-shaped notes and C-clefs. Latin lyrics are printed beneath each part.</image-desc>

<margin>Atalanta fleeing.</margin>
The Stone is said to be a vile dross <note>original: "Vile recrementum"</note> and to lie by chance in the paths, so that the rich and the poor alike may obtain it for themselves from there.

<margin>Hippomenes following.</margin>
The Stone is said to be a vile dross and to lie by chance in the paths, so that the rich and the poor alike may obtain it for themselves from there.

<margin>The delaying Apple.</margin>
The Stone is said to be a vile dross and to lie by chance in the paths, so that the rich and the poor alike may obtain it for themselves from there.

### XXXVI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

It is said that the Stone is a rejected and base thing,
  Lying in the road so that rich and poor may rightfully possess it.
Others say that it is to be seen on high mountains,
  Or in the air; others say that it nourishes itself in the floodplains <note>original: "Awen," referring to water-meadows or riparian lands.</note>.
All is true, according to its proper understanding; I wish for you to perceive
  Such a gift upon the mountains, where it is most easily found.

<meta>catchword: EMBLEM-</meta>

<summary>Emblem XXXVI describes the Philosopher's Stone (identified as Mercury) as a substance that is simultaneously considered worthless dross and the highest treasure, found everywhere from mountain peaks to common roads.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, elements, Atalanta, Hippomenes, dross, mountain, air, water</keywords>

<header>EMBLEM XXXVI. Concerning the secrets of Nature.</header>
<sig>AB</sig>
<meta>catchword: V (referring to the first letter of the following page's discourse)</meta>

## The Stone is cast forth upon the earth, and exalted on the mountains, and it dwells in the air, and feeds in the river—that is, Mercury.

---

<image-desc>A copperplate engraving of a landscape. In the foreground, two men in period attire walk along a path littered with rectangular stone blocks; one man gestures toward the stones. A river flows through the center of the image, with a small boat and more stone blocks floating in the water. In the sky, three additional stones are depicted as if falling or floating. The background features rolling hills and two small towns, each with a prominent church steeple.</image-desc>

---

### EPIGRAM XXXVI.

> The **STONE** <note>original: "LAPIS"</note> is said to be a vile dross <note>original: "recrementum"; this refers to the "First Matter" of alchemy, which is often described as something common or filthy that people throw away.</note> and to lie
> By chance in the roads, so that from here the rich and poor alike may provide for themselves.
> Others have placed it on the highest mountains, or through the breezes
> Of the air; while others say it is fed in the rivers.
> All these things are true in their proper sense, but I urge you
> To seek such great gifts in mountainous places.

<vocab>Mercury <note>The transformative principle in alchemy, not just the liquid metal.</note>, The Stone <note>The goal of the alchemical work, representing perfection and immortality.</note>, Nature, Epigram, secrets, mountains, exalted, dross <note>recrementum: waste material or slag.</note></vocab>

<summary>Emblem XXXVI identifies the Philosopher's Stone with "Mercury" and describes its paradoxical nature as a substance that is both common enough to be found in the streets and noble enough to dwell in the heavens and mountains.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Mercury, four elements, alchemy, nature's secrets, omnipresence, First Matter</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page begins the Discourse (Discursus) for Emblem XXXVI. It follows the previous page's introduction of the emblem's motto and image, which described the Stone (Mercury) as being present in the earth, mountains, air, and water. This discourse expands on the philosophical and allegorical locations where the alchemical "First Matter" can be found, citing various classical and medieval authorities.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXVI.</header>

By all those who have heard the name and power of the stone <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the ultimate goal of alchemy, capable of transmuting metals and granting long life.</note> even once—unless they are among the utterly incredulous—the question is usually asked: where is it to be found, so that they might run toward it, as if on a straight path? The Philosophers respond in two ways: First, that Adam brought it with him out of Paradise, that it is in you, in me, and in every human being, and that birds carry it with them from distant places. Second, that it is found in the earth, the mountains, the air, and the river. Which path, then, should be followed? This one or that? Both, in my opinion, but each in its own respect; although this latter path <note>The physical search in the elements.</note> pleases us more and seems more secure.

It is said to be "cast forth upon the earth" because the element of earth first appears in the dark and black body <note>This refers to the *nigredo*, or "blackening," the first stage of the alchemical process where the material must decay or "die."</note>. Furthermore, because it is a vile thing of little price, which is trodden underfoot in the paths of travelers and in the very dung itself. Hence the *Rosary* <note>original: "Rosarius"; likely referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical compendium.</note> says: "And if I were to call it by its true name, the foolish would not believe it to be so." And Morienus <note>A legendary hermit-alchemist said to have taught the secrets of the art to the Arab king Khalid ibn Yazid.</note>, responding to Calid’s question of whether much of it could be found, said: "This is only, as the wise man says, for the rich and the poor, for the generous and the miser, for the traveler and the one sitting still. For this is cast forth in the streets and trodden in its own dung-heaps, and many have already dug in dung-heaps to extract it, and in this they have been deceived."

Mundus <note>A character/philosopher within the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the earliest Latin translations of Arabic alchemical texts.</note> also says in the *Assembly*: "If the sellers knew what it was, they would not sell it at such a cheap price." And Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> asserts that the stone can be had for free, in as much quantity as anyone wants, nor should anyone be asked about it for that reason. All of which things are true. For who, unless they are inhuman, would deny earth and water to one who asks for them?

When the ancient Cimbri <note>A Germanic-Celtic tribe that fought the Roman Republic in the late 2nd century BCE.</note> asked for these same gifts <note>Historically, "earth and water" were traditional tokens of submission or requests for land to settle.</note> from the Romans and could not obtain them, they poured into Italy with their forces and slaughtered many thousands of Romans along with their Consuls, as histories testify. For Earth, as the mother of all things, is most precious; yet as the final matter of things that have decayed, it is most vile. Nothing is viler than mud or filth, which is nevertheless nothing other than earth mixed with water. What is more common than a clod of earth? Yet Eurypylus, son of Neptune, offered one as a <term>guest-gift</term> <note>original: "xenio"; from the Greek *xenion*, a gift given by a host to a guest.</note> to the Heroic Argonauts; after it was not refused but accepted with a grateful heart, and later dissolved in water, Medea prophesied many things from it. For it is necessary that the earth be dissolved in water; otherwise, neither the one nor the other is of any value. In this way, the stone is "cast forth upon the earth," in which, however, it does not remain cast away,

<meta>The text continues on the next page, beginning with the word "but" (sed).</meta>

<summary>This discourse explores the paradox of the Philosopher's Stone: though it is the most precious substance, it is found in the most common and "vile" places, such as dirt, dung, and the human body. Maier cites various alchemical authorities and Greek myths to emphasize that the "Earth" of the stone must be dissolved in "Water" to be effective.</summary>

<keywords>First Matter, Adam, Paradise, Rosarium Philosophorum, Morienus, Khalid ibn Yazid, Turba Philosophorum, Arnold of Villanova, Cimbri, Argonauts, Medea, earth and water, nigredo</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XXXVI, further detailing the symbolic and physical locations of the alchemical Mercury. It connects the "Stone" to volcanic mountains, the four elements, and the mythological attributes of the god Mercury, arguing that the substance sought by alchemists is omnipresent and foundational to ancient civilization.</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>
### DISCOURSE XXXVI.

...but it is raised up to the mountains, such as *Athos*, *Vesuvius*, *Etna*, and other similar flame-vomiting peaks, many of which are seen in various parts of the world. For in these a perpetual fire burns, which sublimates the stone and carries it up to its highest dignity. In the mountains, just as it grows in a crude form from sulfur and quicksilver <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the archaic term for mercury</term>, so it is ripened and perfected at the summit of the mountains. There, an herb also grows, without which the fire cannot be tempered; for when this cold and moist herb is cast into the fire, its violence is blunted by its opposite. From the mountains, it passes to the air, where it finds a dwelling. For the air becomes its house, by which it is surrounded; this is nothing other than being "carried in the belly of the wind" and "born in the air," terms about which we have spoken previously. <note>These phrases are famous lines from the *Emerald Tablet*, a foundational Hermetic text.</note> Finally, it is fed in the rivers—that is, in the waters—this *Mercury*. Hence, in his honor, the *Athenians* celebrated the *Hydrophoria* <note>A Greek festival involving the pouring of water, here interpreted as an alchemical allegory.</note>, because the material of the Philosophers' Stone is water, as the *Rosary* <note>original: "Rosarius"; the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a key alchemical text.</note> says, and this is understood as the water of those three elements.

For this reason, *Mercury* was also called "triple-headed," being at once marine, celestial, and terrestrial, because he was present in the water, the earth, and the air. He is said to have been raised by *Vulcan* <note>The Roman god of fire and craftsmanship.</note> and is famous for his thievery; this is because *Mercury* is taught to become accustomed to the fire, yet he is volatile and carries away with him whatever he is mixed with. In ancient times, he dictated laws and discipline to the *Egyptians*, and indeed to the *Theban* priests and a great part of the world, he dictated religion. This is because from chemical <term>chymicis: in this context, referring to alchemical or Hermetic secrets</term> principles the *Egyptians* derived their governance and sacred matters, and from them the *Greeks*, from them the *Romans*, and from those many other nations, as has been demonstrated more extensively elsewhere.

He slew *Argus* with a rock—or stone—and he turned *Battus* into a touchstone. <note>In mythology, Mercury used a stone to kill the hundred-eyed giant Argus and transformed the treacherous shepherd Battus into a "stone of index" or touchstone used to test the purity of gold.</note> Why say more? All the volumes of the chemists emphasize nothing but *Mercury*, and they sufficiently confirm its power with this single little verse: *Whatever the wise seek is in Mercury.* This, therefore, must be sought until it is found, whether it lingers in the air, fire, water, or earth. For he is a wanderer, running now here, now there, in the service of the chemical gods, as if at their feet—an office indicated by the fact that some ascribe to him a daughter named *Angelia* <note>original: "Angelia," the Greek word for 'message' or 'tidings'.</note>.

V ij

<meta>catchword: Three (original: "Drey")</meta>

<summary>This page explains the elemental journey of Mercury through fire, air, and water, linking alchemical processes to the mythology of the god Mercury and the origins of ancient Egyptian religion.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Vulcan, sulfur, quicksilver, four elements, Egyptian religion, Argus, Battus, alchemy, sublimation</keywords>

# EMBLEM XXXVII. Concerning the secrets of Nature.

V 3

<meta>catchword: AS</meta>

->Three things suffice for the mastery: white smoke—that is, water; the green lion—that is, the copper of Hermes <term>æs Hermetis: often interpreted as a symbolic "philosophical" metal or the base material of the work</term>; and stinking water.<-

<image-desc>A detailed engraving featuring a majestic lion standing in a landscape, adorned with a laurel wreath upon its head. In the background to the left, a large volcano (possibly Etna) erupts, sending a thick cloud of smoke into the sky. To the right, the landscape features rolling hills, sparse trees, and a stone fortress or complex of buildings perched on a hilltop.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XXXVII.

*There are three seeds of the mastery: the stinking Water,* <note>original: "fœtida Lympha," likely referring to the foul-smelling "First Matter" or crude mercurial water</note>
  *And the snowy vapor, and the Lion with the green hide:*
*The parent wave <note>water</note> brought forth the remaining elements; and for the Sages,* <note>original: "Sophis," referring to the practitioners of the Hermetic art</note>
  *So that they might make the stone, it is both the last and the first.*
*But the Green Lion is the copper of Hermes, and the stone known*
  *From the chapters of the books; and the white smoke is water.*

<summary>This page defines the three essential components required for the alchemical "Mastery": white smoke (water), the green lion (Hermetic copper), and stinking water. It frames water as the primary element and the "parent" from which the Philosopher's Stone is ultimately produced.</summary>

<keywords>mastery, white smoke, green lion, copper of Hermes, stinking water, seeds of alchemy, elements, Philosopher's Stone</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem XXXVII, which introduced the three essential "seeds" of the alchemical work: white smoke, the green lion, and stinking water. Having previously defined these terms in the epigram, the text now expands on the first of these—the stinking water—comparing its role to the foundation of a building.</meta>

<header>DISCOURSE XXXVII.</header>

Just as three essentials are required for the construction of any building, without any one of which no perfection can exist—namely, the foundation, the walls, and the roof—so too are three things required to complete the "composition" of the Philosophers, which are named here by their proper titles. The *Author of the Aurora* <note>original: "Author Auroræ," referring to the *Aurora Consurgens*, a famous 15th-century alchemical manuscript</note>, speaking in chapter 20 about the separation of the elements, says: "Earth is left behind in that same place, so that the other three elements may take root in it; if Earth itself were not there, the elements would have no foundation upon which to build the new Treasure-house." This foundation is called here the **stinking water**, which is the mother of all elements, according to the *Rosary* <note>original: "Rosario," likely the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a key alchemical florilegium</note>, from which, through which, and with which the Philosophers prepare the <term>Elixir: the "medicine" or "stone" that perfects base metals into gold</term> at the beginning and at the end.

It is called "stinking" because it gives off a sulfurous stench and the odor of graves. This is that water which **Pegasus** struck from **Parnassus** with his hoof; the water which Mount **Nonacris** in **Arcadia** sends forth from its ridges, breaking out from the rock, which can only be held in a horse’s hoof because of its most powerful force. <note>Maier is referencing the legendary River Styx, which was said to flow from Mount Nonacris; ancient myth claimed its water was so corrosive it would dissolve any vessel except one made from the hoof of a horse or mule.</note> This is the **dragon’s water**, as the *Rosary* names it, which must be produced by an <term>alembic: a distillation apparatus</term> without any other substance being added, and in the making of which a great stench is produced. 

When certain people heard these words, they turned themselves to distilling human or animal dung; in that operation they certainly sensed a great stench, but they found only dung within dung. But lest you think the Philosophers are **beetles** <note>original: "scarabæos," a common alchemical metaphor for "puffers" or false alchemists who work with foul, literal materials</note> who labor in filth, you should know that the stench, if any be present, is soon changed into a great fragrance. This is as **Lull** <note>Ramon Lull, a 13th-century polymath to whom many alchemical works were attributed</note> testifies concerning his *Quinta Essentia* <note>The "Fifth Essence" or quintessential spirit</note>, to which he assigns such sweetness of odor (if it be rightly made) that if placed in an upper room of a house, it would lure flying birds to it and make them stop. He places his quintessence in dung, however, so that by its most tempered heat that fragrance may follow. Some have attempted this with strong wine, but in vain, and they therefore accused Lull of vanity—though they themselves should rather be convicted of folly, having never tasted Lull's "wine." But that golden poet understood Lull better, singing thus in the first book of his *Chrysopoeia* <note>original: "Chrysopœia," an alchemical poem by Giovanni Aurelio Augurello, published in 1515</note>:

> ->*But indeed, he did not mean what he seemed to say*<-
> ->*At first glance; nor was he then mixing*<-
> ->*Trickling wines, etc.*<-

<meta>catchword: After</meta>

<summary>This discourse identifies "stinking water" as the vital foundation of the alchemical Work, comparing it to the base of a building. Maier warns readers not to take the term literally (as dung), but to understand it as a potent, transformative substance that eventually yields a heavenly fragrance.</summary>

<keywords>stinking water, foundation, Aurora Consurgens, Rosarium Philosophorum, Pegasus, Styx, distillation, Ramon Lull, quintessence, Chrysopoeia</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Discourse XXXVII, moving from the first essential (the "stinking water") to the second and third: the Green Lion and the White Smoke. It expands on the alchemical symbolism of the color green as a sign of life and potential.</meta>

<header>DISCOURSE XXXVII.</header>

After that stinking water, the **Green Lion** appears. Regarding this, the *Rosary* <note>original: "Rosarius," referring to the *Rosary of the Philosophers*</note> says: "You have asked about greenness, thinking that copper is a leprous body because of the greenness it possesses. But I tell you that everything perfect in copper is that greenness alone, which is within it. For that greenness, through our mastery, is quickly turned into our truest gold, and this we have experienced. Yet you can prepare no stone without the green and liquid <term>duenech: an Arabic-derived alchemical term often referring to antimony or green vitriol</term>, which is seen to be born in our mines. O blessed greenness, which brings forth all things! You should know that no vegetable or fruit appears in its budding without a green color. Similarly, know that the generation of this thing is green, which is why the Philosophers have called it the 'seed' itself."

Thus far the *Rosary*. This is the gold and the copper of the Philosophers and the "stone" known in the various chapters as smoke, vapor, water, and the **spittle of the moon** <note>original: "sputum lunæ"</note>, which is joined to the light of the Sun. Here the **Green Lion** fights with the dragon; but he is overcome by it and, in the fullness of time, is devoured. Once the lion has putrefied, it is hoped that sweetness will come forth from its mouth (just as it did from the lion slain by **Samson** <note>A reference to the biblical story in Judges 14, where honey was found in the carcass of a lion</note>). The Dragon, having become superior, so fills itself with the lion's flesh that it soon bursts and dies. From this—since the fat of a Lion by itself may heal daily fevers, and grace and favor may be won for those anointed with it before kings and peoples—a most excellent **Medicine** can be made, which is highly useful for many ailments.

Thirdly follows the **white smoke**, which, if it is coagulated, becomes water; and this water performs the duty of washing, dissolving, and wiping away stains like soap. This is the **fire against nature** <note>original: "ignis contra naturam"</note>. See that you find it; it is so named because it is contrary to nature by unweaving and destroying that which nature has composed with diligent care. This fire is not ignited from spirits of wine or oil, but from an incombustible matter of equal duration and heat. It is a fire without light or burning, yet of great power and efficacy. To find it in the darkness, since it does not shine, is of no small difficulty—but to apply it to the work in the proper manner is far greater. We have sufficiently described its circumstances and properties in various places.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page describes the "Green Lion" as the living, transformative force in alchemy, identifying greenness as the source of gold. It also introduces "white smoke" or "fire against nature," a lightless, solvent heat used to break down substances.</summary>

<keywords>Green Lion, Rosary of the Philosophers, duenech, spittle of the moon, Samson, white smoke, fire against nature, alchemy, medicine</keywords>

### *XXXVIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.*

The ancients gave the name Rebis to a double thing,
Because it is both man and woman together in one body.
For Hermaphroditus <note>In mythology, the son of Hermes (Mercury) and Aphrodite (Venus) who became physically merged with the nymph Salmacis</note> was chosen upon two mountains,
Whom Venus bore to Mercury;

For afterward it can give birth to the King <note>The "King" in alchemy represents the completed Philosopher's Stone or the perfection of gold</note> for you.

<image-desc>A detailed copperplate engraving showing an alchemical allegory. ## EPIGRAM XXXVIII.

The ancients called the **REBIS** a *double* thing, because in one
Body it becomes both male and female, an <term>Androgyne: a being possessing both masculine and feminine characteristics, symbolizing the union of opposites</term>.
For **HERMAPHRODITUS** <note>In mythology, the son of Hermes (Mercury) and Aphrodite (Venus) who became physically merged with a nymph; in alchemy, this represents the perfected "Stone"</note> is said to be born on two mountains,
Whom nourishing Venus bore to Hermes.
Do not despise this double sex, for a King to you
This same man and one and the same woman shall give.

## The Rebis is born like a Hermaphrodite from the two mountains of Mercury and Venus.

<margin>Atalanta Fleeing.</margin>

### *XXXVIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.*

The ancients gave the name Rebis to a double thing,
Because it is both man and woman together in one body.
For Hermaphroditus was born upon two mountains,
Whom Venus bore to Mercury;
Do not despise this double gender, which is both woman and man,
For afterward it can give you a King <note>The "King" refers to the Red King or the final, perfected state of the Alchemical Gold.</note>.

<summary>This page introduces Emblem XXXVIII, focusing on the Rebis or Hermaphrodite, an alchemical symbol for the union of the solar (male/Mercury) and lunar (female/Venus) principles. It explains that this dual-natured being is the source of the "King," or the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Rebis, Hermaphrodite, Mercury, Venus, Androgyne, Alchemical Marriage, Union of Opposites, Michael Maier</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Discourse XXXVIII. The previous page introduced the emblem and epigram, establishing the Rebis as a dual-natured being born from the union of Mercury and Venus. This discourse expands on the "homeland" and identity of this alchemical figure.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXXVIII.

**SOCRATES**, when asked from what country he came, replied that he was a **Cosmopolitan** <note>original: "Cosmopolitam"</note>, or a citizen of the world. By this he meant to imply that, although he was born at Athens in his body, in his mind he could freely travel through the whole world as his homeland and through all things contained within it; for to a wise man, every land where he may live well is his country. In the same way, if anyone should ask the Philosophers from what country their **Hermaphrodite** <note>a symbol of the union of opposites, often representing the alchemical Stone</note> comes, they reply that it is "of the world," or that it is available in every corner of the earth where the elements are found. It is the child of the wise, who share a common homeland with them. 

But since it does not happen that anyone is born twice or many times, nor in various places, but enters this light first in one specific location (just as Socrates is recognized as an Athenian), so the **REBIS** <note>from the Latin 'res bina', meaning a "double thing" or dual matter</note> is considered an inhabitant of two mountains: namely, those of **Mercury** and **Venus**. From these two parents, it was given the name **Hermaphroditus** <note>a name combining Hermes (Mercury) and Aphrodite (Venus)</note>. These are its ancestral hearths and its high homeland, and thus it emerges as one born from a high place. 

Indeed, a noble and grand homeland is of no small importance for the performing of great deeds; in such a place, citizens are preferred over foreigners and promoted to public offices so they do not remain hidden in obscurity (as happens in a lowly place). Thus, some light from their country may reach them. Yet it is a greater thing to emerge through one's own virtues, even from a narrow home, and to provide light to one's own country from oneself. In this manner, these mountains—unknown to many—acquire fame from the **Hermaphrodite** because of its illustrious deeds and its name, which is most celebrated throughout the whole world. 

For who, even slightly versed in the books of the Philosophers, has not recognized the **REBIS**? Who has not seen or noticed the two-headed **Androgyne** <note>a being possessing both masculine and feminine characteristics</note>? This figure is known even as far as the Indians, and its fame is spread further than that of King **Alexander**. Many people set out from distant shores to see and speak with some learned man, or one famous for singular military industry, art, or science. However, far more would travel to these mountains called **REBIS**, provided they knew where they were to be found. 

With what great zeal and care **Morienus** <note>a legendary alchemist said to have lived as a hermit near Jerusalem</note> set out from Rome to seek **Adfar the Alexandrian**, and finally found him, he himself testifies in his book. Therefore, he is to be judged the more fortunate, and favored by God, because he learned of this thing—that is, the birthplace of the **REBIS**—from a living teacher and not from "mute masters" <note>referring to books, which cannot answer questions</note>, and saw it with his own eyes.

<summary>This discourse explores the origins of the Rebis (Hermaphrodite), comparing its "world-citizen" status to Socrates while identifying its specific alchemical "parents" as Mercury and Venus. It emphasizes that while the Rebis is found everywhere, its true nature is revealed only through the union of these two specific "mountains" or principles.</summary>

<keywords>Rebis, Hermaphrodite, Socrates, Mercury, Venus, Morienus, Adfar of Alexandria, Androgyne, alchemical origins, citizenship of the world</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Discourse XXXVIII. The previous page established the dual nature of the Rebis—the "Double Thing"—as a child of Mercury and Venus, belonging to a "homeland" that is both universal and specific to the peaks of their respective mountains.</meta>

<header>DISCOURSE XXXVIII. 163</header>

Those who seek the homeland of the **Rebis** <note>from the Latin 'res bina', a "double thing" representing the union of opposites in the alchemical work</note> for themselves, guided by reason and the evidence of books, must employ no less persistence and diligence. Yet, even if clarity occasionally seems to be present in books, a great obscurity surrounds and veils it on every side, so that the light can scarcely be recognized or distinguished from the darkness. Therefore, one must proceed cautiously with these writings, lest those prepared as a remedy be used as a poison. They are certainly an immense ocean, in which wandering sailors—though they be skilled in astronomical instruments and can determine their latitude (the elevation of the equator above the horizon) as the magnet points to the North Pole—can by no means determine their longitude (how many degrees they are distant from the first meridian near the **Fortunate Isles** <note>original: "insulis Fortunatis"; the Canary Islands, which served as the traditional zero-point for longitude in the 17th century</note>). Consequently, they remain uncertain of their position between the West and the East.

What then should be done here? Exactly what those same sailors do: consult **Experience** alongside **Reason**, and learn from her how to determine a long journey through specific signs—promontories, islands, and other landmarks—lest they incautiously strike against sandbanks and reefs. Yet here there is less danger if the work does not succeed, but if it does, there is greater profit than in seafaring, where property and life can be lost in a single hour. 

Now, the mountain of the **Philosophical Mercury** is not **Nonacris** <note>a mountain in Arcadia where Hermes was said to be born</note>, nor **Atlas**, where he is elsewhere believed to have been born, but rather the twin-peaked **Parnassus**. On one of its peaks dwells **Hermes** <note>Mercury</note>, and on the other, **Venus**. Here also is **Apollo** with the **Muses**, and the Pegasus-fountain, the **Hippocrene**, with its evergreen laurel. It is one mountain in name, but twin in reality, just as the **Hermaphrodite** is seen as two-headed and two-limbed in a single body. But who among a thousand perseveres to reach the summit of this mountain? Who does not get stuck at the roots, hindered by some unknown delay? How many reach even its middle "navel"?

> *For the ascent is not light if one seeks the heights;*
> *Great sweat carries him upward; he dies over the sleepless*
> *Oil of the night lamp; he erases what he had just praised in himself,*
> *He who desires to be gifted with the honor of the eternal leaf.*

Thus it is no wonder if barely one out of ten thousand exhausts himself in these **Herculean labors** <note>a common alchemical metaphor for the long, arduous stages of the Great Work</note> to set foot upon the mountain peak and enjoy the immortal prize of the laurel. It is to be uniquely hoped that all those of good mind, who are teachable and dedicated to virtue and letters, may rejoice in this, while the **swine** and **greyhounds** <note>original: "Grilli verò & vertagi"; "Grillus" refers to a companion of Odysseus turned into a pig who refused to return to human form, representing the willfully ignorant; "vertagi" are swift hunting dogs, representing those who pursue the art with greedy haste</note> are frustrated.

<meta>catchword: Oedypus</meta>

<summary>Maier compares the search for the alchemical Rebis to a dangerous sea voyage, warning that seekers must balance book-learning with experience. He identifies the true "homeland" of the Stone as a philosophical Parnassus where Mercury and Venus reside together.</summary>

### XXXIX. German version of the Latin Epigram.

> The Sphinx, who was most terrifying to the Thebans with her riddles,
> Was cunningly brought to her death by Oedipus through his art.
> The question was asked: What has four feet in the morning,
> Only two at noon, and in the evening carries three feet again?
> The conqueror of this riddle slays his father, who would not give way,
> And by right takes his own mother as his wife.
> <note>The "morning, noon, and evening" refers to the stages of human life (infancy, adulthood, old age), but in alchemy, it refers to the stages of the Great Work. <meta>This page transitions from the emblem's verse to the discursive prose, continuing the investigation into the myth of Oedipus as an alchemical allegory. It establishes the Sphinx not as a literal monster, but as a symbol for the "tangles" and obscurity of the Great Work.</meta>

DISCOURSE XXXIX.

<meta>catchword: equi-</meta>

Bacasser, a Philosopher in the *Assembly* <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum", an influential medieval alchemical text</note>, says: "What you seek is by no means of small price: for you seek the greatest treasure and the most excellent gift of God." And know, O investigators, what the Philosophers long ago intimated when they said: **THAT WHICH IS RIGHT** is only discerned by error, and nothing generates more sorrow in the heart than error in this art and work. For while one thinks he has finished, and holds the world in his hands, he will find nothing in his hands. These same things the ancient Philosophers wished to suggest by proposing the Sphinx, as if she herself denoted the obscurity and the tangles of the art.

Hence the Egyptians, in the Isiac rites <note>ceremonies dedicated to the goddess Isis</note> which were performed in honor of Osiris by mitred priests with shaven heads, clothed in white, ankle-length linen garments—so that these things might remain hidden and unknown to the common people—erected at the head of the altar a statue of Silence, which was called Sigalion <note>a name for Harpocrates, the god of silence and secrets</note>. Those assisting were commanded to "favor with their tongues" <note>original Latin: "favere linguis"; a ritual command to maintain a holy silence</note> and turn their eyes toward that image. For the same reason, they added statues of **SPHINXES** at the corners of the altar, which denoted the secret knowledge of sacred things; as Boissard <note>Jean-Jacques Boissard (1528–1602), a famous antiquarian of the era</note> demonstrates from the ancients. 

For the Sphinx is a most obscure kind of monster, proposing riddles to the Thebans; and not only to them, but as it was first for the Egyptians, so it is later for all others aspiring to the art. As the Philosopher says in his book: she lies in wait as if before the gates of Thebes; so that if anyone passes the monster by, he suffers no harm from her, but he who, relying on the audacity of his mind or wit, tries to dissolve her riddles, unless he does so, prepares destruction for himself—that is, sorrow in his heart and loss of his property from error in this work. 

He who refers such allegorical things to literal history has a mushroom for a brain and a pumpkin for a heart <note>a common Roman comedic insult for a fool</note>, as the Comic poet says, and he knows no more than one who wanders from the straight path. These things are too childish and worthy only of old women if they are taken literally; otherwise, they are the tokens and signs of profound doctrine. It is indeed said that there are monstrous wild beasts in Africa by the name of Sphinxes <note>likely referring to baboons or apes</note>, but they are not the subject of inquiry here, even if the origin and naming of this fictional creature seem to be taken from them. The Sphinx of the Philosophers understood and used human speech—specifically the Greek language—as well as subtle "gryphs" <note>technical term for intricate riddles or puzzles; from the Greek 'griphos'</note> and enigmatic questions in which a singular sharpness of intelligence and doctrine appears, not so obvious to other men (from which the beasts are furthest removed). He who is at least somewhat practiced in these dogmas will easily understand that the Philosophers' teachings are such. For where one thing is said, and another is understood, there

<summary>This discourse explains the Sphinx as a symbol for the dangerous obscurity of alchemical secrets, warning that literal interpretations lead to ruin while hidden truths require silence and sharp wit.</summary>
<keywords>Sphinx, Turba Philosophorum, Silence, Sigalion, Osiris, alchemical allegory, error, Boissardus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the Sphinx and Oedipus, moving from the historical context of Egyptian silence to the specific alchemical interpretation of the Oedipus myth. It bridges the gap between the riddle as a literal story and its hidden meaning in the "Great Work."</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXXIX.

<meta>catchword: Out of</meta>

Ambiguity breeds error, which to the **Philosophers of the Sun** <note>Alchemists, who often referred to their art as the "solar" work</note> is not only permitted but even commanded. Thus, when the city of Thebes had long been vexed by the riddles of the Sphinx, a certain **Oedipus** finally appeared; he responded to the questions offered in such a way that the Sphinx herself was forced to cast herself down from a rock. And who was this Oedipus? He was the son of the King of the Thebans. At his birth, an oracle predicted to his father that he would be killed by his son. Consequently, the king ordered Oedipus to be killed. Having been suspended from a tree with a rope passed through his feet and abandoned, he was rescued and raised by another countryman. When he grew older, he indeed possessed "swollen feet" <note>the literal meaning of the name "Oedipus" in Greek is "swollen foot"</note>, but he demonstrated a vigorous genius above all others in the unraveling of this riddle proposed by the Sphinx.

It is said that the Sphinx had many riddles, but this one was the principal one presented to Oedipus: *What is it that is four-footed in the morning, two-footed at noon, and three-footed in the evening?* What was actually answered by Oedipus is unknown <note>Maier suggests the "human" answer is a decoy for the uninitiated</note>; but others have interpreted it as the ages of man, yet they are mistaken. For first, one must consider the **quadrangle** or the four elements; from here one arrives at the **hemisphere** (having two lines, one straight and one curved), which is to say, the white Moon; from there, one proceeds to the **Triangle**, which consists of body, spirit, and soul—or the Sun, Moon, and Mercury. 

Hence **Rhases** <note>Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (865–925), a famous Persian physician and alchemist</note> says in his *Epistle*: *“The Stone is triangular in its Being, and quadrangular in its quality.”* <note>original: "Lapis, inquit, Triangulus est in Esse, quadrangulus in qualitate."</note> The twenty-first emblem and its exposition also look toward this same truth. Furthermore, Oedipus is noted for **parricide and incest**, which are the two foulest vices that can ever be imagined; nonetheless, they promoted him to the kingdom (which was otherwise due to him). This happened because he killed his father, who refused to yield the way to him, and he took the queen, the wife of Laius and his own mother, as his wife. 

But this was not written for anyone as a literal history, nor as an example to be imitated; rather, it was feigned and introduced allegorically by the Philosophers solely to reveal the secrets of their doctrine. For in this work, both things occur: the first efficient cause (that is, the father) is removed and overthrown by its own effect (that is, the son); and afterward, the same effect joins the second efficient cause to itself until it becomes one with it. In this way, the son is joined to his mother in marriage and possesses his father’s kingdom by a triple right: by arms, by marriage, and by succession. He has swollen feet because he cannot run; he is like a **bear** (holding a great secret) or a **toad** <note>the toad/bufo is a common alchemical symbol for the "fixed" matter at the bottom of the vessel</note> walking with a slow step. This is because he is the "fixed" thing that fixes others, neither fleeing nor fearing the fire—a base medium that the Philosophers need most of all.

<summary>Maier reinterprets the myth of Oedipus as an alchemical allegory where the Sphinx's riddle refers to the transition between geometric states of matter (elements, moon, and trinity) rather than the ages of man.</summary>
<keywords>Oedipus, Sphinx, Riddle, Four Elements, Triangle, Rhases, Parricide, Alchemical Allegory, Fixed Matter, Toad</keywords>

## Make one out of two waters, and it will be the Water of Health.

### XL. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Two springs well up with a strong flow, clear and sweet;
One is the little boy's, which gives warm water in abundance;
The other, however, is cold, called the little spring of the Virgin;
Mix these, and out of two waters let there be only one;
Then this water holds the combined power of both within itself,
Just as the Spring of Jupiter Ammon <note>An ancient oasis spring in Egypt, famous in the classical world for being cold during the day and warm at night; alchemists used it as a metaphor for the union of opposites.</note> is both warm and cold.

# EMBLEM XL. Of the Secrets of Nature.
## Y
<meta>catchword: Tot</meta>

->From two waters, make one, and it will be the water of health.<-

### EPIGRAM XL.

> There are two springs leaping <note>original: "salientes"</note> from a liquid whirlpool,
> From here, one provides the warm water of the Boy:
> The other has cold, which is called the Virgin’s Wave <note>original: "Virginis Unda"</note>,
> Join this to that, so that the two waters may be as one:
> And this stream will hold the combined strength of both,
> Just as the spring of Jupiter Ammon <note>A famous ancient oasis in the Egyptian desert. Legend held its waters were cold by day and boiling by night; here it serves as a symbol for the union of opposites.</note> is both hot and cold.
