<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical explanation of the "Two Waters" introduced in the previous emblem's epigram. While the previous page provided the poetic and musical foundation, this discourse provides historical, geographical, and alchemical justifications for the union of these opposites, drawing on classical natural history and the authority of Ramon Lull.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XL.

The wonders of various waters are so many and so great that they could hardly be grasped in a single large volume, and various authors have written of them in scattered places. But above all, two **Philosophical waters** <term>Philosophical waters: The primary liquid agents in alchemy, often representing the "mercury" and "sulfur" or the "fixed" and "volatile" principles.</term> are celebrated by that name, because they not only emulate but also surpass the powers and properties of all others.

The **Sybaris**, the **Axus** of Macedonia, and the **Melas** of Boeotia are rivers that turn sheep black if they drink from them; but the **Crathis**, the **Clitumnus** of Mevania, and the **Cephisus** are rivers that turn them from black to white. The **Sinuessan waters** in the region of Campania remove sterility in both sexes. The river **Aphrodisius** makes women sterile. The **Cabura** spring of Mesopotamia has a pleasantly scented water; the water of the **Anygrus** in the Peloponnese stinks greatly. The fountain of **Jupiter Ammon** <note>A famous ancient oasis; its fluctuating temperature was a favorite paradox for classical and alchemical writers.</note> is cold by day, hot by night, and lukewarm in the morning and evening by turns. 

Not to dwell on the rest, all these properties—even those contrary to one another—are provided by the waters of the Philosophers. Concerning these, **Lullius** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher to whom many influential alchemical texts were attributed.</note> says in his book *On the Fifth Essence*, distinction 3, regarding **inceration** <term>Inceration: The alchemical process of adding liquid to a dry substance until it reaches a soft, wax-like consistency.</term>: "And so there is a double consideration in the Art: namely, to compose from one nature of one metal two liquids that are contrary in their composition. One has a fixing, congealing, and hardening power; the other is volatile, unfixed, and soft. This second liquid is hardened, fixed, and congealed by the first. From both these liquids results one congealed, fixed, and hardened stone, which has the power of congealing that which is not congealed, hardening the soft, and softening the hard."

From these words, it is apparent what those two waters are, and why they must be reduced into one water. For the **Stone** is called "water" because it is melted, and conversely, the "water" is called a "stone" because it is ground. Truly, these waters are drawn from different places, sometimes over a long distance—as can be seen in Rome regarding the **Virgin Water** <note>original: "Aqua Virginis"; one of the great aqueducts of ancient Rome, still feeding the Trevi Fountain today.</note> and other artificial springs—and then they must be mixed through a confluence so that one is made from two. For if one is of a hot virtue and the other cold, when mixed they will obtain combined powers and will temper each other in a marvelous way. From this, medicated waters and the most excellent thermal baths will arise, which will drive away all kinds of diseases and ailments, and restore a person to firm health. Nature, indeed, by her secret craft of composition, mixes and blends many waters in the womb of the earth with the diverse virtues of minerals, which for many sick people are...

<meta>catchword: saluti-</meta>

<summary>This discourse explores the paradoxical properties of natural springs from antiquity to explain the alchemical "Two Waters." It argues that by unifying a "fixing" and a "volatile" liquid, the alchemist creates a "Water of Health" capable of curing all diseases.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophical waters, Ramon Lull, Jupiter Ammon, fixed vs. volatile, inceration, Aqua Virginis, alchemy of health, union of opposites</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the "Two Waters," specifically focusing on the distinction between artificial mixtures and natural unions. The previous page introduced the concept of the "Fifth Essence" through the authority of Ramon Llull, and this page deepens that philosophical inquiry by comparing medicinal compounds like Theriac to the indivisible substance sought by alchemists.</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XL.</header>

...health-giving; but if art <note>The OCR "siars" is a likely error for the Latin "si ars" (if art).</note> intervenes with the proper regimen—once universal evacuations and other necessary steps have been taken—and mixes together what ought to be mixed, that composition will be far more effective. Though it may seem artificial, it is nonetheless purely natural, because the thing becomes one and simple, a homogeneous substance made from different parts, which can never be achieved by art alone. 

A mere **mixture** <term>Crama: From the Greek "krama," referring to a blend or a mixture of ingredients, often used in medicine for a compound drug.</term> and confusion is caused by art without the help of nature—it is not a true and natural union, which is produced by nature alone. In **Theriac** <term>Theriac: A famous ancient and medieval medicinal paste composed of dozens of ingredients, used as a universal antidote.</term>, there is an artificial mixing of various **simples** <note>"Simples" were individual medicinal herbs or minerals used as ingredients before being combined into a "compound."</note> through grinding and fermentation; yet no one would claim it is a natural composition, much less a homogeneous medicine, unless they speak rashly. 

It is well known regarding the artificial mixing of substances that they do not penetrate one another at the level of their smallest parts <note>Latin: "per minima." In Aristotelian physics, this refers to the smallest possible units of a substance that still retain its character.</note>, even though human industry may not be able to discern or separate them again. But regarding the mixing of all qualities, it is questioned whether the primary qualities of all the simples in Theriac have transformed into a single **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence (Quinta essentia): The purest, most refined form of matter, believed to be the underlying celestial essence above the four elements.</term>, or whether they still remain in their powders or substances, as before, like accidents in a subject or color on a wall <note>A philosophical metaphor: "accidents" (like color) are properties that exist in a "subject" (like a wall) without changing the wall's essential nature.</note>. 

And then, what should be said of the secondary, tertiary, and quaternary qualities? It is probable that all qualities still cling to their own subjects and are not joined together by a true natural mixing. Otherwise, if the qualities left their bodies, there would be four fifth-essences in any artificial compound—corresponding to the order of qualities (primary, secondary, etc.)—existing without their bodies and separable; but this is not the case. 

They write of **hare's rennet** <term>hare's rennet (coagulo leporino): A substance derived from a hare's stomach, prized in traditional medicine for its paradoxical ability to both clot and thin blood.</term> that by its influence it stops and as it were curdles thin blood, yet it cuts through and dissolves the same blood when it is clotted or in lumps. Likewise, vinegar, lead, and many other things have contrary operations according to their different uses, because nature has mixed them so marvelously. So too the **Philosophical water** possesses diverse and contrary powers, because nature—with the assistance of art—has mixed it from contraries into one indivisible substance, which is nothing other than the Fifth Essence in relation to the other things to be mixed with it.

<meta>catchword: Adonis</meta>

It argues that the Philosophical Water is a true Fifth Essence because it achieves a homogeneous, indivisible state capable of contrary actions.</summary>

## Adonis is killed by a wild boar; Venus, rushing to his side, stained the Roses with her blood.

<image-desc>A classical copperplate engraving illustrating the death of Adonis. In the center foreground, Adonis lies prostrate on a grassy mound, holding a spear. He appears dead or severely wounded. To the left, Venus is depicted running towards him with her arms raised in a gesture of despair. ### EPIGRAM XLI.

From her own father, Myrrha conceived the beautiful Adonis, <note>original Latin: "Ex patre, Myrrha suo pulchrum suscepit Adonim." In Greek mythology, Myrrha was transformed into a myrrh tree; the infant Adonis was born when the tree's bark split open. Alchemically, this signifies a substance born from its own "root" or source.</note>
The darling of the Cyprian <note>The "Cyprian" is a common title for the goddess Venus, referring to her legendary birth from the sea foam near the island of Cyprus.</note>, whom a boar laid low in death.
Venus runs to him, and her foot having been wounded,
She herself bestowed a red color on the rose, which was previously white. <note>This "staining" of the white rose with red blood is a potent alchemical metaphor for the *rubedo*, the final "Reddening" stage of the Great Work where the medicine reaches its full power.</note>
The Goddess weeps (the Syrians weep; the mourning is universal in the world), <note>Adonis was a central figure in Near Eastern vegetation cults; his death and rebirth symbolized the cycle of the seasons.</note>
And she also laid him upon soft lettuces. <note>original: "lactucis mollibus." In myth, lettuce was associated with the cooling of passion and the bed of the dying god.</note>

| Key Terms | Meaning in Context |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Adonis** | The solar youth or the "Philosophical Sulfur" in its nascent state. |
| **Venus** | The goddess of love, representing the "Copper" or the feminine principle. |
| **Myrrha** | The mother of Adonis, representing the vegetable or resinous source. |
| **Boar** | The "aper," a symbol of the sharp, acidic solvent or the destructive force of Mars. |
| **Rose** | The flower of the Work, transitioning from white (*albedo*) to red (*rubedo*). |
| **Lettuce** | A cooling herb used to temper the "heat" of the wounded spirit. |
| **Syria** | The historical region where the lamentations for Adonis (Tammuz) originated. |

<meta>catchword: fœcun- (from the Latin fœcundiores, meaning "more fruitful")</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLI.

How improperly some mythologists explain the allegory of Adonis has been sufficiently exposed and refuted by us elsewhere. These writers refer him now to the Sun; now to the wild boar by which he was killed (representing the bristling frost of Winter); and now to the seeds of grain, which spend six months below the earth with Proserpina <note>The Roman name for Persephone, Queen of the Underworld, whose seasonal movement explains the cycle of growth.</note> and just as many above the earth with Venus. Here, however, we declare—with the agreement of all authorities—that the **Philosophical Sun** is the true meaning understood beneath the figure of Adonis. Hence the verse:

->*All are the same: Dionysus, the Sun, and Adonis:*<-

And Orpheus says: *You who delight in the various names of Adonis,*

->*The creator is both twin and the same, at once both boy and girl:*<-

These descriptions are in no way to be understood as the Sun in the heavens, but rather the **Philosophical Sun** <note>In alchemy, the "Philosophical Sun" represents the perfected Gold or the Stone, which contains both active/masculine and passive/feminine principles.</note>. For this Philosophical Sun expresses both sexes, whereas the celestial Sun does not. Thus, they attribute the very same qualities to Dionysus and the Sun as they do to Adonis, and vice versa—just as they also do with Osiris.

Adonis, however, is slain by a Boar; that is, by the **sharpest vinegar** <note>original: "aceto acerrimo." Alchemists often used "vinegar" as a code for a potent acidic solvent used to break down metals.</note> or **dissolving water**, which possesses boar-like and lightning-fast teeth with which it strikes Adonis. This is because the Philosophical Sun is lethally wounded by that boar; he is dissolved in his members and torn apart. But Venus strives to bring aid to her lover, and when he was dead, she placed him among lettuces and guarded him.

In the same way, Osiris is killed by Typhon and dissected into various parts, which Isis, the wife of Osiris, gathered back together and committed to a joint burial. The same mourning that followed the death of Osiris in Egypt every year also followed the death of Adonis in Syria and neighboring kingdoms; where for several days wailing and lamentation were heard, and afterwards signs of joy and dances were given, as if he who had been dead now lived anew, having been carried up to heaven. From this arose the vanity of their ethnic religion or superstition, which grew immensely as the Devil provided the opportunity and procured false miracles.

Adonis was born from Cinyras (as they imagine), the King of Cyprus, and his daughter Myrrha. He is said to be born of incest—if the history is viewed literally, it is a wicked crime; if viewed as an allegory, it is not illicit, but primarily necessary. For unless a union is made between mother and son, or between father and daughter, and the offspring is born from that, nothing is perfected in this Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy. This "incest" is a metaphor for combining two substances that share the same "root" or elemental origin to ensure their chemical compatibility.</note>. For here, the more closely the spouses are related to one another by blood—whether in the first degree of kinship or the second—the more **fruitful** they are...

<summary>This page argues that Adonis represents the "Philosophical Sun" (the alchemical Stone) and interprets his death by a boar as the chemical dissolution of gold by a sharp solvent. It draws parallels between the myths of Adonis and Osiris, explaining that the "incestuous" birth of Adonis represents the necessary union of chemically related substances.</summary>

<keywords>Adonis, Sol Philosophicus, Philosophical Sun, Osiris, Isis, Boar, chemical dissolution, solvent, alchemy, allegory, Myrrha, Cinyras</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the identity of Adonis, moving from his mythological death to the alchemical significance of his "incestuous" origins. It establishes that in alchemy, the closer the kinship of the substances, the more powerful the reaction, contrasting this with human morality.</meta>

<page-num>175</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XLI.</header>

...more fruitful; and conversely, the more distant they are, the more unfruitful they become—a condition that is not tolerable in human marriage. For this reason, Oedipus marries his own mother; Jupiter his sister; and so too do Osiris, Saturn, the Sun, the **Red Servant** <note>original: "servus rubeus." An alchemical term for a specific state of matter or a chemical agent, often associated with the "Red Man" or the solar principle.</note>, and **Gabritius** <note>A common figure in alchemical allegory, usually the brother of Beya; their union symbolizes the joining of the fixed and the volatile principles.</note>. Concerning Adonis, the Sun (speaking through the **Belinian metaphor** <note>Refers to the alchemist Belinus, to whom several medieval alchemical works were attributed.</note> found in the *Rosary of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Rosarium." A famous 16th-century series of alchemical illustrations and texts.</note>) speaks about himself in this way: 

> Know that my father, the Sun, has given me power over every power, and has clothed me in a garment of glory. And shortly after: For I am unique and I am made like my father, etc. I draw my servants out of their own power and nature, and I clothe them in my beautiful splendor and light, which my father gave to me in all their works. For I am excellent, I who exalt and cast down all things; and none of my servants can prevail over me, except one, to whom it is given because he is my opposite. And he destroys me, yet he does not destroy my nature. And he is **Saturn** <note>In alchemy, Saturn usually represents Lead or the state of "blackness" (nigredo). Here, he acts as the agent of dissolution that breaks down the matter.</note>, who separates all my members. Afterward, I go to my mother, who gathers all my divided and separated members. I am the one illuminating all that is mine, and I cause light to appear clearly on the path from my father Saturn, and also from my mother, who is hostile to me. 

The things said thus far are so clear that—even for one only moderately well-versed in reading the authors—they dispel the shadows from the eyes of the mind and reveal the solar light. This light is abundantly seen in the connections brought forth here between things and persons. For things that are true, even if hidden under the veil of allegory, agree with one another with a wonderful consensus; those that are false conflict with themselves and with others, and wander off in different directions.

<meta>catchword: Dem</meta>

<summary>This page explains that alchemical "incest" (the union of closely related substances) is necessary for fertility in the Work, using the speech of the Sun from the Rosarium to illustrate the cycle of death by Saturn and rebirth through the Mother.</summary>
<keywords>incest allegory, Oedipus, Jupiter, Gabritius, Rosarium Philosophorum, Saturn, dissolution, solar light, philosophical sun</keywords>

### XLII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Let Nature be your leader, whom you must follow from afar,
->Willingly; otherwise you stray, where she does not guide you.<-
Let Reason be your staff, and Experience must strengthen
Your sight, so that you may see what is laid out far and wide;
Let Reading be like a lamp, shining bright and clear in the darkness,
Through which you may avoid the danger of things and words.

### EPIGRAM XLII.

> The eyes <note>original: "lumina." Literally "lights," often used poetically to mean eyes or the faculty of sight.</note>, so that they may see things placed far off.

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem XLII, expanding upon the allegory of the four requirements for the alchemist's journey. It moves from the poetic epigram on the previous page to a detailed philosophical discourse on why Nature, Reason, Experience, and Reading are essential.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLII.

The accidents that can happen to travelers are countless, especially if they resolve to journey on foot at night through slippery and dangerous places. For such a journey, four things are required as being supremely necessary, to say nothing of travel money or a robust body: First, a companion or guide who is not ignorant of the places through which one must go. For if the ignorant leads the ignorant, the same thing happens to them as happens to the blind: if they do not fall into a pit, they both nonetheless plunge into errors and wanderings. Second, a staff or walking stick <note>original: "bacillus vel scipio"</note> by which the slipperiness of the path is prevented from causing harm. Third, healthy eyes; for such journeys are most dangerous for the blind or bleary-eyed. Fourth, a lamp or a lit torch, so that the hazards of the roads can be distinguished. In the same way, if anyone commits themselves to that most difficult journey to seek out *Philosophical Medicine* <note>A term for the Philosopher's Stone, which was believed to heal both "sick" metals and the human body.</note>, besides the expenses and bodily strength, they will require four things parallel and corresponding exactly to those mentioned before: namely, *Nature, Reason, Experience, and Reading.* If one or another of these is missing, the rest will help little or not at all. For the Philosophical chariot proceeds upon these as if upon four wheels; if one wheel is missing, the others cannot be used, and even if they remain, they are of no benefit. Nature presupposes natural bodies and spirits as the subjects first provided by nature, upon which Art <note>In alchemy, "Art" refers to the human-directed chemical process that completes or perfects what Nature started.</note> afterwards acts by preparing, purifying, and rendering them fit so that what the Art promises as its end can be made from them. Thus, the potter takes water and earth; the glassmaker, ashes and sand; the smith, iron, copper, tin, lead, silver, or gold; the tanner, raw hides; and so others take other things.

So also the practitioner of *chemistry* <note>original: "chymiæ"</note> looks to his own materials. For the others, their materials are very well known even on the first day; for the chemist, when he begins, they usually remain unknown for many years, if not for his entire life. Nature indeed points her finger at the materials, but there are many things that obscure the impression of nature so that she cannot be recognized. Therefore, the first intention is to intimately contemplate nature—how she proceeds in her operations—to the end that the natural subjects of *Chemistry* may be obtained without defect or excess. Hence, let Nature be the guide and companion of so great a journey, whose footsteps must be followed. Second, let *Reason* be like a staff to steady the steps and stabilize the feet so they do not falter. Without reason—

<meta>catchword: lest</meta>

<summary>Michael Maier explains the four essential tools for the alchemist—Nature, Reason, Experience, and Reading—by comparing the alchemical quest to a dangerous journey at night. He emphasizes that while a potter or smith knows their raw materials immediately, the alchemist must spend years studying Nature to even identify the correct "natural subjects" for their work.</summary>

<keywords>alchemical journey, four wheels of alchemy, Philosophical Medicine, Nature as guide, Reason as staff, raw materials, Art vs Nature, Atalanta Fugiens</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the four necessary requirements for the alchemical journey: Nature, Reason, Experience, and Reading. Having established Nature as the guide on the previous page, it now focuses on how Reason prevents error, how Experience acts as a lens for the mind, and why Reading is a vital lamp for the intellect.</meta>

### DISCOURSE XLII.

...for lest anyone should be prone to falling into errors. Whence the Philosophers say: Whatever you hear, reason whether it can be so, or not. For no one is driven to believe or perform impossible things, unless he himself is of weak memory, dull wit, and foolish imagination, such that he deceives himself by accepting falsehoods for truths and rejecting truths for falsehoods. They also say they do not care about words—whatever might be said—but rather about the things themselves and what is understood; and that words exist for the sake of things, and not things for the sake of words. For example, someone might say that glass becomes malleable <note>Capable of being hammered or shaped without breaking—a legendary alchemical feat.</note> through the *Philosophical Tincture* <note>A term for the Philosopher's Stone in its potent, liquid, or powdered form.</note>. Why should I not believe this, if only reason dictates it?

Thirdly, **Experience** will provide spectacles <note>original: "perspicilia"</note> through which remote things can be seen; these are optical instruments helping and correcting the weakness of human eyes, invented and made by art. Very similar to these are the experiments attempted concerning mineral matter of every kind, whether seen or truly heard; the more of these there are in the memory, the more reasoning will take from there and compare them with one another, so that it may notice what is true and what is not.

Fourthly, **Reading** kindles, as it were, a clear lamp in the intellect, without which there will be darkness and thick clouds everywhere. However, the reading of good authors must often be repeated; otherwise it will be of no benefit. Hence Bacasser <note>A figure cited in early alchemical literature, likely a corruption of a Greek or Arabic name.</note> in the *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: "in turba," referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the most famous and influential early Latin alchemical texts.</note> says: 

> *He therefore who is long-suffering, and freely enjoys patience, will walk in the right path of this art; but he who thinks he can gather fruit from our books more quickly is deceived, and it would have been better for him not to have looked into them at all, than to have ever happened upon them. And those things which follow in the same place.*

### XLII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Upon the high peak of the mountain sits the Vulture, crying aloud
Without ceasing: "I alone am the white and the black,
The lemon-colored <note>original: "Citron Farb," referring to *Citrinitas*, the yellowing stage that follows the white stage.</note> and the red <note>original: "roht," referring to *Rubedo*, the final red stage of the Great Work.</note>: I do not lie; that same is also the Raven
That flies, even though its wings have been clipped off,
In the dark night, even when the sun stands high at noon;
For this one, or that one, is that from which the whole Art <note>The "Art" refers to the Alchemical *Magnum Opus* or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> proceeds."

### EMBLEM XLIII. *Concerning the secrets of Nature.*

->Listen to the talkative vulture, who by no means deceives you.<-

### EPIGRAM XLIII.

> On the high summit of the mountain stands a vulture <note>The vulture in alchemy often represents the volatile principle or the "spirit" that must be "fixed" or brought down to earth.</note>
> Constantly crying out: "I am white and black,
> Yellow and red I am called: I lie not: the same is <note>The vulture claims to embody all the primary color stages of the alchemical process: *Nigredo* (black), *Albedo* (white), *Citrinitas* (yellow), and *Rubedo* (red).</note>
> The crow, who is accustomed to fly without feathers <note>original: "Corvus, qui pennis absque volare solet." A crow "without feathers" is a common alchemical paradox referring to a substance that has been "fixed"—rendered non-volatile—so that its spirit can no longer "fly" away.</note>
> In the dark night, and in the middle of the light of day,
> For either that one or this one is the head of your art." <note>The "art" refers to alchemy; the "head" implies that these symbols represent the essential starting material or the primary mystery of the work.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Emblem XLIII, transitioning from the visual representation of the "talkative vulture" to a scholarly discourse on its nature and alchemical symbolism. It connects the mimicry of birds to the self-declaration of the Alchemical Stone.</meta>

### DISCOURSE XLIII.

We daily and everywhere hear talkative birds, or those that mimic the human voice, namely parrots, crows, jackdaws, and magpies. Thus Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist whose *Natural History* was a primary source for medieval and Renaissance science.</note> writes that in his own time, when he published his history, Agrippina, the wife of Claudius Caesar, possessed a thrush that could imitate human speech. The young Caesars <note>Referring to the sons or heirs of the Roman Emperors.</note> also had a starling and nightingales trained in both the Greek and Latin languages, which were constantly speaking new things in a long thread of words. Since such birds are not rarely encountered, they are now considered less worthy of wonder, provided that practice and training can render any bird endowed with a broad tongue vocal and talkative in this manner.

However, that vulture of which the Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists, the "Philosophers of Nature."</note> make mention did not learn the words it utters from practice; rather, its very nature silently expresses them. Indeed, the Philosophers say that it cries out constantly and proclaims in a loud voice who it is and of what sort, in which it imitates great princes. These rulers always wish to make their titles and inheritances known at the beginning of their proclamations, not as a mark of any pride, but for the sake of others, so that they may know which princedoms they rule or over which they claim hereditary right. 

Thus, it is of great importance that it be recognized by what colors—as if by the insignia of arms and titles—the Philosophical bird rejoices and excels all others. "I am," it says (as the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rose Garden of the Philosophers), a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> quotes from Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical texts are attributed.</note>), "the black of the white, and the yellow of the red, and certainly I am truthful and not lying." <note>original: "sum nigrum albi, & Citrinus rubei, & certe veridicus sum & nō mentiens." This refers to the four stages of the Great Work: *Nigredo* (black), *Albedo* (white), *Citrinitas* (yellow), and *Rubedo* (red).</note> It affirms itself to be black, white, yellow, and red; and such it truly is, for even if it does not yet possess the last three colors in actuality, it nevertheless awaits their inheritance. 

Hence Rosinus <note>Zosimos of Panopolis, an early alchemist often called "Rosinus" in Latin translations.</note> says in his *Book of Divine Interpretations*: "Take," he says, "the stone which is black, white, red, and yellow, the miraculous bird which flies without wings in the darkness of night and the clarity of day." For from the bitterness existing in its throat, the coloration is received, but from its very blood pure water is received, just as Alexander <note>Likely referring to Alexander the Great, who was often reimagined as an initiate of the alchemical arts in medieval lore.</note> said: "Take the stone of a four-colored thread." The books of the Philosophers dictate to the point of nausea that all these colors, which are the principal ones, are present in the stone in a successive order. 

Yet, it is not without reason that the Philosophical subject is called a "Vulture." Among vultures, the black ones prevail; they are predatory, but they fly slowly because of the mass of their bodies. They say this bird conceives without the seed of males, and without 
<meta>catchword: conjunction</meta>

<summary>Maier compares the "talkative" nature of alchemical substances to the speech of birds and the titles of princes, establishing the Vulture as a symbol for the changing colors of the Alchemical Stone.</summary>
<keywords>vulture, Pliny, Agrippina, colors of the work, Nigredo, Albedo, Citrinitas, Rubedo, Hermes Trismegistus, Rosarium Philosophorum, Rosinus, Alexander, alchemical succession</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the Philosophical Vulture, moving from its symbolic titles to its natural history and its allegorical significance in the alchemical process, particularly regarding its reproduction and the location of the "Stone."</meta>

### DISCOURSE XLIII.

...generate through union, and their offspring live to a great age, even up to a hundred years. They nest in high cliffs, and no one has touched their nests. Generally, two young are seen. They are a help against serpents. They are said to be impregnated by the East Wind <note>A common belief in antiquity, recorded by Pliny and others, that vultures were all female and conceived through the wind.</note>. When they begin to lay eggs, they bring something from the Indian regions which is like a nut, having something inside that moves and makes a sound from time to time; when they place this near themselves, they produce many offspring, but only one remains, which is called the **Immusulus** <note>The *immusulus* was considered a smaller, perhaps less noble, species of eagle or vulture in Roman augury.</note>.

Hermodorus Ponticus is a witness (by way of Coelius) that vultures are the most innocent of all animals because they touch absolutely nothing that humans sow, plant, or nourish. Furthermore, they kill no living creature. They even abstain from other birds, even dead ones, out of a certain understanding of kinship. For this reason, they were considered the most excellent in auguries, as the origins of the City of Rome indicate <note>Refers to the legend of Romulus and Remus, where the sighting of vultures determined the founding of Rome.</note>. 

Since the Philosophical bird expresses almost all these properties of vultures, the vulture is not undeservedly named by Hermes and others; it is both slow in flight and black in color. It conceives from itself: for as the *Rosarius* <note>original: *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a foundational 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> says near the end: *“And he is the dragon who marries himself and impregnates himself and gives birth in his own day,”* etc. And Rosinus <note>An Arabic alchemical author, likely Zosimos, whose name was Latinized as Rosinus.</note> in the work to Sarratant says: *“And it is the serpent sporting with itself, impregnating itself, and giving birth in a single day,”* etc. It lives for a very long time, survives, and multiplies itself. For what Virgil writes of the Phoenix bird applies equally to this one (because they are the same):

> *The crow outlives the swift-footed stag three times over,*
> *But the Phoenix, the bird of rebirth, multiplies that ninefold.* <note>These lines are often attributed to the "Ausonian" fragments or pseudo-Virgil, discussing the legendary lifespans of animals.</note>

It is very difficult to reach its nests. It fights with the Mercurial serpent and overcomes it—that is, the Sun with the Moon. It is conceived from the wind, carried in its belly, and born in the air. The eagle-stone <term>Aetites: a "pregnant" stone containing a loose pebble inside, thought to aid in childbirth</term>, which has a small stone sounding within it, is called the "whole" by many. At least one *immusulus* is found in the nest of the Philosophers. 

The bird of the Philosophers is also most innocent because it harms no one, profits all who know it, and is most excellent in auguries. But why does it nest on a mountain and cry out while sitting there? Rosinus answers from Rasi <note>Rhazes (al-Razi), a famous Persian polymath and alchemist.</note>, who says: *“Contemplate the highest mountains, which are on the right and the left, and ascend there: there our stone is found; and on another mountain, which bears every kind of pigment, and spirits or species, it is likewise there.”* Morienus <note>A legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist.</note> says: *“Ascend the high mountains, planted with trees, because there our stone is found and is hidden.”* And Hermes: *“Receive the blessed stone, crush and wash the red stone from which it is extracted, which is found in the mountains and especially sometimes in old sewers.”* <note>The alchemical "Stone" is often described paradoxically as being found in both the highest, most sacred places and the lowest, most discarded places like "cloacis" (sewers/drains).</note>

<summary>This page describes the natural and mystical properties of the vulture, linking its self-conception and "innocence" to the alchemical dragon and the discovery of the Philosophical Stone in high or lowly places.</summary>
<keywords>vulture, Immusulus, Phoenix, Rosarium Philosophorum, Hermetic Dragon, eagle-stone, Morienus, mountain symbolism, alchemy</keywords>

### XLIV. German version of the Latin Epigram.

**S**yria has Adonis; Greece has Dionysus likewise;
Egypt has Osiris; these are given as the Sun to the Sages.
Isis is the sister, also the mother and the wife of Osiris,

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XLIV, which provides the theoretical and mythological foundation for the previous emblem's image. Maier is explaining that the Egyptian myth of Osiris—his death, dismemberment by Typhon, and reconstruction by Isis—is not a literal history or a religious tale, but a sophisticated alchemical allegory for the purification and recombination of matter. This page specifically focuses on "syncretism," the idea that all ancient myths (Greek, Roman, and Egyptian) actually describe the same chemical process using different names.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLIV.

We have reduced and fully explained the allegory of Osiris elsewhere, namely in the first book of our *Hieroglyphics*, to its true origin, which is Chemical <note>original: "Chymica." In the 17th century, alchemy and chemistry were not yet distinct disciplines; Maier uses the term to mean the art of transforming matter.</note>. Therefore, we think it superfluous to repeat the same things here (although the same things ought to be said about them). Nevertheless, we will institute a parallel discourse here, which will always move and remain within the boundaries of Ancient Alchemy (which has been entirely sung of and symbolized by the poets). 

Will you persuade me that Osiris is a God, or an Egyptian king? I will not believe it, even if you have persuaded me to believe it: for as the proverb goes, "dogs smell quite differently than swine" <note>Maier uses this proverb to suggest that the "scent" or essence of the truth is entirely different from the superficial story the common people believe.</note>. I plainly deny he is a God, and you will agree with me, unless you are a Pagan or of an opinion distorted from right reason. That he was not a King is demonstrated by all the circumstances which are considered elsewhere. 

He is the Sun, but the Sun of the Philosophers <note>original: "Sol... Philos." This refers to the "Philosophical Gold" or the perfected essence of the Stone, rather than the literal sun in the sky.</note>; since his name is read as being attributed to it everywhere, the common people—who have known no other light except that of the world—have interpreted him as such. From the sun of the world, the Sun of the Philosophers takes its name, because it contains the properties of nature descending from that celestial sun, or agreeing with it. 

Therefore, Osiris is the same as Dionysus, Bacchus, Jupiter, Mars, Adonis, Oedipus, Perseus, Achilles, Triptolemus, Pelops, Hippomenes, and Pollux <note>Maier claims these diverse heroes all represent the "Solar" or active principle in the alchemical Great Work.</note>. Truly, the Moon <note>The "Lunar" or passive, feminine principle of the work.</note> is Isis, Juno, Venus, the mother of Oedipus, Danaë, Deidamia, Atalanta, and Helen: likewise Latona, Semele, Europa, Leda, Antiope, and Thalia. 

And these are the parts of the compound, which before the operation is called the Stone, and by the name of every metal, <term>Magnesia</term> <note>In alchemy, "Magnesia" refers to the "First Matter," a chaotic starting substance containing the potential for all metals.</note>: after the operation, it is called Orcus, Pyrrhus, Apollo, or Aesculapius. The adjuncts <note>the antagonistic forces or catalysts</note> are Typhon, Python, and the Boar; the Artist <note>the Alchemist performing the work</note> is Hercules, Ulysses, Jason, Theseus, or Pirithous: and the labors and dangers are innumerable, which were endured by these artists. Consider the labors of Hercules, the wanderings of Ulysses, the dangers of Jason, the attempts of Theseus, and the delays of Pirithous. 

This is a great volume of matter and doctrine, through which Vulcan, Mercury, and Saturn run and rerun on every page; the latter <note>Saturn, associated with lead and the beginning of the work</note> as the father of all and the indispensable cause <note>original: "causa, sine qua non."</note>, the middle one <note>Mercury, the transformative substance</note> as the matter and form, and the former <note>Vulcan, representing the fire of the furnace</note> as the efficient cause. The Sun takes his sister the Moon to be his wife. Jupiter takes Juno, as Saturn takes Rhea, and Osiris takes Isis: Dionysus is snatched from his mother Semele (who was consumed by the lightning of Jupiter) to be brought to maturity in the thigh of his father Jupiter, just as Aesculapius was snatched from his mother Coronis:

<meta>catchword: Diony-</meta>

<summary>Maier argues that Osiris and other mythological figures are not historical or religious icons, but personifications of alchemical principles like the Sun (Gold) and Moon (Silver). He identifies the gods with specific roles in the "Great Work," casting the Alchemist as a hero like Hercules or Jason.</summary>
<keywords>Osiris, Isis, Sol Philosophicus, Magnesia, Chemical Allegory, Vulcan, Mercury, Saturn, Mythological Syncretism, Alchemical Artist</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Discourse XLIV that ancient myths are not literal histories but encrypted alchemical instructions. Having established Osiris as a symbol for the "Philosophical Sun" or Gold, Maier now provides a rapid-fire catalog of various Greek and Roman myths. He points out their logical inconsistencies as historical events—such as Helen of Troy being an old woman by the time of the war—to prove that these stories must actually represent universal chemical principles like purification by fire, the "sowing" of matter, and the transformation of base elements into gold.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLIV.

Dionysus, when grown, showed the new drink of wine to men, undertaking an expedition even into India. Osiris and Triptolemus showed the sowing and use of grain; Aesculapius, the administration of medicine. Dionysus, so called by the Greeks, is Bacchus to the Romans; he is Osiris to the Egyptians, and Adonis to the Syrians. Oedipus killed his father and married his mother; Perseus slew his grandfather, Typhon slew his brother Osiris, a boar slew Adonis, and Ceres—the nurse of Triptolemus—slew his father Eleusius. Hippomenes defeated Atalanta with a golden apple; Tantalus, the father of Pelops, won Hippodamia in a chariot race. Osiris was cut into pieces and joined together again by Isis, who was at once his mother, sister, and wife. Pelops, as a boy, was cooked and boiled, and then restored to life after Ceres had eaten his shoulder, with an ivory shoulder added in its place. 

Achilles and Triptolemus were placed under hot coals by night and nourished with milk by day—the latter by his nurse Ceres, the former by his mother Thetis <note>Maier identifies "nourishing by fire" as an alchemical metaphor for the constant, gentle heat required to mature the Stone.</note>. Achilles and Helen were the causes of the Trojan War: she as the impulsive cause, and he as the efficient cause. Helen was born from an egg, and at the wedding of Peleus and Thetis (from whom Achilles was born), the apple of Eris—the first cause of Helen's abduction—was thrown. Pollux was present with the Argonauts, who are presumed to have lived nearly fifty years before the start of the Trojan War (if they lived at all), and Helen emerged from the same egg with Pollux. Therefore, Helen was an old woman when she was snatched by Paris. 

Medea married Achilles in the Elysian Fields, she being then a toothless old woman—unless perhaps she restored youth to herself, as she did for Aeson, the father of Jason, and as Ceres did for Pelops, whence he is called "twice-youthful" <note>original: "bis pubescens." This refers to the alchemical process of "rejuvenation," where a substance is returned to a "prime matter" state to be perfected again.</note>. Perseus received a winged horse from Pallas, and brought her the head of Medusa in return; Mercury provided him a curved sword <note>original: "harpen." A sickle-shaped blade, often symbolizing the "cutting" or separation of elements in alchemy.</note>, and other Gods provided other weapons. Triptolemus received a chariot with winged dragons from Ceres. 

When Pallas was born from the brain of Jupiter, it rained gold at Rhodes, just as it did when the Sun lay with Venus. And Jupiter, in the form of gold, compressed <note>In this context, "compressed" is a polite 17th-century translation for a sexual encounter, here used to describe the union of chemical principles.</note> Danaë; as a swan, he compressed Leda; as a cuckoo, his sister Juno; as a bull, Europa; as a satyr, Antiope. And thus, there is a concordance in all these things.

<summary>Maier catalogs numerous Greek and Egyptian myths to demonstrate their shared patterns of birth, death, and transformation, arguing they are all alchemical allegories. He highlights the "concordance" between these tales—such as heroes being tempered by fire or gods transforming into gold—to prove they describe chemical processes rather than history.</summary>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Discourse XLIV. While the previous section concluded the preceding argument, this discourse turns specifically to the Egyptian allegory of Osiris, connecting it to the broader web of Greek and Roman myths discussed previously as symbols for alchemical transformation.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLIV.

We have reduced the allegory of Osiris to its true origin, which is Chemical, and explained it fully elsewhere—namely, in the first book of my *Hieroglyphics*. <note>Maier refers to his work *Arcana Arcanissima*, where he argues that Egyptian hieroglyphs were actually encoded alchemical recipes.</note> Therefore, we consider it unnecessary to repeat the same things here (although the same things ought to be said about them). Nevertheless, we will establish a parallel discourse here, which will always move and remain within the boundaries of Ancient Chemistry (which was entirely sung and represented in figures by the poets).

Will you try to persuade me that Osiris was a God, or an Egyptian king? I will not believe it, even if you try to convince me: for dogs smell one way, and pigs smell quite another (as the proverb says). <note>Maier uses this proverb to distinguish between the "scent" of philosophical truth and the vulgar literalism of common history.</note> I plainly deny he was a God, and you will agree with me, unless you are a Pagan or hold an opinion distorted from right reason. That he was not a king either is demonstrated by all the circumstances considered elsewhere. He is the Sun, but the Philosophical Sun <term>Philosophical Sun: A symbol for alchemical Gold or the perfected state of matter, as opposed to the literal sun in the sky</term>; since this name is found attributed to him everywhere, the common people—who knew no other light but that of the world—interpreted it as such. The Sun of the Philosophers takes its name from the sun of the world because it contains the properties of nature descending from or corresponding to that celestial sun.

Thus the Sun is Osiris, Dionysus, Bacchus, Jupiter, Mars, Adonis, Oedipus, Perseus, Achilles, Triptolemus, Pelops, Hippomenes, and Pollux. The Moon, truly, is Isis, Juno, Venus, the mother of Oedipus, Danaë, Deidamia, Atalanta, and Helen; likewise Latona, Semele, Europa, Leda, Antiope, and Thalia. And these are the parts of the "compound," which before the operation is called the Stone, and by the name of every metal, Magnesia <term>Magnesia: In alchemy, this refers not to the modern element magnesium, but to the "raw" primordial matter or "the mother" of the Stone</term>; after the operation, it is called Orcus, Pyrrhus, Apollo, or Aesculapius.

The adjuncts are Typhon, Python, and the Boar <note>These represent the "impurities" or destructive forces that must be overcome during the chemical process.</note>. The Craftsmen are Hercules, Ulysses, Jason, Theseus, and Pirithous; [the myths describe] the countless labors and dangers which were endured by these craftsmen. See the labors of Hercules, the wanderings of Ulysses, the dangers of Jason, the attempts of Theseus, and the delays of Pirithous. This is a great volume of matter and doctrine, through which Vulcan, Mercury, and Saturn run and return on every page: the latter <note>Saturn</note> as the father of all and the indispensable cause; the former <note>Mercury</note> as the matter and form; and that one <note>Vulcan, representing Fire</note> as the efficient cause. The Sun takes his sister, the Moon, as his wife. Jupiter takes Juno, as Saturn takes Rhea, and Osiris takes Isis. Dionysus was snatched from his mother Semele (who was consumed by Jove’s lightning) to be ripened in the thigh of his father Jove; Aesculapius was snatched from his mother Coronis:

<meta>catchword: Diony-</meta>

<summary>Maier argues that Osiris and other mythological figures are not historical kings or gods, but rather alchemical symbols representing the "Philosophical Sun" and various chemical states. He categorizes famous heroes as "craftsmen" and monsters as "impurities" in the work of the Great Art.</summary>
<keywords>Osiris, Philosophical Sun, Magnesia, Alchemical Allegory, Greek Mythology, Egyptian Mythology, Mercury, Saturn, Vulcan, Chemical Marriage</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the beginning of Discourse XLIV, where Maier asserts that the myths of Osiris and other gods are not historical accounts but chemical allegories. Here, he provides a dense list of mythological parallels to show a "concordance" or underlying unity in these ancient "fables."</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLIV.

When **Dionysus** reached adulthood, he showed men the new drink of wine, undertaking an expedition as far as **India**; **Osiris** and **Triptolemus** showed the sowing and use of grain; **Aesculapius**, the administration of medicine. **Dionysus** is so-called by the **Greeks**, but to the **Romans** he is **Bacchus**; to the **Egyptians** he is **Osiris**, and to the **Syrians** **Adonis**. **Oedipus** killed his father and married his mother; **Perseus** slew his grandfather; **Typhon** killed his brother **Osiris**; a boar killed **Adonis**; and **Ceres**, the nurse of **Triptolemus**, killed his father **Eleusius**. **Hippomenes** defeated **Atalanta** with a golden apple; **Tantalus**, the father of **Pelops**, won **Hippodamia** in a chariot race. **Osiris** was cut into pieces and joined together again by **Isis**, his mother, sister, and wife. **Pelops**, as a boy, was cooked and boiled; after his shoulder was eaten by **Ceres**, he was restored to life with an ivory shoulder added.

**Achilles** and **Triptolemus** were placed under glowing coals at night and nourished with milk by day—the latter by his nurse **Ceres**, the former by his mother **Thetis**. **Achilles** and **Helen** were the causes of the **Trojan** war: she as the impulsive cause, he as the efficient cause. **Helen** was born from an egg; and at the wedding of **Peleus** and **Thetis** (from whom **Achilles** was born), the apple of **Eris** <note>original: *Eridos*. Eris is the goddess of Discord; Maier identifies her golden apple as the catalyst for the alchemical "war" or the separation of elements.</note>—the primary cause of **Helen's** abduction—was thrown. **Pollux** was present with the **Argonauts**, who are presupposed to have lived nearly fifty years before the start of the **Trojan** war (if they lived at all); and **Helen** came forth from the same egg as **Pollux**. Therefore, **Helen** was an old woman when she was abducted by **Paris**. 

**Medea** married **Achilles** in the **Elysian** fields, and she was then a toothless old woman—unless perhaps she restored her youth to herself, as she did for **Aeson**, the father of **Jason**, or as **Ceres** did for **Pelops**, who for this reason is called "twice-youthful." <note>original: *bis pubescens*. In alchemical literature, this "double youth" refers to the process of rejuvenation, where old, "dead" matter is purified and brought back to a potent, perfected state.</note> **Perseus** received the winged horse from **Pallas** and brought her the head of **Medusa** as a reward; **Mercury** provided him with a scythe <term>harpe: A specialized curved sword or sickle-shaped blade often depicted in the myths of Perseus and Saturn.</term>, and other Gods provided other weapons. **Triptolemus** received a chariot with winged dragons from **Ceres**. 

When **Pallas** was born from the brain of **Jupiter**, it rained gold at **Rhodes**, just as it did when the **Sun** lay with **Venus**. And **Jupiter** embraced **Danae** as gold, **Leda** as a **Swan**, his sister **Juno** as a cuckoo, **Europa** as a **Bull**, and **Antiope** as a **Satyr**—and thus there is a concordance in all things.

### XLV. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The Sun, the clear light of heaven, cannot pass through thick bodies;
->Therefore, a shadow remains in the places it cannot see.<-
Although that same shadow is considered a very lowly thing among all others,
->It nonetheless serves the Astronomers well.<-
But the Sun and its shadow have given a greater gift to the Sages,
->For through them, they bring the Golden Art to completion in deed.<-

# EMBLEM XLV. Concerning the secrets of Nature.

->The Sun and its shadow complete the work.<-

<image-desc>An engraving of an astronomical and alchemical diagram. In the upper left, the Sun is depicted with a human face, emitting a wide fan of rays. ### EPIGRAM XLV.

> The SUN, the bright torch of the heavens, does not penetrate dense bodies,
> *Hence, on the opposite side, a shadow remains for it:*
> Though this shadow is more worthless than all other things,
> *Yet, in its use, it has brought many advantages to Astronomers:*
> But the Sun and its shadow have given more gifts to the Sages <note>the alchemists</note>,
> *Since it completes the work of the gold-bearing art <note>alchemy</note>.*

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse of Emblem XLV, expanding on the relationship between light and shadow. The previous page established the "Sun and its shadow" as the completion of the alchemical work; here, the author explains this through both physical astronomy and Hermetic philosophy, transitioning from the celestial mechanics of day and night to the "hidden" shadow within the alchemical gold.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLV.

<margin>Concept: Continuity</margin>
Just as in a round palace or one of spherical shape, a fire lit in one place illuminates all the walls around it, both the upper and lower parts—except for those places where a table or board standing in the middle blocks it with its presence and allows a dark shadow—so also the Sun, positioned in that great palace of the sky or vaulted Theater, illuminates with its rays the entire concavity of heaven and the translucent bodies contained within it that are receptive to light (that is, all the stars, both wandering <note>the planets</note> and fixed), unless where the density of the intervening Earth forbids it. For there, a black and dark shadow, which is called night, remains until it is chased away by the presence of the sun, and its light is poured out and seen in its place. Therefore, shadow and night are the deprivation or absence of solar light; day, on the contrary, is its irradiation and surrounding flow. The shadow is that which cannot endure the sight of the sun; for that reason, it flees and hides itself now on this part of the earth, now on that, according as the sun is on the opposite side. The Sun and the shadow have never seen each other, although at any moment, if nature permitted it, they could; but the sun, hearing that the shadow is its enemy, always pursues it as it flees, yet can never capture it in its weariness, as Buchanan <note>George Buchanan (1506–1582), a Scottish scholar; the reference is to his poem *Sphaera*, which explains astronomical principles.</note> beautifully sang in his book on the sphere. Following the imitation and example of that great sun and its shadow, the Philosophers <note>the alchemists</note> observed that a black, cloudy, and fleeting shadow is also present in their own "sun" <note>the philosophical gold or sulfur</note>. Hence Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> says: "Son, extract from the ray its shadow." That is: see to it that you lead your sun around through the *primum mobile* <note>the "First Mover," the outermost sphere of the heavens which in ancient cosmology drove the daily rotation of all other spheres</note>, over which Vulcan <note>the god of fire</note> is placed, so that even that part of your Earth

<margin>The Antipodes: Cold</margin>
which is now covered by the shadowy night may enjoy the clear solar light. For unless the whole Firmament of heaven, with all its contents, were led around once every natural day (that is, every twenty-four hours) by the "first motion," but instead the Sun were moved only by its own "proper" motion (the second, called the annual motion), it would happen that the Antipodes <note>those living on the opposite side of the globe</note> who are beneath us would have night for nearly a six-month space of time, and we would have day; and afterward, vice versa, they would have day and we night. Thus the entire year would consist of only one night and one day, just as these alternating turns are now proven by experience and very true reasoning to occur under both poles. But it seemed far otherwise to Divine Providence, which therefore ordained the double motions of the planets, the first and the second, and thus distributed the year into many days.

<summary>The author compares the astronomical creation of night and day to an alchemical process, citing George Buchanan and Hermes Trismegistus to explain how the "shadow" must be removed from the alchemical Sun. He details how the rotation of the heavens (the primum mobile) prevents permanent darkness, a physical reality that mirrors the purification of the philosophical work.</summary>

<keywords>astronomy, alchemy, Sun and Shadow, George Buchanan, Hermes Trismegistus, primum mobile, Vulcan, Antipodes, geocentrism, planetary motion</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page concludes the astronomical metaphor from the previous page, where the author was discussing how the Sun's light interacts with objects to create shadows. It shifts from physical science into alchemical theory, identifying the "Philosophical Shadow" with the dark stage of the Great Work (the nigredo) and providing a chronological guide for the alchemical process based on the signs of the zodiac.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLV.

...[provided]: Moreover, that shadow and the sun together create day and night, which the sun alone could not do by itself, whose nature is to illuminate all opposite places and bodies, but not to create shadow, except accidentally by its absence. In the same way, the **Philosophical Sun** with its shadow makes day—that is, light—and night or darkness, namely **Latona** or **magnesia** <note>In alchemy, Latona and Magnesia refer to the "prima materia" or the dark, unrefined state of the matter.</note>, whose shadow **Democritus** <note>An ancient Greek philosopher often cited in alchemical texts as a foundational authority.</note> teaches must be destroyed and burned away by a fiery medicine <note>original: "ignito pharmaco"</note>, as is clear at the beginning of Book 3 of the *Golden Table* <note>original: "Aureæ mensæ"; this refers to Michael Maier’s own work, *Symbola Aureae Mensae* (1617).</note>. The utility of shadows in astronomical matters is so great that without them that science could hardly be completed; the **Chemists** also attribute to their shadows the fact that their art reaches perfection. For what is the Sun here without shadow? What a clapper <note>original: "pistillum," literally a pestle, but used here for the internal striking part of a bell.</note> is without a bell: the former indeed makes the first movement so that a sound may be given, but the latter gives the sound; the former is the pick <note>original: "plectrum"</note>, the latter the instrument; the former is the tongue, the latter the great mouth.

Shadow is a most vile thing, next to non-existence; so also the shadow of the **Philosophers** is something "blacker than black," as they call it, or "viler than seaweed" <note>original: "vilius algâ," a classical Latin idiom for something utterly worthless.</note>, not because of itself, but because of human opinion and its abundance. What is more useful than fire, what more precious than water, what more lovable than the earth which gives flowers and all lovely things? What more pleasant than air, which if cut off, all pleasant things cease to be? Nevertheless, because they are readily available for human use in their wide-spreading spheres, they are considered most vile by a backwards imagination.

Thus both the common and **Philosophical** shadow are disparaged. Those who dwell long in subterranean shadows, if they are suddenly brought out into the bright light of the sun, lose their sight and the sharpness of their eyes; so those who stay and labor in only the **Philosophical shadow**, and do not join the sun to it, are deprived of judgment and the eyes of the mind, and are frustrated in the result. At noon, when the celestial sun is high, the heat is greater and the shadow is smaller; so also here, when the heat is increased, the shadow is diminished, and vice versa. One must therefore begin when the sun turns back again from the southern side toward our zenith, in **Capricorn** <note>The winter solstice, marking the return of the light.</note>; and the first operation will be completed by the time of **Aries** <note>The spring equinox.</note>; then the <term>opus mulierum</term> <note>Translated as "the work of women," this alchemical term refers to a phase of the process that requires gentle heat and patience, often compared to washing laundry or cooking.</note> begins, lasting until **Leo**; and afterward labor follows upon labor, until the year takes its tail in its head, like a snake <note>A reference to the Ouroboros, the serpent eating its own tail, symbolizing the completion of a cycle.</note>, that is, until it is made complete.

Zwey <note>German: Two</note>

<meta>catchword: Zwey</meta>

<header>FUGUE XLVI. Reciprocal.</header>

# Two eagles, one from the East and the other from the West, come together.

*Let the first voice begin from the start:*

Jupiter is said to have sent twin eagles from Delphi to the Eastern and Western regions.

### XLVI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

> Jupiter of Delphi sent out two eagles,
> Toward the sunrise and the sunset, as far as the earth ends,
> Because he wished to know and understand the center of the earth,
> Which (as they say) was seen again at Delphi at the same time;
> But there are two lights <note>original: "Schein"; in this context, it refers to the eagles or the alchemical principles they represent.</note>—this from the East and that from the West—
> Which come together and fix themselves into one.

<meta>catchword: EMBLE-</meta>

   (As the story goes) these returned to Delphi at the same time.

### XLVI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

> Jupiter of Delphi sent out two eagles,
> Toward the sunrise and the sunset, as far as the earth ends,
> Because he wished to know and understand the center of the world:
> These both returned at the same time to the place of Delphi.
> However, these are two stones, one from the morning and one from the evening,
> Which meet together in a very good way.

<note>Maier uses the Greek myth of Jupiter (Zeus) releasing two eagles to find the center of the world as an alchemical allegory. In the myth, the eagles met at Delphi, marking it as the "omphalos" or navel of the earth. In alchemy, the "two stones" represent the dual nature of the primary matter—often symbolized as fixed and volatile, or sulfur and mercury—which must be brought together from their "opposite" states to find the perfect equilibrium at the center of the work.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the illustration and epigram of Emblem 46 (regarding Jupiter's eagles finding the world's center at Delphi) into the detailed discourse. The text begins by interpreting the mythology of Apollo through an alchemical lens, identifying the god with the "Philosophical Sun."</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLVI.

Cicero, in his book *On the Nature of the Gods*, records that the most ancient **Apollo** was born of **Vulcan** and was the guardian of Athens. This opinion, if it is transferred to allegory as it ought to be, is most true; because **Vulcan** <note>The god of fire, representing the alchemist's heat</note> produces the **Philosophical Sun**, which is **Apollo**. 

But the view prevailed that he was born of **Jupiter**. When **Latona** bore the twins **Apollo** and **Diana** in her womb, whom she had conceived by **Jupiter**, the jealous **Juno** sent **Python**, a serpent of horrific size, to pursue and harass the pregnant woman. The wretched woman, after long wanderings, was finally carried by ship to the island of **Ortygia**, to her sister **Asteria**, who ruled there. And since that island was almost entirely flooded by the sea, she gave a place to the laboring **Latona**; hence it was called **Delos** <note>Greek for "visible" or "manifest"</note>, whereas it was previously *Adelos* <note>Greek for "unseen" or "hidden"</note>. 

There, then, she gave birth to the children. **Diana** came forth first from the womb, and she acted as a midwife to her mother as she labored in the birth of her brother **Apollo**. From this it came to pass that pregnant women in childbirth would invoke her Divinity, calling her either **Lucina** or **Ilithyia**, because she showed the light <note>original: "lucem"</note> to newborn infants by opening their eyes. 

**Apollo**, therefore, having been born and grown to adulthood, finished off the **Python**—the tormentor of his mother—with arrows. He also killed the **Cyclopes** because they had forged the lightning bolt for **Jupiter** to kill his son **Aesculapius**. **Jupiter** had struck **Aesculapius** down to the underworld because he had restored **Hippolytus**, who had been torn apart by horses, back to life. 

We have demonstrated in many places that these stories are purely **Chemical** <note>Maier uses "Chymica" to mean Alchemical; he views Greek myths as encrypted instructions for the Great Work</note>. For **Latona**, **Cynthia** <note>Another name for Diana, the Moon</note>, **Apollo**, and **Python** are the requirements of the art, which relate to one another in the manner described. But since these things were made public in the writings of the most ancient poets—such as **Orpheus**, **Linus**, **Musaeus**, and **Homer**—they provided an occasion for religion and the veneration of **Apollo** among the ignorant. 

Hence, **Apollo** was worshipped in many places in Europe and Asia, and innumerable temples were erected to him. Especially at **Delphi**, he had a temple to be venerated with ancient religion, in which many statues of solid gold and silver, of great weight and craftsmanship, were deposited by kings and princes, along with other most precious offerings stored in the sanctuaries by people of every rank for the sake of religion. **Pausanias** reports that a bronze skeleton of admirable craftsmanship was hung in the dome of the temple by **Hippocrates**. To Apollo...

<summary>This page provides an alchemical interpretation of the birth of Apollo and Diana, arguing that these myths describe the preparation of the Philosophical Sun (Gold) and Moon (Silver). It details the transition of the island Delos from "hidden" to "manifest" and lists historical offerings at the Temple of Delphi.</summary>

<keywords>Apollo, Vulcan, Latona, Diana, Python, Delos, alchemy, allegory, Delphi, Hippocrates, Philosophical Sun</keywords>

### DISCOURSE XLVI.

Also consecrated was that famous **tripod**, which was given to **Pelops** by **Mulciber** <note>Another name for Vulcan, the god of fire and smithing</note> and presented to him when he married **Hippodamia**, the daughter of **Oenomaus**, the king of **Elis**. This tripod was erected in the middle of the temple; sitting upon it, the **Pythia** <note>The high priestess of the Temple of Apollo at Delphi</note> received the inspiration of a **Demon** <note>original: "Dæmonis"; here referring to a supernatural spirit or "daimon" that provided the oracular breath</note> breaking forth from a deep chasm. Seized by this spirit, she would prophesy and give answers to those inquiring about the outcome of future events. **Delphi** itself was situated in **Boeotia** at the foot of **Mount Parnassus**. 

Near the temple was the prophetic spring, **Cassiotis**, which had a strange property: if burning torches were moved toward it, it extinguished them; but if they were moved far away, they were suddenly ignited and caught fire. Moreover, the water of this spring, when drunk, provided the power of prophecy, yet those who drank its waters were granted a shorter span of life.

Since there was a great gathering from all parts of **Europe** and **Asia** to the **Delphic oracle**, the poets told the story that this place—namely **Parnassus**—was in the middle of the earth. They attempted to prove this by the example of **Jupiter**, who had tested it by releasing two eagles. But since this matter does not rely on the reliability of history, it is not far from the truth to ascribe it to the **chemists** <note>The "chymici" or alchemists</note>, especially since **Apollo** is entirely chemical in origin, as has been said—even though a demon later confirmed the superstition of men under that name and gave prophecies. 

The **two eagles** are **two stones**, of which one comes from the East and the other from the West, which the **Philosophers** <note>Alchemists often referred to themselves simply as "The Philosophers"</note> have demonstrated in many ways. **Jupiter** sent them out as his armor-bearers. Furthermore, the **eagle** seems to be a friend to **Apollo**, or the **Sun**, because it tests its chicks against the Sun; if they cannot endure its gaze, it rejects them as degenerate. Its feathers are said not to rot when mixed with other things, and even to devour the feathers of other birds, and they easily admit **gilding** <note>original: "inaurationem"; a reference to the alchemical process of making gold</note>. 

The eagle does not die of old age or sickness, but of hunger. For the growing curvature of its upper beak eventually prevents it from being able to take food; when this beak is cast off, the eagle plunges itself three times into a spring, and in that way it is returned to youth. Hence the **Psalmist** says: *Your youth shall be renewed like the eagle's* <note>A reference to Psalm 103:5</note>. Among flying creatures, it is never struck by lightning. It is in a state of constant war with the **dragon**, who for that reason stalks the eagle's eggs. All these gifts of its nature have provided the reason why the **Philosophers** proclaim the **eagle** in their **Art** and liken the **Stone** to it. Since countless examples of this matter are found in their books, we shall not bring forward more here.
