# DISCOURSE XLVII.

Philosophers in many places mention two stones given to us for free, such as Isaac <note>likely Isaac Hollandus, a 15th-century Dutch alchemist</note>, Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist</note>, and others, among whom Avicenna says that they lie cast out in the dung, neglected by the common people, which, if they are joined together, complete the mastery. Some extol the "Western Mercury," which preferred itself to gold and overcame it. But best of all, the author of the *Consilium Conjugii* <note>The *Counsel on the Marriage of the Sun and Moon*, an influential alchemical text</note>, drawing from the *Letter of Aristotle*, describes the two stones where he says: *That there are two principles of this art, a white and a red one of wonderful nature: The white begins to appear upon the face of the waters at sunset, hiding itself until midnight, and afterwards it turns into the deep; but the red works in the opposite way because it begins to rise over the waters at sunrise until midday, and afterwards it descends into the deep.* <note>original: "Quod hujus artis duo sunt principales, albus & rubeus mirabilis naturæ: Albus in occasu solis incipit apparere super facies aquarum, abscondens se usque ad mediam noctem & postea vergit in profundum: Rubeus verò ex opposito operatur quia incipit ascendere super aquas in ortu solis usque ad meridiem & postea descendit in profundum."</note> These stones, therefore, are the aforementioned waters sent out by Jupiter at Delphi; <note>Refers to the myth of Zeus releasing two eagles from opposite ends of the earth to find its center, the Omphalos or "navel-stone."</note> these are also the wolf and the dog coming from different or opposite regions of the earth, of which one has bitten the other, and both have become rabid, as Rhasis <note>Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (865–925), a Persian polymath and alchemist</note> testifies in his *Letter*. 

These stones are the truest <term>Bezoar</term> <note>A mass found in the stomach or intestines of animals, historically prized as a universal antidote to poison.</note>, of which the East Indies sends the most excellent kind, taken from the bellies of wild beasts, while the West Indies—Peru—gives another of less efficacy, taken from tame animals <note>This likely refers to bezoars found in llamas or alpacas, as opposed to those from Persian wild goats.</note>. Thus the East gives the most ferocious wolf, and the West gives the dog familiar to men; that is, <term>Sulphur</term> comes from the Eastern region and <term>Mercury</term> from the Western. From these, the latter is soft and manageable, while the former is choleric and angry. As soon as these two encounter one another, they fall into mutual biting. The dog, notable for its size, wins the first victory by prostrating the wolf and rendering it half-dead. But the wolf, regaining its strength, casts down the dog and presses upon it until total slaughter; meanwhile, it receives no fewer or less lethal wounds from the dog than it had inflicted, until they finish and kill one another with mutual bites. 

*Concerning the wolf, Rosinus says to Euthicia <note>Rosinus is the Latinized name of Zosimos of Panopolis (c. 300 AD); Euthicia was his student.</note> that it is a victorious soldier of the two, strengthened, of great price and intense vigor, piercing bodies when it meets them; it is white in appearance, red by experiment, and it is the male who has taken the moon as his wife, whom some think to be gold of a most precious connection, whose congealed state is never dissolved, nor are its traces deleted, which God has granted to the holy and chosen Philosophers: Know, that nature took* <note>original: "De lupo Rosinus ad Euthiciam, inquit quod sit miles victor duorum roboratus multi precii & intensissimi roboris, perforans corpora, cum iis obviat, & est albus apparitione, rubeus experimento, estq; mas qui lunam duxit in uxorem, quem nonnulli putant aurum esse preciosissimæ conexionis, cujus congelatum nunquam dissolvitur, neque vestigia delentur quod Deus sanctis Philosophis & electis largitus est: Scito, quod natura cepit"</note>

<summary>This page explains the chemical allegory of the fighting wolf and dog as the union of Sulphur (from the East) and Mercury (from the West). Maier cites various authorities to identify these "stones" as common but powerful substances that must undergo a violent mutual transformation to succeed in the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>Bezoar, Sulphur, Mercury, Avicenna, Aristotle, Rosinus, alchemy, duality, East and West</keywords>

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XLVII, further exploring the chemical marriage and conflict between two symbolic substances—the Oriental Wolf and the Occidental Dog—which represent the active and passive principles of the alchemical Work.</meta>

<page-num>199</page-num>

### DISCOURSE XLVII.

I would compare it as an enemy. Thus he <note>Referring to the author of the *Consilium Conjugii* mentioned on the previous page</note> says. And soon after, he says: because sulphur is very strong and fights against the fire, it both contains and is contained. For from these joined together, a most precious color emerges, and the sulphur, which naturally flees <note>fleeing refers to volatility, or the tendency of a substance to evaporate when heated</note>, can never flee afterward because the soul has likewise pierced it, and the tincture of the soul is mixed into the body; the body has contained the soul and prevented its natural flight. And then, to one asking which of the stones is stronger, he replies: The stone that is not a stone <note>original: "Lapis qui non est lapis" — a classic alchemical paradox describing the Elixir, which has the weight of a stone but properties beyond nature</note> is stronger than the other enemy; but the red is stronger than that which strengthened its companions by its own fortitude. Therefore, the **Oriental Wolf** is stronger than the **Western Dog**, although it does not enjoy the outcome of victory, but dies together with its enemy: out of both, however, a staining poison <note>The word "poison" here refers to a potent, transformative chemical agent</note> is made. The difference between a wolf and a dog is slight, since the Molossian <note>A large, ancient breed of guard dog</note> or very large dog displays a wolf-like form and appearance, so that it seems to be a wolf by origin, but made tame through long breeding. In the same way, **sulphur** and **mercury** differ little from each other; because the former takes its origin from the latter, or the latter from the former. Mercury indeed produced sulphur, but sulphur purified Mercury and made it such as it is. Regarding these things, the same **Rosinus** <note>Rosinus is a common medieval corruption of the name Zosimos of Panopolis, a 3rd-century Egyptian-Greek alchemist</note>, when he asks *Whence comes its color?* answers: *from its most intense bitterness.* And she asks: *Whence comes its bitterness and intensity?* He answers: *from the impurity of its metal.* And she: *Does its red color never prevail?* He answers: *Yes indeed.* And she: *Is it not even hotter than fire?* He answers: *Fire, in comparison to it, is like water in comparison to fire.* And she: *Is it stronger than fire?* He answers: *No.* And she: *Why then do you assert that it is stronger than fire?* He answers: *Because when fires encounter each other, one consumes the other.* It is clear, therefore, that one becomes the nourishment and food of the other, and as much as it decreases for one, so much it increases for the other, until the increasing one prevails and the **dragon** has devoured the **serpent**. In great battles it often happens that those who have suffered a greater slaughter hold the field and victory; so too the dog, though prostrated, has not yet fallen completely defeated, since he holds the enemy so tightly that the latter cannot live without him, nor can the former die without the latter.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a combat between a wolf and a dog to describe the chemical union of sulphur and mercury, emphasizing how they consume and stabilize one another to create a "staining" red tincture. It highlights the paradoxical nature of the alchemical "stone" and its relationship to heat and impurity.</summary>

### XLVIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

To a King mighty in goods and people, a certain spring water above all others,
Which he commanded his servants to bring, was pleasing;
He drank this often, until he found his veins burdened <note>original: *beschwerd* — meaning heavy, obstructed, or oppressed by the "watery" humor</note> by it;
Quite misshapen <note>original: *ungestalt* — referring to a loss of the healthy, "royal" form due to dropsy or chemical impurity</note>, he was taken in by famous doctors,
By whom he was purged through sweat, stool, and also through the mouth;
In that hour, his cheeks appeared as red as roses. <note>The "redness" signifies the achievement of the Rubedo, the final stage of the alchemical process where the matter attains its full strength and perfection.</note>

It details a medical-chemical process of purging impurities to achieve the final "red" state of the Work.</summary>

# EMBLEM XLVIII. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

<image-desc>A copperplate engraving depicting an alchemical allegory. The king is wearing a crown, an ermine-trimmed robe over ornate armor, and decorative boots. Two attendants stand behind the king, one of whom holds a halberd. # EPIGRAM XLVIII.

A King, mighty in wealth and in peoples, loved
The waters of a spring, which he demanded his servants bring to him:
These he drinks and drinks again, until his veins are filled;
Soon, pale and discolored <note>original: "Discolor"; this represents the *nigredo* or blackening stage, where the matter "dies" and loses its form.</note>, he is taken in hand by famous physicians;
Once he was purged by them—by sweats, by the bowels,
And by the mouth <note>These three purifications refer to the alchemical processes of sublimation, calcination, and distillation used to strip away "accidental" impurities from the "essential" matter.</note>—both his cheeks were soon stained with roses. <note>The "rosy cheeks" signify the *rubedo*, or the Red stage, the final perfection of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

**King, spring, water, physicians, sweats, cheeks with roses, purged**

<summary>This page illustrates the alchemical process of "The King's Sickness," an allegory for the dissolution of gold or the "fixed" principle in a mercurial solvent. It describes his subsequent purification by "physicians" to reach the final red stage of the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>King, dissolution, physicians, purification, rubedo, health, spring water, Michael Maier</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues the prose commentary (Discursus) for Emblem XLVIII. The previous page introduced the King’s illness caused by drinking common water and mentioned the three stages of medical purging required for his recovery. This page expands on the historical and allegorical context of this "healing," comparing the wealth gained by royal physicians to the infinite riches of the Alchemical Stone.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>
### DISCOURSE XLVIII.

The most powerful King of the Persians, Xerxes, while leading his army through dry and uncultivated lands under the sweltering heat, did not refuse a draught of turbid water offered by a certain soldier, though he was parched with thirst. Instead, he drank it most gratefully and adorned the one who brought it with a most ample reward. Indeed, if anyone were to travel through the borders of Persia at this very time (as some very recent histories testify), it is said that they would very rarely find springs of sweet water, since the stagnant waters are salty <note>original: "falsæ"; referring here to brackish or saline water common in desert regions</note>, and the soil itself offers a vast abundance of saltiness upon its surface. In that same way, the King mentioned by the Philosophers, vexed by thirst, commanded that an abundance of sweet water be provided for him; once brought, he drank to satiety, as is evident to anyone from Merlin’s allegory <note>The "Allegory of Merlin" is a famous alchemical text where a king drinks water and becomes bloated, eventually requiring a "dissolution" and "rebirth" to be cured.</note>.

The healing of the sick and discolored King is undertaken by various physicians. The Egyptians, having administered their medicines, stirred up humors that were still raw—which Hippocrates <note>The "Father of Medicine" (c. 460 – c. 370 BC).</note> asserts ought first to be concocted <note>term: concocted; in ancient medicine, this meant "ripening" or "digesting" the bodily fluids so they could be safely expelled.</note> before being purged, unless they are already fluid and wandering. For only then must they be led out quickly, lest they should by chance make an assault and rush into the more noble parts or internal organs. Consequently, dangerous symptoms occurred for the King, namely fainting <note>original: "lipothymia"</note> and sudden loss of consciousness <note>original: "syncope"</note>. However, the Alexandrian physicians, arriving last during this chronic illness, were considered more successful, for they restored the King to his former health.

It is a worthwhile labor to heal such a King, who, once made healthy, offers a benevolent hand and a serene face to his physician. We read that the cures of many were excellently recompensed by various kings: such as those of Democides by Polycrates, the tyrant of the Samians, with two talents; or those of Erasistratus (whom Pliny writes was a disciple of Chrysippus, born from the daughter of Aristotle), who was rewarded with a hundred talents by Ptolemy for healing King Antiochus, who was sick out of love for his stepmother Stratonice. We might also mention Jacques Coictier, the physician of the French King Louis XI, from whom he received four thousand crowns every month as a salary, not to mention others of more recent times. 

Yet the healing of *this* King <note>Referring to the "Alchemical King" or the Stone itself.</note> is rewarded with a far greater wage or prize. For as Hermes and Geber say in the *Rosarium* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (Rose Garden of the Philosophers), a seminal alchemical treatise.</note>: "He who shall once perfect this art, if he were to live a thousand thousand years and feed four thousand men every day, he would never be in want." Senior <note>Zadith Senior, an authority often cited in alchemical literature.</note> confirms this, saying: "He who possesses the Stone from which the Elixir is made is just as rich as he who possesses fire,"

<meta>catchword: can</meta>

<summary>This discourse compares the legendary rewards given to historical royal physicians with the infinite wealth promised to the alchemist who "heals" the King (the Matter). It emphasizes that the Alchemical Stone provides a self-sustaining source of abundance, likened to the way a single flame can light a thousand others.</summary>

<keywords>Xerxes, Persia, Hippocrates, Alexandrian physicians, Democides, Erasistratus, Jacques Coictier, Rosarium Philosophorum, Hermes, Geber, Senior, Elixir, alchemical wealth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the healing of the King from Page 202. Having established the King’s initial sickness and the medical preparation required, the text now transitions into the rewards of this healing—comparing the vast material wealth of historical figures like Pythius to the infinite, spiritual, and physical abundance of the completed Alchemical Stone.</meta>

<page-num>203</page-num>
### DISCOURSE XLVIII.

He can give fire to whom he wishes, and when he wishes, and as much as he wishes, without his own peril or loss. The father of Democritus <note>The Greek "laughing philosopher" (c. 460 – c. 370 BC).</note> was so wealthy that he provided a banquet for the entire army of Xerxes. Likewise, a certain extremely opulent man named Pythius offered the same Xerxes the wages for his entire army and provisions for five months, provided that he might be allowed to keep at home just one of his five sons—the youngest, who was the comfort of his old age—so that he would not be forced to go to the royal camp. However, the barbarian king, bearing Pythius's petition most indignantly, ordered the younger son to be cut into two parts and fixed upon poles on both sides of the royal road, through which the entire army was to pass, as Sabellicus <note>Marcantonio Sabellico (1436–1506), a Venetian historian known for his universal history, *Enneades*.</note> notes in Book 2, Ennead 3. Yet the riches of these men are nothing compared to the wealth of **this King**, which is without dimension or number. Once this King was healed and freed from the waters, all other kings and the powerful of other regions honored and feared him. And when they wished to see something of his wonders, they would place one ounce of well-washed Mercury <note>original: "Mercurii bene loti"; in alchemy, "washed" mercury refers to quicksilver purified of its surface dross, often using vinegar or salt.</note> into a crucible and cast upon it a single grain (like a grain of millet) of his nails, or his hair, or his blood; they would then blow lightly upon the coals and let it cool with them, and they would find **the stone**, such as I know.

This is he whom Count Bernard <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous alchemist who wrote about the "King" being dissolved in a bath to be reborn.</note> mentions, saying that he gives as much of a kingdom to six of his courtiers as he himself possesses—provided only that they wait until he has recovered his youth in the bath and is adorned in various garments: namely, a **black breastplate**, a **white shirt**, and **purple blood**. For then he promises that he will give to each of them from his own blood and make them participants in his riches.

<column-break/>

### XLIX. German version of the Latin Epigram.

As Phoebus <note>The Sun god.</note>, Mercury, and the Fire-god <note>Vulcan.</note>, as the fables show,
Placed their powerful seed into the hide of an ox,
And thus became three fathers at once to the great Orion;
So too it is said that there are three fathers to the Son of Wisdom:
For the Sun is the first, Vulcan is recognized as the second,
And the Artist <note>The Alchemist/Chemist performing the work.</note> is named as the third father.

<header>EMBLEM XLIX. Concerning the secrets of Nature.</header>

## The Philosophical Child recognizes three fathers, just like Orion.

<image-desc>A copperplate engraving depicting a scene from classical mythology, specifically the birth of Orion. Five men are gathered in a landscape with hills and trees. In the center, three figures representing the gods Phoebus (Apollo), Vulcan, and Hermes stand around a bull's hide spread on the ground. Hermes is identified by the caduceus lying at his feet. They appear to be performing the ritual of contributing to the hide to create Orion. To the left, a man in a simple tunic watches. To the right stands a warrior in a plumed helmet and classical armor, holding a staff or sword. In the far distance, a high mountain peak is visible.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XLIX.

> The story is told that Phoebus, Vulcan, and Hermes <note>Phoebus represents the Sun, Vulcan represents Fire, and Hermes represents Mercury or the Alchemical Spirit.</note>
> Deposited their seeds into the hide of an ox;
> And these three were together the fathers of great ORION: <note>In mythology, Orion was born from an ox-hide buried in the earth after three gods—Jupiter, Neptune, and Mercury (or Apollo, Vulcan, and Hermes here)—urinated upon it. Maier uses this unusual "triple paternity" as an alchemical metaphor.</note>
> In the same way, they say the Offspring of Wisdom <note>original: "Sobolem Sophiæ"; refers to the Alchemical Stone or the "Philosophical Child."</note> has three fathers:
> For the SUN is called the first, while Vulcan is the second,
> And the third father is he who excels in the art. <note>original: "huic præstans tertius arte pater." This third father is the Alchemist himself, whose skill completes the work of Nature and Fire.</note>

<summary>This page presents the 49th emblem of the collection, using the mythological birth of Orion to symbolize the "triple paternity" of the Alchemist's Stone. It identifies the three necessary components for success: the celestial influence of the Sun, the physical power of Fire, and the skillful Art of the practitioner.</summary>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XLIX, moving from the poetic epigram and image on the previous page to a prose explanation. It connects the biological concept of "superfetation" and the historical legend of Margaret of Henneberg to the alchemical allegory of the "Philosophical Child" having multiple fathers.</meta>

<page-num>206</page-num>

<header>DISCOURSE XLIX.</header>

**WOMEN** who prostitute themselves to various men rarely conceive viable offspring because of the confusion of different seeds. For Nature, in the generation of humans and most animals, does not allow for superfetation <note>the formation of a second fetus when one is already present in the uterus</note>, except perhaps very rarely. Thus, whatever offspring is born—whether one or many—comes from a single father and mother. This is evident from history and the outcome for those who judge otherwise, particularly in the case of Margaret, the wife of Count Herman of Henneberg. In the year 1276, she gave birth to three hundred and sixty-five children. All of them were baptized; the males were named John and the females Elizabeth. After they died, they were buried in the church at Loosduinen <note>original: "Lausdunensi templo"</note>, located one mile from the Hague <note>Haga comitum</note> toward the sea in Holland. To this day, one may still see the brass basin in which they were baptized and an inscription of the story. 

The cause of this event, they say, was that the Countess saw a poor woman carrying twins in her arms and called her an adultress, claiming it was impossible for multiple children to be produced in one conception by one man, but must necessarily come from different men. Thereupon, a curse was made by the poor woman—who knew herself to be pure of that crime—that the Countess might conceive as many children in her womb at one time and from one man as there are days in a year. This was indeed a miracle, yet it was a natural work that occurred through divine vengeance. 

In the Philosophical work <note>the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>, however—which is in other ways contrary to nature—this concept is easily admitted under the cloak of allegory. For here, one offspring is said to have three fathers, or even two, as well as mothers. Hence Raymundus <note>Raymond Lull, a 13th-century philosopher and alchemist</note>, as cited by the *Rosary* <note>The "Rosarium Philosophorum," a famous alchemical treatise</note>, says: "Our infant has two fathers and two mothers; and because he was dearly nourished from his entire substance in the fire, he never dies." 

Likewise, Dionysus (or Bacchus) is called "twice-mothered" <note>original: "bimater"</note>. When his mother was consumed by fire while he was still immature, he was snatched from her womb and sewn into his father's thigh, so that the father became a mother. But this is explained more clearly in the conception of Orion, who is said to have been born from the mixed seeds of Apollo, Vulcan, and Mercury enclosed in an ox-hide for ten months. These things would be entirely monstrous, and not merely mythical, if a secret of nature did not lie hidden beneath these coverings, which is not obvious to everyone. Lullius, in his *Theory of the Testament* <note>original: "Theorica testamenti"</note>, attributes just as many and nearly the same fathers to this same Philosophical offspring: namely, the Sun, who is Apollo 

->or<-

<summary>Maier discusses the biological rarity of multiple births from different fathers, citing the legend of Margaret of Henneberg's 365 children as a "natural miracle." He then transitions to alchemical allegory, where the "Philosophical Child" (the Stone) is said to have multiple fathers, like the mythical Orion or Bacchus.</summary>

<keywords>superfetation, Margaret of Henneberg, Loosduinen, alchemical generation, Raymond Lull, Orion, Bacchus, triple paternity, Philosophical Child</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XLIX. The previous page discussed the legend of Margaret of Henneberg as a natural (though miraculous) example of multiple births. This page pivots to the alchemical allegory, explaining how the "Philosophical Child" (the Philosopher's Stone) can be said to have three fathers: the Sun, Vulcan (Fire), and the Alchemist (the Craftsman).</meta>

<page-num>197</page-num>

<header>DISCOURSE XLIX.</header>

...or the celestial Sun is the first author of this generation, who by his inexpressible and secret or astral power works upon a certain matter known to the Philosophers <note>alchemists</note>, as if into the womb of a woman, and in it produces a son or offspring similar to himself. To this son he will later, by paternal right, hand over and leave his arms and the heraldic signs of his virtues—that is, the power to ripen the immature, and to tint and purify what is not yet tinted or purified. 

For whatever the Sun completes in a thousand years, this son of his will perform in half an hour. Therefore, so that a force a thousand times stronger may be created in him than in the Sun, the father hands him over to Vulcan <note>the Roman god of fire and the forge, representing the alchemist's furnace</note> for training, and to the craftsman at the same time, so that his noble character may be cultivated by them and his strength multiplied. Indeed, it is well known that much depends on habits formed from childhood; thus Achilles, Jason, and Hercules were handed over to be educated by Chiron <note>the wise centaur who tutored many Greek heroes</note> for the same purpose. For Milo of Croton <note>a famous Greek wrestler</note>, who as a boy carried a calf, eventually lifted an ox as a man through the strength of habit. 

Not without reason, moreover, besides the Sun, Vulcan and the craftsman are called the fathers of this boy. The first caused him to *exist*, while the others caused him to be *of such a kind* and *so great*. Truly, no equivalent fee <note>original Greek: "didactron"</note> can be paid to teachers for their instruction, just as no reward can fully repay parents for the act of generation. The parents fashion the body, but the teachers fashion the soul. If the soul is more powerful than the body, then no less gratitude is owed to the former than to the latter. 

In the birth of Orion <note>In myth, Orion was born from the combined efforts of three gods</note>, Mercury provided the matter, Apollo the form, and Vulcan provided the heat or the external efficient cause. So also it is fitting that in the Philosophical Work <term>Philosophical Work: the "Great Work" or Opus, the process of creating the Philosopher’s Stone</term>, three fathers should seem to have conspired to produce a single offspring, in which the delights of the Philosophers reside.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical allegory of the "Three Fathers" of the Philosopher’s Stone—the Sun, Fire (Vulcan), and the Alchemist—likening their roles to the education of ancient heroes. It argues that while the Sun provides the initial life, the Alchemist and the fire are equally vital in perfecting and empowering the "Philosophical Child."</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Sun, Vulcan, Fire, Alchemist, Philosopher's Stone, Chiron, Milo of Croton, Orion, Mercury, Apollo, education, Great Work</keywords>

Let a deep grave be dug for the poisonous dragon, to whom the woman
Shall be firmly bound by her body;
While he, like a husband, enjoys love with her,
She dies, and is buried in the earth together with him,

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page concludes the series of 50 emblems in Michael Maier's 'Atalanta Fugiens' (1617), continuing the exploration of the chemical "Great Work" through mythological allegory.</meta>

# EMBLEM 50. On the Secrets of Nature.
Dd
<meta>catchword: Dragon-</meta>

## The dragon kills the woman, and she kills him, and they are both bathed in blood.

### EPIGRAM 50

> *Let a deep grave be dug for the venomous Dragon,* <note>original: "Draconi." In alchemy, the dragon often represents crude, "volatile" matter—unrefined, acidic, or gaseous substances that must be stabilized.</note>
> *With whom a woman is tightly bound in her own embrace:* <note>The woman often symbolizes the "fixed" principle or the receptive "mercury" of the philosophers. Their union represents a chemical marriage where opposites are joined.</note>
> *While he tastes the joys of the marriage bed,*
> *She dies, and the Dragon is buried with her in the earth.* <note>In the alchemical tradition, death and burial are metaphors for "putrefaction"—the stage where materials are broken down and decomposed in a sealed vessel until they turn black.</note>
> *From this, his body is surrendered to death and stained*
> *With blood: This is the true path of your work.* <note>original: "operis" (the Work). The "blood" signifies the vital force or the appearance of a red color (rubedo) that heralds the completion of the alchemist's goal.</note>

<summary>This final emblem illustrates the concept of "putrefaction," where the union of opposing chemical principles results in a metaphorical death and burial. This stage is presented as the essential "true path" for achieving the final transformation of matter into the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Michael Maier, dragon, putrefaction, chemical marriage, transmutation, rubedo, Great Work</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous emblem's epigram, providing the philosophical discourse (Discursus) for the 50th and final emblem of the 'Atalanta Fugiens.' It explains the symbolic relationship between the dragon and the woman/eagle as a representation of chemical and elemental union.</meta>

### DISCOURSE 50.

<meta>catchword: post</meta>

The dwelling of dragons is in the caverns of the earth, but that of humans is above the earth in the nearby air; these two are contrary elements <note>original: "elementa contraria." In alchemy, Earth (the dragon) and Air (the human/eagle) represent the heavy, fixed principle and the light, volatile principle respectively.</note> and are ordered by the Philosophers to be joined, so that the action of one upon the other may occur. By the "woman," others understand what Basil Valentine says in his second "key": *For it is not useful,* he says, *for an eagle to place its nest in the Alps: For its chicks would die from the living cold on the mountain tops. But if you were to take a cold dragon—which has long had its habitation in the rocks and creeps forth from the caves of the earth—and join it to the eagle, and place them both in the infernal chair,* <note>The "infernal chair" (infernalis sella) refers to the alchemical furnace or the intense heat required for the reaction.</note> *then Pluto will blow a wind and elicit a fiery, volatile spirit from the cold dragon. This spirit, with its great heat, will burn the eagle’s feathers and stir up a sweat-inducing bath, so much so that the snow on the highest mountains will melt and become water; by this, the mineral bath is well prepared, and bestows fortune and health upon the king.* It is indeed remarkable that a cold dragon should emit a fiery spirit from itself; yet experience declares this to be true in the case of burnt serpents, which emit a poisonous flame that infects those standing nearby.

Nor is it for nothing that dragons are called fire-breathing guardians of chemical treasures <note>original: "thesaurorum chymicorum." Maier interprets classical myths as coded instructions for alchemy.</note>, such as the Golden Fleece, the gardens of the Hesperides, Cadmus’s dragon, and others like them. This dragon lives in the narrow places of subterranean rocks, where it must be captured and joined to the eagle or the woman—to the woman in a grave, or to the eagle, if you prefer, in a nest: for the nature of the dragon is otherwise to lie in wait for the eagle’s eggs and to wage a deadly war with the eagle.

There are Greek writers who report that a dragon once loved a girl and lay with her. What wonder is it then if the Philosophers wish their dragon to be enclosed in a single cavern with a woman? Greverus <note>Jodocus Greverus, a 16th-century author of alchemical texts.</note> joins red and black dragons in the deepest abyss of a mountain and burns them with fire; and as the black ones perish, he says the guardian of the mountain searches for them everywhere and brings them into the mountain. Merlin <note>Referring to the "Allegory of Merlin," a popular alchemical text attributed to the legendary figure, describing a king's death and rebirth.</note> in his vision (unless it be counterfeit) makes mention of a white and a red dragon; these dragons, whoever they may be, whether one is a woman or a female dragon, act upon each other until both die and emit blood from their wounds, with which they are drenched. Here, the dragon is understood as the elements of earth and fire, and the woman as those of air and water; hence the <term>Clangor Buccinae</term> <note>A famous alchemical treatise whose title translates to "The Clang of the Trumpet."</note> says that the dragon is the matter remaining at the bottom...

<summary>This discourse concludes the work by explaining the elemental symbolism of the dragon and woman, citing famous alchemical authorities like Basil Valentine and Merlin to describe the volatile and fixed principles of the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Michael Maier, Basil Valentine, Merlin, dragon, elements, chemical marriage, Clangor Buccinae, transmutation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the 50th Emblem. The previous page discussed the nature of the dragon and the eagle as metaphors for chemical principles, ending with a description of dragons as guardians of hidden depths. This page begins by describing the process following the distillation of water.</meta>

### DISCOURSE 50.

after the distillation of water from it: And from **Hermes**: *The water of the air, existing between heaven and earth, is the life of every thing. For this water dissolves the body into spirit, and makes the living from the dead, and creates the marriage between the man and the woman. Indeed, it performs the entire benefit of the art.* <note>original: "Aqua aëris inter cælum & terram existens..." Hermes Trismegistus is the legendary founder of alchemy; this "water of the air" refers to a volatile substance that acts as a universal solvent.</note> Concerning the earth, he also says: *And understand further, that this very earth which we tread upon is not the true element, but rather it is "elementated" from its own true fifth element: Nor does the elemental fifth substance depart from its elemental body from which the earth is formed.* <note>original: "quinta substantia." The "fifth element" or quintessence was believed to be the pure, celestial essence latent within physical matter.</note> And later: *But in the center of the earth is a virgin and the true element which fire cannot burn:* This is the dragon of which we speak, creeping into the very center of the earth, where, since there is great heat, it conceives a fiery tide within itself, with which it burns the woman or the eagle.

The **woman** or the **eagle**, however, is aerial water; which some call the *white* or *celestial eagle*, and they labor to create it from common Mercury or sublimated salts, following in this the lead of certain blind men in this art, pretending to be lynx-eyed; <note>original: "lynceos." A reference to Lynceus, the Argonaut famous for his superhumanly sharp sight. Maier is criticizing those who claim to have "vision" but follow false chemical paths.</note> But truly I say to you, says **Count Bernard** <note>Bernard of Trevisan, a 15th-century alchemist.</note> in his *Epistle*, that *no water dissolves a metallic species by natural reduction, except that which remains with it in matter and form, and which the metals themselves can recongeal:* And later: *Nor does water belong to bodies in solutions if it does not remain with them in congealing:* And a little later: *Truly I say to you, that the oil which naturally waxes <note>The term "incerans" (waxing) refers to the process of softening a solid substance to a wax-like consistency so it can be combined with others.</note> and joins natures together, and naturally introduces the medicine into other bodies to be tinged, is not composed of anything foreign, but only from the vitals of the body to be dissolved.*

Thus, the **eagle** and the **woman**, as well as the **dragon** and almost the secrets of the entire art, are understood once this is grasped; these things we have perhaps exposed and declared too openly to the "sons of doctrine" <note>A traditional term for students or initiates of the alchemical art.</note> in the open lap of nature, so that from this, **GLORY BE TO GOD.**

It serves as a final revelation for the reader, emphasizing that the secrets of the Great Work are found in the marriage of internal, natural principles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Discourse 50, focusing on the metaphorical union of the dragon and the eagle (or woman). The previous page concluded by identifying the "woman" or "eagle" as aerial water, while the "dragon" represents the fire latent within the earth's center.</meta>

### DISCOURSE 50.

The dwelling of **dragons** is in the caverns of the earth, while that of **men** is above the earth in the nearest air; these two are contrary elements, and the **Philosophers** <note>In alchemical literature, the "Philosophers" are the masters of the Great Work who wrote the foundational texts.</note> command them to be joined, so that the action of one may take place upon the other. By the "woman," however, others understand—as **Basilius** <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist. This refers to the "Twelve Keys of Basil Valentine," a famous series of alchemical allegories.</note> says in his second key: *For it is not useful*, he says, *for the eagle to place its nest in the Alps: for its chicks would die from the cold at the mountain peaks. But if you add the cold dragon, who has had his dwelling in the rocks for a long time and creeps forth from the caves of the earth, and place them both in the "infernal chair,"* <note>original: "infernalem sellam." This refers to the "Athanor" or alchemical furnace designed for steady, prolonged heat.</note> *then **Pluto** <note>Pluto, god of the underworld, here symbolizes the subterranean or bottom-fed heat of the furnace.</note> will blow the wind, and from the cold dragon he will draw out a fiery volatile spirit, which by its great heat will burn the eagle’s feathers and stir up a sweat-inducing bath, so that the snow on the highest mountains melts and becomes water; by which the mineral bath is well prepared, and grants fortune and health to the king.* It is indeed a marvel that a cold dragon would emit a fiery spirit from itself; yet experience declares this to be true in the case of burnt serpents, which emit a poisonous flame that infects those standing nearby. 

Nor are **dragons** called the flame-vomiting guardians of chemical treasures in vain, such as those of the **Golden Fleece**, the **Gardens of the Hesperides**, **Cadmus**, and others like them. <note>These mythological references describe the "Secret Fire" or the crude matter that must be conquered to obtain the "Gold" of the Philosophers.</note> This dragon lives in narrow places among subterranean rocks; it must be captured there and joined to the eagle or the woman—to the latter in a tomb, to the former, if you prefer, in a nest. For the nature of the dragon is otherwise to lie in wait for the eagle's eggs, and it wages a deadly war with the eagle. There are those among the **Greek writers** who report that a dragon once loved a young girl and lay with her. What wonder is it, then, if the Philosophers wish their dragon to be shut up in one cavern with a woman? 

**Greverus** <note>Jodocus Greverus, a 16th-century author who wrote on the transformation of metals.</note> joins red and black dragons in the lowest abyss of a mountain and scorches them with fire; and as the black ones perish, he says the guardian of the mountain searches for them everywhere and carries them into the mountain. **Merlin** in his vision (unless it be a forgery) <note>original: "supposititia." This refers to the *Allegoria Merlini*, a popular alchemical text attributed to the legendary wizard.</note> makes mention of a white and a red dragon. These dragons, whoever they may be—whether one is a woman or a female dragon—act upon each other until both die and pour out blood from their wounds, with which they are drenched. Indeed, here by the **dragon** the elements of **earth** and **fire** are understood, and by the **woman**, those of **air** and **water**. Hence the *Sound of the Trumpet* <note>original: "Clangor buccinæ," an influential alchemical treatise often cited for its descriptions of the "putrefaction" stage of the work.</note> says: *that the dragon is the matter remaining at the bottom*

<summary>This page explores the alchemical allegory of the dragon and the eagle, representing the union of fixed (earth/fire) and volatile (air/water) principles through the heat of the furnace. It cites various authorities like Basil Valentine and the Allegory of Merlin to explain how these opposing forces must "destroy" one another to produce the philosopher's stone.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of alchemical symbols in Discourse 50, moving from the commentary on Basil Valentine to citations from Hermes Trismegistus and Bernard of Trevisan. It clarifies the nature of the "aerial water" (the Eagle) and the "true element" of the earth (the Dragon), concluding the discourse with a warning against using common mercury or external substances.</meta>

### DISCOURSE 50.

after the distillation of water from it: And from Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the founder of alchemy, or the "Hermetic Art."</note>:

> *The water of the air existing between heaven and earth is the life of every single thing. For this water itself dissolves the body into spirit, and from the dead makes the living, and makes the marriage between the man and the woman. Indeed, it performs the whole benefit of the art:*

Concerning the earth also he says thus:

> *And understand further, that this very earth which we tread upon is not a true element, but rather is made of elements from its own true fifth element <term>Quinta Essentia (Quintessence)</term>: nor does the elemental fifth substance depart from its elemented body from which the earth is formed.*

And presently:

> *But in the center of the earth is a virgin and true element which fire cannot burn: This is the dragon of which we speak, working its way into the very center of the earth, where, since there is great heat, it conceives a fiery tide within itself, by which it burns the woman or the eagle.*

The woman indeed, or eagle, is aerial water; which some call the white or celestial eagle, and they busy themselves trying to make it from common Mercury <note>original: "Mercurio vulgari." Alchemists often warned that the "Mercury of the Philosophers" was not the same as the quicksilver found in shops.</note> or sublimated salts, following in this the lead of certain blind men in this art who pretend to be lynx-eyed <note>meaning sharp-sighted or perceptive.</note>; But truly I say to you, says Count Bernhard <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), an Italian alchemist famous for his long search for the Philosopher's Stone.</note> in his Epistle:

> *that no water by natural reduction dissolves a metallic species, except that which remains with it in matter and form, and which the metals themselves are able to re-congeal:*

And presently:

> *Nor does water belong to bodies in solutions, which does not remain with them in congelations:*

And a little after:

> *Verily I say to you, that the oil which naturally waxes <note>original: "incerans." In alchemy, "ceration" is the process of softening a substance until it has the consistency of wax.</note> and joins natures, and naturally introduces the medicine into other bodies to be tinged, is not composed of anything foreign, but only from the vitals of the body to be dissolved:*

Therefore, with this understood, the eagle and the woman, as well as the dragon and almost all the secrets of the art, are grasped—things which perhaps we have exposed and declared until now with a too-open bosom of nature to the sons of doctrine <note>The "sons of doctrine" are the students or initiates of alchemical knowledge.</note>, so that from here **GLORY BE TO GOD.**

<meta>This page serves as an "Errata" or correction list for the musical scores found throughout the book. It follows the index of fugues and addresses printing errors that occurred while the author, Michael Maier, was away from the press.</meta>

# ADVICE TO THE MUSIC-LOVER.

*I had certainly hoped to play the part of Prometheus <note>Prometheus: A figure from Greek mythology whose name means "forethought." Maier is saying he tried to plan ahead perfectly.</note> rather than Epimetheus <note>Epimetheus: Prometheus's brother, whose name means "afterthought." Maier regrets having to fix mistakes after the fact.</note> before this; therefore, I wrote and revised everything concerning those musical fugues with the greatest diligence. However, well-laid plans sometimes turn out poorly; and so, while I was away, many of the notes of these fugues were somehow transposed and not printed correctly as was desired. I am therefore forced to place here a corrective warning, especially for the preceding songs. Any skilled musician will easily notice, even without my warning, that the individual fugues <note>Maier uses "fuga" (fugue) to describe the canons where one voice follows another.</note> must be identical to each other in all values, and in the ascent and descent of individual notes—except that one voice follows another at different pitches. Hence, the reader may correct the typographical error himself if he encounters anything placed otherwise than correctly. In the meantime, I will list here several faults as examples (though outside the usual musical practice) that I noted while glancing through them:*

*In Fugue 2, page 16, line 3: let the fourth note be a quarter note <term>semiminima: a quarter note or crotchet</term>, not on the pitch A, but on D, above the text Romulus.*
*In Fugue 6, page 32, line 1: let the sixth note be a whole note <term>semibrevis: a whole note or semibreve</term> on the pitch C, above the text "pingui" <note>original: "pingui" (rich/fat).</note>.*
*In Fugue 9, page 44, line 1: let the 19th note be a whole note on the pitch F, above the text "aurea" <note>original: "aurea" (golden).</note>.*
*In the same fugue, line 3: let the 15th note, a quarter note on the pitch A, be deleted entirely as superfluous.*
*In Fugue 11, page 52, line 1: the fifth note on the pitch G should not be a whole note, but a half note <term>minima: a half note or minim</term>, above the text "sobolem" <note>original: "sobolem" (offspring).</note>.*
*In the same place, line 2: let the 2nd note on the pitch G be not a half note, but a whole note, above the text "fert" <note>original: "fert" (it bears).</note>.*
*In Fugue 12, page 56, line 3: let the 13th note on the pitch G be an eighth note <term>fusa: an eighth note or quaver</term> with a dot on the preceding quarter note, above the text "Helicona" <note>Helicon: A mountain sacred to the Muses.</note>.*
*In the same place, line 4: let the 15th note be not a quarter note, but a half note, on the pitch D, above the text "petendus" <note>original: "petendus" (to be sought).</note>.*
*In Fugue 13, page 60, line 4: let the first note be a half note on the pitch E, at the text "Inde" <note>original: "Inde" (from there).</note>.*
*In Fugue 14, page 64, line 2: let notes 6 and 7 be eighth notes on the pitches B and A, above the text "humana" <note>original: "humana" (human).</note>.*
*In the same place, line 6: the B clef at the beginning is transposed.*
*In Fugue 16, page 72, line 4: let the 4th note on A be a quarter note.*
*In Fugue 17, page 76, line 14: let the note on F be a half note, not a whole note, above the text "ignis" <note>original: "ignis" (fire).</note>.*
*In Fugue 19, page 84, line 1: let the 14th note be a half note, not on C, but on D, above the text "ordine" <note>original: "ordine" (in order).</note>.*

*In the same place, line 2: from the beginning it should be thus:* <image-desc>five-line musical staff with four notes: a diamond-shaped whole note on the middle line (C), followed by two half notes on the space above the middle line (D), and a final diamond-shaped whole note on the middle line (C)</image-desc> *above the text "terræ quorum" <note>original: "terræ quorum" (of whose lands)</note>; for the first note should be a whole note on C, the second and third should be half notes on D, and the fourth a whole note on C.*
*In the same place, line 4: let the second note be a whole note, not on G, but on E.*
*In Fugue 22, page 96, line 4: let the 7th note be a half note on B, at the text "Saturni" <note>Saturn: The god associated with lead in alchemy.</note>.*
*In Fugue 23, page 100, line 1: let notes 23 and 24 on D and C be eighth notes, at the text "fecit" <note>original: "fecit" (he made).</note>.*
*In the same place, in line 3: let the 7th note be a dotted half note on C, at the text "mira" <note>original: "mira" (wonderful things).</note>.*
*In Fugue 24, page 104, line 1: let the 13th note be a whole note, not on E, but on G, at the text "cura" <note>original: "cura" (care/anxiety).</note>.*
*In Fugue 25, page 108, line 4: let the 5th note on the pitch B be not a quarter note, but a half note, at the text "funere" <note>original: "funere" (at the funeral/death).</note>.*
*In Fugue 26, page 112, line 3: let the 12th note on F be an eighth note.*
